biology.bigb
= Biology
{wiki}

<Biology> studies living systems: how <biological cells> maintain themselves, how <organisms> develop and reproduce, and how <evolution> generates diversity. Its scales connect <molecules>, <genes>, <biological cells>, <organisms> and the interactions studied by <ecology>. These are complementary viewpoints: a <protein>'s chemistry can explain a cellular mechanism, while <evolution> explains why inherited variants of that mechanism persist. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/1-1-the-science-of-biology[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: the science of biology].

Two organizing ideas make this immense subject navigable: shared cellular organization and descent with inherited modification. A <neuron> and a <gliding bacterium> differ dramatically in shape and behavior, yet both depend on a <cell membrane> and <metabolism>. Their differences become intelligible through specialization and <evolution>.

Keep observations separate from the <mathematical models> used to explain them. A measured <membrane potential> is evidence; an idealized <two-state ion-channel gating> equation proposes a mechanism. A <mathematical model> earns trust through predictions and comparisons with measurements. Its assumptions delimit its usefulness, and disagreement can reveal missing biology rather than a faulty measurement.

\Include[biology-author]
\Include[biology-bibliography]

= Signal transduction
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

<Signal transduction> converts a detected signal into a change inside a <biological cell>. For chemical signals, distinguish reception by a <receptor>, intracellular <signal transduction>, and the response: altered <gene expression>, <enzyme> activity or <ion channel> opening, for example. A <cell-surface receptor> can relay a <ligand>'s presence across a <cell membrane> without the <ligand> entering the <cytoplasm>. Thus a <hydrophilic> signal need not cross the membrane to influence the interior. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/9-2-propagation-of-the-signal[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 9.2: intracellular signal propagation].

\Image[cell-signal-transduction-cascade.png]
{title=Reception, intracellular <signal transduction> and a cellular response}
{description=Original cartoon of a <ligand> outside a <biological cell>, a <cell-surface receptor> spanning its <cell membrane>, and intracellular relays branching toward <gene expression> or another response. Arrows denote causal steps, not transport of the <ligand> through the membrane. Shapes, distances and relay counts are illustrative.}

For a computed example of possible <signal amplification>, suppose one activated <receptor> activates five relay <proteins>, and each relay activates twenty target <proteins> during a chosen interval. If the targets are distinct and available, $1\times5\times20=100$ targets are activated. Those assumed counts illustrate multiplication, not a measured pathway or a universal gain. Actual <signal transduction> can branch, saturate or inhibit activity. Changes in <gene expression> and changes in existing <enzyme> activity are different responses. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/9-3-response-to-the-signal[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 9.3: cellular responses].

<Signal amplification>, <signaling feedback> and <signaling adaptation> are possible properties of <signal transduction>, rather than requirements of every cascade. Intracellular relays can use <proteins> or <second messengers>; different <biological cells> can interpret the same <ligand> differently because they contain different <receptors> and relay machinery. The useful and striking idea is that recognition and response are separable: an extracellular chemical message can control many internal tasks without itself performing those tasks. See <Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others/General Principles of Cell Communication>.

An original experimental route to this separation appears in T. W. Rall and Earl W. Sutherland's 1958 work on <cAMP>. They described a heat-stable factor formed from <ATP> in separated <biological cell> preparations; it affected <enzyme> activity in other preparations where the initiating chemical signals had no direct effect. This helped distinguish an initial signal from a molecule carrying its effect to another target. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0021-9258(19)77422-5[Rall and Sutherland, Formation of a cyclic adenine ribonucleotide by tissue particles, Journal of Biological Chemistry 232 (1958), pp. 1065–1076, introduction]{ref}

= Second messenger
{parent=Signal transduction}

A <second messenger> is a small intracellular <molecule> or <ion> that relays a received signal to target <proteins>. Its production, release, removal or binding changes its availability after signal reception. For a <cell-surface receptor>, distinguish the extracellular <ligand>, often called the first messenger, from the intracellular <second messenger>. An activated relay <protein> is not thereby a <second messenger>: the term identifies small chemical mediators, rather than every downstream component of <signal transduction>. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27481708/[Newton, Bootman and Scott, Second Messengers (2016): definition and classes]{ref}

Two examples clarify what changes. A <biological cell> can make <cAMP> from <ATP>; alternatively, release of stored <calcium> <ions> can raise the free <calcium> <concentration> in its <cytoplasm>. The latter need not increase the total <calcium> content of the <biological cell>. Availability to target <proteins>, not merely the total amount present, carries the message. Removal and sequestration help terminate such signals. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK10794/[Purves et al., Neuroscience, 2nd edition: second messengers and calcium storage]{ref} <The Cell: A Molecular Approach, 2nd edition by Geoffrey M. Cooper/Pathways of Intracellular Signal Transduction>{ref}

The experimental motivation is unusually tangible: can a <hormone>'s effect be transferred to machinery that barely responds to the <hormone> itself? In Rall, Sutherland and Berthet's 1957 experiment, washed cat-liver particles were incubated with <ATP> and <magnesium> <ions>, with or without <epinephrine> and <glucagon>, two <hormones>. After five minutes at $30\,{}^{\circ}\mathrm C$, the mixtures were heated in boiling water for three minutes, chilled, and separated to remove insoluble material. Small samples of the clarified extracts were transferred to a separate soluble dog-liver preparation, again supplied with <ATP> and <magnesium> <ions>. The readout was the change in <glycogen phosphorylase> activity over five minutes, not the amount of newly synthesized <protein>. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0021-9258(18)65045-8[Rall, Sutherland and Berthet (1957), Figure 4 and pp. 470–471: preparation and transfer]{ref}

The <hormone>-conditioned extract caused a larger increase in <glycogen phosphorylase> activity. Water, extract made without <hormones>, and <hormones> added directly to the soluble preparation produced smaller effects. These comparisons distinguish transferred activity from baseline activity, preparation effects and simple <hormone> carryover. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0021-9258(18)65045-8[Rall, Sutherland and Berthet (1957), Figure 4: controls and observations]{ref}

\Image[second-messenger-transfer.png]
{title=Separating <hormone> reception from a transferable intracellular effect}
{description=Original schematic of the classical extract-transfer experiment. Responses are qualitative comparisons, not reconstructed measurements; details of the separation and assay are abbreviated. See the primary references in the surrounding text.}

The inference is a transferable, heat-stable intermediary between the <hormone>-sensitive particles and the soluble machinery. This does not identify the intermediary merely from heat resistance, establish its direct target, or show that every <hormone> uses the same mechanism. Rall and Sutherland's 1958 paper reports the factor's identification as <cAMP> in accompanying chemical work, and follows its production from <ATP>. It also reports an important limit: washed heart and skeletal-muscle particles produced detectable <cAMP> with <epinephrine> under their test conditions, whereas brain particles produced similar amounts with and without it. Tissue and preparation matter. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0021-9258(19)77422-5[Rall and Sutherland (1958), introduction and Table IV: cAMP identity and tissue-dependent responses]{ref}

The useful and beautiful separation is between recognition and execution: an extracellular <ligand> can control intracellular <enzymes> through a chemically different intermediary. Making many <second messenger> <molecules> can support <signal amplification>, while different target <proteins> give the same mediator different consequences. A <second messenger> identifies a mode of relaying information; its presence alone does not specify the size or direction of the response. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27481708/[Newton, Bootman and Scott (2016): second messengers and their targets]{ref}

= Inositol trisphosphate
{parent=Second messenger}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{IP}_3$}

In <signal transduction>, <inositol trisphosphate> usually denotes inositol 1,4,5-trisphosphate, a small <second messenger> that can release stored <calcium> into the <cytoplasm>. It is distinct from the <receptor> sensing the extracellular <ligand> and from the <ion channel> carrying the eventual membrane <electric current>. The intracellular-injection experiment under <metabotropic receptor> illustrates that distinction.

= Cyclic adenosine monophosphate
{parent=Second messenger}
{wiki}

= cAMP
{c}
{synonym}

= Cyclic AMP
{synonym}

<Cyclic adenosine monophosphate> is a cyclic <nucleotide> made from <ATP>. It can serve as an intracellular <second messenger>, changing the activity of target <proteins> after a signal has been received. Different <biological cells> can respond differently because they contain different targets. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/9-2-propagation-of-the-signal[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, §9.2: cyclic AMP as a second messenger]{ref}

= Signal amplification
{parent=Signal transduction}

<Signal amplification> is an increase in a specified downstream response relative to an initiating signal. In a molecular-count description, one activated component can generate or activate many downstream <molecules>. Repeated <catalysis> by an <enzyme> supplies a mechanism: the <enzyme> remains available for another <chemical reaction>. Successive stages can multiply the response. For a biological example, Cooper describes the <epinephrine>-to-<glycogen phosphorylase> pathway, where production of many <cAMP> <molecules> and repeated downstream <protein> <phosphorylation> provide opportunities for <signal amplification>. This is an example, not a claim that every <signal transduction> pathway has the same stages or gain. <The Cell: A Molecular Approach, 2nd edition by Geoffrey M. Cooper/Pathways of Intracellular Signal Transduction>{ref}

Here is a counting toy extending the example under <signal transduction>. Suppose $N_0$ activated <receptors> each activate $a=5$ distinct relay <proteins> during a first interval. During a subsequent interval, each relay activates $b=20$ distinct target <proteins>. Assume enough inactive relays and targets, no shared targets, and no loss of activity during the count. Then
$$
N_1=aN_0,\qquad N_2=bN_1=abN_0.
$$
For $N_0=1$, this gives $N_1=5$ and $N_2=100$: the target-to-<receptor> count <ratio> is $100$. These are assumed integer counts, not measured rates or a universal physiological amplification factor. The intervals are sequential; the equations do not specify a time course.

\Image[second-messenger-amplification.png]
{title=A counting toy for <signal amplification> and a finite target pool}
{description=Original schematic and computed count curves, not measured biological data. The upper cascade assumes five relays per activated <receptor> and twenty distinct targets per relay. The lower comparison limits the entire target pool to $T=250$ initially inactive <proteins>, with enough time and access to activate every reachable target and no deactivation. The joining lines only guide the eye between integer counts.}

Availability limits the multiplication. As a second toy assumption, let there be only $T=250$ initially inactive target <proteins> shared by all branches, and suppose the branches can activate distinct targets until this pool is exhausted. Then
$$
N_2=\min(abN_0,T)=\min(100N_0,250).
$$
Two activated <receptors> give $200$ active targets, whereas three give $250$, not $300$. At three activated <receptors>, the total count <ratio> is $250/3\simeq83.3$; adding a fourth activates no additional targets. Thus total output per input and the increment caused by an extra input are different notions of gain. This finite-pool rule is an illustrative bound, not an <enzyme kinetics> law.

<Signal amplification> makes a small initiating signal capable of a larger response, but it does not supply the material or <energy> for downstream <chemical reactions>. In the <second messenger> transfer experiment, both stages still required added <ATP> and <magnesium> <ions>. Counts, changes in <concentration>, and changes in <enzyme> activity are different readouts; an amplification claim should name its input, output and observation interval. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0021-9258(19)77422-5[Rall and Sutherland (1958), methods: production and assay of cAMP]{ref}

= Hormone
{parent=Signal transduction}
{wiki}

A <hormone> is a chemical messenger released by <biological cells> that regulates target <biological cells> bearing suitable <receptors>. In endocrine signaling, <hormones> travel to distant targets through the bloodstream. The extract-transfer experiment under <second messenger> isolates part of this <signal transduction> from intact <biological cells>. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/9-1-signaling-molecules-and-cellular-receptors[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 9.1: hormonal signaling]{ref}

= Adrenaline
{parent=Hormone}
{wiki}

= Epinephrine
{synonym}

= Glucagon
{parent=Hormone}
{wiki}

<Glucagon> is a <peptide hormone> made by <alpha cells> in the <pancreatic islets>. Its mature <polypeptide> has 29 <amino acid residues>. <Alpha cells> store <glucagon> in <secretory granules> and release it by <exocytosis>. <Glucagon> is a message about fuel availability; <glucose> is the <sugar> whose supply it helps regulate. A falling <blood glucose> <concentration> favors <glucagon> release, whereas a rise usually suppresses it. <Amino acids> can also stimulate release, so a meal containing <protein> need not produce the same <hormone> pattern as a meal dominated by <carbohydrates>. <Endotext/Glucagon Physiology>{ref}

The principal <blood glucose>-raising target is the <liver>. In a <hepatocyte>, the <glucagon receptor> is a <G protein-coupled receptor>: activation of a stimulatory <G protein> drives <adenylyl cyclase>, increasing the <second messenger> <cAMP> and activating <protein kinase A>. Downstream <phosphorylation>, including the <phosphorylase kinase> relay to <glycogen phosphorylase>, favors <glycogenolysis>. <Glucagon> also supports <gluconeogenesis>. These are different supplies: <glycogenolysis> draws on a finite <glycogen> reserve, whereas <gluconeogenesis> makes <glucose> from other <substrates> and depends on their availability. Longer fasting depletes the <glycogen> reserve; a stronger <hormone> signal cannot release material that is no longer there. <Endotext/Glucagon Physiology>{ref} <Anatomy and Physiology 2e by J. Gordon Betts and others/17.9 The Endocrine Pancreas>{ref}

Do not copy the <liver> pathway into every target of <insulin>. <Skeletal muscle> uses its own <glycogen> reserve locally; its <glucose> uptake and the <liver>'s <glucose> output are different flows. Nor is direct <glucagon>-stimulated <lipolysis> in human <adipose tissue> as firmly established as its action in the <liver>. <Glucagon> action depends on the tissue, other <hormones>, and available <substrates>. <Endotext/Glucagon Physiology>{ref}

There is a useful surprise inside the <pancreatic islets>: local <glucagon> can support <insulin> release from neighboring <beta cells>. The <islet perifusion experiment> distinguishes that interaction from <glucagon>'s action on the <liver>. Thus the two <hormones> can oppose each other's effects on <blood glucose> while cooperating locally. <Delta cells> supply another local brake through <somatostatin>. <Intraislet glucagon signaling is critical for maintaining glucose homeostasis by Lu Zhu and others>{ref} <Anatomy and Physiology 2e by J. Gordon Betts and others/17.9 The Endocrine Pancreas>{ref}

= Islet perifusion experiment
{parent=Glucagon}

<Intraislet glucagon signaling is critical for maintaining glucose homeostasis by Lu Zhu and others> tested whether local <glucagon> supports <insulin> release. For human <pancreatic islets>, a computer-controlled flow system delivered a <buffer solution> at $1\,\mathrm{ml}/\mathrm{min}$ through a chamber holding 500 <pancreatic islets> on a nylon filter at $37\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$. A fraction collector sampled the outgoing fluid. With an <amino acid> mixture present, the experiment changed <glucose> from $3$ to $16.7\,\mathrm{mM}$ and compared <GLP-1 receptor> blockade with fluid lacking the blocker. The readout was released <insulin> and <glucagon>: blockade reduced <glucose>-stimulated <insulin> release without significantly changing <glucagon> release. <Intraislet glucagon signaling is critical for maintaining glucose homeostasis by Lu Zhu and others>{ref}

Complementary mouse experiments suppressed <alpha cell> activity using an engineered <G protein-coupled receptor>. <ELISA> assays of minute-by-minute effluent samples measured <insulin> and <glucagon> <concentrations>, providing the <hormone> release readout. Drug-treated control <pancreatic islets> lacking that engineered <receptor> checked for drug effects; adding <glucagon> restored <insulin> release in the suppressed preparation. Together, blockade and rescue support local <signal transduction> from <alpha cells> to <beta cells>. Isolated <pancreatic islets> omit intact circulation and nervous input; the human preparation came from five donors, and blocker specificity and donor variation limit generalization. A chamber response does not directly measure whole-body <blood glucose> control. <Intraislet glucagon signaling is critical for maintaining glucose homeostasis by Lu Zhu and others>{ref}

= Signaling feedback
{parent=Signal transduction}

<Signaling feedback> returns the effect of a downstream event to an earlier stage of <signal transduction>, closing a loop analogous to <feedback control>. Trace the causal arrows: if an earlier relay activates a later relay and the later relay changes the earlier relay's activity, the return path closes the loop. <Negative feedback> opposes the initiating change; <positive feedback> reinforces it. <Signaling feedback> is a possible property of <signal transduction>, not a requirement of every pathway.

For a schematic example, let relay $A$ activate relay $B$. If $B$ inhibits $A$, increased $A$ eventually produces an opposing influence: <negative feedback>. If $B$ activates $A$, increased $A$ produces a reinforcing influence: <positive feedback>. These signs describe effects on activity, rather than the direction in which <molecules> travel. A closed loop containing two inhibitions can have <positive feedback>, because inhibiting an inhibitor reinforces the original change. The sign belongs to the whole loop.

\Image[signaling-feedback-loops.png]
{title=<Negative feedback>, <positive feedback> and an <incoherent feedforward loop>}
{description=Original causal schematics. Arrows activate; flat-ended lines inhibit. The upper loops return from downstream relay $B$ to earlier relay $A$. In the lower <incoherent feedforward loop>, input $s$ drives both a fast activating branch $x$ and a slower inhibitory branch $a$; their contributions meet at readout $r$. There is no return arrow from $r$ to either branch or to $s$. Shapes and connections illustrate the distinction, rather than a measured pathway.}

<Negative feedback> can restrain excessive activation and help preserve a usable range of responses. <Positive feedback> can support a decisive transition: Alberts and colleagues describe an <enzyme> whose product increases its own activity. Such reinforcement can contribute to switchlike behavior, but a <positive feedback> arrow alone does not prove <bistability>. A different structure, an <incoherent feedforward loop>, sends opposing influences forward from the same input; inhibition there is not a downstream return. <Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others/General Principles of Cell Communication>{ref} https://cqb.pku.edu.cn/tanglab/pdf/78.pdf[Ma et al., Defining Network Topologies that Can Achieve Biochemical Adaptation, Cell 138 (2009), pp. 760–773: definitions on p. 764]{ref}

A worked <mathematical model> shows why <negative feedback> does not automatically produce complete <signaling adaptation>. Let $s$ be a fixed normalized input, $r$ a normalized response, $g\geq0$ a dimensionless <feedback gain>, and $\tau>0$ a <time constant>. Suppose response removal contributes $-r$ and a return proportional to the response contributes $-gr$. The <ordinary differential equation> is
$$
\tau\frac{dr}{dt}=s-r-gr=s-(1+g)r.
$$
Setting the <derivative> to zero gives the <equilibrium point> $r_*=s/(1+g)$. A displacement $\delta r=r-r_*$ obeys $\tau\,d(\delta r)/dt=-(1+g)\delta r$, so its decaying <exponential function> establishes <asymptotic stability>. For a unit input switched on at $t=0$, <initial condition> $r(0)=0$, $g=2$ and $\tau=1$ second,
$$
r(t)=\frac{1-e^{-3t/\tau}}{3},\qquad r(1\,\mathrm{s})\simeq0.316737,\qquad \lim_{t\to\infty}r(t)=\frac13.
$$
The <negative feedback> reduces the eventual response from $1$ to $1/3$, but this response rises monotonically and never returns to its zero baseline. Attenuation and complete <signaling adaptation> are different properties. This deliberately simple <mathematical model> specifies no molecular pathway; <integral action> provides a contrasting way to remove a lasting offset in a stable <feedback control> system. https://www.fbswiki.org/wiki/index.php/Feedback_Principles[Åström and Murray, Feedback Systems, Chapter 2: proportional and integral feedback]{ref}

= Negative feedback
{parent=Signaling feedback}

<Negative feedback> closes a causal loop whose net effect opposes an initiating change. In <signal transduction>, activation followed by a downstream inhibition of the earlier activating stage is one example of <negative feedback>. Loop sign alone does not establish <asymptotic stability> or complete <signaling adaptation>; see the worked <signaling feedback> example.

A useful biological example is <chemotaxis feedback regulation>: an <Escherichia coli> <biological cell> adjusts its response to a sustained attractant instead of remaining at its immediate response. Increased <CheA> activity promotes <CheB> <phosphorylation>; <CheB> reverses <receptor methylation>, opposing their activation. After attractant addition, reduced activity instead favors <receptor methylation> by <CheR>, restoring activity. This is a return from <receptor> activity to the machinery regulating that activity. The response need not return exactly at every dose. <Precision and Kinetics of Adaptation in Bacterial Chemotaxis by Yigal Meir and others>{ref}

A motivating measurement is the <chemotaxis FRET assay>. <CheY> and <CheZ> carrying yellow and cyan fluorescent tags were observed in immobilized <Escherichia coli> <biological cells> on a coverslip in a flow chamber. Adding and removing attractant changed the yellow-to-cyan emission <ratio>, reporting the association of phosphorylated <CheY> with <CheZ>. This measures a molecular readout upstream of motor behavior. Comparisons with <biological cells> lacking <CheR>, <CheB>, or both tested the dependence on <receptor> modification; these perturbations also change starting activity and sensitivity, so they are not pure changes of one <feedback gain>. <Receptor sensitivity in bacterial chemotaxis by Victor Sourjik and others>{ref}

The later <chemotaxis feedback regulation> experiment used only the <Tar receptor> and nonmetabolizable methylaspartate, supplied and removed by flow. Long recordings separated slow recovery from incomplete <signaling adaptation>; correction for bleaching addressed drift in the optical readout. Activity recovered incompletely after sufficiently large additions, with detectable imprecision already at $10\,\mathrm{\mu M}$ in that preparation. Restricting the <receptor> type and preventing consumption of the stimulus help interpret the perturbation, but limit transfer to other <receptors> and conditions. The readout infers <CheA> activity through <CheY>-<CheZ> association, rather than directly measuring every molecular step. <Precision and Kinetics of Adaptation in Bacterial Chemotaxis by Yigal Meir and others>{ref}

<Negative feedback> can also overshoot or sustain <oscillations> if its return arrives too late. The worked <signaling feedback> example gives the contrasting instantaneous case; the <delayed negative feedback model> below isolates the effect of timing. Loop sign describes causation, while <asymptotic stability> describes what happens to displacements over time.

\Image[negative-feedback-delay-modes.png]
{title=<Negative feedback> with an instantaneous or delayed return}
{description=Original synthetic <delay differential equation> modes, not measured <chemotaxis> responses. All variables and time are normalized. The same negative return coefficient is used throughout; only its delay changes. Each curve has its own matching history. A growing <oscillation> signals instability of this linear <mathematical model>, not a bounded biological rhythm.}

= Delayed negative feedback model
{parent=Negative feedback}
{title2=$d$}

A <feedback delay> is a time interval between an event and its returning influence. Multiple biochemical steps can introduce an effective lag; a fixed delay is an idealization, not a universal description of <signal transduction>. To isolate timing, let $x$ be a signed displacement from an <equilibrium point> and use normalized <dimensionless variables>:
$$
\frac{dx}{dt}=-x(t)-2x(t-d),\qquad d\geq0.
$$
The local removal is $-x$ and the returning influence is negative at every delay. At $d=0$ the <ordinary differential equation> reduces to the decay already illustrated under <signaling feedback>. For $d>0$ a <delay differential equation> requires a history on $[-d,0]$, not just an <initial condition> at one instant. Negative values of $x$ are displacements, not negative chemical <concentrations>.

An <exponential function> $x=e^{\lambda t}$ gives the <characteristic equation of a delay differential equation>
$$
\lambda+1+2e^{-\lambda d}=0.
$$
Putting $\lambda=i\omega$ and equating real and imaginary parts gives $\cos(\omega d)=-1/2$ and $\omega=2\sin(\omega d)$. The first crossing with $\omega>0$ therefore occurs at
$$
\omega=\sqrt3,\qquad d_c=\frac{2\pi}{3\sqrt3}\simeq1.20920.
$$
For this particular <mathematical model>, the <equilibrium point> is <asymptotically stable> for $0\leq d<d_c$. No nonnegative real <delayed feedback characteristic root> can satisfy the equation; the first loss of <asymptotic stability> is the indicated pair of imaginary <delayed feedback characteristic roots>. Differentiating the <characteristic equation of a delay differential equation> along a root yields
$$
\frac{d\lambda}{dd}=-\frac{\lambda(\lambda+1)}{1+d(\lambda+1)},\qquad \operatorname{Re}\left(\frac{d\lambda}{dd}\right)_{d=d_c}=\frac{3}{(1+d_c)^2+3d_c^2}>0.
$$
Thus the pair moves into growth as the delay increases through $d_c$. The neutral mode at $d_c$ is a <periodic function>, but its amplitude depends on history: this linear <mathematical model> does not select an attracting <limit cycle>.

The figure plots $x(t)=e^{at}\cos(bt)$ using a <delayed feedback characteristic root> $\lambda=a+ib$ and that same formula on the history interval. At $d=0.7$ the displayed mode decays; at $d=1.5$ it grows. These are exact modes up to numerical root accuracy, not step responses with identical histories. Molecular bounds and a changed return law would be needed to interpret what happens after an unstable biological response becomes large.

= Delayed feedback characteristic root
{parent=Delayed negative feedback model}
{title2=$\lambda$}

A <delayed feedback characteristic root> solves the <characteristic equation of a delay differential equation> and supplies an <exponential function> mode. Its <real part> determines decay or growth of that mode.

= Chemotaxis feedback regulation
{parent=Negative feedback}

In <Escherichia coli>, <chemotaxis feedback regulation> couples <receptor> activity to modification of the <receptors> by <CheR> and <CheB>. It is a molecular example of <negative feedback> supporting <signaling adaptation>, with precision and kinetics assessed by the <chemotaxis FRET assay> above.

= Chemotaxis feedback proteins

= CheA
{parent=Chemotaxis feedback proteins}
{c}

<CheA> is the signaling <enzyme> that promotes <phosphorylation> of <CheY> and <CheB> in the <chemotaxis feedback regulation> pathway.

= CheB
{parent=Chemotaxis feedback proteins}
{c}

<CheB> is an <enzyme> that reverses <receptor methylation> in <chemotaxis>; its regulation contributes to <negative feedback>.

= CheR
{parent=Chemotaxis feedback proteins}
{c}

<CheR> is an <enzyme> that catalyzes <receptor methylation> in <chemotaxis>, contributing to <signaling adaptation>.

= CheY
{parent=Chemotaxis feedback proteins}
{c}

The <phosphorylation> state of the <protein> <CheY> connects <receptor> signaling to motor behavior in <Escherichia coli>.

= CheZ
{parent=Chemotaxis feedback proteins}
{c}

<CheZ> is an <enzyme> that binds phosphorylated <CheY> and accelerates removal of its <phosphate>.

= Chemotaxis FRET assay
{parent=Chemotaxis feedback regulation}

The <chemotaxis FRET assay> uses <Förster resonance energy transfer> between fluorescent tags on <CheY> and <CheZ> to report their association. Interpreting this emission <ratio> as <CheA> activity requires the coupling between <CheY> <phosphorylation> and <CheZ> binding described in the experimental sources above.

= Positive feedback
{parent=Signaling feedback}

<Positive feedback> closes a causal loop whose net effect reinforces an initiating change. A loop with two inhibitory links can therefore have <positive feedback>. In <signal transduction>, a product that increases the activity of the <enzyme> making it illustrates reinforcement; the consequences depend on the full <mathematical model>, not just the loop sign. See <signaling feedback>.

A biological example is <cell-cycle activation feedback>. <Cyclin B> binds <Cdc2>, whose activity promotes activation of <Cdc25>; <Cdc25> removes inhibitory <phosphorylation> from <Cdc2>, permitting further activation. <Cdc2> also inhibits <Wee1>, an <enzyme> that inhibits <Cdc2> through <phosphorylation>. That second return contains two inhibitions and therefore also has <positive feedback>. These are regulatory paths, not a claim that every connection is a single direct molecular encounter. <Multisite phosphoregulation of Cdc25 activity refines the mitotic entrance and exit switches by Lucy X. Lu and others>{ref}

Reinforcement can preserve a decision after its initiating stimulus weakens: returning from the high state may require a lower input than entering it. The <cell-cycle hysteresis experiment> below tests this history dependence at matched <cyclin B> <concentrations>. A steep response alone is insufficient evidence of <bistability>, and finite observation times can make slow relaxation look like memory. A proposed switch must specify its input, readout and observation interval.

\Image[positive-feedback-switch-hysteresis.png]
{title=A synthetic <positive feedback> switch and its <hysteresis> thresholds}
{description=Original computed <equilibrium points> of a normalized saturating <mathematical model>, not <cyclin B> measurements or a fit to <Cdc2>. Filled intersections are <asymptotically stable>; the open intersection is unstable. With $\beta=1.8$, a slow sweep follows stable branches until their folds; with $\beta=1$, the same positive return has only one stable <equilibrium point>.}

= Saturating positive feedback
{parent=Positive feedback}

<Saturating positive feedback> limits the reinforcing rate as activity increases. Here is an explicitly synthetic <ordinary differential equation> for normalized nonnegative input $u$, response $x$, and return strength $\beta$:
$$
\frac{dx}{dt}=f(x;u,\beta)=u+\frac{\beta x^2}{1+x^2}-x,\qquad u\geq0,\quad\beta\geq0.
$$
The return is an increasing, nonproportional function of $x$, while removal is proportional to $x$. Time and activities are <dimensionless variables>. At $x=0$ the <vector field> points into the nonnegative domain, and $dx/dt\leq u+\beta-x$ prevents unbounded growth there. This makes the toy useful for asking about a bounded switch; it supplies no molecular mechanism for the chosen rate law.

At an <equilibrium point>, $f=0$. For a simple <equilibrium point>, the sign of the <derivative>
$$
f_x=\frac{2\beta x}{(1+x^2)^2}-1
$$
determines local <asymptotic stability>. If $\beta=1$, the maximum reinforcing slope is $3\sqrt3/8<1$, so $f$ decreases strictly: every $u\geq0$ has one <asymptotically stable> <equilibrium point> despite <positive feedback>.

Three <equilibrium points> become possible only if the reinforcing slope exceeds removal's slope somewhere. Its maximum is $3\sqrt3\beta/8$, at $x=1/\sqrt3$. Consequently this toy requires
$$
\beta>\frac{8}{3\sqrt3}\simeq1.53960
$$
for two distinct folds. This is a necessary threshold for a <bistability> interval in this toy, not a universal biochemical number. At a fold, both $f=0$ and $f_x=0$, giving
$$
(1+x^2)^2=2\beta x,\qquad u=x-\frac{\beta x^2}{1+x^2}.
$$
For $\beta=1.8$, the folds are at $(x,u)\simeq(0.350003,0.153563)$ and $(0.887240,0.094405)$. Between these input values there are two <asymptotically stable> <equilibrium points> separated by an unstable one. At $u=0.12$, the three activities are approximately $0.171331$, $0.621230$ and $1.127439$; the low and high ones are stable.

A sufficiently slow increasing sweep follows the low branch to $u_{\mathrm{on}}\simeq0.153563$ and then jumps upward. A decreasing sweep follows the high branch to $u_{\mathrm{off}}\simeq0.094405$ and then jumps downward: <hysteresis>. These are limiting quasistatic thresholds, with relaxation between input changes and negligible perturbations that could cause earlier switching. Finite sweep rates and fluctuations can alter observed thresholds. A switchlike response with only one stable <equilibrium point> does not have this equilibrium <hysteresis> mechanism.

= Cell-cycle activation feedback
{parent=Positive feedback}

<Cell-cycle activation feedback> is the reinforcement of <Cdc2> activation through <Cdc25> and suppression of <Wee1>. Its role is to help make transitions in the <cell cycle> decisive; the <cell-cycle hysteresis experiment> tests the resulting states rather than inferring them solely from circuit arrows.

= Cell-cycle hysteresis experiment
{parent=Cell-cycle activation feedback}

A 2003 study split a <Xenopus laevis egg extract> into portions with different histories. One received <calcium> to destroy endogenous <cyclin B> before receiving nondegradable <cyclin B>; the other received nondegradable <cyclin B> first, then <calcium>. The same sustained <cyclin B> dose was approached from low or high <Cdc2> activity. A <phosphorimager> quantified <histone H1> <phosphorylation>, sampling every $15$–$30$ minutes until an apparent stationary response. At $45$–$60\,\mathrm{nM}$, the dose maintained high activity but failed to induce it from the low state.<Building a cell cycle oscillator: hysteresis and bistability in the activation of Cdc2 by Joseph R. Pomerening and others>[pp. 3–4, Figure 3a–c: history protocols and H1 readout; p. 6, Methods: phosphorimaging]{ref}

Controls tracked labeled endogenous <cyclin B> disappearance after <calcium> treatment and persistence of nondegradable <cyclin B>, addressing unequal remaining input. The comparison supports <hysteresis> under extract conditions; finite observations cannot establish indefinite persistence. It does not isolate each reinforcing path or establish thresholds in intact <biological cells>.<Building a cell cycle oscillator: hysteresis and bistability in the activation of Cdc2 by Joseph R. Pomerening and others>[p. 3, control discussion; p. 4, Figure 3h; reported 60–90 minute approach to apparent steady state]{ref}

= Xenopus laevis egg extract
{parent=Cell-cycle hysteresis experiment}
{c}

A <Xenopus laevis egg extract> is a preparation of egg contents retaining biochemical machinery of the <cell cycle> outside intact <biological cells>.

= Histone H1
{parent=Cell-cycle hysteresis experiment}

<Histone H1> is the <protein> used as a substrate for the <Cdc2> activity readout in the <cell-cycle hysteresis experiment>.

= Phosphorimager
{parent=Cell-cycle hysteresis experiment}

= Cyclin-dependent kinase 1
{parent=Cell-cycle activation feedback}

= Cdc2
{synonym}
{c}

<Cdc2> is a <protein> <enzyme> regulated by binding <cyclin B> and by <phosphorylation>. The experimental source uses the historical name <Cdc2>.

= Cyclin B
{parent=Cell-cycle activation feedback}

<Cyclin B> is the regulatory <protein> binding <Cdc2> in the <cell-cycle activation feedback> example.

= Cdc25
{parent=Cell-cycle activation feedback}
{c}

<Cdc25> is an <enzyme> removing inhibitory <phosphorylation> from <Cdc2>.

= Wee1
{parent=Cell-cycle activation feedback}
{c}

<Wee1> is an <enzyme> inhibiting <Cdc2> through <phosphorylation>.

= Signaling adaptation
{parent=Signal transduction}

<Signaling adaptation> is a decline of a response toward its earlier level while the initiating <signal> remains present. Specify which response is measured and its baseline: complete <signaling adaptation> means eventual return to that baseline; partial <signaling adaptation> leaves a lasting displacement. The continuing <signal> distinguishes <signaling adaptation> from simply switching off the stimulus. <Signaling adaptation> describes behavior, while <signaling feedback> describes a causal arrangement, so neither implies the other.

By reducing a persistent response to background <ligand> <concentration>, <signaling adaptation> can make a <biological cell> sensitive to changes rather than only to absolute levels. Resetting the response helps leave room for another change over a useful range of <ligand> <concentrations>. This is valuable in <chemotaxis>: swimming <bacteria> encounter changing chemical conditions, and their <signal transduction> influences whether a run continues or a tumble occurs. <Signaling adaptation> helps separate a change encountered during motion from a lasting background. This usefulness does not imply exact return or equal sensitivity at every possible <concentration>. <Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others/General Principles of Cell Communication>{ref} https://groups.csail.mit.edu/mac/projects/amorphous/6.978/papers/doyle-PNAS-2000.pdf[Yi, Huang, Simon and Doyle, PNAS 97 (2000), pp. 4649–4653: chemotaxis and adaptation]{ref}

Here is a solvable <incoherent feedforward loop> toy that exhibits complete <signaling adaptation> without <signaling feedback>. The input $s(t)$ jumps from $0$ to $1$ at $t=0$ and remains $1$, a <Heaviside step function>. Let $x$ be a fast activating branch, $a$ a slower inhibitory branch, and $r=x-a$ a readout. The activities $s,x,a,r$ are <dimensionless variables>; physical time is in seconds. Choose <time constants> $\tau_x=1$ second and $\tau_a=5$ seconds, with <initial conditions> $x(0)=a(0)=0$:
$$
\tau_x\frac{dx}{dt}=s-x,\qquad \tau_a\frac{da}{dt}=s-a,\qquad r=x-a.
$$
Both branches read $s$ directly. Neither branch depends on $r$, and $a$ does not change $x$: the subtraction only defines the readout. Thus the slow opposing influence is feedforward, with no closed <feedback control> loop.

For $t\geq0$, solving the two <ordinary differential equations> gives
$$
x(t)=1-e^{-t/\tau_x},\qquad a(t)=1-e^{-t/\tau_a},\qquad r(t)=e^{-t/\tau_a}-e^{-t/\tau_x}.
$$
Writing $t$ for the numerical time in seconds, these are $x=1-e^{-t}$, $a=1-e^{-t/5}$ and $r=e^{-t/5}-e^{-t}$. Initially $r=0$, then $x$ rises faster than $a$, producing a pulse. At long times $x$ and $a$ both approach $1$, so $r$ returns to $0$ despite sustained $s=1$. For this upward step $e^{-t/5}\geq e^{-t}$, hence $r(t)\geq0$. The striking feature of this <signaling adaptation> toy is that both branches remain active while the readout becomes quiet: the continuing background and the response to its arrival are separable.

\Image[signaling-adaptation-step-response.png]
{title=A sustained unit input and a transient readout in an <incoherent feedforward loop> toy}
{description=Original computed curves for <time constants> of $1$ and $5$ seconds, zero <initial conditions>, and normalized <dimensionless variables>. The upper panel shows the sustained input and the two branches; the lower panel shows the readout $r=x-a$, its maximum near $2.0118$ seconds, and its approach to the zero baseline. Values and time scales are chosen for illustration, not fitted to a measured <signal transduction> pathway.}

Find the pulse maximum by differentiating the <exponential functions>. With numerical time in seconds, the <derivative> is
$$
r'(t)=-\frac15e^{-t/5}+e^{-t}.
$$
The stationary condition is $e^{-4t/5}=1/5$, so the <natural logarithm> gives
$$
t_{\mathrm{peak}}=\frac54\ln5\simeq2.0118\ \text{seconds},\qquad r_{\mathrm{peak}}=\frac45\,5^{-1/4}\simeq0.53499.
$$
Before this time the <derivative> is positive; afterwards it is negative. Together with $r(0)=0$ and $\lim_{t\to\infty}r(t)=0$, this proves that it is the unique pulse maximum. As another numerical check, at $t=10$ seconds $x\simeq0.999955$, $a\simeq0.864665$ and $r\simeq0.135290$: the slow branch is still catching up.

The subtraction $r=x-a$ is a mathematical readout, not a claim that an actual chemical <concentration> equals this difference for arbitrary inputs. Nonnegativity was established only for the specified upward step; other input histories can give a negative signed readout. Complete <signaling adaptation> here also relies on equal eventual branch gains: changing the readout to $r=x-0.9a$ leaves $r_*=0.1$ for $s=1$. This toy illustrates cancellation and different <time constants>; it does not establish molecular implementation or robustness to arbitrary parameter changes. Ma and colleagues' 2009 study separately analyzed biochemical <mathematical models> with <negative feedback> and <incoherent feedforward loops>, identifying additional conditions for <signaling adaptation> in those models. https://cqb.pku.edu.cn/tanglab/pdf/78.pdf[Ma et al., Cell 138 (2009): adaptive architectures and their kinetic conditions]{ref}

A historical connection to <feedback control> comes from Yi, Huang, Simon and Doyle's 2000 analysis of a bacterial <chemotaxis> model. They identified <integral action> in its regulation of <receptor> activity: accumulated deviations alter a slower adjustment, which in turn changes the measured activity. At a stationary state, an adjustment that integrates a nonzero error cannot remain constant, explaining how zero error can follow. Their conclusion depended on stated biochemical assumptions and stable behavior; it was not a claim that every <biological cell> uses that mechanism. A transient pulse alone, as the <incoherent feedforward loop> toy shows, cannot identify the underlying <signaling feedback> architecture. https://groups.csail.mit.edu/mac/projects/amorphous/6.978/papers/doyle-PNAS-2000.pdf[Yi et al., Robust perfect adaptation in bacterial chemotaxis through integral feedback control, PNAS 97 (2000): Results and Discussion]{ref}

= Incoherent feedforward loop
{parent=Signal transduction}

An <incoherent feedforward loop> carries influences from a shared input to a shared output along routes with opposite net signs. In the <signaling adaptation> toy, $s$ drives fast $x$ and slow $a$, which contribute with opposite signs to $r=x-a$. Despite the conventional word "loop", those routes do not return the output to an earlier stage: this arrangement is distinct from <negative feedback>. Opposing routes alone do not guarantee complete <signaling adaptation>. https://cqb.pku.edu.cn/tanglab/pdf/78.pdf[Ma et al., Cell 138 (2009), p. 764: definition of an incoherent feedforward loop]{ref}

= Centrifugation
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

<Centrifugation> separates suspended material by its motion relative to a rotating liquid. A <centrifuge rotor> creates an outward <centrifugal acceleration> in the <rotating reference frame>; material denser than its surroundings tends to move outward, opposed by <drag force>. Collected material can form a <centrifugation pellet>, leaving a <supernatant>, or remain in separated zones within a <density gradient>. The useful question is which physical difference the separation exploits: a difference in <sedimentation coefficient> or a difference in <buoyant density>.

\Image[centrifuge-rotor-and-separated-fractions.png]
{title=<Centrifuge rotor> geometry and the <centrifugation pellet>–<supernatant> distinction}
{description=Original schematic of one swung-out tube during rotation and a conceptual pelleting comparison. <Rotor radius> increases toward the tube's outer end. Colors and particle counts illustrate incomplete separation and trapping; they are not measured recovery fractions. The tube remains horizontal in this depiction of the rotating geometry.}

<Centrifugation> makes otherwise slow settling useful for studying <biological cells>, <organelles>, <proteins> and <DNA>. Separation and identification are different steps: a <centrifugation pellet> is a collected fraction, not proof that it contains only one kind of <organelle>. The <lysozyme wall-removal experiment> illustrates a complementary readout: testing material released into the separated liquid can be more informative than inspecting the retained solids.

= Centrifuge rotor
{parent=Centrifugation}

A <centrifuge rotor> holds sample vessels around the rotation axis. A fixed-angle <centrifuge rotor> holds tubes at a fixed inclination; a swinging-bucket <centrifuge rotor> lets them swing outward during rotation. Material moves away from the axis, so a <centrifugation pellet> can first collect against an outer tube wall rather than travel along the entire tube axis. Geometry sets both the <rotor radius> and the travel distance.<Basics: Centrifugal Force & Centrifuge Type by Eppendorf>[Basics: Centrifugal Force & Centrifuge Type, Fixed-angle or swing-bucket rotors? and Fixed-angle rotor: tube orientation and pellet position]{ref}

= Rotor radius
{parent=Centrifuge rotor}
{title2=$r$}

The <rotor radius> $r$ is the perpendicular distance from the rotation axis to a specified sample position, not the tube's length. A filled tube spans a range $r_{\min}\leq r\leq r_{\max}$. Consequently, one rotational speed produces different local <centrifugal accelerations> along that range. A reported <relative centrifugal force> needs a stated reference position, such as the outermost sample radius.

= Relative centrifugal force
{parent=Centrifuge rotor}
{title2=$\mathrm{RCF}=a/g_0$}

<Relative centrifugal force> is the <dimensionless> ratio of the local <centrifugal acceleration> magnitude $a$ to the conventional gravitational reference $g_0=9.80665\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$. Despite its name, it is an acceleration ratio: the actual <force> on a specified <mass> $m$ is $ma$. With <angular speed> $\omega$ and <rotor radius> $r$, $a=\omega^2r$. If $n$ is the numerical rotational speed in revolutions per minute and $r_{\mathrm{cm}}$ the numerical <rotor radius> in centimetres, unit conversion gives
$$
\omega=\frac{2\pi n}{60}\,\mathrm{s^{-1}},\qquad
\mathrm{RCF}=\frac{(r_{\mathrm{cm}}/100)(2\pi n/60)^2}{9.80665}
\simeq1.118\times10^{-5}r_{\mathrm{cm}}n^2.
$$
Thus rpm alone does not specify the <centrifugal acceleration>.<Basics: Centrifugal Force & Centrifuge Type by Eppendorf>[Important definitions, Rcm vs rpm, and How to calculate relative centrifugal force: radius dependence and acceleration ratio]{ref}

\Image[centrifuge-radius-speed-relative-acceleration.png]
{title=<Relative centrifugal force> for stipulated <rotor radii> and rotational speeds}
{description=Original theoretical curves calculated from $a=\omega^2r$ with $g_0=9.80665\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$. The <rotor radii> of $5$, $10$ and $15\,\mathrm{cm}$ are chosen examples, not specifications of real <centrifuge rotors>. The plotted range is not an instrument operating limit or a recommendation.}

For a stipulated $3000\,\mathrm{rpm}$, <rotor radii> of $5$ and $10\,\mathrm{cm}$ give approximately $503$ and $1006$ times $g_0$. Doubling the <rotor radius> doubles <relative centrifugal force>; doubling rpm quadruples it. Even equal <relative centrifugal forces> do not guarantee equal separation: travel distance, elapsed time, <viscosity>, sample <density> and particle shape still matter. These are comparisons of a <mathematical model>, not instructions for running an instrument.

= Centrifugation pellet
{parent=Centrifugation}

A <centrifugation pellet> is material accumulated against the vessel wall by <centrifugation>. Its existence establishes collection, not purity; other suspended material can be trapped or co-sediment.

= Supernatant
{parent=Centrifugation}

A <supernatant> is the liquid remaining above settled or pelleted material. It can contain dissolved <molecules> and particles that have not reached the <centrifugation pellet>; a visually clear <supernatant> need not be chemically empty.

= Sedimentation coefficient
{parent=Centrifugation}
{title2=$s=v/(\omega^2r)$}

The <sedimentation coefficient> $s$ is radial drift <velocity> divided by local <centrifugal acceleration>. It has units of time and combines particle properties with those of the surrounding liquid. In a dilute, deterministic <mathematical model> with negligible particle <inertia>, an effective displaced volume $V$, particle <mass> $m$, local liquid <density> $\rho_f$ and <linear friction coefficient> $f>0$ give
$$
f v=(m-\rho_f V)\omega^2r,\qquad s=\frac{v}{\omega^2r}=\frac{m-\rho_f V}{f}.
$$
The subtraction represents <buoyancy>. Equal <mass> does not imply equal $s$: changing shape changes <drag force>, and changing the medium changes both <buoyancy> and <viscosity>.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/ultracentrifugation/[An Introduction to Ultracentrifugation, The Physics Behind the Technology: mass, size, shape, medium density and viscosity]{ref}

For an isolated <sphere> of radius $b$ and uniform <density> $\rho_p$, <Stokes's law> supplies $f=6\pi\eta b$, where $\eta$ is dynamic <viscosity>, and $m=(4\pi/3)b^3\rho_p$. Therefore
$$
s=\frac{2b^2(\rho_p-\rho_f)}{9\eta}.
$$
This original force-balance check predicts fourfold $s$ on doubling $b$ with everything else fixed, and zero drift when $\rho_p=\rho_f$. It applies to dilute isolated <spheres> in <Stokes flow>, not arbitrary folded <proteins> or crowded suspensions. If $s$ and $\omega$ remain constant in a uniform medium, the <ordinary differential equation> $dr/dt=s\omega^2r$ has the <exponential function> solution $r(t)=r(0)e^{s\omega^2t}$. Arrival at an outer boundary therefore depends on starting position as well as $s$; a separation has no universal size cutoff.

= Differential centrifugation
{parent=Centrifugation}
{wiki}

<Differential centrifugation> successively separates <centrifugation pellets> from <supernatants> by differences in settling rate. A first <centrifugation pellet> can enrich faster-settling material; a later <centrifugation pellet> from its <supernatant> can enrich slower-settling material. The fractions overlap because particles have different starting positions and can become trapped. <Differential centrifugation> is consequently an enrichment method, not automatic identification or complete purification.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/principles/particle-separation/[Particle Separation: differential pelleting, smaller particles initially near the tube bottom, and incomplete separation]{ref}

= Density-gradient centrifugation
{parent=Centrifugation}

<Density-gradient centrifugation> uses a liquid whose <density> varies along the separation direction. A band does not by itself establish which mechanism produced it: <rate-zonal centrifugation> separates by migration rate before equilibrium, whereas <isopycnic centrifugation> uses equilibrium positions set by <buoyant density>.

= Isopycnic centrifugation
{parent=Density-gradient centrifugation}

= Equilibrium density-gradient centrifugation
{synonym}

In <isopycnic centrifugation>, a species concentrates near the position where the liquid's <density> matches its <buoyant density>. In a <density gradient> increasing outward, material displaced inward encounters less-dense liquid and tends to move outward; material displaced outward encounters denser liquid and tends to move inward. <Diffusion> broadens the resulting band. If the <density gradient> never reaches the species' <buoyant density>, a matching equilibrium position need not exist within the vessel.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/ultracentrifugation/[An Introduction to Ultracentrifugation, Isopycnic Centrifugation: matching buoyant density and diffusion in equilibrium gradients]{ref}

The 1958 paper <The replication of DNA in Escherichia coli by Matthew Meselson and Franklin W. Stahl> (pp. 672–676, Figures 1–4) makes the distinction concrete. Its separation apparatus was a Spinco Model E ultracentrifuge with an <Escherichia coli> lysate in a <cesium chloride density gradient>. Ultraviolet <wave absorption> photographs of the rotating sample were scanned with a recording microdensitometer: peak position reported <buoyant density>, while signal above baseline reported <DNA> <concentration>.<The replication of DNA in Escherichia coli by Matthew Meselson and Franklin W. Stahl>[p. 676, Experimental: Spinco Model E and recording microdensitometer; p. 675, Figure 4: peak position and baseline]{ref}

Mixed lysates labeled fully with the <nitrogen> <isotope> $^{15}\mathrm{N}$ and unlabeled lysates supplied reference bands; a mixture including intermediate material checked its position relative to those references. Figure 1 followed band formation over time, helping distinguish a migrating distribution from an approximately stationary one. These controls address resolution, calibration and approach to equilibrium. They do not make every ultraviolet-absorbing component identifiable as <DNA>, or turn a band into a direct image of individual strands. The <DNA replication> article develops the copying inference; here the key result is a separation by <buoyant density>, not a claim that heavier-labeled <DNA> is physically larger.<The replication of DNA in Escherichia coli by Matthew Meselson and Franklin W. Stahl>[pp. 672–673, Figures 1–2; p. 675, Figure 4 density references and microdensitometer baseline; p. 676, Experimental: Spinco Model E and ultraviolet photographs]{ref}

= Buoyant density
{parent=Isopycnic centrifugation}
{title2=$\rho_b$}

<Buoyant density> is the surrounding liquid <density> at which a species has no net centrifugal drift under the stated conditions. It depends on interactions with the medium, including associated solvent, rather than necessarily equaling the dry substance's <density>. Equal <buoyant densities> can give overlapping bands even for differently sized <proteins> or <DNA> fragments.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/ultracentrifugation/[An Introduction to Ultracentrifugation, Isopycnic Centrifugation: buoyant density depends on the surrounding medium]{ref}

= Cesium chloride density gradient
{parent=Isopycnic centrifugation}

A <cesium chloride density gradient> forms when the <salt> $\mathrm{CsCl}$ redistributes in a rotating <chemical solution>. It can supply matching-density positions for <DNA> during <isopycnic centrifugation>.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/density-gradient-centrifugation/[Types of Density Gradients and Types of Gradient Material: self-forming CsCl gradients and nucleic-acid separation]{ref}

= Rate-zonal centrifugation
{parent=Density-gradient centrifugation}

<Rate-zonal centrifugation> resolves an initially narrow sample zone by differences in <sedimentation coefficient> during a finite interval. Its bands remain transient: extending the interval can carry material into a <centrifugation pellet>. Thus a <density gradient> need not imply an equilibrium <buoyant density> measurement.https://www.beckman.com/resources/technologies/centrifugation/density-gradient-centrifugation/[Types of Density Gradients, Rate Zonal Centrifugation and Table 1: migration rates before equilibrium or complete sedimentation]{ref}

= Biophysical measurement
{parent=Biology}

= Förster resonance energy transfer
{parent=Biophysical measurement}
{c}

= FRET
{synonym}
{c}

<Förster resonance energy transfer> is a nonradiative transfer of <energy> from an <excited state> of a <FRET donor> to a nearby <FRET acceptor>, through coupling of their <electric dipoles>. The transfer step does not emit a <photon> that the <FRET acceptor> subsequently absorbs. It competes with the <FRET donor>'s own <fluorescence> and other routes out of its <excited state>. A fluorescent <FRET acceptor> can then emit <fluorescence>; an absorbing but nonfluorescent <FRET acceptor> can still receive the transferred <energy>.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.1: nonradiative dipole coupling and a potentially nonfluorescent acceptor]{ref}

The striking use of <FRET> is to turn a change in molecular proximity into an optical readout. <Proteins> carrying a <FRET donor> and a <FRET acceptor> may change the readout when they associate, as in the <chemotaxis FRET assay>; two labels on one <protein> may report a change in its shape. Interpretation depends on the <FRET spectral overlap>, <FRET dipole orientation factor> and labeling geometry, as well as separation. A change in <FRET> is therefore evidence about proximity under specified conditions, rather than a direct measurement of an arbitrary distance.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.1 and §3.3: dependence on distance and orientation; inter- and intramolecular applications]{ref}

For a single pair with fixed <Förster distance> $R_0$ and label separation $r$, the <FRET efficiency> is
$$
E=\frac{1}{1+(r/R_0)^6}.
$$
At $r=R_0$, half the <FRET donor> excitations transfer <energy>. At $r=R_0/2$, the model gives $E=64/65\approx98.46\%$; at $r=2R_0$, it gives $E=1/65\approx1.54\%$. The sixth power makes <FRET> especially useful near the <Förster distance>, with little sensitivity to further separation once transfer is already negligible. These are model calculations, not measurements of a particular pair.

\Image[forster-distance-transfer-efficiency.png]
{title=<FRET efficiency> versus separation relative to the <Förster distance>}
{description=Original single-pair <mathematical model> with fixed <Förster distance>. The shaded interval is $0.2\leq E\leq0.8$. The curve does not establish a universal working range or justify the <electric dipole> approximation for overlapping labels.}

= FRET donor
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}

A <FRET donor> supplies the initial <excited state> <energy>. Its <fluorescence> provides both an emission channel and a reference <FRET donor lifetime> when no <FRET acceptor> is present. Calling a label a <FRET donor> describes its role in a chosen pair, rather than a permanent classification of the <molecule>.

= FRET acceptor
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}

A <FRET acceptor> receives the <excited state> <energy>. Its absorption must support <FRET spectral overlap> with the <FRET donor>. <FRET sensitized emission> requires a fluorescent <FRET acceptor>; measuring a shortened <FRET donor lifetime> does not.

= FRET efficiency
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}
{title2=$E$}

<FRET efficiency> is the <probability> that an excitation of the <FRET donor> ends in transfer to the <FRET acceptor>. Write $k_D=k_{\rm r}+k_{\rm nr}$ for the total decay rate without a <FRET acceptor>, including radiative <fluorescence> and other decay, and $k_T$ for the transfer rate. For constant competing rates,
$$
k_T=k_D\left(\frac{R_0}{r}\right)^6,\qquad E=\frac{k_T}{k_D+k_T}=\int_0^\infty k_T e^{-(k_D+k_T)t}\,dt.
$$
The <integral> counts transfer events among all excitations in a population with <exponential decay>. Substitution gives the separation law above. This short rate argument assumes one <FRET donor>, one <FRET acceptor>, weak coupling, an available <FRET acceptor>, and fixed geometry during the decay; it is not a model for arbitrary interacting labels.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.1, equations 14–16 and discussion before §3.3: single-pair assumptions and transfer efficiency]{ref}

= Förster distance
{parent=FRET efficiency}
{c}
{title2=$R_0$}

= Förster radius
{synonym}
{c}

The <Förster distance> is the separation giving <FRET efficiency> $E=1/2$ for the specified pair and conditions. It is a property of the optical pair and its environment, rather than a universal length. In the usual pair model,
$$
R_0^6\propto \kappa^2 Q_D J n^{-4},
$$
where $\kappa^2$ is the <FRET dipole orientation factor>, $Q_D$ the <FRET donor quantum yield>, $J$ the <FRET spectral overlap> <integral>, and $n$ the medium's <refractive index>. The omitted proportionality constant depends on the unit convention for $J$.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.1, equations 16–18: factors controlling Förster distance]{ref}

If <FRET efficiency> is known and those assumptions hold, inversion gives
$$
r=R_0\left(E^{-1}-1\right)^{1/6},\qquad \frac{d\log r}{dE}=-\frac{1}{6E(1-E)}.
$$
The <derivative> explains why errors become troublesome near $E=0$ or $E=1$. An illustrative pair with $R_0=4$ nanometres and another with $R_0=6$ nanometres both give $E=1/2$ at their different <Förster distances>: knowing <FRET efficiency> alone does not determine $r$.

A heterogeneous population adds another ambiguity. In a synthetic example with equal weights per <FRET donor> excitation, efficiencies $0.1$ and $0.9$ average to $0.5$, but their separations are $9^{1/6}R_0$ and $9^{-1/6}R_0$. Their average separation is approximately $1.068R_0$, whereas inverting the average <FRET efficiency> gives $R_0$. A measured emission <ratio> has additional detection weights; it need not equal even this excitation-weighted average. The transferable lesson is that an average readout does not uniquely specify a distribution of molecular separations.

= FRET spectral overlap
{parent=Förster distance}
{c}
{title2=$J$}

<FRET spectral overlap> quantifies overlap between the <FRET donor>'s emission and the <FRET acceptor>'s absorption as functions of <wavelength> $\lambda$. With normalized donor emission $f_D$ and acceptor molar absorption coefficient $\varepsilon_A$, one convention is
$$
\int_0^\infty f_D(\lambda)\,d\lambda=1,\qquad J=\int_0^\infty f_D(\lambda)\varepsilon_A(\lambda)\lambda^4\,d\lambda.
$$
Matching two emission colors is insufficient: the receiving absorption matters.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.1, equation 17 and Figure 19: normalized donor emission and acceptor absorption overlap]{ref}

= FRET dipole orientation factor
{parent=Förster distance}
{c}
{title2=$\kappa^2$}

The <FRET dipole orientation factor> describes the geometry of the two transition <electric dipoles> relative to their separation. Let $\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_D$, $\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_A$ and $\widehat{\mathbf r}$ be their <unit vectors>. Then
$$
\kappa^2=\left[\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_D\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_A-3(\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_D\cdot\widehat{\mathbf r})(\widehat{\boldsymbol\mu}_A\cdot\widehat{\mathbf r})\right]^2,\qquad 0\leq\kappa^2\leq4.
$$
For a separation along $z$, parallel <electric dipoles> along $z$ give $\kappa^2=4$, parallel ones along $x$ give $1$, and one along $x$ with the other along $y$ gives $0$. Thus rotating a pair can change <FRET efficiency> without changing its separation.

The familiar value $\kappa^2=2/3$ requires suitable rapid, independent orientation averaging during the <FRET donor lifetime>. Random orientations across a population of slowly rotating fluorescent <proteins> do not establish that dynamic condition. <Quantitative time domain analysis of lifetime-based Förster resonant energy transfer measurements with fluorescent proteins: Static random isotropic fluorophore orientation distributions by Yuriy Alexandrov and others>{ref}

= FRET donor quantum yield
{parent=Förster distance}
{c}
{title2=$Q_D$}

The <FRET donor quantum yield> is the emitted <fluorescence> <photon> fraction per absorbed excitation <photon>, measured without a <FRET acceptor>. In the constant-rate model used for <FRET efficiency>, $Q_D=k_{\rm r}/(k_{\rm r}+k_{\rm nr})$. Changing the <FRET donor quantum yield> changes the <Förster distance>, even if label positions remain fixed.

= FRET donor lifetime
{parent=FRET efficiency}
{c}
{title2=$\tau_D,\tau_{DA}$}

The <FRET donor lifetime> is the mean time spent in the <excited state>. For the constant-rate model of <FRET efficiency>,
$$
\tau_D=\frac1{k_D},\qquad \tau_{DA}=\frac1{k_D+k_T},\qquad E=1-\frac{\tau_{DA}}{\tau_D}.
$$
After a sufficiently brief excitation pulse, the <FRET donor>'s <fluorescence> follows <exponential decay>, $I(t)=I(0)e^{-t/\tau}$. With the illustrative values $\tau_D=4$ nanoseconds and $\tau_{DA}=2$ nanoseconds, the inferred <FRET efficiency> is $1/2$. Multiplying $I(0)$ changes brightness without changing this decay time.

The lifetime comparison requires the same nontransfer decay rates in the reference and paired samples. Changes in the <FRET donor>'s environment or a different nonradiative loss can also shorten the <FRET donor lifetime>. Heterogeneous <FRET efficiencies> or slowly varying <FRET dipole orientation factors> need an appropriate decay model; substituting any fitted average lifetime into the single-pair formula can mislead. <Quantitative time domain analysis of lifetime-based Förster resonant energy transfer measurements with fluorescent proteins: Static random isotropic fluorophore orientation distributions by Yuriy Alexandrov and others>{ref}

\Image[fret-fluorescence-lifetime-readouts.png]
{title=<FRET sensitized emission>, optical controls and the <FRET donor lifetime>}
{description=Original schematic and synthetic curves. The spectral panel assumes equal <fluorescence> yields and collection efficiencies for the two labels; its amplitudes are illustrative, not a calibration or measured cyan/yellow spectra. The lifetime panel uses $\tau_D=4$ and $\tau_{DA}=2$ nanoseconds. Dashed optical paths require <FRET optical crosstalk> controls; they do not represent the nonradiative transfer mechanism.}

= FRET sensitized emission
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}

<FRET sensitized emission> is <FRET acceptor> <fluorescence> caused by transfer from an excited <FRET donor>. Excite predominantly the <FRET donor> and collect both emission bands. Reduced <FRET donor> emission and increased <FRET acceptor> emission are useful together, but an acceptor-to-donor emission <ratio> is not automatically the <FRET efficiency>: brightness, label abundance and channel collection also enter.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.4.2: sensitized emission and dependence on optical correction and label concentrations]{ref}

= FRET optical crosstalk
{parent=FRET sensitized emission}
{c}

<FRET optical crosstalk> includes <FRET donor> emission leaking into the <FRET acceptor> detection band and direct excitation of the <FRET acceptor> by the nominal <FRET donor> excitation light. Measure donor-only, acceptor-only and unlabeled preparations with the same <fluorescence microscope> settings to separate these contributions and background.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.4.2, samples i–iii and equations 20–21: spectral correction controls]{ref}

An explicit linear measurement model makes the controls' purpose clear. Let $D$ be background-subtracted donor-band counts under donor excitation, $A$ acceptor-band counts under acceptor excitation, and $F$ acceptor-band counts under donor excitation. Donor-only and acceptor-only preparations determine leakage coefficients $a$ and $b$. Then
$$
S=F-aD-bA
$$
is a corrected <FRET sensitized emission> signal under the assumed linear, fixed optical response. In a synthetic example $D=100$, $A=80$, $a=0.10$, $b=0.05$ and $F=34$ give $S=20$. Even a nontransferring sample would give $F=14$ from these two contaminants. Correcting that signal still does not convert $S$ into <FRET efficiency> without a calibration of excitation, yields and detection.

A biological comparison also needs a pairing-negative preparation and a known transferring positive preparation. Match label abundance and localization where possible; changing <concentration> can change encounter frequency as well as brightness. Check that attaching the labels preserves the <protein> function being investigated. Otherwise the measurement can faithfully report a perturbation introduced by the labels themselves.

= FRET photobleaching controls
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}

In <FRET photobleaching controls>, selectively destroy the <FRET acceptor>'s ability to absorb and test whether <FRET donor> <fluorescence> increases. Under unchanged excitation and detection, complete loss of transfer, and an intact <FRET donor>,
$$
E=1-\frac{I_{D,\rm before}}{I_{D,\rm after}}.
$$
This is a destructive comparison. Monitor acceptor loss, donor-only samples exposed to the bleaching illumination, and an unbleached reference region or time trace. Incomplete bleaching, donor damage, movement or label replenishment violate the comparison. During ordinary recordings, gradual unequal bleaching can instead produce an apparent change of <FRET> without a biological change.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§3.4.1.1–3.4.1.2: donor recovery, sample damage, acceptor replenishment and photoconversion artifacts]{ref}

= FRET proximity readout experiment
{parent=Förster resonance energy transfer}
{c}

In <Receptor sensitivity in bacterial chemotaxis by Victor Sourjik and others>, <Victor Sourjik> and <Howard C. Berg> used a cyan fluorescent tag on <CheZ> as <FRET donor> and a yellow tag on <CheY> as <FRET acceptor>. <Escherichia coli> <biological cells> adhered to a polylysine-coated coverslip in a continuously perfused chamber. A <fluorescence microscope> excited them with a $442$-nanometre <laser>; split emission bands fed two <photon>-counting detectors. The yellow/cyan emission <ratio> fell when attractant was added and rose when it was removed.<Receptor sensitivity in bacterial chemotaxis by Victor Sourjik and others>[Introduction, Materials and Methods: Preparation of Cells and Data Acquisition; Results and Figure 1]{ref}

Saturating attractant increased cyan emission by an amount matching selective bleaching of the yellow tag: removing the <FRET acceptor> supported relief of <FRET donor> transfer. Strains carrying the tagged pair but lacking other <chemotaxis> machinery showed no stimulus-dependent emission <ratio> change. Weak illumination reduced bleaching drift.<Receptor sensitivity in bacterial chemotaxis by Victor Sourjik and others>[Data Analysis and Results: acceptor bleaching comparison, low illumination, and flhC negative control]{ref}

The inference is a change in the population of nearby tagged <CheY>/<CheZ> pairs associated with <CheY> <phosphorylation>. It neither measures each pair's separation nor directly observes motor motion. The readout also averages many <biological cells>, and perfusion takes finite time. The pathway interpretation belongs to the <chemotaxis FRET assay>; the general measurement lesson is that molecular association can alter <FRET> while the geometry of an individual associated pair stays the same.

= Model organism
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

A <model organism> is an <organism> used to investigate biological processes through accessible, repeatable experiments. Its value combines <experimental tractability> with a carefully delimited relationship to other <organisms>. For example, a <gene deletion> in <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> can test whether a <gene> is required for growth under specified conditions; adding another <gene> can test <functional complementation>. Such interventions support <causal inference> more directly than merely noticing that a <gene> and a <phenotype> occur together. <Budding Yeast for Budding Geneticists: A Primer on the Saccharomyces cerevisiae Model System by Andrea A. Duina and others>[Duina, Miller and Keeney (2014), introduction and Experimental Toolkit and Related Resources: manipulation of genes and phenotypic effects]{ref}

The striking opportunity is to ask two experimentally separate questions: what does this component do here, and which part of that function survives a change of <species> or conditions? The <yeast cross-species complementation experiment> makes both questions concrete. <Model organism evidence transfer> requires specifying the function being compared; a useful <model organism> need not reproduce every feature of another <organism>.

= Experimental tractability
{parent=Model organism}

<Experimental tractability> is the practical ability to cultivate a system, manipulate the variables of interest, and obtain an interpretable readout with appropriate <scientific controls>. In <Saccharomyces cerevisiae>, inexpensive cultivation, accessible <haploid> and <diploid> states, and tools for introducing <DNA> make controlled changes of <genotype> feasible. A <haploid> <biological strain> exposes a changed <allele> without a second copy at that <genetic locus>, while crosses and <meiosis> permit tests of inheritance. <Budding Yeast for Budding Geneticists: A Primer on the Saccharomyces cerevisiae Model System by Andrea A. Duina and others>[Duina, Miller and Keeney (2014), introduction, Figure 2 and Experimental Toolkit and Related Resources]{ref}

An accessible readout is not necessarily a direct measurement of the mechanism. Growth of a <biological colony> integrates many cellular functions. Failure to grow could reflect the intended <gene deletion>, unsuitable conditions, failed <genetic transformation>, or another <mutation>. Matching the <genetic background> and using <scientific controls> narrows these alternatives. Ease of doing an experiment and strength of its inference are distinct advantages.

= Yeast cross-species complementation experiment
{parent=Model organism}

Can a <gene> from <Schizosaccharomyces pombe> replace a missing function in <Saccharomyces cerevisiae>? A modern laboratory project tested <functional complementation> of <methionine> synthesis. For each tested <gene deletion>, the investigators compared <plasmids> carrying the corresponding <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> <gene>, a candidate <Schizosaccharomyces pombe> <gene>, and an unrelated <gene>. The first was a positive <scientific control>; the last was a negative <scientific control>. The paper describes this design rather than publishing a complete set of individual growth measurements. <Pathways over Time: Functional Genomics Research in an Introductory Laboratory Course by Todd D. Reeves and others>[Reeves, Warner, Ludlow and O’Connor (2018), Course Activities, pp. 5–6 and Table 1: three plasmids, replica plating and protein-expression checks]{ref}

The recipient <biological strains> also lacked the ability to make <uracil>. The pYES2.1/V5-His <plasmids> carried URA3, a <gene> restoring that ability, and used the GAL1 <gene promoter> to control the inserted <gene>. Initial plates lacked <uracil> but supplied <methionine>: this selected <biological colonies> containing <plasmids> without requiring successful <methionine> synthesis. Subsequent plates lacking <methionine> tested the missing function, comparing <galactose induction> with <glucose> conditions. Thus selecting for a <plasmid> and testing its functional contribution were separate operations. <Pathways Over Time Plasmids Engage Students in Functional Genomics Research by Clare O’Connor>[O’Connor (2018), Plasmid features enable multiple student experiments and Complementation reveals conservation of function]{ref}

The apparatus can be simple and informative. In the associated protocol, <replica plating> uses a block covered with velveteen to transfer small samples of <biological colonies> from a master plate onto plates with different nutrient conditions. Orientation marks preserve correspondence between locations; gentle contact limits the transferred material. The specified replicas omit <uracil> and <methionine> with either inducing <sugar> or <glucose>, and include a fresh <glucose> plate supplying <methionine>. The readout is growth at corresponding locations, not a direct measurement of <enzyme> activity. <Investigations in Molecular Cell Biology by Clare M. O’Connor/12.6: Exercise 2 - Replica plating and complementation>[O’Connor, Investigations in Molecular Cell Biology, §12.6, introductory paragraphs and steps 1–3: velveteen block, orientation marks and three replica media]{ref}

\Image[yeast-gene-complementation-experimental-design.png]
{title=Separating <plasmid> selection from a <yeast> <functional complementation> test}
{description=Original schematic of the reported experimental design, not copied growth data. All three constructs enter the same <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> <gene deletion> background. Plate labels identify distinct comparisons; arrows show sample transfer, not observed rescue. The candidate <Schizosaccharomyces pombe> outcome must be measured.}

The <scientific controls> answer different objections. Growth on the <methionine>-supplied replica checks survival and transfer. Rescue by the native <gene> checks that this <biological strain> and assay can reveal <functional complementation>. The unrelated <gene> tests whether carrying a <plasmid> alone is sufficient. Comparing induction conditions checks dependence on <gene expression>, although changing the <sugar> also changes <metabolism>: it is not an isolated change of <gene expression> in every other respect.

If the candidate <gene> restores growth while those comparisons behave as intended, it supplies a function sufficient for the tested growth requirement in this recipient.
