Failure to rescue is less decisive: the candidate <protein> might not be produced, might be altered by its attached tag, or might not work in this cellular context. The investigators therefore also used <Western blots> to examine <protein> production. A detected <protein> still does not establish correct <protein folding> or catalytic activity. <Pathways over Time: Functional Genomics Research in an Introductory Laboratory Course by Todd D. Reeves and others>[Reeves et al. (2018), Course Activities, pp. 5–6: functional conservation, tag interference and Western-blot follow-up]{ref}

= Model organism evidence transfer
{parent=Model organism}

Evidence transfers at a stated level. <Sequence alignment> can suggest a candidate relationship between <genes>; <functional complementation> tests whether a candidate can supply a particular function in a chosen recipient. Neither alone establishes identical native <gene expression>, identical <signal transduction>, or identical consequences in every <species>. The <yeast cross-species complementation experiment> is especially useful because it tests a conserved function by intervention rather than assuming it from resemblance. <Pathways over Time: Functional Genomics Research in an Introductory Laboratory Course by Todd D. Reeves and others>[Reeves et al. (2018), project design, pp. 3–4, and Course Activities, pp. 5–6]{ref}

\Image[model-organism-evidence-transfer-boundaries.png]
{title=What a <model organism> experiment establishes, and what needs another test}
{description=Original evidence map using the <yeast cross-species complementation experiment>. Solid arrows indicate an experimental question that can be addressed in the recipient; dashed arrows mark additional claims requiring evidence. This is a schematic of inference boundaries, not a <phylogenetic tree>, a measured pathway, or a claim about medical outcomes.}

Even successful <functional complementation> occurs within a specific <genetic background>, nutrient supply and imposed <gene expression>. The <enzyme> may work with the recipient’s available <substrates> and partner <proteins> at an experimentally imposed abundance. Establishing the same role in the donor <species> calls for experiments there; establishing behavior under another condition calls for experiments under that condition. A conserved component can therefore coexist with context-dependent behavior of an entire pathway. This separation makes <model organisms> powerful: an accessible experiment supplies a precise mechanistic foothold and a better question for the next system.

= Organism
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

An <organism> is an individual living system, made of one <biological cell> or an integrated assembly of <biological cells>. An <Escherichia coli> cell is an entire <organism>; a <neuron> is normally a specialized part of an <animal>. <Metabolism>, regulated exchange through <cell membranes>, and inherited information connect these very different scales. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/1-2-themes-and-concepts-of-biology[OpenStax Biology 2e, 1.2: living systems and levels of organization].

Keep two questions separate: how many <biological cells> make the <organism>, and how those <biological cells> are organized. A <yeast> and an <Escherichia coli> are both <unicellular organisms>, but the <yeast> is a <eukaryote> and the <Escherichia coli> a <prokaryote>. A <plant> and an <animal> are <multicellular organisms> with <eukaryotic> cellular organization. Neither distinction ranks <organisms> as better or worse: <natural selection> acts on reproduction in particular environments.

= Unicellular organism
{parent=Organism}
{wiki}

A <unicellular organism> is an <organism> whose living body consists of one <biological cell>. That <biological cell> maintains itself, grows and participates in <reproduction>; it is not merely one specialized component of a larger body. <Escherichia coli> and ordinary free-living <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> illustrate <prokaryotic> and <eukaryotic> examples respectively. Cellular organization and the number of <biological cells> are separate questions. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-1-studying-cells[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 4.1: cells as units of organisms].

How can one <biological cell> do enough to sustain an <organism>? Its <cell membrane> retains useful material while <membrane transport> allows exchange; <metabolism> transforms nutrients and supplies usable <energy>; <gene expression> makes <proteins> needed to maintain and rebuild the machinery. These functions operate together inside the same boundary. In <Saccharomyces cerevisiae>, for example, <glucose> can support <alcoholic fermentation> and production of <ATP>, while the <cell cycle> and <budding> produce descendants. A single <biological cell> therefore combines chemical processing, regulated exchange and inherited organization. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26864/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell (2002): universal cellular machinery] and the experimental accounts under <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> and <yeast>.

\Image[unicellular-multicellular-organization.png]
{title=One <biological cell>, a <biological colony>, <clonal development> and <developmental integration>}
{description=Original conceptual schematic, not micrographs or an evolutionary ladder. Colors mark illustrative lineages in the middle panels and illustrative functions in the right panel; they are not measured traits. A <biological colony> can also be clonal, and <clonal development> need not produce specialized <biological cells>. The integrated panel illustrates a division of functions, not a particular <species>.}

Being a <unicellular organism> does not mean isolated or independent of all other life. <Unicellular organisms> can interact and cooperate in groups. A <biological colony> describes association; <clonal development> describes descent; <developmental integration> describes coordination of the developing whole. Neither shared ancestry nor a visible clump alone establishes the latter. This distinction makes single-cell experiments easier to interpret: measuring the growth of one <biological cell>, the expansion of a group and the <reproductive success> of a reproducing cluster can answer different questions. See https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2781869/[Queller and Strassmann, Beyond society: the evolution of organismality (2009): cooperation and the limits of simple organism criteria].

= Multicellular organism
{parent=Organism}
{wiki}

A <multicellular organism> has a body of multiple <biological cells> whose growth and activities are integrated into the life of the whole <organism>. Extensive <cell differentiation> is common in complex examples, but specialized <biological cells> are not a prerequisite for every simple form. Ask how the body develops, how its parts cooperate and how it produces descendants, rather than counting attached <biological cells> alone. The boundary with a <biological colony> depends on the degree of integration and the criteria being investigated; there is no universal cell-count threshold. See the Queller and Strassmann account cited under <unicellular organism>.

Specialization makes the whole capable of combinations that one general-purpose <biological cell> cannot achieve in the same way. In an <animal>, a <neuron> transmits signals while a <red blood cell> carries <oxygen>; each still requires a working <cell membrane> and <metabolism>. A mature mammalian <red blood cell> lacks a <cell nucleus> and <mitochondria>, illustrating how a useful part need not retain the equipment of a self-reproducing <organism>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-2-components-of-the-blood[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 40.2: red blood cell specialization]. <Cell differentiation> generally involves different <gene expression>, rather than a different inherited <genome> for each function. <Biological development> must coordinate growth, position, interactions and specialization: increasing the number of <biological cells> is only one component. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK21046/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell, chapter 21 (2002): construction of multicellular bodies].

\b[A motivating experiment: select for a property of groups.] Ratcliff, Denison, Borrello and Travisano's 2012 study began with <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> growing as <unicellular organisms>. Daily transfer favored rapidly settling material. Their initial protocol allowed a $10\,\mathrm{mL}$ culture to settle for $45\,\mathrm{min}$, discarded the upper $9.9\,\mathrm{mL}$, and transferred the bottom $100\,\mathrm{\mu L}$ into fresh medium. After seven days, they instead sampled $1.5\,\mathrm{mL}$ and centrifuged it at $100$ times gravitational acceleration for $10\,\mathrm{s}$, again transferring the bottom $100\,\mathrm{\mu L}$. Faster settling made it more likely that a lineage would seed tomorrow's culture; the procedure selected a physical outcome without specifying how it must arise. These protocol details are explicitly recounted in https://academic.oup.com/evolut/article/73/5/1012/6727309[Gulli, Herron and Ratcliff, Evolution of altruistic cooperation among nascent multicellular organisms (2019): original settling protocol].

\Image[unicellular-multicellular-settling-selection.png]
{title=Settling selection and an explicitly stipulated enrichment example}
{description=Original qualitative transfer schematic with toy numerical bars. The workflow represents the initial settling protocol described above. The lower panel assumes equally weighted tracked units and transfer probabilities $0.8$ and $0.1$; these values and the resulting frequencies are invented for explanation, not fitted to <snowflake yeast> measurements. The equal-transfer comparison is a model control, distinct from the experimental comparisons described below.}

The primary 2012 paper reports <snowflake yeast> dominating all ten initial replicate populations by 60 transfers. To distinguish the joining of previously separate <biological cells> from <clonal development>, the researchers enzymatically separated clusters and followed individual <biological cells> for $16\,\mathrm{h}$: attached descendants rebuilt clusters, with no such joining observed. Staining localized attachments at bud scars. Time-lapse observations also showed clusters releasing smaller multicellular offspring, called propagules; small clusters grew before they reproduced. See https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3277146/[Ratcliff et al., Experimental evolution of multicellularity (2012), figures 1–3: emergence, development and reproduction].

The experimental comparisons ask different questions. Competition with the <unicellular organism> ancestor showed a reported $34\%$ advantage under settling selection. Without settling selection, the estimated $10\%$ disadvantage had a one-sided $P=0.06$, so it was suggestive rather than decisive evidence of a cost. Separately, three populations derived from one evolved isolate retained the cluster form through 35 transfers without settling selection. That persistence tests stability, not whether clusters always outperform single <biological cells>. See Ratcliff et al. (2012), figure 2 and the cluster-formation results cited above.

A 2015 follow-up added a causal test: deleting the <gene> ACE2 in the <unicellular organism> ancestor produced <snowflake yeast>, while supplying functional ACE2 to one evolved isolate restored separation into single <biological cells>. Sequence changes altering the encoded <protein> were found in ACE2 in five of ten examined lineages; the study did not identify that same sequence-change mechanism in every lineage. The deletion and restoration comparisons support a mechanism beyond the observation that clusters settle quickly. See https://www.nature.com/articles/ncomms7102[Ratcliff, Fankhauser, Rogers, Greig and Travisano, Origins of multicellular evolvability in snowflake yeast (2015), figure 1].

\b[What follows, and what does not.] The evidence supports inherited cluster formation, <clonal development> and a reproducing collective whose properties can respond to <natural selection>. Calling these simple <multicellular organisms> recognizes those demonstrated properties; it does not turn every clump into an <animal> or establish animal-like <cell differentiation>. This laboratory route also does not reconstruct the actual ancestry of <animals> or show that complex <developmental integration> must follow. The interesting achievement is narrower and testable: a change in how descendants remain connected creates a new scale at which growth and <reproductive success> can be measured.

For the toy figure, let $p$ be the fraction of tracked units of type C before transfer and let $s_C,s_U$ be the respective <probabilities> of transfer for C and U. Assume equally weighted units and equal multiplication between transfers. When overall transfer <probability> is nonzero, conditioning on transfer gives
$$
p_{\mathrm{after}}=\frac{p s_C}{p s_C+(1-p)s_U}.
$$
With $p=1/5$, $s_C=4/5$ and $s_U=1/10$, the numerator is $4/25$ and the denominator $6/25$, giving $p_{\mathrm{after}}=2/3$. If both types have the same nonzero transfer <probability>, the fraction stays $1/5$. This is <conditional probability>, not a model of the full experimental life cycle: real cluster sizes, growth and fragmentation affect which quantity a frequency measures. In particular, a fraction of clusters is not automatically a fraction of <biological cells>.

= Clonal development
{parent=Multicellular organism}

<Clonal development> builds a group through successive divisions of founding <biological cells>, rather than by gathering previously separate lineages. In <snowflake yeast>, daughter <biological cells> remain attached after <budding>. Common ancestry does not imply that every descendant remains genetically identical forever: new <mutations> can arise. See the 2015 study cited under <multicellular organism>.

= Snowflake yeast
{parent=Clonal development}

<Snowflake yeast> are cluster-forming <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> with branching connections between mothers and attached daughter <biological cells>. They provide an experimental system for studying early transitions to simple <multicellular organisms>; see the protocol, observations and limits under <multicellular organism>.

= Developmental integration
{parent=Multicellular organism}

<Developmental integration> here means coordination of cellular growth, organization and function into a developing whole. It describes what the parts do together, whereas <clonal development> describes how they descend from founders. Interactions through <signal transduction> can help coordinate <cell differentiation>; see the Alberts et al. development chapter cited under <multicellular organism>.

= Cellular differentiation
{parent=Developmental integration}
{wiki}

= Cell differentiation
{synonym}

<Cell differentiation> is the acquisition of a specialized <cell state>, involving changes in structure, function and <gene expression>. A <neuron> and a <red blood cell> illustrate different outcomes: signaling and <oxygen> transport. Different functions usually do not require a different inherited <DNA> sequence for each function. The shared-<genome> principle concerns nucleated precursors and most nucleated descendants: a mature mammalian <red blood cell> has lost its <cell nucleus> and <mitochondria>, so it is not an example of two mature <biological cells> both retaining the same nuclear <DNA>. <Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[Biology 2e, §40.2, Red Blood Cells: specialization and absence of a nucleus and mitochondria at mammalian maturity]{ref}

The puzzle is how a shared set of <genes> supports different jobs. <Gene expression> changes which <RNA> and <proteins> a <biological cell> produces, and in what amounts. A <transcription factor> can regulate several <genes>; different combinations of regulators can interpret the same signal differently. Stable regulatory patterns can persist through the <cell cycle>, providing memory of developmental history. Specialization therefore usually changes use of inherited information, rather than replacing the <genome> wholesale. <Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others>[Alberts et al., The Molecular Genetic Mechanisms That Create Specialized Cell Types, subsections The Expression of a Set of Genes Can Be Coordinated by a Single Protein and Stable Patterns of Gene Expression Can Be Transmitted to Daughter Cells, and Summary]{ref}

\Image[cell-differentiation-shared-genome-expression.png]
{title=Shared <genes>, different <gene expression> and specialized <cell states>}
{description=Original schematic with three invented <genes> H, A and B. Both nucleated <biological cells> retain all three <genes>; colored outputs indicate different illustrative expression patterns. These are not measured <RNA> or <protein> amounts, named real <genes>, or a claim that real <gene expression> is simply on or off. The separate mammalian <red blood cell> note marks an important limit to the shared nuclear <DNA> comparison.}

A small counting example separates sequence from activity. Suppose the same three toy <genes> are present in every nucleated <biological cell>, and independently assign each <gene> an on/off state. There are $2^3=8$ possible expression profiles with no <DNA> replacement. The figure shows only two: H and A on versus H and B on. Real <gene expression> is graded and coordinated, so this count illustrates possible descriptions, not a prediction of eight actual cell types. The interesting consequence is that a stable <cell state> can depend on how a shared record is used.

<Stem cells> combine self-renewal with production of differentiated descendants; <progenitor cells> generally have more restricted developmental options and renewal capacity. Neither term means that a <biological cell> must give rise to every cell type. <Terminal differentiation> denotes advanced specialization within a lineage, often accompanied by exit from the <cell cycle>. <Cell fate> concerns the developmental outcome of a <biological cell> or its descendants, whereas <cell state> describes its current properties. A temporary change in <gene expression> after <signal transduction> is not, by itself, proof of a new <cell fate>. <Anatomy and Physiology 2e by J. Gordon Betts and others>[Anatomy and Physiology 2e, §3.6, Stem Cells and Differentiation: renewal, restricted differentiation potential and regulation of gene expression]{ref}

= Cell fate
{parent=Cellular differentiation}

<Cell fate> is the developmental outcome of a <biological cell> or its descendants in a specified setting. What it normally becomes and what it can become after an experimental intervention are different questions. The <intestinal nucleus transfer experiment> tests potential after <cellular reprogramming>, rather than directly tracing the normal <cell fate> of the donor.

= Terminal differentiation
{parent=Cellular differentiation}

<Terminal differentiation> is advanced specialization at the end of a developmental lineage. It often includes withdrawal from the <cell cycle>; the term does not mean that every nuclear <gene> needed for another <cell fate> has been deleted. A mature mammalian <red blood cell> is an especially restrictive example because it lacks a <cell nucleus>; the <intestinal nucleus transfer experiment> used nucleated donors.

= Cellular reprogramming
{parent=Cellular differentiation}

<Cellular reprogramming> changes an established <cell state> so that a different developmental program becomes accessible. Experimentally induced <cellular reprogramming> must be distinguished from the ordinary progression of <cell differentiation>. An <ovum>'s <cytoplasm> can reset aspects of a transferred <cell nucleus>; this does not mean that a specialized donor normally returns to an early developmental state in its original surroundings. <Mature cells can be reprogrammed to become pluripotent by Nobel Assembly at Karolinska Institutet>[Nobel Assembly, Mature cells can be reprogrammed to become pluripotent, Scientific background: Gurdon nuclear transfer and distinction between differentiation and reprogramming]{ref}

= Somatic cell nuclear transfer
{parent=Cellular reprogramming}
{wiki}

<Somatic cell nuclear transfer> introduces a body-cell <cell nucleus> into an <ovum> whose own nuclear contribution has been removed or inactivated. The donor nuclear <genome> and recipient <cytoplasm> have different origins; successful development does not show that the donor <cell nucleus> acts independently of the recipient's machinery.

= Intestinal nucleus transfer experiment
{parent=Somatic cell nuclear transfer}

\b[Can a specialized donor still supply information for other cell types?] The 1962 https://doi.org/10.1242/dev.10.4.622[The Developmental Capacity of Nuclei taken from Intestinal Epithelium Cells of Feeding Tadpoles by J. B. Gurdon] used mid-intestinal cells of feeding <Xenopus laevis> tadpoles. Donors were recognized by columnar shape, basal <cell nuclei> and a striated absorptive border. Dissociation used Versene solution; this made individual donors accessible. The readout was subsequent development, including normal feeding tadpoles, rather than just division or survival. <The Developmental Capacity of Nuclei taken from Intestinal Epithelium Cells of Feeding Tadpoles by J. B. Gurdon>[Gurdon (1962), Material and Methods and Normal tadpoles; inspected primary text reproduced in the indexed Paperity preview]{ref}

\b[Apparatus and intervention.] A microscope with <ultraviolet> illumination directed light at the surface region containing the unfertilized <ovum>'s <chromosomes>, inactivating the recipient nuclear contribution. Irradiation also softened the surrounding jelly so a glass micropipette could enter. The pipette delivered donor nuclear material into recipient <cytoplasm>. In the technique's validation, fertilizing irradiated eggs produced <haploid> embryos, supporting destruction of the maternal nuclear contribution. This is inactivation, not a diagram of surgically removing an intact <cell nucleus>. <The Egg and the Nucleus: A Battle for Supremacy by J. B. Gurdon>[Gurdon, The Egg and the Nucleus: A Battle for Supremacy, The Technique of Nuclear Transfer in Xenopus; indexed authored Nobel lecture text inspected]{ref}

\Image[frog-intestinal-nucleus-transfer-controls.png]
{title=<Somatic cell nuclear transfer>: intervention, developmental readout and distinct controls}
{description=Original apparatus-and-inference schematic, not a tracing of an experimental photograph. The <ultraviolet> beam indicates inactivation of recipient nuclear material; the injection pipette supplies a donor <cell nucleus>. Donor nuclear markers test origin; interspersed embryonic-donor transfers test recipient quality. Successful development is a possible outcome, not an asserted success rate. The figure omits exact dimensions, light dose and many technical steps.}

\b[Controls ask different questions.] Donor <cell nuclei> carried a marker absent from recipients; post-<blastula> embryos carried that marker. The marker distinguished diploid donors with one <nucleolus> per <cell nucleus> from the usual two-<nucleolus> recipient background, rather than mistaking one <nucleolus> for proof of a <haploid> state. <The Egg and the Nucleus: A Battle for Supremacy by J. B. Gurdon>[Gurdon, Nobel lecture, The Technique of Nuclear Transfer in Xenopus, p. 233: one-nucleolus diploid marker and its use to establish donor nuclear origin]{ref} Interspersed transfers of <blastula> or later embryonic <cell nuclei> checked variable recipient quality. A positive embryonic-donor control thus tests the preparation, while the marker tests donor origin. Abnormal development remained common. Neither control establishes universal transfer competence. <The Developmental Capacity of Nuclei taken from Intestinal Epithelium Cells of Feeding Tadpoles by J. B. Gurdon>[Gurdon (1962), Material and Methods (nuclear marker), Results (interspersed embryonic controls), and Normal tadpoles (abnormal outcomes); primary text inspected through the indexed preview]{ref}

\b[Inference and limits.] A successful marked donor demonstrates that specialization had not irreversibly removed all information required to build other cell types. Failure is less decisive: transfer damage, recipient condition and incomplete <cellular reprogramming> can also prevent development. The marker identifies nuclear origin; it is not a complete <DNA> sequence comparison. The experiment tests donor nuclear potential in an altered <cytoplasm>, rather than normal donor <cell fate>, a universal capacity of every donor, or development from a mature mammalian <red blood cell>. The Nobel account also describes serial transfers through embryonic intermediates; these must not be silently combined with direct transfers into one success rate. <Mature cells can be reprogrammed to become pluripotent by Nobel Assembly at Karolinska Institutet>[Nobel Assembly, Scientific background, Gurdon's nuclear-transfer experiments and serial transplantation]{ref}

= Colony
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Organism}
{wiki}

= Biological colony
{synonym}

A <biological colony> is an associated group of <organisms> or <biological cells>. The name alone specifies neither <cell differentiation> nor a particular mode of <biological development>. A colony can arise from related descendants or from previously separate members; see <clonal development> and the Queller and Strassmann discussion cited under <unicellular organism>.

= Prokaryote
{parent=Organism}
{wiki}

= Prokaryotic
{synonym}

A <prokaryote> is an <organism> whose <biological cells> lack a membrane-bounded <cell nucleus>. The <DNA> of its <chromosomes> occupies a <nucleoid> within the <cytoplasm>. <Bacteria> and <archaea> have this cellular organization. Both contain <ribosomes> that perform <biological translation> and a selective <cell membrane>; neither is a bag of unorganized chemicals. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-2-prokaryotic-cells[OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.2: prokaryotic organization].

The word <prokaryotic> describes organization, not a single exclusive <clade>. Grouping <bacteria> and <archaea> by the absence of a <cell nucleus> is useful for comparing <biological cells>, but does not establish that they share a more recent ancestor with one another than either does with <eukaryotes>. See <three-domain system> and <two-domain system> for the different ancestry models.

<Prokaryotic> does not mean membrane-free. <Cyanobacteria> contain internal <thylakoid> membranes for <oxygenic photosynthesis>, despite lacking <chloroplasts> and a <cell nucleus>. Many <prokaryotes> have circular <chromosomes>, but circular shape and a single <chromosome> are not defining requirements. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/22-2-structure-of-prokaryotes-bacteria-and-archaea[OpenStax Biology 2e, 22.2: bacterial and archaeal structures].

\Image[prokaryote-eukaryote-cell-organization.png]
{title=Cell organization: an <Escherichia coli> example beside a nucleated <animal> cell}
{description=Original schematic cross-sections, not micrographs and not at exact relative scale. Both show a <cell membrane>, <cytoplasm>, <DNA> and <ribosomes>. The <Escherichia coli> envelope is simplified: its outer membrane is omitted. The <animal> cell additionally shows a <nuclear envelope> and <mitochondria>; many other structures are omitted. The outlined <nucleoid> is a region, not a membrane.}

= Bacteria
{parent=Prokaryote}
{wiki}

<Bacteria> are a major evolutionary lineage of <organisms> with <prokaryotic> cellular organization. <Escherichia coli> and <cyanobacteria> illustrate its variety: the same broad cellular plan supports different kinds of <metabolism>. Most <bacteria> have a <cell wall> containing <peptidoglycan>; <archaea> do not have <peptidoglycan> walls. A <cell wall> and a <cell membrane> are distinct structures, and some <bacteria> lack a <cell wall>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/22-2-structure-of-prokaryotes-bacteria-and-archaea[OpenStax Biology 2e, 22.2: bacterial envelopes].

The usefulness of recognizing <bacteria> extends beyond naming: it identifies a shared evolutionary background for comparing <genes>, <proteins> and cellular mechanisms. A familiar shape or the absence of a <cell nucleus> alone cannot distinguish <bacteria> from <archaea>; molecular comparisons, including <ribosomal RNA>, supply additional evidence.

= Alphaproteobacteria
{c}
{parent=Bacteria}
{wiki}

<Alphaproteobacteria> are a bacterial group implicated in the ancestry of <mitochondria>. This relationship concerns <common descent>, not descent from a named modern member of <Alphaproteobacteria>.

= Escherichia coli
{c}
{parent=Bacteria}
{wiki}

= E. coli
{c}
{synonym}

<Escherichia coli>, often abbreviated <E. coli>, is a bacterial <species> widely used to study <DNA replication> and <gene expression>. Its <chromosome> is in a <nucleoid>, and its <ribosomes> work in the <cytoplasm>, outside any <cell nucleus>. A result from one laboratory strain should not automatically be generalized to every member of the <species>.

The original 1997 report by Blattner and colleagues presented the K-12 MG1655 <genome> sequence as a circular <chromosome> of $4{,}639{,}221$ <base pairs>. See https://www.genome.wisc.edu/sequencing/U00096.1.htm[Blattner laboratory: original K-12 sequence and 1997 paper]. As a bookkeeping example, one complete round of <DNA replication> from one such <chromosome> produces two daughter sequence copies totaling $2\times4{,}639{,}221=9{,}278{,}442$ <base pairs>. This is twice the copied material, not twice the length of the reference <genome>; it does not specify the instantaneous <DNA> content of a rapidly growing <E. coli> cell.

= Cyanobacteria
{parent=Bacteria}
{wiki}

<Cyanobacteria> are <bacteria> that perform <oxygenic photosynthesis>: <light-dependent reactions> release <oxygen> from <water>. Their photosynthetic machinery operates on <thylakoid> membranes without a <chloroplast> or a <cell nucleus>. This is a particularly useful counterexample to equating <photosynthesis> with being a <plant>. <Chloroplasts> descend from an ancestral cyanobacterial partner through <endosymbiosis>; present-day <cyanobacteria> are independent descendants, rather than <chloroplasts> waiting to become <plants>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26819/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: chloroplasts and cyanobacteria].

= Gram-positive bacteria
{c}
{parent=Bacteria}
{wiki}

Typical <Gram-positive bacteria> have a relatively thick <peptidoglycan> layer outside the <cell membrane>, without the <bacterial outer membrane> of typical <Gram-negative bacteria>. This is a representative <cell envelope> pattern, not a complete description of every member of <bacteria>.

= Gram-negative bacteria
{c}
{parent=Bacteria}
{wiki}

Typical <Gram-negative bacteria> have a relatively thin <peptidoglycan> layer between the <cell membrane> and a <bacterial outer membrane>.

= Bacillus megaterium
{c}
{parent=Bacteria}

<Bacillus megaterium> is the historical species name used for strain KM in the <lysozyme wall-removal experiment>.

= Archaea
{parent=Prokaryote}
{wiki}

<Archaea> are a major evolutionary lineage distinct from <bacteria>. The cellular comparison here concerns the <prokaryotic> members of <archaea>; see <two-domain system> for broader <clade> usage. Their <biological cells> have a <nucleoid> rather than a membrane-bounded <cell nucleus>, yet aspects of <DNA replication>, <transcription> and <biological translation> resemble those of <eukaryotes>. Their characteristic <lipids> differ chemically from the usual bacterial membrane <lipids>, and their <cell walls>, when present, lack <peptidoglycan>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/22-2-structure-of-prokaryotes-bacteria-and-archaea[OpenStax Biology 2e, 22.2: archaeal distinctions].

Some <archaea> perform <methanogenesis>, producing <methane> as part of their <metabolism>. <Archaea> also inhabit ordinary soils and oceans: extreme habitats do not define the lineage. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/22-1-prokaryotic-diversity[OpenStax Biology 2e, 22.1: moderate and extreme environments]. Woese and Fox's 1977 analysis of <ribosomal RNA> distinguished the methanogenic group they called archaebacteria from typical <bacteria>. Their proposed three primary lines of descent were a historical interpretation, rather than the final resolution of deep <phylogenetics>. See https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.74.11.5088[Woese and Fox, 1977: Phylogenetic structure of the prokaryotic domain].

= Asgard archaea
{c}
{parent=Archaea}
{wiki}

<Asgard archaea> are an archaeal group important in studies of <eukaryotic> origins. Molecular comparisons connect their <genes> to the nuclear lineage of <eukaryotes>, rather than making modern <Asgard archaea> the direct ancestors of modern <animals> or <plants>. See the original analysis cited under <two-domain system>.

= Eukaryote
{parent=Organism}
{wiki}

= Eukaryotic
{synonym}

A <eukaryote> is an <organism> belonging to the lineage characterized by a membrane-bounded <cell nucleus> and extensive cellular compartmentalization. A <nuclear envelope> surrounds the nuclear <genome>; nuclear <chromosomes> are typically linear. <Ribosomes> perform <biological translation> outside the <cell nucleus>. See https://openstax.org/books/microbiology/pages/3-4-unique-characteristics-of-eukaryotic-cells[OpenStax Microbiology, 3.4: nuclear organization and protein synthesis]. Specialized <biological cells> can lose their <cell nucleus>, as mature mammalian <red blood cells> do, while remaining parts of <eukaryotic> <organisms>; see https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-2-components-of-the-blood[OpenStax Biology 2e, 40.2: mature red blood cells].

<Yeasts>, <plants> and <animals> are all <eukaryotes>. Typical cells in these groups have <mitochondria>, but <chloroplasts> belong to photosynthetic <plant> and <algae> lineages, not to every <eukaryote>. A <cell wall> occurs in typical <plant> and <fungus> cells but is absent from <animal> cells. These are comparisons of representative cells, not a checklist every specialized cell must satisfy. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-3-eukaryotic-cells[OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.3: nucleus, organelles and representative cells].

The interesting feature is controlled separation: <cell membranes> and <organelle> membranes let different cellular processes occupy distinct compartments while <membrane transport> connects them. The <evolution> of <eukaryotes> also involved partnership: <mitochondria> descend from a bacterial <endosymbiosis>. Modern <phylogenetics> supports an archaeal ancestry for the nuclear lineage, alongside this bacterial contribution; see <two-domain system>. Thus the history of a whole <eukaryote> cannot be read as the history of just one of its <genes>.

\Image[cell-lineages-and-shared-machinery.png]
{title=Shared cellular machinery and distinguishing organization across <bacteria>, <archaea> and <eukaryotes>}
{description=Original comparison table, not a <phylogenetic tree>. The shared functions do not require identical <molecules>. Entries describe representative cellular organization and explicitly qualify exceptions; column positions imply neither ancestry nor evolutionary rank.}

= Fungus
{parent=Eukaryote}
{wiki}

A <fungus> is a <eukaryote> that obtains nutrients by absorption rather than <photosynthesis>. <Fungi> include unicellular <yeasts> and multicellular forms. Their <cell walls> differ from bacterial <peptidoglycan> walls; having a <cell wall> does not make a <fungus> a <plant>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/24-1-characteristics-of-fungi[OpenStax Biology 2e, 24.1: fungal nutrition and organization].

= Yeast
{parent=Fungus}
{wiki}

<Yeasts> are <fungi> whose growth is predominantly unicellular, commonly through <budding> or division into two cells. The word describes a growth form across different fungal lineages, rather than one <species> or an exclusive <clade>; some <fungi> can switch between unicellular and filamentous growth. Thus <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> is a <yeast>, but <yeast> is not another name for that one <species>. See https://booksite.elsevier.com/samplechapters/9780444521491/9780444521491.pdf[Kurtzman, Fell and Boekhout, The Yeasts, chapter 1 (2011): definition and classification].

A <yeast> cell has <eukaryotic> organization, including a <cell nucleus>, <mitochondria> and a <cell wall>. Small size and a unicellular lifestyle do not make it a member of <bacteria>. These compartments let an experimentally accessible <biological cell> retain much of the organization shared with other <eukaryotes>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/24-1-characteristics-of-fungi[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 24.1: fungal cells and yeast growth].

\b[A motivating experiment: can <fermentation> continue without intact <yeast> cells?] In 1897, Eduard Buchner ground brewers' <yeast> with quartz sand and diatomite, added <water>, and pressed out a liquid extract. Adding concentrated cane <sugar> produced <carbon dioxide> after 15–60 minutes. A cell-retaining filter still yielded an active filtrate: with sterilized cane <sugar> in an icebox, <fermentation> began after about a day. Saturated lactose gave no observed <fermentation>. These are reported observations, not invented control results. See https://web.lemoyne.edu/~giunta/buchner.html[Buchner, Alcoholic fermentation without yeast cells (1897), translated excerpt hosted by Carmen Giunta].

\Image[yeast-cellfree-fermentation-evidence.png]
{title=Removing intact <yeast> cells while retaining <fermentation> activity}
{description=Original qualitative evidence schematic based on Buchner's report and Harden's later summary cited here. No measured rates are plotted; the preparations had different conditions and are not a matched quantitative comparison.}

The filtration comparison addresses the objection that surviving intact <yeast> cells caused the activity. The lactose comparison tests <substrate> dependence; it is not a no-<sugar> blank, nor a comparison at matched <sugar> <concentrations>. A proposed modern follow-up would also compare the same extract with and without added <sugar>, accounting for material already in the extract. That is an experimental suggestion, not a result attributed to Buchner. His report specifies brewers' <yeast>; it does not identify the preparation here as a pure <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> strain.

Heat treatment asks a different question: does destroying activity in the extract stop <fermentation>? Buchner's preliminary report described weak or absent activity after heating and filtration, rather than one clean universal threshold. Harden's 1914 account of the subsequent work summarized loss of activity on heating the juice to $50\,{}^\circ\mathrm{C}$. Harden also described requirements for both <enzymes> and <coenzymes>. Cell-free therefore means neither chemical-free nor a preparation containing one purified <enzyme>. See https://www.gutenberg.org/files/44985/44985-h/44985-h.htm[Arthur Harden, Alcoholic Fermentation (1914), chapters II and VI: cell-free activity and the fermenting complex].

The inference is that extracted cellular machinery can sustain these <chemical reactions> without an intact living <biological cell>. This does not show that the extract reproduces, grows or maintains itself like a <yeast>. Moreover, gas evolution alone does not identify <ethanol>: <cellular respiration> can also release <carbon dioxide>. Product identification is a separate measurement; Harden's account describes both <ethanol> and <carbon dioxide> as products of cell-free <alcoholic fermentation>. The beautiful separation is between the <organism> that produces and maintains the machinery and the machinery that catalyzes a particular transformation.

= Schizosaccharomyces pombe
{c}
{parent=Yeast}
{wiki}

= S. pombe
{c}
{synonym}

<Schizosaccharomyces pombe> is a <yeast> <species> used as a <model organism>. It supplies the candidate <genes> in the <yeast cross-species complementation experiment>; the recipient remains <Saccharomyces cerevisiae>.

= Saccharomyces cerevisiae
{c}
{parent=Yeast}
{wiki}

= S. cerevisiae
{c}
{synonym}

<Saccharomyces cerevisiae> is a particular <species> of <yeast>, used in baking, brewing and laboratory research. During ordinary vegetative growth it reproduces by <budding>: a smaller daughter grows from the mother before separation. Laboratory strains can be maintained as either <haploid> or <diploid> <biological cells>. This makes it possible to investigate <genes> in a single <eukaryote>, then use controlled crosses and <meiosis> to connect a <genotype> to a <phenotype>. See https://smburgess.faculty.ucdavis.edu/wp-content/uploads/sites/33/2019/08/Burgess-Powers-Mell-eLS.pdf[Burgess, Powers and Mell, Budding Yeast Saccharomyces cerevisiae as a Model Genetic Organism (2017), pp. 1–2].

The practical value of <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> as a <model organism> is the combination of easy laboratory cultivation, accessible <genetics>, and cellular functions shared with other <eukaryotes>. A <haploid> strain has one set of <chromosomes>, so a changed <allele> is not accompanied by a second copy at that <genetic locus>. But a <mutation> need not produce an observable <phenotype> under every condition. The <yeast> is also a whole <organism>, not a miniature human: transferring a result requires evidence that the relevant function is shared in the target system. See https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4012490/[Duina, Miller and Keeney, Budding Yeast for Budding Geneticists (2014): cultivation, genetic tools and conserved functions].

For a concrete example, Hartwell, Culotti and Reid's 1970 work followed temperature-sensitive <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> strains with successive microscope photographs. Failure to complete a particular part of the <cell cycle> helped identify required <gene> functions. Their distinction between when a function is required and where a cell finally stops is especially instructive: the visible endpoint need not be the time of the underlying defect. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/5271168/[Hartwell, Culotti and Reid, Genetic control of the cell-division cycle in yeast. I. Detection of mutants (1970): primary abstract]. This is a useful way to turn an observable change in a <biological cell> into a question about <genes> and causal order.

In <alcoholic fermentation>, <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> converts <sugars> into <ethanol> and <carbon dioxide>. The <carbon dioxide> helps dough rise; the <ethanol> is useful in alcoholic drinks. The <metabolism> also supports production of <ATP>, a usable cellular <energy> carrier. This is one metabolic ability of one <species>, not the definition of every <yeast>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/7-5-metabolism-without-oxygen[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 7.5: alcohol fermentation] and the Burgess, Powers and Mell account above.

For <glucose>, the ideal net <stoichiometry> is
$$
\mathrm{C}_6\mathrm{H}_{12}\mathrm{O}_6\longrightarrow 2\mathrm{C}_2\mathrm{H}_5\mathrm{OH}+2\mathrm{CO}_2.
$$
One <molecule> of <glucose> supplies six <carbon> <chemical atoms>: four end up in the two <ethanol> <molecules> and two in the two <carbon dioxide> <molecules>. The twelve <hydrogen> and six <oxygen> <chemical atoms> also balance. This is a net material balance across several <enzyme>-catalyzed steps, not a single <chemical reaction> step or the complete bookkeeping for cellular <energy> carriers. The standard pathway accounting yields a net two <ATP> per <glucose>: the initial breakdown produces the <ATP>, while the later conversion to <ethanol> regenerates a <coenzyme> needed to keep the breakdown going. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/7-2-glycolysis[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 7.2: net ATP accounting] and the section 7.5 cited above.

\Image[saccharomyces-fermentation-carbon-balance.png]
{title=Ideal <alcoholic fermentation>: <glucose> <carbon> divides between <ethanol> and <carbon dioxide>}
{description=Original schematic and theoretical toy calculation, not measured culture data. The dots count <carbon> <chemical atoms>; they do not depict molecular bonds or traced individual atoms. Starting with $10\,\mathrm{mmol}$ of <glucose>, conversion of $x\,\mathrm{mmol}$ leaves $10-x$ and forms $2x$ each of <ethanol> and <carbon dioxide>. The coincident product lines count <molecules>, not equal masses.}

At complete conversion, this toy gives $20\,\mathrm{mmol}$ of <ethanol> and $20\,\mathrm{mmol}$ of <carbon dioxide>. The <carbon> balance is $6\times10=2\times20+1\times20=60\,\mathrm{mmol}$ of <carbon> <chemical atoms>. A real culture also builds cellular material, can use other metabolic routes, and may retain dissolved <carbon dioxide>; the toy is a reference balance rather than a prediction of collected gas or beverage yield.

Although <alcoholic fermentation> does not require <molecular oxygen> as a reactant, the presence of <molecular oxygen> does not force <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> to stop fermenting. De Deken's 1966 experiments reported predominantly fermentative <glucose> use during exponential growth in air. Consequently, bubbles do not establish either absence of <molecular oxygen> or an exclusively fermentative <metabolism>. See https://www.microbiologyresearch.org/content/journal/micro/10.1099/00221287-44-2-149[De Deken, The Crabtree Effect: A Regulatory System in Yeast (1966): primary abstract].

= Plant
{parent=Eukaryote}
{wiki}

<Plants> are <eukaryotes>. A typical photosynthetic leaf cell has a <cell nucleus>, <mitochondria>, <chloroplasts>, and a <cell wall> outside its <cell membrane>. A root cell need not have <chloroplasts>; the <plant>'s parts specialize for different functions. <Plants> perform <cellular respiration> as well as <photosynthesis>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-3-eukaryotic-cells[OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.3: plant cell organization].

{parent=Plant}

= Flower
{parent=Plant}

= Stamen
{parent=Flower}

= Stigma
{disambiguate=botany}
{parent=Flower}

= Seed
{parent=Plant}

= Algae
{parent=Eukaryote}
{wiki}

<Algae> is a broad name for several photosynthetic <eukaryotic> groups, rather than one exclusive <clade>. The older name blue-green algae for <cyanobacteria> is misleading in a comparison of cellular organization: <cyanobacteria> are <bacteria>, with neither <chloroplasts> nor a <cell nucleus>.

= Reclinomonas americana
{c}
{parent=Eukaryote}

<Reclinomonas americana> is a microbial <eukaryote> whose mitochondrial <genome> retains an unusually rich set of bacterial features. The 1997 sequence report provides a concrete observation under <evidence for bacterial ancestry of organelles>.

= Animal
{parent=Eukaryote}
{wiki}

<Animals> are <multicellular organisms> with <eukaryotic> cellular organization. A representative <animal> cell has a <cell membrane>, <cell nucleus> and <mitochondria>, but no <cell wall> or <chloroplasts>. A <neuron> illustrates how this common plan can acquire a specialized shape and signaling function. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-3-eukaryotic-cells[OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.3: animal cell organization].

= Insect
{parent=Animal}

= Drosophila pseudoobscura
{c}
{parent=Insect}
{wiki}

<Drosophila pseudoobscura> is the <species> of <insect> used in the <dietary divergence and mating isolation in Drosophila pseudoobscura> experiment. The independently maintained experimental <biological populations> were not already different <species>.

= Amphibian
{parent=Animal}
{wiki}

= Frog
{parent=Amphibian}
{wiki}

= Xenopus laevis
{c}
{parent=Frog}
{wiki}

<Xenopus laevis> is a <frog> used in <developmental biology>, including the <intestinal nucleus transfer experiment> and <Xenopus laevis egg extract> preparations.

= Fish
{parent=Animal}
{wiki}

<Fish> are aquatic <animals>; their feeding roles depend on their diet and life stage. Small and large <fish> in the illustrative <food web> are generic groups chosen to distinguish two feeding positions, rather than a measured relationship between named <species>.

= Taxonomy
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Taxonomy
{synonym}

<Taxonomy> names and classifies <organisms>. Classification can organize known diversity and make comparisons useful, but ranks and names are not themselves evidence of ancestry. <Phylogenetics> infers that ancestry; a <clade> includes an ancestor and all its descendants. A shared feature such as lacking a <cell nucleus> need not identify an exclusive <clade>. The <organism> branch here organizes cell types for reading and is not a proposed <phylogenetic tree>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/20-1-organizing-life-on-earth[OpenStax Biology 2e, 20.1: classification and evolutionary history].

= Domain
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Taxonomy (biology)}
{wiki}

A biological <domain (biology)> is a high-level rank used to classify <organisms> in the <three-domain system>. Distinguish a taxonomic rank from a primary branch in an inferred <phylogenetic tree>: retaining familiar names does not settle the branching relationships.

= Three-domain system
{parent=Domain (biology)}
{wiki}

The <three-domain system> recognizes <bacteria>, <archaea> and <eukaryotes> as three domains. In 1990, Woese, Kandler and Wheelis proposed the domain rank and the names Bacteria, Archaea and Eucarya, using molecular comparisons to argue that the older <prokaryote>/<eukaryote> division hid important differences. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2112744/[Woese, Kandler and Wheelis, 1990: Towards a natural system of organisms].

The associated three-domain ancestry model places the nuclear lineage of <eukaryotes> alongside <archaea>, rather than within them. This historically influential interpretation should be distinguished from the <two-domain system>; listing three familiar groups does not establish three independent primary branches.

= Two-domain system
{parent=Domain (biology)}
{wiki}

The <two-domain system> recognizes <bacteria> and <archaea> as the two primary lineages, placing the nuclear ancestry of <eukaryotes> within <archaea>. In this model, <archaea> as a complete <clade> includes that <eukaryotic> lineage; restricting the name to <prokaryotic> members describes a different grouping. Williams and colleagues' study, published online in 2019 and in a 2020 journal issue, found support for this model and a close relationship to <Asgard archaea>. See https://doi.org/10.1038/s41559-019-1040-x[Williams et al., 2020: Phylogenomics provides robust support for a two-domains tree of life].

This updates the interpretation of the 1977 and 1990 classifications rather than undoing their discovery of deep differences between <bacteria> and <archaea>. Deep <phylogenetic trees> depend on the sampled <genes>, <organisms> and inference model; the precise closest archaeal relatives and early sequence of events require further evidence. Bacterial ancestry of <mitochondria> through <endosymbiosis> is a separate part of <eukaryotic> history.

= Species
{parent=Taxonomy (biology)}
{wiki}

A <species> is a named unit of biological classification. The useful criteria vary among groups: reproductive compatibility can inform classification in many <animals>, while microbial comparisons also use <genomes> and other characters. <Escherichia coli> is a <species>; K-12 MG1655 identifies a particular strain within it. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/18-2-formation-of-new-species[OpenStax Biology 2e, 18.2: species concepts].

= Species concept
{parent=Species}

A <species concept> states what should make <organisms> members of the same <species>; an operational criterion states what observations will test that claim. <Reproductive isolation>, distinguishable <morphology> and ancestry inferred through <phylogenetics> answer different questions. In a lineage-based interpretation, they are evidence for separation of evolving <biological populations>, and need not appear together. <Species Concepts and Species Delimitation by Kevin de Queiroz>[pp. 880–883, Table 1, Figure 1 and “Conceptualization versus Delimitation”: distinguish defining properties from operational evidence]{ref}

= Biological species concept
{parent=Species concept}

The <biological species concept> groups naturally interbreeding <biological populations> with viable, fertile offspring, distinguished from other such groups by <reproductive isolation>. An operational test examines mating, <fertilization>, offspring survival and offspring <reproduction>, rather than only whether two adults can be paired. <Sexual reproduction> is its scope: <fossils> and <organisms> studied only through <asexual reproduction> cannot supply the same mating evidence. <Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[§18.2, “Species and the Ability to Reproduce”, “Speciation” and “Reproductive Isolation”]{ref}

For an original hypothetical example, imagine two separated <biological populations> that reproduce successfully after contact. The separation alone did not demonstrate <reproductive isolation>. Conversely, failure to mate in one unfamiliar chamber could reflect the assay conditions. Field observations of <gene flow> and tests under suitable conditions address the biological question more directly. A successful laboratory pairing does not establish frequent interbreeding in nature.

= Morphological species concept
{parent=Species concept}

A <morphological species concept> distinguishes <species> using <morphology>: measurable differences in form or structure. It can be applied to preserved specimens and <fossils> when mating cannot be observed. Similar appearance need not imply the same <species>, and different appearance need not imply different <species>. <Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[§18.2, “Species and the Ability to Reproduce”, paragraphs accompanying Figures 18.9–18.10: appearance can mislead]{ref}

An original operational example is to compare a chosen structure in specimens of comparable age, sex and rearing conditions. Suppose two apparent groups differ in size, but one group contains only juveniles. The observed difference does not yet diagnose two <species>. Specify the measured <phenotype>, variation within each sample, and whether the distinction persists across comparable specimens. A single striking specimen is a poor substitute for that comparison.

= Phylogenetic species concept
{parent=Species concept}

<Phylogenetic species concepts> use ancestry and diagnostic inherited differences; versions differ over requiring a <clade>, an exclusive ancestry pattern at <genetic loci>, or a distinguishable character combination. State which criterion is intended. <Species Concepts and Species Delimitation by Kevin de Queiroz>[p. 880, Table 1: distinct monophyletic, genealogical and diagnosable versions of phylogenetic criteria]{ref}

For an original operational example, suppose a <phylogenetic tree> inferred from one <genetic locus> separates two sampled <biological populations>. Compare further <genetic loci> and additional local samples before interpreting that branch as a <species> boundary. Otherwise the test may merely identify the particular ancestry of the sampled <alleles>. Adding more sites from the same <genetic locus> improves that locus's evidence without creating an independent ancestry test.

= Species delimitation
{parent=Species}

<Species delimitation> infers the boundaries and number of <species> from evidence. No single arbitrary percentage of <nucleotide sequence> difference is a universal boundary. Divergence within an old, widespread <species> can exceed divergence between recently separated <species>; geography and multiple kinds of evidence matter. <Species Concepts and Species Delimitation by Kevin de Queiroz>[pp. 884–885, “Alternatives to the Traditional Properties”: divergence cutoffs and geographic differentiation]{ref}

An original counterexample to a universal percentage rule: an assay could examine a strongly conserved <gene> in one comparison and a rapidly changing <nucleotide sequence> in another. Equal percentages then answer different historical questions. Even the denominator needs specifying: all sites, aligned sites, or only a filtered region? A threshold is an operational rule for a stated assay and group of <organisms>, whose errors need evaluating. It is not a definition that automatically transfers to every <species>.

\Image[species-evidence-criteria.png]
{title=Contrasting operational evidence for <species delimitation>}
{description=Original hypothetical comparison of <morphology>, mating and ancestry evidence. Each observation tests a different claim; the combinations are thought experiments, not a dataset or verdicts about named <species>. “Not tested” does not mean absent.}

The diagram's hypothetical pair B has similar <morphology> but reduced mating and distinct ancestry evidence. A <morphological species concept> could miss that distinction. Pair C instead has different <morphology> but compatible mating and ancestry that fails to separate the samples. That combination calls for checking variation in <phenotype> within a <species>. Pair A has only a structural difference observed: the other criteria remain untested. These examples show why missing evidence, disagreeing evidence and agreeing evidence require different interpretations.

= Species, populations and strains
{parent=Species}

A <species> can encompass many <biological populations>. A <biological population> specifies a set of <organisms> in a place and time; a <biological strain> identifies a maintained variant or lineage, often in a laboratory. A <species> name, a local census and a <biological strain> designation therefore describe different things. Multiple independently maintained cultures can also share the same <biological strain> designation.

For example, a culture identified as <Escherichia coli> K-12 MG1655 specifies more than the <species> alone. Two flasks inoculated from that culture are separate experimental <biological populations>; the separate containers do not establish two <species> or two different <biological strains>. This is an original experimental bookkeeping example, not a claim that every <biological strain> name encodes a complete <genotype>.

= Morphology
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Morphology
{synonym}

<Morphology> concerns biological form and structure; a structural difference is one aspect of <phenotype>, not automatically a <species> boundary.

= Developmental biology
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Biological development
{parent=Developmental biology}

= Blastula
{parent=Biological development}
{wiki}

A <blastula> is an early multicellular stage of <animal> <biological development>. The <intestinal nucleus transfer experiment> distinguishes development beyond this stage from the earliest divisions.

= Paleontology
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Fossil
{parent=Paleontology}
{wiki}

= Ecology
{parent=Biology}

The goal of <ecology> is to explain where <organisms> live, how many there are, and how their interactions with one another and their surroundings change those patterns. Living partners include <prey>, <predators> and <parasites>; nonliving conditions include <light>, <temperature> and <water>. An <ecosystem> brings these interacting parts into one study system. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/44-1-the-scope-of-ecology[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 44.1: the scope of ecology].

Start with an invented aquatic <food web>: photosynthetic <algae> feed <zooplankton>, which feed small <fish>, which feed large <fish>. That <food chain> has four feeding positions and three feeding arrows. Dead material also reaches <decomposers> through <detritus>, so the <food web> branches rather than forming a single ladder.

\Image[illustrative-food-web.png]
{title=An illustrative aquatic <food web>: feeding arrows point from resource to <consumer>}
{description=Original schematic, not a measured <ecosystem>. Solid arrows mean RESOURCE → CONSUMER; dashed arrows mean transfer of dead material to <detritus>. Box sizes and arrow widths do not represent abundance or transfer rates. <Algae>, <zooplankton>, small <fish> and large <fish> are generic groups, not identified <species>. Selected dead-material transfers are shown; further feeding and recycling routes are omitted.}

The appeal of <ecology> is that an <organism>'s surroundings include other <organisms> that change those surroundings. <Photosynthesis> links <light> to living material; <predation> transfers that material between <organisms>; <decomposers> return its constituent <chemical elements> to forms that can be used again. These connections help explain why changing one part of an <ecosystem> can affect distant parts of its <food web>, although an arrow alone does not predict the size or even the direction of an indirect change. <Population dynamics> supplies quantitative tools when the question concerns changing counts.

In 1866, Ernst Haeckel's \i[Generelle Morphologie der Organismen] included a section titled \i[Oecologie und Chorologie] in volume 2, beginning on page 286. This places the terminology of <ecology> in an early systematic treatment of <organisms> and their relationships. See https://books.google.com/books?id=tKFbIVgJS8wC[Haeckel, Generelle Morphologie der Organismen, volume 2 (1866), contents and p. 286]. A later quantitative landmark is Raymond L. Lindeman's 1942 treatment of <ecosystems> through feeding relationships and <energy flow>. See https://doi.org/10.2307/1930126[Lindeman, The Trophic-Dynamic Aspect of Ecology, Ecology 23 (1942), 399–417].

= Population
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Ecology}

= Biological population
{synonym}

A <biological population> is a set of <organisms> of a <species> occupying a specified area or experimental setting at a specified time. State the boundary for the question being studied: a flask, a breeding locality, or a wider region connected by <gene flow>. A <biological population> need not be perfectly mixed or isolated.

= Ecosystem
{parent=Ecology}
{wiki}

An <ecosystem> is a system of interacting <organisms> and their nonliving surroundings. A pond study might include <algae>, <fish>, <bacteria>, <water>, dissolved <chemical compounds> and incoming <light>. The useful step is to state the boundary and time interval: the pond surface, bottom and shoreline might enclose the study region, but <water>, <organisms> and <energy> can cross them. The chosen boundary specifies what counts as internal storage, input or output; it does not make the <ecosystem> sealed. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/46-1-ecology-of-ecosystems[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 46.1: ecosystems and their components].

For a toy pond <water> budget over one day, begin with $1000\,\mathrm{m^3}$, add $120\,\mathrm{m^3}$ crossing inward, and remove $90\,\mathrm{m^3}$ crossing outward. With all other transfers omitted by assumption, the final amount is $1000+120-90=1030\,\mathrm{m^3}$. The same bookkeeping asks whether a change inside an <ecosystem> came from internal processes or exchanges with its surroundings. Changing the boundary to include an upstream reservoir changes which transfers are internal, while preserving the accounting.

= Primary producer
{parent=Ecosystem}

A <primary producer> builds organic <chemical compounds> from inorganic sources. Photosynthetic <algae>, <plants> and <cyanobacteria> use <light> <energy> for this role through <photosynthesis> and <carbon fixation>. The role supplies new living material to a <food web>; it does not mean that the <organism> creates <energy>.

= Consumer
{disambiguate=food chain}
{parent=Ecosystem}
{wiki}

= Consumer
{synonym}

A <consumer> obtains organic material by feeding on other <organisms> or their products. A <consumer> feeding on <primary producers> is a primary <consumer>; one feeding on primary <consumers> is a secondary <consumer>. These names describe positions in a <food chain>, rather than permanent labels for a <species>.

= Decomposer
{parent=Consumer (food chain)}
{wiki}

A <decomposer> breaks down dead organic material and absorbs products of that breakdown; many <bacteria> and <fungi> perform this role. <Decomposers> connect <detritus> from different <trophic levels> to material recycling, so placing all <decomposers> at one final step of every <food chain> is misleading. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/46-1-ecology-of-ecosystems[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 46.1: grazing and detrital food webs].

= Omnivore
{parent=Consumer (food chain)}
{wiki}

An <omnivore> feeds on both <primary producers> and <consumers>. Its mixed diet explains why a <trophic level> need not be a whole number or a fixed label throughout an <organism>'s life.

= Detritus
{parent=Ecosystem}
{wiki}

<Detritus> is nonliving organic material, including dead remains and organic wastes. It is a resource for <decomposers>, not itself an <organism> or a <predator>. In the illustrative <food web>, the dashed arrow from <algae> to <detritus> denotes a material transfer; the solid arrow from <detritus> to <decomposers> denotes resource use.

= Trophic level
{parent=Ecosystem}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{TL}$}

A <trophic level> describes feeding position relative to <primary producers>. Assign <primary producers> level 1; in the illustrated <food chain>, <zooplankton> occupy level 2, small <fish> level 3, and large <fish> level 4. Following a single feeding path adds one level at each step. The labels make dependence on the initial resource visible, but a branching <food web> can give an <organism> several feeding paths. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/46-1-ecology-of-ecosystems[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 46.1: feeding positions and branching food webs].

An <omnivore> can obtain food from more than one <trophic level>. As an explicitly assumed diet-weighted example, suppose an <omnivore> gets half its assimilated food <energy> from level-1 <primary producers> and half from level-2 <consumers>. Define its <trophic level> as one plus the weighted <mean> level of its food: $1+(1/2)1+(1/2)2=2.5$. This computed position depends on the specified diet and weighting convention; it is not an immutable property of the <species>. A different life stage or diet can change it.

= Energy flow
{disambiguate=ecology}
{parent=Ecosystem}
{wiki}

= Energy flow
{synonym}

<Energy flow> tracks <energy> entering an <ecosystem>, passing through <organisms> and leaving its boundary. To see its quantitative effect, assume net production at one <trophic level> is $P_1=1000\,\mathrm{kJ\,m^{-2}\,day^{-1}}$ and assume a transfer efficiency $\varepsilon=0.10$ at each of two successive steps. Here $P_i$ measures the <energy> incorporated into new living material per unit <area> per day, after that level's own <cellular respiration>; it is not a count of <organisms> or a standing stock. The <joule> supplies the <energy> unit, with $1\,\mathrm{kJ}=1000\,\mathrm J$.
$$
P_{i+1}=\varepsilon P_i,\qquad P_2=100,\qquad P_3=10\quad\mathrm{kJ\,m^{-2}\,day^{-1}}.
$$

\Image[trophic-energy-budget.png]
{title=An assumed <energy flow> budget: 1000 → 100 → 10 kJ per square metre per day}
{description=Original toy calculation with 10 percent transfer efficiency assumed at both steps. Bars use one linear scale; the labels retain visibility of the smallest bar. The differences 900 and 90 are production not transferred along the selected <food chain>, not measurements of <heat> alone. The example is separate from the four-position illustrative <food web>.}

Only $10/1000=0.01$, or one percent, of the initial production reaches level 3 in this toy <food chain>. The first step leaves $1000-100=900$ and the second $100-10=90$ outside the next level's new production. These differences can include uneaten material, wastes, and <energy> released as <heat> during <cellular respiration>; their separate amounts have not been assigned. <Detritus> can still carry <energy> to <decomposers>. The 10 percent efficiency is an assumption, not a universal law: transfer efficiencies vary with the <organisms>, food and conditions. The production ratio $P_{i+1}/P_i$ also differs from the fraction of an individual meal converted into growth. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/46-2-energy-flow-through-ecosystems[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 46.2: production and transfer efficiency].

The shrinking budget explains why supporting another <trophic level> requires enough production below it. It does not prove an exact maximum length for every <food chain>, particularly when a <food web> imports resources or has branching diets. Lindeman's 1942 paper grouped feeding relationships into <trophic levels>, compared transfers and <cellular respiration>, and emphasized the <ecosystem> as a unit connecting living and nonliving components. That viewpoint made different <organisms> comparable through a common <energy> budget. See https://doi.org/10.2307/1930126[Lindeman, The Trophic-Dynamic Aspect of Ecology (1942)].

Material recycling and <energy flow> describe different bookkeeping. <Carbon>, <nitrogen> and <phosphorus> can be reused through <chemical reactions> and transfers between <organisms> and their surroundings. <Energy> transferred as <heat> is conserved, but is no longer the same store of chemical <energy> available to a <consumer>; photosynthetic <primary producers> need continuing <light> input. A <decomposer> helps recycle material and also releases <heat> through <metabolism>. Thus loss from one feeding budget means transfer into other forms or routes, never destruction of <energy>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/46-3-biogeochemical-cycles[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 46.3: material cycles]. <Conservation of energy> applies to the whole accounting.

= Zooplankton
{parent=Ecosystem}
{wiki}

<Zooplankton> are drifting aquatic <consumers>, including small <animals>. In the illustrative <food web>, the chosen <zooplankton> feed on <algae> and serve as <prey> for small <fish>; this assumed diet is not a claim about every kind of <zooplankton>.

= Endosymbiosis
{parent=Ecology}

<Endosymbiosis> is an ecological association in which one <organism>, the <endosymbiont>, lives inside another. The definition specifies location, not that both partners benefit, nor that the internal partner has become an <organelle>. An intracellular association can remain an association between distinct <organisms>. <Endosymbiotic theory> addresses a different question: how particular ancestral <bacteria> became inherited, functionally integrated <organelles> during <evolution>.

The striking consequence is that a <eukaryotic> <biological cell> can combine histories: its nuclear lineage and its <mitochondria> need not have the same ancestry. <Mitochondria> have bacterial ancestry related to <Alphaproteobacteria>; the <plastids> of <plants> and many <algae>, including <chloroplasts>, have ancestry within <cyanobacteria>. These are ancestral lineages, not a claim that a named living bacterial <species> is the literal ancestor. Present-day <bacteria> and <organelles> have both evolved since their shared ancestors. <Origin and Evolution of Plastids and Photosynthesis in Eukaryotes by Geoffrey I. McFadden>[Opening paragraphs and “The endosymbiont: when and who?”: bacterial origins and uncertainty about closest relatives]{ref}

\Image[endosymbiosis-organelle-integration.png]
{title=From an internal partner to an integrated <organelle>}
{description=Original conceptual schematic, not an observed historical sequence. Persistence, metabolic exchange, <endosymbiotic gene transfer>, <organelle protein import> and coordination of division illustrate integration; they are not dated stages in a compulsory order. The illustrated partner is generic, and the host outline does not specify the ancestral host of <mitochondria>. The protein-delivery arrow abbreviates nuclear <gene expression>, <biological translation> in the <cytosol> and <organelle protein import>; it does not place <protein> synthesis inside the <cell nucleus>.}

= Endosymbiont
{parent=Endosymbiosis}

An <endosymbiont> is the internal partner in an <endosymbiosis>. Calling an <organism> an <endosymbiont> does not by itself classify it as an <organelle> or imply an irreversible evolutionary transition.

= Endosymbiotic theory
{parent=Endosymbiosis}

<Endosymbiotic theory> explains the bacterial origins of <mitochondria> and <plastids> through enduring internal associations and subsequent integration. Survival inside a <biological cell> is only part of the problem: exchange through <membrane transport>, inherited division, <gene expression> and delivery of required <proteins> must work together. The elegance is evolutionary reuse of an existing cellular capability, followed by changes that make the partners operate as one system. <Origin and Evolution of Plastids and Photosynthesis in Eukaryotes by Geoffrey I. McFadden>[Abstract and “How to tame your symbiont”: metabolic, genetic and division integration]{ref}

The historical <On the origin of mitosing cells by Lynn Sagan> (1967, abstract and Introduction, pp. 225–226) synthesized a symbiotic account of <eukaryotic> origins and proposed tests using molecular evidence. Its Introduction explicitly acknowledged earlier proposals. It also hypothesized origins for other structures; the modern evidence for <mitochondria> and <plastids> should be assessed separately from the rest of that historical scheme.

= Evidence for bacterial ancestry of organelles
{parent=Endosymbiotic theory}

Resemblance is a clue, not sufficient evidence of <common descent>. A small size, two surrounding membranes, division, retained <DNA> or bacterial-looking <ribosomes> does not uniquely identify an ancestor. Internal membranes exist outside these <organelles>, and a similar function can favor similar structures. The stronger question is whether independently examined molecular characters support the same historical relationship.

A concrete genomic observation is the mitochondrial sequence reported in 1997 for <Reclinomonas americana>: $69{,}034$ <base pairs> containing 97 identified <genes>, including four for a bacterial-type multisubunit <RNA polymerase>. This is a reported <genome> inventory, not a calculation from the pictured shape of a <mitochondrion>. Retention of such bacterial features strengthens the ancestry argument; a living <Reclinomonas americana> is not itself an ancestral <eukaryote>. The inspected abstract supplies these observations but not the detailed sequencing protocol or contamination controls, so those details are not attributed to this report here. <An ancestral mitochondrial DNA resembling a eubacterial genome in miniature by B. Franz Lang and others>[Abstract, Nature 387 (1997), pp. 493–497: genome length, gene inventory and bacterial-type RNA polymerase]{ref}

A sequence-based investigation obtains <nucleotide sequences> by <DNA sequencing>, identifies comparable <genes>, makes a <sequence alignment> and uses <phylogenetics> to infer relationships. The readout is a proposed relationship with support and uncertainty, not a photograph of an ancient encounter. Comparisons of <plastid> sequences with bacterial sequences support cyanobacterial ancestry, while the exact closest sampled cyanobacterial group has depended on the chosen data and inference. A bacterial affinity in one <gene> is weaker than coherent evidence across several <genes> and cellular systems. <Origin and Evolution of Plastids and Photosynthesis in Eukaryotes by Geoffrey I. McFadden>[“The endosymbiont: when and who?”: alternative phylogenetic placements and lateral gene transfer]{ref}

For such an analysis, useful checks include distinguishing <organelle> <DNA> from bacterial contamination, verifying sequence quality, comparing corresponding rather than unrelated <genes>, sampling several bacterial groups and testing sensitivity to the <sequence alignment> and evolutionary <mathematical model>. These are methodological requirements, not a claim that this article has rerun a particular published analysis. <Horizontal gene transfer>, <gene> loss and unequal evolutionary change can make an individual <gene>'s history differ from a whole <organism>'s history. A supported bacterial origin therefore need not identify an extant donor <species>, an exact date or the original mode of entry. <Origin and Evolution of Plastids and Photosynthesis in Eukaryotes by Geoffrey I. McFadden>[“The endosymbiont: when and who?”: ancient origin and confounding lateral transfer]{ref}

= Endosymbiotic gene transfer
{parent=Endosymbiotic theory}

<Endosymbiotic gene transfer> moves genetic material from an <endosymbiont> or its descendant <organelle> into the host <genome>, notably the nuclear <genome>. A transferred <DNA> segment can remain silent. For a transferred <gene> to replace an <organelle>-encoded function, nuclear <gene expression> must work and its <protein> must reach the right compartment through <organelle protein import>. Transfer of <DNA> and transport of <proteins> are distinct processes in opposite directions. <Experimental Reconstruction of Functional Gene Transfer from the Tobacco Plastid Genome to the Nucleus by Sandra Stegemann and Ralph Bock>[Introduction, p. 2869, and Discussion, p. 2875: transferred sequences and the additional targeting problem]{ref}

<Gene> loss and <endosymbiotic gene transfer> are different: absence from an <organelle> <genome> does not by itself show that a functional nuclear copy exists. Conversely, an imported <protein> may derive from a host <gene> rather than a transferred <gene>. The surviving <organelle> <genome> is therefore a partial record, not a complete inventory of either ancestral <bacteria> or the modern <organelle>'s machinery. <Origin and Evolution of Plastids and Photosynthesis in Eukaryotes by Geoffrey I. McFadden>[“How to tame your symbiont”: losses, transfers, host recruitment and repatriation of proteins]{ref}

= Functional plastid-to-nucleus gene transfer experiment
{parent=Endosymbiotic gene transfer}

<Experimental Reconstruction of Functional Gene Transfer from the Tobacco Plastid Genome to the Nucleus by Sandra Stegemann and Ralph Bock> (2006, Results, pp. 2870–2874) separated relocation of <DNA> from activation of a relocated <gene>. Engineered tobacco <chloroplasts> carried an nptII <gene> with nuclear expression signals beside an aadA <gene> with plastid expression signals. Selection on kanamycin recovered plants with the segment in the nuclear <genome>. Crosses then replaced the engineered <chloroplasts> with ordinary <chloroplasts>: the nuclear segment remained, but the plants were initially sensitive to spectinomycin and streptomycin. The transferred aadA <gene> was not yet functional in its new setting.

Leaf pieces were cultured on regeneration medium containing both drugs. Resistant regenerated plants supplied the functional readout. Using both drugs reduced selection of spontaneous plastid <mutations> conferring resistance mainly to one drug.
