Reciprocal crosses and selfing distinguished maternal plastid inheritance from nuclear <Mendelian segregation>; eight of sixteen initial candidates had the latter pattern. <Experimental Reconstruction of Functional Gene Transfer from the Tobacco Plastid Genome to the Nucleus by Sandra Stegemann and Ralph Bock>[Figure 1, Tables 1–2 and Results, pp. 2870–2871: selection, eliminated candidates and inheritance controls]{ref}

The molecular readout came from <RNA> hybridization and <DNA sequencing>. A <Northern blot> detected aadA <messenger RNA> in activated lines, with different transcript sizes. Thermal asymmetric interlaced <polymerase chain reaction> recovered upstream <DNA>; products present in activated lines but absent in original-line and ordinary-plant controls were sequenced. The mapped rearrangements connected aadA to the upstream nuclear <gene promoter>. Thus resistance, inheritance, <RNA> detection and changed <nucleotide sequences> supported activation of the transferred nuclear <gene>, rather than resistance alone. <Experimental Reconstruction of Functional Gene Transfer from the Tobacco Plastid Genome to the Nucleus by Sandra Stegemann and Ralph Bock>[Figures 3–4, Results, pp. 2872–2874, and “PCR and DNA sequencing”/“TAIL-PCR”, pp. 2876–2877]{ref}

\Image[endosymbiosis-evidence-gene-transfer.png]
{title=Separate evidence for ancestry, <DNA> transfer and functional activation}
{description=Original evidence map and simplified experimental logic. Boxes summarize questions, readouts and controls; no measured <phylogenetic tree>, <nucleotide sequence>, deletion map or quantitative response is copied. <Organelle protein import> remains a separate question beyond this tobacco experiment.}

This engineered test demonstrates one route to functional nuclear <gene expression> after <plastid> <DNA> transfer. It does not establish a natural transfer rate, replay ancient <endosymbiosis> or demonstrate that the resulting <protein> is imported into a <chloroplast>. The authors explicitly identify acquisition of targeting information as an additional step not reproduced by this experiment. <Experimental Reconstruction of Functional Gene Transfer from the Tobacco Plastid Genome to the Nucleus by Sandra Stegemann and Ralph Bock>[Discussion, p. 2875: antibiotic selection and missing transit-peptide acquisition]{ref}

= Parasitism
{parent=Ecology}

<Parasitism> is an interaction in which a <parasite> obtains resources from a <host>, reducing some component of the <host>'s performance. Its demographic effects depend on exposure, within-host processes and transmission opportunities.

= Parasitoid
{parent=Parasitism}

A <parasitoid> develops on or in a <host> and eventually kills it. Host-parasitoid transmission therefore has different offspring accounting from ordinary <predation> or a nonlethal parasite-burden model.

= Host
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Parasitism}

= Host
{synonym}

A biological <host> provides the environment or resources used by a <parasite> or other associated organism.

= Parasite
{parent=Parasitism}

A <parasite> is an organism benefiting from <parasitism>. <Microparasite> and <macroparasite> distinguish useful transmission-model descriptions of within-host multiplication and distributed <parasite> burden.

= Macroparasite
{parent=Parasite}

A <macroparasite> is typically modelled using the number of <parasites> carried by each <host>. Adult parasitic worms illustrate why <within-host parasite burden>, developmental stages, mating and <host> exposure heterogeneity matter beyond an infected/not-infected classification.

= Within-host parasite burden
{parent=Macroparasite}
{title2=$j$}

The <within-host parasite burden> is the number of <parasites>, or a specified parasite-load measure, carried by one <host>. A population's <mean> burden and its infected-host <prevalence> are different summaries of the burden distribution.

= Microparasite
{parent=Parasite}

A <microparasite> is typically modelled as multiplying rapidly within a <host>, so infection status rather than the exact organism count is the first between-host state variable. <SIS model>, <SIR model> and <SEIR model> descriptions are examples. Additional within-host structure can still be required.

= Predation
{parent=Ecology}

<Predation> is the consumption of <prey> by a <predator>, affecting the demographic rates of both populations.

In the usual animal-feeding sense, a <predator> kills and eats its <prey>. This distinguishes the interaction from feeding on already-dead material and from the prolonged exploitation of a living <host> in <parasitism>. In the illustrative <food web>, a small <fish> eating <zooplankton> is a <predator>; a large <fish> eating that small <fish> makes the same small <fish> its <prey>. These roles belong to a particular interaction. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/45-6-community-ecology[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 45.6: predation and community interactions].

For a toy count, suppose 50 small <fish> begin a day and 8 are eaten by large <fish>. With births, other deaths and movement explicitly omitted, 42 small <fish> remain, and the fraction removed by <predation> is $8/50=0.16$. This measures removal of <prey>, not production of new <predators>. Food intake can support survival and reproduction, but eight meals do not imply eight new <predators>; <energy flow> and life-history details intervene. <Lotka-Volterra equations> provide one idealized account of such <population dynamics>, rather than a guarantee that every interacting pair must cycle.

= Prey
{parent=Predation}

<Prey> is an <organism> consumed by a <predator> in <predation>. The name describes the receiving end of that interaction: a small <fish> can be <prey> for a large <fish> while being a <predator> of <zooplankton>. In a <food web> drawn with resource-to-<consumer> arrows, a feeding arrow goes outward from the <prey> toward its <predator>. This convention makes the route of food and <energy> easy to follow.

= Predator
{parent=Predation}

A <predator> is an <organism> that consumes <prey> through <predation>. A <predator> need not occupy the highest <trophic level> of an <ecosystem>, and the label does not specify its size or guarantee that it has no <predators> of its own. In the illustrative <food chain>, the small <fish> is a <predator> at level 3 and also <prey> for the large <fish> at level 4. Separating these relational labels from <species> names prevents a misleading division of the world into permanent <predator> and <prey> categories.

= Protein
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

A <protein> is a biological <molecule> made of one or more <polypeptide chains>: ordered <amino acid residues> joined by <peptide bonds>. <Biological translation> assembles a chain in the order specified by <messenger RNA> <codons>. <Protein folding> then brings parts of that chain into spatial relationships that support its function. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9879/[Cooper, The Cell: protein composition and structure]. These processes can overlap in a <biological cell>; the sequence-to-shape explanation does not require waiting until <biological translation> finishes before any folding begins. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9843/[Cooper, The Cell: protein folding and processing].

The useful connection is from information to physical work. A <protein>'s <protein primary structure> records an order; its <protein tertiary structure> can bring distant <amino acid residues> together at an <active site>. An <enzyme> uses such an arrangement to recognize a <substrate> and accelerate a <chemical reaction>. Other <proteins> serve as <receptors>, <membrane transport proteins> or structural material. For example, <actin> assembles into filaments whose mechanical properties can be described by <biopolymer mechanics>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26911/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: protein function and binding].

Order matters beyond composition. Let A stand for <alanine> and M for <methionine>, and read from the <N-terminus> to the <C-terminus>. The illustrative strings AMAMAMAMAMAM and AAAAAAMMMMMM each contain exactly six A and six M <amino acid residues>. Each has $12-1=11$ <peptide bonds> when made into one unbranched <polypeptide chain>. In the first string all eleven neighboring pairs have different letters; in the second only the pair at positions 6 and 7 does. A composition <histogram> therefore loses information about which <amino acid residues> are neighbors. Their arrangements can affect <protein folding> and function; these short invented strings neither specify a known functional <protein> nor establish what shape either would adopt.

\Image[protein-sequence-to-fold.png]
{title=From <amino acid> order to an imagined fold and binding site}
{description=Original schematic using two twelve-residue strings with equal composition. The imagined fold uses the first string: colors and numbers preserve its <protein primary structure>, while positions 4 and 9 approach one another. The binding-site cartoon explains spatial cooperation, not an actual <enzyme> or a measured <protein structure>. No actual coordinates, physical scale or exact <protein folding> predictor are implied.}

<Protein structure> is a moving arrangement, not a permanently rigid outline. <Substrate> binding can change an <enzyme>'s shape through <induced fit>, and a functional <protein> can include flexible regions. Sequence, surroundings and interacting <molecules> must therefore be considered together. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26911/[Alberts et al.: binding, conformational changes and protein function].

= Antibody
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

An <antibody> is a <protein> that recognizes particular molecular targets through binding. <Antibodies> participate in <immunity>; laboratory assays such as <Western blots> exploit their selective binding to detect chosen <proteins>.

An <IgG> gives a concrete architecture: two <immunoglobulin heavy chains> and two <immunoglobulin light chains>, joined by <disulfide bonds>. Each arm ends in a <paratope> assembled from an <antibody variable region> of each kind of chain. The two <paratopes> recognize the same <epitope> in this conventional <IgG>; the flexible hinge allows different arm separations. The <antibody constant regions> form the remaining portions. This is an illustrative <IgG>, rather than the architecture of every <antibody> format. <Immunobiology: The Immune System in Health and Disease, 5th edition by Charles A. Janeway, Jr. and others/The structure of a typical antibody molecule>[Immunobiology, 5th edition, §§3-1, 3-2 and 3-4, Figures 3.2 and 3.4: four chains, paired sites and hinge flexibility]{ref}

\Image[antibody-igg-chains-and-bivalent-binding.png]
{title=<IgG> chains, <paratopes> and two-arm binding}
{description=Original schematic of a conventional <IgG>. Color distinguishes <immunoglobulin heavy chains> from <immunoglobulin light chains>; darker tips mark <antibody variable regions>. Each outlined <paratope> includes both chain tips. The second panel contrasts one contact with two contacts to appropriately spaced <epitopes> on an imagined surface. Shapes, spacing and chain paths are illustrative, with no structural coordinates or physical scale.}

Three questions must be kept separate. <Antibody specificity> asks which <antigens> are recognized. <Antibody affinity> describes one <paratope>–<epitope> interaction. <Avidity> describes the combined attachment when several contacts can cooperate. A selective <antibody> can have modest <antibody affinity>, and a <monoclonal antibody> can still show <cross-reactivity>. For an assay, the practical question is whether the observed readout identifies the intended <antigen> under the tested conditions; <Western blot antibody specificity controls> make that question concrete.

= Primary antibody
{parent=Antibody}

In an indirect <Western blot>, the <primary antibody> binds an <epitope> on the proposed target <protein>. Its binding must be tested in the actual sample and assay conditions through <Western blot antibody specificity controls>.

= Secondary antibody
{parent=Antibody}

A <secondary antibody> recognizes another <antibody>, rather than the proposed target <protein> directly. In <Western blot antibody detection>, its attached label makes retained <primary antibodies> visible. A <secondary antibody> must recognize the kind of <primary antibody> used.

= Epitope
{parent=Antibody}
{wiki}

An <epitope> is the molecular feature recognized by an <antibody>. <Protein denaturation> can expose an <epitope> or destroy the spatial arrangement required for its recognition; an <antibody> that works on a folded <protein> need not work in <SDS-PAGE>-based <Western blot antibody detection>.

= Immunoglobulin G
{c}
{parent=Antibody}
{wiki}

= IgG
{c}
{synonym}

<Immunoglobulin G> is an <antibody> class whose conventional four-chain architecture supplies the example above.

= Immunoglobulin heavy chain
{parent=Antibody}
{wiki}

An <immunoglobulin heavy chain> extends through an arm and the stem of the illustrated <IgG>.

= Immunoglobulin light chain
{parent=Antibody}
{wiki}

An <immunoglobulin light chain> accompanies an arm of the illustrated <IgG>, contributing to its <paratope>.

= Antibody variable region
{parent=Antibody}
{title2=$V_H,V_L$}

The paired <antibody variable regions> of an <immunoglobulin heavy chain> and an <immunoglobulin light chain> contribute to an <antibody binding site>. Variation refers to differences between <antibody> sequences, not to a tip continually changing identity while binding.

= Antibody constant region
{parent=Antibody}
{title2=$C_H,C_L$}

An <antibody constant region> is a chain portion outside the <antibody variable region>. Constant does not mean identical across every <antibody> class.

= Fragment antigen-binding
{parent=Antibody}
{wiki}

= Fab fragment
{c}
{synonym}

A <Fab fragment> contains one <antibody binding site>, allowing comparison with the two-site intact <IgG> in the <antibody surface plasmon resonance experiment>.

= Paratope
{parent=Antibody}
{wiki}

= Antibody binding site
{synonym}

A <paratope> is the recognizing surface on an <antibody>; the <epitope> is the recognized feature on its <antigen>. Contacts depend on spatial arrangement and chemistry, including <hydrogen bonds> and <van der Waals forces>. Thus a <paratope> is not simply a slot for a <protein primary structure> string. A folded <protein> can bring distant <amino acid residues> into one <epitope>. <Immunobiology: The Immune System in Health and Disease, 5th edition by Charles A. Janeway, Jr. and others/The interaction of the antibody molecule with specific antigen>[Immunobiology, 5th edition, §§3-6, 3-8 and 3-9: paired variable regions, discontinuous epitopes and reversible noncovalent contacts]{ref}

= Antigen
{parent=Antibody}
{wiki}

In <antibody> binding, an <antigen> is material bearing a recognized <epitope>. An <antigen> need not be a <protein>; <polysaccharides> can also carry <epitopes>. One <antigen> may present several different <epitopes>, so recognizing one feature does not imply recognizing every part of the target. <Immunobiology: The Immune System in Health and Disease, 5th edition by Charles A. Janeway, Jr. and others/The interaction of the antibody molecule with specific antigen>[Immunobiology, 5th edition, §3-8: small recognized regions of protein and polysaccharide antigens]{ref}

= Antibody specificity
{parent=Antibody}

<Antibody specificity> describes preferential recognition of selected <epitopes>. It is established relative to tested alternatives and assay conditions, rather than inferred from a reagent's name. <Western blot antibody specificity controls> distinguish recognition of the proposed <protein> from retained label caused by other material.

= Cross-reactivity
{parent=Antibody specificity}
{wiki}

<Cross-reactivity> occurs when an <antibody> also recognizes another <antigen> beyond the one against which it was raised. This may involve related features, but obvious overall similarity is not required. <Immunobiology: The Immune System in Health and Disease, 5th edition by Charles A. Janeway, Jr. and others/Appendix I. Immunologists' Toolbox>[Immunobiology, 5th edition, Appendix I, antiserum generation: cross-reactive antigens and absorption]{ref}

For an imagined example, a <paratope> contacts a small surface patch shared by two otherwise different <proteins>. Both can bind even though their remaining surfaces differ. Increasing <antibody> <concentration> can reveal weak binding that was previously undetectable. Conversely, an extra <Western blot> band need not prove <cross-reactivity>: the intended <protein> may occur in multiple forms, or detection reagents may contribute background. The target-deleted and no-<primary antibody> comparisons under <Western blot antibody specificity controls> test different explanations.

= Monoclonal antibody
{parent=Antibody}
{wiki}

A <monoclonal antibody> preparation derives from one selected antibody-producing <biological cell> and its descendants, or reproduces their specified <antibody> sequences. Monoclonal describes the origin and composition of the preparation; it does not guarantee absence of <cross-reactivity> or establish <antibody affinity>. <Immunobiology: The Immune System in Health and Disease, 5th edition by Charles A. Janeway, Jr. and others/Glossary>[Immunobiology, 5th edition, Glossary: Monoclonal antibodies]{ref}

= Avidity
{parent=Antibody}
{wiki}

<Avidity> is the combined strength of multivalent attachment. An intact <IgG> can make two contacts when accessible <epitopes> have suitable spacing. <Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others>[Chapter 24, B Cells and Antibodies, A Typical Antibody Has Two Identical Antigen-Binding Sites; The Strength of an Antibody-Antigen Interaction Depends on Both the Number and the Affinity of the Antigen-Binding Sites: avidity]{ref} If one contact breaks while the other remains, the detached arm stays nearby and can bind again before the whole <antibody> escapes. That geometric advantage does not require changing either <paratope>'s intrinsic <antibody affinity>. Two arms binding two separate freely moving <antigens> do not automatically provide the same tethering advantage. <Epitope> spacing, accessibility and surface density matter; <avidity> is not obtained by adding two <dissociation constants>.

= Antibody affinity
{parent=Antibody}
{title2=$K_d$}

<Antibody affinity> concerns one <paratope> binding one <epitope>. A <Fab fragment> supplies a single-site comparison. <Immunobiology: The Immune System in Health and Disease, 5th edition by Charles A. Janeway, Jr. and others/Glossary>[Immunobiology, 5th edition, Glossary: affinity, single-site binding]{ref} In the dilute <chemical equilibrium> model below, smaller <dissociation constant> $K_d$ means larger <antibody equilibrium occupancy> at the same free <antigen> <concentration>. State the <antigen>, <temperature> and <buffer solution>: a <dissociation constant> is not a context-free score. It does not alone specify how fast attachment or detachment occurs.

= Antibody equilibrium occupancy
{parent=Antibody affinity}
{title2=$\theta$}

<Antibody equilibrium occupancy> counts occupied <antibody binding sites>, rather than retained assay label. For an original 1:1 model, let $A$ denote an unoccupied site, $E$ free <antigen> bearing one accessible <epitope>, and $AE$ their complex. At <chemical equilibrium>, write
$$
A+E\rightleftharpoons AE,\qquad c=[E],\qquad K_d=\frac{[A]c}{[AE]},\qquad A_T=[A]+[AE],\qquad \theta=\frac{[AE]}{A_T}=\frac{c}{K_d+c}.
$$
Square brackets mean <concentrations>. The <dissociation constant> $K_d$ here has <concentration> units. This is the same independent-site calculation as <receptor binding occupancy>, now applied to an <antibody binding site>. The last equality follows by substituting $[AE]=[A]c/K_d$ into the occupied fraction and cancelling $[A]$.

Assume identical independent sites, reversible 1:1 binding, dilute <chemical solution> behavior, fixed <temperature> and <buffer solution>, and enough time for <chemical equilibrium>. Exclude two-arm bridging, <cross-reactivity> and inaccessible sites from this model. Crucially, $c$ is free <antigen> <concentration>; substituting the amount supplied per volume requires negligible depletion by binding. Independent <IgG> arms could obey this model site by site, but it does not describe <avidity> from bridging a surface.

Stipulate $K_d=20\,\mathrm{nM}$, with $1\,\mathrm{nM}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$. Free <antigen> <concentrations> $2$, $20$ and $200\,\mathrm{nM}$ give $\theta=1/11$, $1/2$ and $10/11$. A second stipulated <dissociation constant> of $200\,\mathrm{nM}$ instead gives $\theta=1/11$ at $c=20\,\mathrm{nM}$. Both curves approach one at large $c$: a nearly saturated readout can conceal an <antibody affinity> difference. These are invented values, with no universal threshold for a useful <antibody>.

\Image[antibody-equilibrium-occupancy-and-assay-readout.png]
{title=<Antibody equilibrium occupancy> and the additional steps in an assay readout}
{description=Original analytic curves for stipulated <dissociation constants> of $20$ and $200\,\mathrm{nM}$; marked examples belong to the first curve. The adjacent schematic separates <epitope> accessibility, binding, washing and detection. It contains no measured sensorgrams, fitted assay parameters or experimental signals. A <logarithmic scale> changes the display, not the independent-site assumptions.}

A <Western blot> readout additionally depends on <Western blot transfer>, accessible <epitopes>, loss during washing and <Western blot antibody detection>. <Western blot response saturation> can flatten the final signal even when binding itself remains responsive. An <ELISA> also reports retained label through an <enzyme> readout, rather than automatically reporting $\theta$. Thus half of an assay's maximum signal need not occur at $c=K_d$. Varying free <antigen> through <concentrations> near the candidate <dissociation constant>, while checking depletion and the readout's response, is more informative than comparing a single saturated point.

= Antibody surface plasmon resonance experiment
{parent=Antibody affinity}

A concrete comparison used a Biacore T100 with a CM5 chip bearing the target <receptor>; unused reactive surface groups were blocked. At $25\,{}^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ in a <pH> 7.4 <buffer solution>, intact <IgG> and its <Fab fragment> were injected for $180\,\mathrm s$ at $5\,\mu\mathrm L/\mathrm{min}$; dissociation was monitored for $3600\,\mathrm s$. The optical response tracks changes in <refractive index> near the surface, rather than counting occupied <paratopes> directly. A reference surface and blank injections corrected background; association and dissociation responses were fitted with a 1:1 model. The reported intact-<IgG> detachment was slower than that of the <Fab fragment>. <Antibody-Unfolding and Metastable-State Binding in Force Spectroscopy and Recognition Imaging by Parminder Kaur and others>[Kaur and others, Materials and Methods, Surface plasmon resonance; Table 1, whole IgG and Fab rows]{ref}

This comparison supports format-dependent attachment, not an intrinsic single-site <antibody affinity> for intact <IgG>. The reference and blanks address background, not two-arm <avidity>, surface accessibility or delivery by <diffusion>. The paper's fitted 1:1 constants alone cannot distinguish those mechanisms. Its <Fab fragment> comparison therefore motivates specifying the binding format before interpreting a <dissociation constant>; a good fit does not establish a unique molecular model.

= Western blot
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

= Immunoblot
{synonym}

A <Western blot> detects selected <proteins> after their separation and transfer to a membrane, using <antibodies>. In the <yeast cross-species complementation experiment>, detection helps distinguish failed <protein> production from failure of an expressed <protein> to restore growth.

The useful idea is to preserve where <proteins> migrated in <gel electrophoresis>, then ask which positions retain an <antibody>. <Western blot separation>, <Western blot transfer> and <Western blot antibody detection> answer different questions. A band is evidence of retained label at a position; <Western blot band interpretation> requires <Western blot controls> before assigning identity or comparing abundance.

\Image[western-blot-transfer-antibody-workflow.png]
{title=<Western blot separation>, <Western blot transfer> and <Western blot antibody detection>}
{description=Original schematic of an <SDS-PAGE>-based workflow, showing transfer towards the <anode> and an indirect <antibody> readout. The drawn bands and binding shapes are explanatory symbols, not measured data or a reconstruction of the original apparatus. The acidic <urea>-gel experiment discussed under <Western blot transfer> used the opposite polarity.}

= Western blot separation
{parent=Western blot}

In the common <SDS-PAGE> version, <protein denaturation> and binding of an anionic <surfactant> reduce differences due to folded shape and intrinsic <electric charge>. An <electric field> drives the resulting <protein> complexes through the gel; smaller chains generally migrate farther. A lane of reference <proteins> supplies positions for estimating apparent <mass>. This estimate depends on the separation conditions: it is not a direct weighing of a <molecule>, and different <proteins> can migrate together.

= Western blot transfer
{parent=Western blot}

<Western blot transfer> moves separated <proteins> out of the gel onto a <Western blot membrane>, using an <electric field> across the gel thickness. Maintaining close contact preserves the lane pattern. Contact gaps, incomplete removal from the gel and passage through the receiving sheet can change how much <protein> is retained.

The original 1979 experiment pressed a wetted, $0.45\,\mu\mathrm m$-pore <nitrocellulose Western blot membrane> against the gel between scouring pads and plastic pipette-tray grids, held by elastic bands. The <urea>-gel assembly faced the <cathode> in acidic solution, with $6\,\mathrm{V/cm}$ applied for one hour; the <SDS-PAGE> variant reversed polarity and used a <glycine>/<methanol> <buffer solution>. Stained gel/blot pairs checked pattern preservation. Corresponding labelled <ribosome> <protein> spots from two gels were compared through <radioactive decay> readouts after combustion. The stained gel remaining after transfer supplied a removal check; a second receiving sheet revealed breakthrough on overloading. These tests supported recovery for the examined <urea>-gel <proteins>, within experimental variation, while the <SDS-PAGE> results showed unequal staining recovery. Preserved band positions therefore did not establish universal quantitative transfer. <Electrophoretic transfer of proteins from polyacrylamide gels to nitrocellulose sheets: procedure and some applications by Harry Towbin and others>[Towbin, Staehelin and Gordon, pp. 4350–4352: Electrophoretic Blotting Procedures, Figure 1, Figures 2–3 and Table 1]{ref}

= Western blot membrane
{parent=Western blot transfer}

A <Western blot membrane> is a thin <protein>-binding support that receives the separated pattern. It is a laboratory support, distinct from a <cell membrane>. Retaining <proteins> on its surface makes them accessible for <Western blot antibody detection>.

= Nitrocellulose Western blot membrane
{parent=Western blot membrane}

A <nitrocellulose Western blot membrane> is a <Western blot membrane> made from nitrated <cellulose>. Its retention capacity is finite; the original <Western blot transfer> experiment tested breakthrough with a second receiving sheet.

= Western blot antibody detection
{parent=Western blot}

Blocking occupies unused binding sites on the <Western blot membrane>. The <primary antibody> is applied, unbound material is washed away, and a labelled <secondary antibody> detects retained <primary antibody>. A <fluorescence> label emits detectable <light> after excitation; an <enzyme>-linked label can instead generate a colored product or <light>. Washing reduces background but does not prove that remaining binding is selective.

In the original experiment, blocking used excess <protein>. An anti-<ribosome>-<protein> serum followed by labelled <secondary antibody> produced colored or <fluorescence> bands; preimmune serum supplied comparison strips. The color readout was an <enzyme> reaction product, rather than a measurement of target <enzyme> activity. <Electrophoretic transfer of proteins from polyacrylamide gels to nitrocellulose sheets: procedure and some applications by Harry Towbin and others>[Towbin, Staehelin and Gordon, pp. 4351–4353: Immunological Detection of Proteins on Nitrocellulose and Figure 4, including preimmune-serum controls]{ref}

= Western blot band interpretation
{parent=Western blot}

Band position gives an apparent <mass>, not unique <protein> identity. Intensity depends on retention, <epitope> recognition and detection response; it does not establish folded state, <enzyme> activity or successful <protein targeting>. Missing bands can reflect missing targets or failed <Western blot transfer> or <Western blot antibody detection>. <Considerations when quantitating protein abundance by immunoblot by Alicia A. McDonough and others>[McDonough and others, “Consider the Specificity of the Antibody for the Target” and “Consider the Linearity of the Detection System”]{ref}

The <yeast cross-species complementation experiment> consequently combines two logically different readouts: <Western blot> evidence about production of an <antibody>-recognized <protein>, and growth evidence about <functional complementation>. A detectable <protein> need not perform the biological task required for growth.

= Western blot controls
{parent=Western blot}

<Western blot loading controls> ask how much sample entered each lane; <Western blot transfer controls> ask what reached the receiving sheet; <Western blot antibody specificity controls> ask what the <antibodies> recognized. Each closes a different gap in <Western blot band interpretation>.

\Image[western-blot-lane-control-interpretation.png]
{title=Conceptual lanes for <Western blot controls>}
{description=Original schematic, with invented qualitative marks only. Candidate bands disappear in a target-deleted sample while a lower band persists; a separate panel shows how a loading or transfer difference can accompany a target-band difference. No lane, darkness, band position or relative amount represents a measurement, a specific real <protein> or confirmed identity. The no-primary comparison denotes a matched strip processed without <primary antibody>.}

= Western blot loading control
{parent=Western blot controls}

A <Western blot loading control> estimates lane-to-lane sample differences. A reference <protein>, such as <actin>, must have stable abundance and detection responsive to amount. Equal saturated bands fail to establish equal loading. <Considerations when quantitating protein abundance by immunoblot by Alicia A. McDonough and others>[McDonough and others, “Consider the Loading Controls”, Figure 4: sample doubling changes claudin detection but not the actin signal]{ref}

= Western blot transfer control
{parent=Western blot controls}

A <Western blot transfer control> tests retention after <Western blot transfer>. Total-<protein> staining of the <Western blot membrane> can reveal uneven lanes or blank patches; staining the gel after transfer tests what remained behind. Transferred reference bands check selected positions, not recovery of every target. A pretransfer loading image alone cannot establish the amount retained on the receiving sheet.

= Western blot antibody specificity control
{parent=Western blot controls}

A known target-containing sample is a positive comparison for <Western blot antibody detection>. A matched sample with <gene deletion> supplies a negative comparison: disappearance of the proposed band supports the assignment, while a persisting band indicates binding unrelated to the deleted target. Deletion must actually remove the recognized <protein>; changes in other <proteins> can complicate the comparison. Omitting the <primary antibody> checks binding or background from the <secondary antibody> and detection reagents, but cannot exclude binding of the <primary antibody> to an unintended <protein>. Independent <antibodies> recognizing different <epitopes> add evidence. <Considerations when quantitating protein abundance by immunoblot by Alicia A. McDonough and others>[McDonough and others, “Consider the Specificity of the Antibody for the Target”, Figure 2: positive tissue, knockout tissue and antibodies to different termini]{ref}

= Western blot normalization
{parent=Western blot}

Total-<protein> <Western blot normalization> compares a target-band signal with a signal representing many transferred <proteins> in that lane. It is distinct from measuring one reference <protein>, and from measuring the target itself. Its denominator is a loading proxy: normalization estimates target abundance relative to detected total <protein>, rather than target amount per <biological cell>. Different sample compositions, selective <Western blot transfer> losses or unequal stain responses can still matter.

For an original calculation, suppose background-corrected target signals $I_A=12$, $I_B=24$ and total-<protein> signals $P_A=30$, $P_B=60$ are in their respective proportional response ranges. Then
$$
\frac{I_B/P_B}{I_A/P_A}=\frac{24/60}{12/30}=1.
$$
The invented signals double together, so this calculation supplies no evidence for a higher target fraction. With $I_B=24$ but $P_B=30$, the same calculation gives $2$. These examples assume a common target response and a common total-<protein> response between lanes, negligible remaining background, and comparable target recovery; they do not equate total-<protein> staining with target abundance. Multiplying both signals in a lane by the same loading factor cancels in their <ratio>; a selective target loss does not cancel.

= Western blot response saturation
{parent=Western blot}

<Western blot response saturation> means that increasing target amount no longer increases the recorded signal proportionally. It can arise before imaging, through limited binding or reagent availability, as well as through detector limits. Shortening exposure can address a detector limit without repairing an already unresponsive assay. Test the target and normalization readouts with a dilution series under the actual assay conditions; merely seeing a gray band does not establish a proportional response.

In a 2013 methods experiment, a <biological cell> extract containing added purified <yeast> <protein> was separated on gels, transferred with a Trans-Blot Turbo to <polymer> membranes, and read with an <enzyme>-linked <secondary antibody>. A ChemiDoc MP imager and film recorded the same dilution-series blots. The target and an abundant reference <protein> had different usable response ranges; transferred total-<protein> imaging and the reference bands did not track loading equally well. This demonstrates the need for assay-specific response checks, rather than a universal conversion from darkness to amount. <A Defined Methodology for Reliable Quantification of Western Blot Data by Sean C. Taylor and others>[Taylor, Berkelman, Yadav and Hammond, Materials and Methods; Figures 2–4 and 5–7: transfer apparatus, film/imager comparison and loading normalization]{ref}

An illustrative response model separates background $b$, target amount $T$, retained fraction $q$, response scale $S$ and characteristic amount $K$:
$$
I(T)=b+S\frac{qT}{K+qT},\qquad S,K>0,\quad 0<q\leq1.
$$
This is an assumed model, not a fitted <Western blot> calibration. When $qT\ll K$, $I-b\approx(Sq/K)T$. When $qT\gg K$, $I-b$ approaches $S$. For two otherwise identical lanes, doubling $T$ gives the background-corrected <ratio>
$$
\frac{I(2T)-b}{I(T)-b}=\frac{2(K+qT)}{K+2qT}.
$$
At $qT=K$ the <ratio> is $4/3$, despite a true twofold amount change. Thus <Western blot normalization> cannot recover a lost amount difference merely by dividing two saturated readouts.

= Protein targeting
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

<Protein targeting> directs a <protein> to its cellular destination. Synthesis in the <cytosol> does not imply that the <protein> works there.

= Organelle protein import
{parent=Protein targeting}

<Organelle protein import> brings externally synthesized <proteins> into an <organelle>. Many <plastid> <proteins> made from nuclear <genes> carry targeting information recognized by machinery that transports them across the envelope membranes. This delivery enables nuclear <genes> to supply functions inside a <plastid>, but the presence of targeting information is not itself evidence that a <gene> was acquired through <endosymbiotic gene transfer>. <Origin and Evolution of Plastids and Photosynthesis in Eukaryotes by Geoffrey I. McFadden>[“How to tame your symbiont”: targeting, membrane passage and host-derived proteins]{ref}

= Polypeptide
{parent=Protein}

= Polypeptide chain
{synonym}

A <polypeptide> is a chain of <amino acid residues> linked by <peptide bonds>. The term describes chain chemistry; calling a chain a <polypeptide> alone does not establish that it is folded or functional. The <gene expression> example N–<methionine>–<alanine>–C contains two <amino acid residues> and one <peptide bond>; the twelve-residue example under <protein> contains eleven <peptide bonds>. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/AminoAcid/A1416.html[IUPAC-IUB: amino-acid symbols and representation of residues in chains].

= Amino acid residue
{parent=Polypeptide}

An <amino acid residue> is the portion of an <amino acid> incorporated into a <polypeptide chain>. For example, the A in the illustrative <protein primary structure> is an <alanine> residue, rather than a free <alanine> <molecule> floating beside the chain. Counting <amino acid residues> counts chain positions; repeated letters are separate positions. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/AminoAcid/A1416.html[IUPAC-IUB, section 3AA-16: symbols for amino-acid residues].

= Tyrosine residue
{parent=Amino acid residue}
{title2=$Y$}

A <tyrosine residue> is an <amino acid residue> whose <amino acid side chain> can accept a <phosphate> group during <receptor tyrosine kinase> signaling. The conventional one-letter sequence symbol is $Y$.

= Phosphotyrosine residue
{parent=Tyrosine residue}
{title2=$\mathrm{pY}$}

A <phosphotyrosine residue> is a <tyrosine residue> modified by <phosphorylation>. Its location and surrounding <protein primary structure> help determine which <SH2 domains> can recognize it.

= Amino acid side chain
{parent=Amino acid residue}
{title2=$R$}

An <amino acid side chain> is the variable group attached to an <amino acid alpha carbon>, conventionally denoted $R$. For <alanine> it is $\mathrm{CH_3}$: a <carbon> bonded to three <hydrogen> <chemical atoms>. In a <beta strand>, successive <amino acid side chains> alternate between the two faces in the idealized arrangement. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-4-proteins[Clark, Douglas and Choi: amino-acid R groups].

= Amino acid alpha carbon
{parent=Amino acid residue}
{title2=$\mathrm{C}_{\alpha}$}

The <amino acid alpha carbon> is the backbone <carbon> carrying the <amino acid side chain> in the usual <protein> <amino acid residue>. Marking successive <amino acid alpha carbons> is a useful way to trace a <polypeptide chain>, but omits intervening backbone <chemical atoms>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-4-proteins[Clark, Douglas and Choi: the central alpha carbon].

= Glutamic acid residue
{parent=Amino acid residue}
{title2=$\mathrm{Glu},\ \mathrm E$}

A <glutamic acid residue> is an <amino acid residue> represented by Glu or E in <polypeptide> sequence notation. GAA and GAG specify <glutamic acid residues> in the standard <genetic code>; the <ribosome> example uses GAA as its third <codon>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/Taxonomy/Utils/wprintgc.cgi#SG1[Elzanowski and Ostell, NCBI: standard genetic code, table 1]. The name denotes an incorporated chain position, as distinguished from a free <amino acid> <molecule>.

= Peptide bond
{parent=Polypeptide}
{wiki}
{title2=$-\mathrm{C}(=\mathrm{O})-\mathrm{NH}-$}

A <peptide bond> is the <covalent bond> between the carboxyl <carbon> of one <amino acid residue> and the amino <nitrogen> of the next. The displayed group shows the usual linkage, with the central carbon–nitrogen connection being the <peptide bond>. In an intact unbranched chain of $n$ <amino acid residues>, adjacent positions supply $n-1$ <peptide bonds>. Cutting one internal <peptide bond> produces two fragments; merely changing the chain's shape need not cut it. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-4-proteins[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: peptide bonds and protein structure].

= Protein backbone
{parent=Polypeptide}

The <protein backbone> is the repeating chain of <chemical atoms> that connects successive <amino acid residues>, including their <peptide bonds>. The attached groups that distinguish <amino acids> project from this shared framework. <Protein folding> changes the framework's spatial arrangement while ordinarily preserving its sequence connectivity. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9879/[Cooper: polypeptide backbone and attached groups].

= Protein structure
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

<Protein structure> describes both the chain's chemical organization and its spatial arrangement. Four levels answer different questions: <protein primary structure> gives the order; <protein secondary structure> gives recurring local patterns; <protein tertiary structure> gives one chain's overall arrangement; <protein quaternary structure> gives the assembly of multiple chains. These are levels of description, not four mandatory stages that every <protein> must pass through. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-4-proteins[Clark, Douglas and Choi: four levels of protein structure].

= Protein domain
{parent=Protein structure}
{wiki}

A <protein domain> is a distinguishable structural and functional region of a <protein>. A catalytic <protein domain> and a binding <protein domain> can carry out different tasks within one chain.

= SH2 domain
{c}
{parent=Protein domain}
{wiki}

An <SH2 domain> is a <protein domain> that recognizes a <phosphotyrosine residue> together with surrounding sequence features. This selective binding recruits a <protein> to a modified <receptor> or docking <protein>; it does not itself imply <catalysis>.<Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others/Signaling through Enzyme-Linked Cell-Surface Receptors>[“Phosphorylated Tyrosines Serve as Docking Sites For Proteins With SH2 Domains”, Figure 15-53]{ref}

= Protein primary structure
{parent=Protein structure}
{wiki}

The <protein primary structure> specifies the ordered <amino acid residues> of each <polypeptide chain>, joined through <peptide bonds>. A chain is conventionally written from its <N-terminus> to its <C-terminus>. In the broader chemical sense, a complete <protein primary structure> also specifies <covalent bond> crosslinks between chain positions, including <protein disulfide bonds>; a bare sequence string leaves that information unstated. This describes chemical connectivity rather than the spatial arrangement of <protein tertiary structure> or <protein quaternary structure>. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/P04843[IUPAC Gold Book: primary structure, sequence and chain crosslinking] and https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/AminoAcid/A1416.html[IUPAC-IUB, 3AA-16: writing residue sequences from the amino end]. Some conventions reserve <protein primary structure> for sequence alone and call the complete chemical connectivity the covalent structure; the distinction should be stated when comparing descriptions. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/misc/noGreek/ppep4.html[IUPAC-IUB polypeptide-conformation recommendations, section 6.1: the narrower sequence-only convention].

\Image[protein-primary-sequence-composition.png]
{title=Equal <amino acid composition> and equal termini do not determine <protein primary structure>}
{description=Original toy <polypeptide chains> MAGAMG and MMAAGG, read from the <N-terminus> to the <C-terminus>. Each contains two <methionine>, two <alanine> and two <glycine> <amino acid residues>, but their internal order differs. Letters denote chain positions rather than free <amino acids>; lines denote <peptide bonds>. Neither string is identified as a natural <protein> or a measured sequence.}

In this toy example M denotes <methionine>, A <alanine> and G <glycine>. Both <polypeptide chains> have six <amino acid residues>, five <peptide bonds>, an M at the <N-terminus> and a G at the <C-terminus>. Their <amino acid composition> is identical, yet position 2 is A in MAGAMG and M in MMAAGG. There are $6!/(2!2!2!)=90$ distinct orders with these counts, a <multinomial coefficient>. Even fixing those two end residues leaves $4!/(1!2!1!)=12$ orders. The counts constrain the answer without determining it.

For multiple <polypeptide chains>, record each sequence separately and specify any <protein disulfide bonds> between <cysteine> positions. Mature <insulin>, for example, has an A chain of 21 <amino acid residues> and a B chain of 30 <amino acid residues>. Their combined 51 residues give $(21-1)+(30-1)=49$ <peptide bonds>, rather than the 50 of one unbroken 51-residue chain. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9879/[Cooper, The Cell: insulin's two chains and cysteine crosslinks, figure 2.16]. A <protein disulfide bond> joins side groups; it does not concatenate the <C-terminus> of one chain with the <N-terminus> of another. The three <insulin> <protein disulfide bonds> connect A6 to A11 within the A chain, and A7 to B7 and A20 to B19 between chains. Positions are numbered from each chain's <N-terminus>. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15653430/[Replacement of the interchain disulfide bridge-forming amino acids A7 and B7 by glutamate impairs the structure and activity of insulin: native disulfide positions].

Why keep this information? <Protein primary structure> lets us distinguish changes of chemical identity from changes of shape, and compare corresponding positions between <proteins>. <Protein folding> can bring distant <amino acid residues> together at a <protein binding site> while preserving their sequence. The attractive idea is that a readable linear record can constrain a complicated three-dimensional object; it is not, by itself, a guarantee of a unique fold in every environment. See <protein folding> and the <ribonuclease A refolding experiment>. Merely bending an intact chain preserves its <protein primary structure>; <hydrolysis> of a <peptide bond>, or changing which positions a <protein disulfide bond> connects, changes its chemical connectivity.

= Amino acid composition
{parent=Protein primary structure}

The <amino acid composition> lists amounts or proportions of the different <amino acid residues> in a <protein> or specified <polypeptide chain>, without giving their order. The toy MAGAMG and MMAAGG each have counts M:2, A:2, G:2, or a proportion $1/3$ of each. Knowing composition therefore does not supply <protein primary structure>. Sanger's account begins by distinguishing the already available composition measurements from the largely unknown residue order. See https://garfield.library.upenn.edu/classics1985/A1985ADE5900002.pdf[Sanger's authored 1985 account of the move from composition to terminal-residue analysis].

= Glycine
{parent=Amino acid composition}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Gly},\ \mathrm G$}

<Glycine> is an <amino acid>, represented by Gly or G for an incorporated <amino acid residue>. Its <amino acid side chain> is a <hydrogen> <chemical atom>. It was identified at one <insulin> <N-terminus> in <Sanger's insulin sequencing experiment>. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/BlueBook/P10.html[IUPAC Blue Book, P-103.1: amino-acid names, symbols and structures].

= Phenylalanine
{parent=Amino acid composition}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Phe},\ \mathrm F$}

<Phenylalanine> is an <amino acid>, represented by Phe or F for an incorporated <amino acid residue>. It was identified at the other <insulin> <N-terminus> in <Sanger's insulin sequencing experiment>. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/BlueBook/P10.html[IUPAC Blue Book, P-103.1: amino-acid names and symbols].

= Cysteine
{parent=Amino acid composition}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{Cys},\ \mathrm C$}

<Cysteine> is an <amino acid> whose <amino acid side chain> contains <sulfur>, represented by Cys or C for an incorporated <amino acid residue>. Two <cysteine> positions can be joined through a <protein disulfide bond>. See https://iupac.qmul.ac.uk/BlueBook/P10.html[IUPAC Blue Book, P-103.1: cysteine's structure and symbols] and https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9879/[Cooper: cysteine crosslinks in proteins]. The C in a sequence means <cysteine>, whereas the C-end label in the toy diagram means <C-terminus>.

= Sanger's insulin sequencing experiment
{c}
{parent=Protein primary structure}

= Insulin sequencing experiment
{synonym}

The motivating question was experimental: how could one recover the order in a <protein> when breaking all its <peptide bonds> destroys that order? Frederick Sanger and colleagues used complementary tests on portions of <insulin>. The following is an explanation of their evidence, not a laboratory recipe.

First, mark ends before destroying the chains. Sanger used the colored reagent fluorodinitrobenzene, abbreviated FDNB, to label free amino groups. After acid <hydrolysis>, the attached label remained on the end-residue products. Separating these derivatives by their different movement through solvents and comparing them with reference derivatives made the labels readable. The <N-terminus> products identified <glycine> and <phenylalanine>. Sanger emphasized that finding a stable label and a way to separate its products were both essential. See https://garfield.library.upenn.edu/classics1985/A1985ADE5900002.pdf[Sanger, Citation Classic (1985), discussing The free amino groups of insulin (1945)]. FDNB also labels suitable free amino groups on <amino acid side chains>, so a labeled product is not automatically an <N-terminus> product. See https://www.nobelprize.org/uploads/2018/06/sanger-lecture.pdf[Sanger, The Chemistry of Insulin, Nobel lecture (1958), pp. 546–547].

Next, separate chains and preserve local order. <Oxidation> with performic acid broke the <protein disulfide bonds>, allowing A and B chains to be isolated. Partial <hydrolysis> and <enzyme> treatments produced fragments; these were separated and their composition and local sequences characterized. Overlaps between different fragment sets linked smaller sequences into longer ones. Many more fragments than minimally necessary were checked for consistency. See https://www.nobelprize.org/uploads/2018/06/sanger-lecture.pdf[Sanger's authored Nobel lecture, pp. 547–552: chain separation, fragment analysis and sequence reconstruction].

\Image[insulin-sequencing-evidence-workflow.png]
{title=Complementary evidence in <Sanger's insulin sequencing experiment>, with a toy overlap puzzle}
{description=Original schematic of tests on separate portions of <insulin>, followed by invented fragments from MAGAMG. The lower rows are aligned after deduction; the isolated fragments do not arrive with position labels. The cut patterns are chosen for teaching and assert no real <enzyme> specificity. This is neither a copied historical illustration nor measured experimental data.}

The toy fragments make the logic visible. Suppose one cut pattern yields MAG and AMG, and another yields MA, GA and MG. The first set alone permits MAGAMG or AMGMAG. The fragment GA in the second set crosses the MAG–AMG boundary in MAGAMG; it does not occur in AMGMAG. Among concatenations of the first set, therefore, the second set selects MAGAMG. This example assumes intact, correctly identified fragments from one homogeneous chain and known fragment membership in each cut pattern. Repeated stretches, missing fragments or sample mixtures can leave ambiguities; the picture illustrates inference from overlaps, not a universal guarantee of reconstruction.

Finally, determine the <protein disulfide bonds> separately. <Oxidation> used to separate whole chains erases their original connections. Sanger and colleagues instead studied fragments still joined by <protein disulfide bonds>, then oxidized the isolated joined fragments to identify their component pieces and locate the connected chain regions. An important trap was rearrangement of <disulfide bonds> during treatment: products can acquire connections absent from the starting <protein>. Their work therefore had to establish conditions that prevented rearrangement before interpreting a linked fragment as evidence. See https://www.nature.com/articles/1711025a0[Ryle and Sanger, A Disulphide Interchange Reaction, Nature 171, 1025–1026 (1953)] and https://www.nobelprize.org/uploads/2018/06/sanger-lecture.pdf[Sanger's Nobel lecture, pp. 553–554: preserving and identifying disulfide connections]. The result was an experimentally constrained chemical structure rather than a composition list or a photograph of a fold.

= Insulin
{parent=Protein primary structure}
{wiki}

<Insulin> is a <protein> <hormone>. Its mature form has two <polypeptide chains>; identifying their sequences and <protein disulfide bonds> provided the motivating example in <Sanger's insulin sequencing experiment>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9879/[Cooper: insulin as a two-chain hormone, figure 2.16].

A meal illustrates the <insulin> side of <blood glucose> <homeostasis>: rising <glucose> stimulates <beta cells> in the <pancreatic islets>, and released <insulin> favors uptake and storage of fuel. <Alpha cells> produce <glucagon>, while <delta cells> produce <somatostatin>, which inhibits release of both <insulin> and <glucagon>. The <pancreas> therefore contains interacting sensor and secretory <biological cells>, rather than one undifferentiated source of <hormones>. <Anatomy and Physiology 2e by J. Gordon Betts and others/17.9 The Endocrine Pancreas>{ref}

<Insulin> and <glucagon> are <peptide hormones>, built from <amino acid residues> joined by <peptide bonds>; <glucose> is a small <carbohydrate> <molecule>. A <hormone> changes <signal transduction>, not the identity of the fuel it regulates. Human mature <insulin> has 51 <amino acid residues> in two <polypeptide chains>. <Beta cells> process <proinsulin> to <insulin> and <C-peptide>, storing the products in <secretory granules> for <exocytosis>. Increased <glucose> <metabolism> raises the <ATP>-to-<ADP> <ratio>, tending to close <ATP-sensitive potassium channels>; <depolarization> and entry of <calcium> <ions> then trigger <exocytosis>. This is an important triggering pathway, with additional amplification and modulatory inputs.<Endotext/Insulin Biosynthesis, Secretion, Structure, and Structure-Activity Relationships>[pp. 1, 4–8, 10–11]{ref}

\Image[insulin-glucagon-tissue-control-circuit.png]
{title=<Insulin> and <glucagon> couple <pancreatic islets> to different tissue responses}
{description=Original qualitative <physiology> circuit. Each outer return closes a <negative feedback> loop for <blood glucose>; the arrows summarize direction, not measured gain or timing. <Insulin receptor> signaling changes <GLUT4> distribution in <skeletal muscle> and <adipose tissue>, while the <glucagon receptor> pathway principally supports <liver> <glucose> output. The local interactions within <pancreatic islets> explain why the two <hormones> are not independent switches.}

Binding to the <insulin receptor>, a <receptor tyrosine kinase>, starts <phosphorylation> of the <receptor> and <insulin receptor substrates>. Recruitment of <phosphoinositide 3-kinase> and activation of <Akt> transmit a major metabolic branch. In <skeletal muscle> and <adipose tissue>, that branch helps move <GLUT4> to the <cell membrane>, increasing <glucose> uptake by <facilitated diffusion>. <GLUT4> is a <membrane transport protein>, not an <insulin receptor>. <Insulin> also favors <glycogenesis> in <skeletal muscle> and the <liver>, and inhibits <lipolysis> in <adipose tissue>. In the <liver>, suppression of <glucose> output involves <enzyme> regulation, <gene expression>, and indirect changes in <substrate> supply. Its relative direct and indirect contributions are more complex than a single <Akt> arrow. <Insulin signaling in health and disease by Alan R. Saltiel>{ref}

<Insulin> is not a universal permission slip for <glucose> entry: <red blood cells> and much of the nervous system use uptake mechanisms that are not acutely dependent on <insulin>. Nor does the <liver> use the same <GLUT4> switch as <skeletal muscle>. This is why reduced <insulin> action can change <blood glucose> dramatically without stopping all <glucose> uptake. <The Genetic Landscape of Diabetes/Introduction to Diabetes>{ref}

<Diabetes> can disrupt production, response, and counterregulation. <Type 1 diabetes> usually involves destruction of <beta cells> and severe loss of endogenous <insulin>; <type 2 diabetes> combines <insulin resistance> with insufficient <beta cell> compensation. High circulating <insulin> can therefore coexist with impaired <insulin> action. Inappropriate <glucagon> release can add to excessive <liver> <glucose> output, and the response to low <blood glucose> can also be impaired: "more <glucagon>" is not a complete description of <diabetes>. These distinctions explain mechanisms, rather than prescribe treatment. <The Genetic Landscape of Diabetes/Introduction to Diabetes>{ref} <Endotext/Glucagon Physiology>{ref}

= Proinsulin
{parent=Insulin}
{wiki}

A single <polypeptide> precursor whose processing yields mature <insulin> and <C-peptide>; it is not the two-chain mature <hormone>. The biosynthetic distinction is described under <insulin>.

= C-peptide
{c}
{parent=Proinsulin}
{wiki}

= Glucose clamp technique
{parent=Insulin}
{wiki}

<Glucose clamp technique: a method for quantifying insulin secretion and resistance. by R. A. DeFronzo and others> separates two questions. In a hyperglycemic clamp, variable <glucose> infusion holds <blood glucose> above baseline while sampled <insulin> reports the response. The 1979 article's abstract reports an early release burst followed by a slower rise. In a euglycemic <insulin> clamp, infused <insulin> is held elevated and variable <glucose> infusion holds <blood glucose> at baseline. Frequent blood sampling, a <glucose> analyzer, and controlled infusion apparatus make the imposed <concentration> a maintained condition rather than an uncontrolled consequence. <Glucose clamp technique: a method for quantifying insulin secretion and resistance. by R. A. DeFronzo and others>{ref}<Endotext/Assessing Insulin Sensitivity and Resistance in Humans>[pp. 4–6]{ref}

The maintained <glucose> level controls an important <confounder>: a change in <blood glucose> would itself change uptake and <hormone> release. At a <steady state>, <conservation of mass> gives uptake equal to infused supply plus endogenous production, after accounting for losses. Consequently, infusion alone estimates uptake only if endogenous production is negligible and the other balance assumptions hold. Labelled <glucose> measurements can estimate residual endogenous production; matching infused <insulin> across comparisons matters. Whole-body uptake cannot by itself identify the responding tissue, and plasma <insulin> reflects both release and removal. This interpretation uses the accessible 1979 abstract and the later authored methods chapter; the original article's full methods were not available for inspection.<Endotext/Assessing Insulin Sensitivity and Resistance in Humans>[pp. 4–6]{ref}

= Protein secondary structure
{parent=Protein structure}
{wiki}

<Protein secondary structure> consists of recurring local arrangements of the <protein backbone>, notably the <alpha helix> and <beta sheet>. <Backbone hydrogen bonds> help stabilize these patterns. A <beta sheet> can bring together stretches far apart in the <protein primary structure>; local refers to the pattern of backbone geometry, not necessarily neighboring sequence positions. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26830/[Alberts et al.: alpha helices, beta sheets and backbone interactions].

= Alpha helix
{parent=Protein secondary structure}
{wiki}
{title2=$\alpha$}

An <alpha helix> is a coiled <protein secondary structure> with a repeated pattern of <backbone hydrogen bonds>. In the standard pattern, the backbone group of <amino acid residue> $i$ bonds to that of residue $i+4$: for example, positions 2 and 6. This is an additional spatial interaction, not a replacement <peptide bond> between those positions. Pauling, Corey and Branson proposed the helical arrangement in their 1951 paper by reasoning from the geometry of small <molecules>. See https://williams.chemistry.gatech.edu/course_Information/6572/papers/pauling_1951_helix.pdf[Pauling, Corey and Branson, The Structure of Proteins: Two Hydrogen-Bonded Helical Configurations of the Polypeptide Chain, PNAS 37, 205–211 (1951)].

= Beta sheet
{parent=Protein secondary structure}
{wiki}
{title2=$\beta$}

A <beta sheet> consists of extended stretches of <protein backbone> lying alongside one another, joined by <backbone hydrogen bonds>. The stretches can belong to different portions of the same <polypeptide chain> or to different chains.

Each extended stretch is a <beta strand>. Neighboring <beta strands> pack side by side, with repeated <backbone hydrogen bonds> between their backbone $\mathrm{C=O}$ and $\mathrm{N-H}$ groups: the <oxygen> is a <hydrogen bond acceptor>, and the backbone <nitrogen> with its attached <hydrogen> provides a <hydrogen bond donor>. These are interactions of the shared <protein backbone>, not merely contacts between <amino acid side chains>. Unlike an <alpha helix>, a <beta sheet> joins adjacent extended stretches rather than coiling one stretch around an axis. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-4-proteins[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 3.4: backbone groups in secondary structure].

Read each <beta strand> in the sequence direction from the <N-terminus> toward the <C-terminus>, even when it is an internal part of a longer <polypeptide chain>. In a <parallel beta sheet>, neighboring <beta strands> run in the same N→C direction; in an <antiparallel beta sheet>, they run in opposite N→C directions. A three-strand example can therefore have arrows right–right–right or right–left–right. A <beta sheet> can also mix the two arrangements. Direction does not identify chain membership: a single <polypeptide chain> can fold back to supply several <beta strands>, and different <polypeptide chains> can contribute to one <beta sheet>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26830/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: beta-sheet strand arrangements].

\Image[beta-sheet-strand-directions.png]
{title=<Parallel beta sheet> and <antiparallel beta sheet>: sequence directions}
{description=Three <beta strands> in each panel, with arrows showing N→C sequence direction. N-side and C-side labels indicate direction toward the <N-terminus> and <C-terminus>, not necessarily free chain ends. Dashed links symbolize interstrand <backbone hydrogen bonds>; they locate no <chemical atoms> and specify neither actual bond angles nor an exact pairing of <amino acid residues>. Chain connections are omitted.}

The pleated appearance comes from the geometry of the <protein backbone>. Successive <amino acid residues> carry <amino acid side chains> on alternating faces of an ideal <beta strand>, conventionally drawn above and below the sheet. The attachment positions are the <amino acid alpha carbons>. Thus a six-position cartoon has groups 1, 3 and 5 on one face and 2, 4 and 6 on the other. This alternation is a spatial pattern, not a rule that alternating positions must contain identical <amino acids>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-4-proteins[Clark, Douglas and Choi: side chains above and below the pleats].

\Image[beta-strand-pleating-and-side-chains.png]
{title=Pleating and alternating <amino acid side chains> along a <beta strand>}
{description=An original two-dimensional side-view cartoon of six consecutive <amino acid alpha carbons>, numbered in N→C sequence order, with generic R groups alternating above and below. Segments between marked positions summarize the intervening <protein backbone>, not every individual <covalent bond> or literal atomic coordinates. No <backbone hydrogen bonds> are drawn: their <hydrogen bond donor> and <hydrogen bond acceptor> groups would have to be shown separately from the marked <carbon> positions.}

Real <beta sheets> commonly twist and curve; pleating does not make them perfectly flat plates. Which <beta strands> are neighbors, how connecting regions join them, and how their faces pack are features of the three-dimensional <protein structure>. An alternating pattern in the <protein primary structure> alone neither establishes a <beta sheet> nor specifies a unique fold independently of surroundings and interacting <molecules>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK6824/[Breda et al., Protein Structure, Modelling and Applications: extended strands, sheet twist and three-dimensional organization] and <protein folding>.

Why this arrangement is useful: repeated <backbone hydrogen bonds> organize extended chain regions into a framework that can contribute to the interior of a folded <protein>. <Amino acid side chains> and connecting loops give that framework chemical variety and can shape a <protein binding site> for a <ligand>. Its attractive feature is the combination of a reusable backbone pattern with variable exposed groups: similar <beta sheets> can support different recognition surfaces. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26830/[Alberts et al.: beta-sheet cores and loops that form binding sites].

Historically, Linus Pauling and Robert B. Corey published \i[The Pleated Sheet, a New Layer Configuration of Polypeptide Chains] in PNAS 37, 251–256 (1951). They reasoned from <peptide bond> geometry and possible <backbone hydrogen bonds>, proposing an idealized layer rather than measuring every modern <beta sheet>. Their initial model should not be identified with all present-day <beta sheet> geometries; they published further pleated-sheet configurations later in 1951. See https://authors.library.caltech.edu/records/esvfg-ahe09[Pauling and Corey: original 1951 paper and scan], https://williams.chemistry.gatech.edu/course_Information/6572/papers/Pauling_PNAS-1951_sheet.pdf[accessible scan of the original paper]{ref}, and https://authors.library.caltech.edu/records/hq2fj-hqw64[Pauling and Corey, Configurations of Polypeptide Chains With Favored Orientations Around Single Bonds: Two New Pleated Sheets, PNAS 37, 729–740 (1951)].

= Beta strand
{parent=Beta sheet}
{title2=$\beta$}

A <beta strand> is an extended segment of <protein backbone> in the geometry characteristic of a <beta sheet>. Several <beta strands> can associate through <backbone hydrogen bonds>. A <beta strand> need not be an entire <polypeptide chain>.

= Parallel beta sheet
{parent=Beta sheet}

A <parallel beta sheet> has neighboring <beta strands> running in the same direction along their <amino acid residue> sequences, from the <N-terminus> toward the <C-terminus>.

= Antiparallel beta sheet
{parent=Beta sheet}

An <antiparallel beta sheet> has neighboring <beta strands> running in opposite N→C sequence directions. This specifies strand orientation rather than whether the <beta strands> belong to one <polypeptide chain> or several.

= Backbone hydrogen bond
{parent=Protein secondary structure}

A <backbone hydrogen bond> is a <hydrogen bond> involving groups of the <protein backbone>, typically a backbone <oxygen> <chemical atom> and a <hydrogen> <chemical atom> already joined by a <covalent bond> to backbone <nitrogen>. It can connect positions that are not adjacent along the <polypeptide chain>. The <peptide bonds> still determine which <amino acid residues> are consecutive. See the original structural argument cited under <alpha helix>.

= Protein tertiary structure
{parent=Protein structure}
{wiki}

The <protein tertiary structure> is the overall spatial arrangement of one <polypeptide chain>. It includes how its <protein secondary structures> and intervening regions fit together. For example, the schematic under <protein> places positions 4 and 9 near each other despite their separation in <protein primary structure>. Such cooperation can help construct an <active site>; sequence distance and spatial separation answer different questions. <Hydrophobicity>, <backbone hydrogen bonds> and other interactions help stabilize folds, and some <proteins> additionally contain <protein disulfide bonds>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9879/[Cooper: tertiary structure and interactions between chain regions].

= Protein binding site
{parent=Protein tertiary structure}

A <protein binding site> is a region of a <protein structure> that interacts with a <ligand>. Its arrangement of <amino acid residues> helps determine recognition; connecting loops supported by a <beta sheet> can contribute to it. An <enzyme>'s <active site> binds a <substrate> and accelerates a <chemical reaction>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26911/[Alberts et al.: protein binding sites and active sites].

= Protein disulfide bond
{parent=Protein tertiary structure}
{title2=$\mathrm{S-S}$}

A <protein disulfide bond> is a <disulfide bond> linking two <sulfur> <chemical atoms> carried by suitable <amino acid residues>. It can connect distant portions of a <polypeptide chain> or different chains. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9879/[Cooper: sulfur-containing residues and disulfide crosslinks]. The four internal <protein disulfide bonds> of <ribonuclease A> are central to the <ribonuclease A refolding experiment>; breaking these crosslinks need not break the <peptide bonds> along its <protein backbone>.

= Protein quaternary structure
{parent=Protein structure}
{wiki}

The <protein quaternary structure> is the arrangement of multiple <polypeptide chains> in a functional assembly. A single-chain <protein> has <protein tertiary structure> without requiring <protein quaternary structure>. For an exact bookkeeping example, two intact model chains of twelve <amino acid residues> each contain $2\times12=24$ residues and $2\times11=22$ <peptide bonds>. Assembly alone adds no <peptide bond> joining their ends. Stable functional assembly should also be distinguished from <protein aggregation>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26830/[Alberts et al.: proteins as assemblies of polypeptide subunits].

= Protein folding
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

<Protein folding> is the process by which a <polypeptide chain> acquires an organized spatial arrangement. The interactions of its <amino acid residues> with one another and the surroundings favor some arrangements over others. <Protein folding> can bring chemically useful groups together at an <active site> without changing their order along the <protein backbone>. Its importance is practical: knowing a sequence alone does not tell us whether a sample has the structure needed for function. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9843/[Cooper: acquiring functional protein structure].

The <ribonuclease A refolding experiment> showed that an isolated <protein> could recover its native properties without a pre-existing folded template under suitable laboratory conditions. Anfinsen interpreted this through a thermodynamic hypothesis: the native arrangement minimizes the whole system's <Gibbs free energy> in the appropriate environment. His Nobel lecture was delivered on 11 December 1972 and published in Science in 1973. The specified environment is part of the claim, not a disposable qualification. See https://web.iitd.ac.in/~amittal/1973_Anfinsen_Science.pdf[Anfinsen, Principles that Govern the Folding of Protein Chains, Science 181, 223–230 (1973): Nobel lecture, especially p. 223].

This does not imply that every <protein> rapidly folds unaided. <Molecular chaperones> can assist <protein folding> and reduce <protein aggregation>; required partners, surroundings and time still matter. Goloubinoff and colleagues' 1989 reconstitution experiments demonstrated assistance from two <molecular chaperones> and <ATP> in recovering an active <enzyme> from unfolded chains. This is experimental evidence that assistance can matter even when the chains already have their specified sequences. See https://www.nature.com/articles/342884a0[Goloubinoff, Christeller, Gatenby and Lorimer, 1989: chaperone-assisted reconstitution of an active enzyme].

= Chaperone
{disambiguate=protein}
{parent=Protein folding}
{wiki}

= Molecular chaperone
{synonym}

A <molecular chaperone> is a <protein> that assists other <proteins> in reaching or maintaining useful arrangements, for example by limiting unwanted association during <protein folding>. Assistance does not rewrite the client's <protein primary structure>. In a crowded <cytoplasm>, reducing <protein aggregation> can make successful <protein folding> more reliable. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9843/[Cooper: molecular chaperones and folding assistance].

= Denaturation
{disambiguate=biochemistry}
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

= Protein denaturation
{synonym}

<Protein denaturation> is the loss of a <protein>'s native spatial organization, often with loss of biological function. Changes in <temperature>, <pH> or surroundings can disrupt <protein secondary structure>, <protein tertiary structure> or <protein quaternary structure>. <Protein denaturation> ordinarily preserves the <peptide bonds> and <protein primary structure>; it is different from cutting the chain. Recovery by <protein folding> is possible in some cases, but removing the denaturing conditions does not guarantee that every <protein> recovers. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-4-proteins[Clark, Douglas and Choi: denaturation, environmental conditions and loss of function].

\Image[protein-folding-and-denaturation.png]
{title=<Protein folding>, <protein denaturation>, chain cleavage and <protein aggregation> are distinct events}
{description=Original toy schematic. Folded and unfolded drawings both preserve all twelve <amino acid residues> in the same order and all eleven <peptide bonds>. Cleavage removes one internal <peptide bond>, leaving two fragments with ten remaining <peptide bonds> in total. The separate <protein aggregation> drawing contains two associated chains with eleven <peptide bonds> each. Refolding is a qualified possibility, not a universal outcome. The drawings are not actual <protein> coordinates.}

In the diagram, unfolding preserves all eleven <peptide bonds>. Cleaving the connection between positions 6 and 7 leaves two six-residue fragments: each contains five <peptide bonds>, so there are $5+5=10$ in total. Simply cooling or otherwise restoring suitable conditions cannot recover the original chain by <protein folding> alone; the missing <covalent bond> would have to be remade. Conversely, <protein aggregation> can impede recovery without breaking any <peptide bond>. These distinctions explain why loss of function does not by itself reveal whether a <protein> unfolded, associated with other chains, or suffered chemical damage.

= Protein aggregation
{parent=Denaturation (biochemistry)}
{wiki}

<Protein aggregation> is the association of <proteins> into clusters, often involving exposed regions of partly folded or unfolded <polypeptide chains>. It can prevent recovery of normal function while leaving the <protein primary structures> intact. It is distinct from both unfolding one chain and the functional assembly described by <protein quaternary structure>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9843/[Cooper: partly folded chains, aggregation and chaperone protection].

= Ribonuclease A
{parent=Protein}

<Ribonuclease A> is an <enzyme> that catalyzes cleavage of <RNA>. The bovine pancreatic <ribonuclease A> studied by Anfinsen and colleagues has 124 <amino acid residues> and four internal <protein disulfide bonds>. It supplied a particularly informative test of the relationship between <protein primary structure>, <protein folding> and biological function: recovery could be measured through restored <enzyme> activity. See the primary sources under <ribonuclease A refolding experiment>.

= Ribonuclease A refolding experiment
{parent=Ribonuclease A}

Anfinsen, Haber, Sela and White's 1961 experiments began by treating bovine pancreatic <ribonuclease A> with <mercaptoethanol> in $8\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$ <urea>. <Urea> promotes unfolding; <mercaptoethanol> breaks <protein disulfide bonds>. The <peptide bonds> along the chain remain intact. After separating the chain from those reagents, the authors allowed its reactive <sulfur> groups to form <protein disulfide bonds> in the presence of <molecular oxygen>, at <pH> $8.2$ and <temperature> $23$–$24\,{}^{\circ}\mathrm C$. They examined <protein> <concentrations> of $0.1$ and $2.0\,\mathrm{mg\,mL^{-1}}$. Measurements showed recovery of native properties under these conditions; the isolated major component had normal activity toward both <RNA> and a synthetic <substrate>. See https://williams.chemistry.gatech.edu/course_Information/6572_fall_2021/papers/ANFINSEN_1961.pdf[Anfinsen, Haber, Sela and White, The Kinetics of Formation of Native Ribonuclease during Oxidation of the Reduced Polypeptide Chain, PNAS 47, 1309–1314 (1961), pp. 1309–1311].

The observation supports the sufficiency of that intact sequence to recover native <protein structure> in the tested surroundings without a folded template. It does not establish that every <protein> refolds, that the original living <biological cell> needed no assistance, or that activity always returns immediately. <Protein folding>, forming <protein disulfide bonds> and recovering an <active site> are related processes rather than interchangeable measurements.

Anfinsen's lecture also discusses forming <protein disulfide bonds> while the chain remains in concentrated <urea>. This gave mostly inactive material; after removing <urea>, a small amount of <mercaptoethanol> permitted rearrangement toward the native form. As an exact combinatorial check, eight labeled reactive <sulfur> groups have $7\times5\times3\times1=105$ ways to be paired into four <protein disulfide bonds>: choose the partner of one group, then repeat with the unpaired groups. Only one pairing is the native one. Equal counting does not mean that all pairings are equally likely experimentally, nor that actual <protein folding> searches them uniformly. See https://web.iitd.ac.in/~amittal/1973_Anfinsen_Science.pdf[Anfinsen's Nobel lecture, 1973 publication: scrambled ribonuclease and the 105 pairings, p. 223].

= Lysozyme
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

<Lysozyme> is a <protein> <enzyme> that attacks the <peptidoglycan> of bacterial <cell walls>. Its <active site> is a cleft in the folded <protein structure>, where a suitable <substrate> can interact with positioned chemical groups. This illustrates why possessing the right <amino acid residues> is insufficient if their spatial arrangement is lost.
