See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26911/[Alberts et al.: lysozyme as a worked example of protein binding and enzyme function].

A concrete structural example is Cheetham, Artymiuk and Phillips's 1992 study of hen egg-white <lysozyme> bound to a short <carbohydrate> that inhibits its action. The bound <carbohydrate> occupies part of the <active site>; it is not a reacting <substrate> in this complex. The study describes specific interactions and changes of shape accompanying binding. Thus a real binding site has chemical interactions and flexibility as well as a geometric outline. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1569548/[Cheetham, Artymiuk and Phillips, 1992: structure of a lysozyme complex with bound inhibitor] and https://www.rcsb.org/structure/1HEW[RCSB PDB entry 1HEW: the authors' structure and bound three-unit carbohydrate]. The diagrams above illustrate the idea; their twelve-residue strings are not <lysozyme> sequences or coordinates.

= N-terminus
{c}
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

The N-terminus is the amino end of a <protein> or <amino acid> chain. Sequence notation lists residues from the <N-terminus> toward the <C-terminus>, the same direction in which <biological translation> builds a chain. The two-residue example under <gene expression> places <methionine> at the N-terminal end before any subsequent processing. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-4-proteins[OpenStax Biology 2e, 3.4: amino acids and polypeptide direction]{ref}

= C-terminus
{c}
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

The C-terminus is the carboxyl end of a <protein> or <amino acid> chain. During <biological translation>, each new residue is added at this end. The example under <gene expression> gives N–<methionine>–<alanine>–C, placing <alanine> at the <C-terminus>; its <stop codon> supplies no further residue. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-4-proteins[OpenStax Biology 2e, 3.4: polypeptide termini]{ref}

= Actin
{parent=Protein}

<Actin> is a <protein> whose monomers assemble into filaments. An idealized actin filament has a bending modulus and persistence length; inferring its effective elastic modulus from those measurements additionally requires a cross-sectional model.

= Epidermal growth factor
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

= EGF
{c}
{synonym}

<Epidermal growth factor> is a <protein> <ligand> for the <epidermal growth factor receptor>.

= Receptor
{disambiguate=biochemistry}
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

= Receptor
{synonym}

A biochemical <receptor> is a <protein> that recognizes a signal and couples its detection to a biological effect. Its binding partner is a <ligand>; recognition depends on compatible shape and chemical interactions at a binding site within the <protein structure>. Specificity is selective recognition, not a guarantee that only one <ligand> can bind. Binding also need not activate the <receptor>: a competing <ligand> may occupy it while preventing activation by another <ligand>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26911/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition: protein binding and specificity].

Compare a <cell-surface receptor>, accessible to an extracellular <ligand>, with an <intracellular receptor>, whose <ligand> must reach the interior. A <cell-surface receptor> can detect a <hydrophilic> <ligand> without transporting it through the <cell membrane>. In contrast, some membrane-permeable signals reach <intracellular receptors> that regulate <gene expression>. Location and downstream coupling are separate distinctions: an <ionotropic receptor> directly gates an <ion channel>, whereas a <metabotropic receptor> works through intracellular <signal transduction>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/9-1-signaling-molecules-and-cellular-receptors[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 9.1: receptor locations and mechanisms].

<Receptor binding occupancy> quantifies recognition separately from response. A <dissociation constant> measures binding in a specified equilibrium model; it does not by itself predict the cellular effect. This separation is useful when comparing signals that bind equally well but produce different responses, or when interpreting a binding experiment alongside a functional measurement.

= Ionotropic receptor
{parent=Receptor (biochemistry)}

An <ionotropic receptor> is a <receptor> in which binding of a <ligand> directly gates an <ion channel> within the same <protein> complex. The binding site detects the chemical signal; the pore supplies a path across the <cell membrane>. At a <chemical synapse>, a <neurotransmitter> therefore changes <synaptic conductance> without an obligatory intervening <G protein> or <second messenger>. <Neuroscience, 2nd edition edited by D. Purves and others/Two Families of Postsynaptic Receptors>{ref}

A familiar example is the nicotinic <acetylcholine> <receptor> of skeletal muscle: <acetylcholine> binding opens a pore passing <cations>, including <sodium> and <potassium>. The muscarinic <acetylcholine> <receptor> instead uses intracellular <signal transduction>; the same <neurotransmitter> can engage different mechanisms. Direct gating does not mean that an <ionotropic receptor> pumps <ions>: <electrochemical gradients> drive passage through the open <ion channel>. Nor does “ionotropic” specify excitation. The <reversal potential>, initial <membrane potential> and other conductances determine whether the effect favors an <action potential>, causes <hyperpolarization>, or supplies <shunting inhibition>. <Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others/Ion Channels and the Electrical Properties of Membranes>{ref}

A concrete electrical approximation separates opening from drive. Let $N$ identical <ion channels> each have open-channel <electrical conductance> $\gamma$ and opening <probability> $p(t)$. At fixed <membrane potential> $V$, with common <reversal potential> $E_{\mathrm{rev}}$, the <expected value> of inward-positive <electric current> is
$$
I_{\mathrm{in}}(t)=N\gamma p(t)(E_{\mathrm{rev}}-V).
$$
For stipulated values $N=100$, $\gamma=20\,\mathrm{pS}$, $p=0.2$, $V=-60\,\mathrm{mV}$ and $E_{\mathrm{rev}}=0$, the mean open count is $20$, the population <electrical conductance> is $0.4\,\mathrm{nS}$ and $I_{\mathrm{in}}=24\,\mathrm{pA}$. One open <ion channel> carries $1.2\,\mathrm{pA}$. These are illustrative values, not measurements. At $V=E_{\mathrm{rev}}$, the net <electric current> vanishes despite open <ion channels>; neither binding nor opening alone determines the readout. The approximation omits changes in <ion> <concentrations> and voltage-dependent open-channel <electrical conductance>.

\Image[ionotropic-metabotropic-receptors-mechanisms.png]
{title=Direct <ionotropic receptor> gating and two routes of <metabotropic receptor> action}
{description=Original mechanism cartoons, not molecular structures or measured pathways. Solid blue arrows show one possible net movement of positive <ions> when the <electrochemical gradient> favors entry; dashed orange arrows relay a signal. In the first panel binding and pore functions belong to one <protein> complex. In the other panels a separate <ion channel> is regulated through a <G protein>, either locally or through an <enzyme>, <inositol trisphosphate> and stored <calcium>. Positions, shapes and arrow lengths encode no measured distances or times.}

A motivating experiment made the elementary electrical events visible. As Bert Sakmann recounts, chronically denervated frog muscle fibers had <acetylcholine>-sensitive <receptors> spread beyond the original nerve contact. Mild <enzyme> treatment exposed the <cell membrane>; a polished glass pipette sealed over a small patch to reduce background noise. Two intracellular electrodes held the local <membrane potential> through <voltage clamp>, while the patch pipette recorded tiny <electric currents>. Applying suberyldicholine, a <ligand> that activates the <acetylcholine> <receptor> and prolongs openings, revealed abrupt, approximately rectangular current events. Their amplitudes agreed reasonably with estimates from whole-membrane current fluctuations. This established discrete opening and closing events; it did not make every <ligand> response instantaneous. Background noise and finite recording bandwidth concealed sufficiently small or brief events.<Elementary Steps in Synaptic Transmission Revealed by Currents through Single Ion Channels by Bert Sakmann>[pp. 33–35]{ref}

Recording an intact membrane patch alone does not exclude intracellular relays. Tank, Huganir, Greengard and Webb supplied a complementary isolation test in 1983: they purified nicotinic <acetylcholine> <receptors> from an electric ray's electric organ, incorporated them into artificial <lipid> membranes, and fused small vesicles into larger vesicles by freezing and thawing. Sealed glass-pipette recordings from isolated membrane patches resolved functional <ion channels> with properties resembling those in muscle. Removing the intact <cytoplasm> and its signaling machinery supports the sufficiency of the <receptor> complex for channel function; an artificial <lipid> environment still cannot reproduce every native regulatory interaction.<Patch-recorded single-channel currents of the purified and reconstituted Torpedo acetylcholine receptor by D. W. Tank and others>[abstract]{ref}

Direct gating supports millisecond communication at many <chemical synapses>. Response duration also depends on <neurotransmitter clearance>, binding and channel-state transitions. Continued <ligand> exposure can prolong activity. Classify an <ionotropic receptor> by its direct connection between recognition and pore gating, rather than a universal duration cutoff. <Neuroscience, 2nd edition edited by D. Purves and others/Two Families of Postsynaptic Receptors>{ref}

= Metabotropic receptor
{parent=Receptor (biochemistry)}
{wiki}

A <metabotropic receptor> couples <ligand> recognition to intracellular <signal transduction>, which can regulate a separate <ion channel>, alter <enzyme> activity or change <gene expression>. In the usual <neurotransmitter> context, <metabotropic receptors> are <G protein-coupled receptors>: receptor activation is relayed by a <G protein>. There need not be a change in <membrane potential> at all. <Neuroscience, 2nd edition edited by D. Purves and others/Two Families of Postsynaptic Receptors>{ref}

Two routes should be distinguished. An activated <G protein> can regulate an <ion channel> locally at the <cell membrane>, without an obligatory diffusible <second messenger>. Alternatively, it can regulate an <enzyme> that changes the availability of a <second messenger>, such as <cAMP> or <inositol trisphosphate>. Downstream <proteins> then change channel opening, <phosphorylation> or other cellular activities. The <receptor>, <G protein>, <second messenger> and final target are distinct components; an activated <G protein> is a relay <protein>, rather than itself a small-molecule <second messenger>.<Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others/General Principles of Cell Communication>[Figure 15-15]{ref}

Do not turn this useful <chemical synapse> classification into the claim that every <receptor> is a <GPCR>, or that every indirect response must pass through a <G protein>. For example, the <insulin> <receptor> is an <enzyme>-linked <cell-surface receptor> whose intracellular catalytic activity initiates <protein> <phosphorylation>. <Intracellular receptors> supply another category. “Metabotropic” commonly names the <GPCR> branch in <neuroscience>; the broader idea of biochemical coupling does not make all non-channel <receptors> structurally identical. <Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others/Signaling through Enzyme-Linked Cell-Surface Receptors>{ref}

Separating recognition from execution allows <signal amplification> and branching. A persistently activated <receptor> can activate several relay <proteins>; a regulated <enzyme> can produce many <second messenger> <molecules>. As an explicitly invented count example, suppose one activated <receptor> activates four relay <proteins> during one interval, and each relay activates a distinct <enzyme> that produces fifteen <second messenger> <molecules> during the next interval. With sufficient substrates and no loss, this gives $4\times15=60$ <molecules>. Those counts do not specify a physiological gain or response duration. A directly gated <ion channel> also passes many <ions> while open, so amplification is not an exclusive property of <metabotropic receptors>: always name the input and output being compared. <The Cell: A Molecular Approach, 2nd edition by Geoffrey M. Cooper/Pathways of Intracellular Signal Transduction>{ref}

A particularly instructive electrophysiology experiment separated a chemical trigger from the <ion channel> carrying its eventual <electric current>. In 1987, Hirono, Ito and Sugiyama injected <messenger RNA> from rat brain into frog egg cells, then tested acquired responses to neurotensin and muscarinic <acetylcholine> stimulation. Under <voltage clamp> at $-60\,\mathrm{mV}$, neurotensin elicited transient, oscillatory inward <electric currents> after a delay of several tens of seconds. This delay refers to that response and preparation. <Reversal potential> measurements implicated increased permeability to chloride <anions>, rather than a pore specific to the applied <ligand>. Both neurotensin and <acetylcholine> responses were inhibited by pertussis toxin, which interferes with susceptible <G proteins>, and by intracellular EGTA, which binds free <calcium>. Injecting <inositol trisphosphate> mimicked the responses; a large prior dose of <inositol trisphosphate> reduced subsequent responses to either extracellular signal. These interventions implicate a shared intracellular relay and <calcium>-dependent channel opening.<Neurotensin and acetylcholine evoke common responses in frog oocytes injected with rat brain messenger ribonucleic acid by C. Hirono and others>[abstract]{ref}

The informative comparison with the nicotinic <acetylcholine> <receptor> experiment is mechanistic: an extracellular <ligand> can produce an inward <electric current> either by directly gating the pore or by mobilizing an intracellular <second messenger> and stored <calcium> that activate another pore. Intracellular <inositol trisphosphate> bypasses reception, whereas EGTA tests the need for free <calcium>. However, neither inhibition nor mimicry alone identifies every molecular connection: a perturbation can affect several targets, and an injected mediator can activate a pathway without proving that it is the only natural route. The egg cell's endogenous machinery and the mixed <messenger RNA> preparation also limit extrapolation to an intact <neuron>. The experiment establishes functional coupling in its preparation, rather than the exact molecular composition or timing of every <metabotropic receptor>.

Even <G protein>-dependent channel regulation need not involve a lengthy <second messenger> cascade. Pfaffinger and colleagues reported in 1985 that <acetylcholine>-evoked <potassium> channel activation in embryonic chick atrial cells required intracellular GTP, a <guanine>-containing <nucleotide>, and was abolished by pertussis-toxin pretreatment. Changes in cyclic <nucleotides>, including <cAMP>, did not account for the channel response. The report discusses intrinsic activation delays of roughly $30$–$100\,\mathrm{ms}$, illustrating relatively rapid <metabotropic receptor> action. These observations support a <G protein> coupling step without cyclic-nucleotide <second messengers>; they do not by themselves exclude every possible intermediary or prove the specific subunit-channel interaction.<GTP-binding proteins couple cardiac muscarinic receptors to a K channel by P. J. Pfaffinger and others>[abstract]{ref}

A small timing model makes the extra steps visible without inventing an experimental trace. Let $u(t)$ jump from zero to one at $t=0$ and remain on. For a direct-gating model output $d$, one relay variable $x$, and its downstream output $y$, take zero <initial conditions> and
$$
\tau_d\frac{dd}{dt}=u-d,\qquad \tau_x\frac{dx}{dt}=u-x,\qquad \tau_y\frac{dy}{dt}=x-y.
$$
All outputs are normalized <dimensionless variables>; they are neither recorded <electric currents> nor fitted <concentrations>. With illustrative <time constants> $\tau_d=2\,\mathrm{ms}$, $\tau_x=20\,\mathrm{ms}$ and $\tau_y=100\,\mathrm{ms}$, their <exponential function> solutions for $t\geq0$ are
$$
d(t)=1-e^{-t/\tau_d},\qquad x(t)=1-e^{-t/\tau_x},\qquad y(t)=1-\frac{\tau_y e^{-t/\tau_y}-\tau_x e^{-t/\tau_x}}{\tau_y-\tau_x}.
$$
Initially $d'(0)=1/\tau_d$, while $y'(0)=0$ because its relay must first build up. Both outputs eventually approach one. Equal normalized final values do not imply equal biological amplitudes or absence of <signal amplification>; normalization deliberately removes that question.

\Image[ionotropic-metabotropic-receptors-timing.png]
{title=One-step and two-step responses to a sustained input: an illustrative timing model}
{description=Original analytic model curves with stipulated <time constants> of $2$, $20$ and $100\,\mathrm{ms}$ and zero <initial conditions>. Both panels show the same functions on different time windows; the dashed curve is the intermediate relay. Values are normalized and do not represent measured <electric currents>, physiological receptor-specific timings, or universal boundaries between <ionotropic receptors> and <metabotropic receptors>. The model omits <signaling feedback>, saturation and input removal.}

The model's useful feature is the delayed buildup created by sequential relaxation, not its arbitrary numbers. Actual kinetics also depend on <ligand> delivery, relay deactivation, <neurotransmitter clearance> and the final target. A local <G protein> route can act promptly, while an <ionotropic receptor> can remain active during continued exposure or feed a longer-lasting intracellular response. The beauty of <metabotropic receptor> coupling is its flexibility: the same recognition event can adjust electrical responsiveness and other cellular tasks through different targets. To classify an observed response, combine timing with molecular identity and controlled perturbations.

= G protein-coupled receptor
{c}
{parent=Metabotropic receptor}
{wiki}

= GPCR
{c}
{synonym}

A <G protein-coupled receptor> is a <cell-surface receptor> that relays activation through a <G protein> on the intracellular side of the <cell membrane>. The activated <G protein> can regulate an <enzyme> or <ion channel>, so a <GPCR> supplies a concrete <metabotropic receptor> example: <ligand> recognition and the eventual response are connected through distinct components. Different <G proteins> couple to different targets. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26912/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition: signaling through G-protein-linked cell-surface receptors].

= Glucagon receptor
{parent=G protein-coupled receptor}
{wiki}

= Glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor
{parent=G protein-coupled receptor}
{wiki}

= GLP-1 receptor
{c}
{synonym}

= Cell-surface receptor
{parent=Receptor (biochemistry)}
{wiki}

A <cell-surface receptor> detects an extracellular signal at the <cell membrane>. Its changed state affects the intracellular side, coupling reception to <signal transduction> or directly to an <ion channel>'s opening.

= Receptor tyrosine kinase
{parent=Cell-surface receptor}
{wiki}

= RTK
{c}
{synonym}

A <receptor tyrosine kinase> combines extracellular <ligand> recognition with an intracellular <protein kinase> <protein domain>. A membrane-spanning segment connects these parts across the <cell membrane>: the signal changes the intracellular machinery without requiring the <ligand> to enter the <cytosol>. The <protein kinase> transfers a <phosphate> group from <ATP> to selected <tyrosine residues> on <proteins>, including the <receptor> itself.<Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others/Signaling through Enzyme-Linked Cell-Surface Receptors>[“Activated Receptor Tyrosine Kinases Phosphorylate Themselves”: extracellular binding, intracellular catalytic domains and ATP-dependent phosphate transfer]{ref}

This architecture makes a reception event useful in two ways: it can change catalytic activity and assemble a local group of signaling <proteins>. <Trans-autophosphorylation> can create <phosphotyrosine residues> recognized by <SH2 domains>. Recruitment positions a downstream <enzyme> or relay <protein> near its partners. Neither every modified site nor every recruited <protein> has the same effect; association is a specific molecular connection, rather than a universal command to activate everything nearby.<Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others/Signaling through Enzyme-Linked Cell-Surface Receptors>[“Phosphorylated Tyrosines Serve as Docking Sites For Proteins With SH2 Domains”, Figure 15-53: signaling enzymes, adaptors and negative regulators]{ref}

Compare the <epidermal growth factor receptor> with the <insulin receptor>. Their common topology does not impose one activation geometry or make assembly equivalent to activity. The <receptor phosphorylation assay> consequently needs more than evidence that two <protein> chains associate.

\Image[receptor-tyrosine-kinase-activation-architectures.png]
{title=<Receptor tyrosine kinase> architecture and distinct activation mechanisms}
{description=Original topology cartoons contrasting <EGFR> with the preassembled <insulin receptor>. Orange dots mark <phosphotyrosine residues>; S–S marks <protein disulfide bonds>. The <EGFR> panel separates asymmetric catalytic contact from partner-tail <phosphorylation>. Shapes, distances and drawn <ligand> counts are explanatory, rather than atomic structures or binding stoichiometry.}

= Epidermal growth factor receptor
{parent=Receptor tyrosine kinase}
{wiki}

= EGFR
{c}
{synonym}

In an illustrative <EGFR> activation route, <EGF> stabilizes an associated pair of <receptor> chains. Asymmetric contact between their <protein kinase> <protein domains> activates one catalytic partner without requiring its activation-loop <phosphorylation>. Subsequent <phosphorylation> of cytoplasmic tails creates recruitment sites. Preassociated inactive <EGFR> populations can also occur: the cartoon's separated starting chains are not a universal initial condition.<Cell signaling by receptor-tyrosine kinases by Mark A Lemmon and Joseph Schlessinger>[Lemmon and Schlessinger, “Mechanisms of Receptor Activation” and “Allosteric activation of TKDs”, Figure 2F]{ref}

= Insulin receptor
{parent=Receptor tyrosine kinase}
{wiki}

The mature <insulin receptor> already contains two extracellular $\alpha$ chains and two membrane-spanning $\beta$ chains, joined through <protein disulfide bonds>, before <insulin> binds. <Insulin> induces rearrangement of this $(\alpha\beta)_2$ assembly. Partner-mediated <phosphorylation> of the inhibitory activation loop permits increased <protein kinase> activity. Ligand-induced creation of a previously absent dimer is therefore an unsuitable universal explanation.<Cell signaling by receptor-tyrosine kinases by Mark A Lemmon and Joseph Schlessinger>[“Mechanisms of Receptor Activation” and “TKD autoinhibition by the activation loop: insulin and FGF receptors”, Figure 2E]{ref}

= Insulin receptor substrate
{parent=Insulin receptor}
{wiki}

An <insulin receptor substrate> is a docking <protein> phosphorylated by the activated <insulin receptor>. Its <phosphotyrosine residues> recruit downstream signaling <proteins>, including <SH2 domain>-containing components of <phosphoinositide 3-kinase>. This connects reception to the pathway described under <insulin>, rather than making the <insulin receptor substrate> another extracellular <ligand>.<Cell signaling by receptor-tyrosine kinases by Mark A Lemmon and Joseph Schlessinger>[“Linking RTK activation to cell signaling”: IRS docking proteins and Figure 3A]{ref}

= Receptor phosphorylation assay
{parent=Receptor tyrosine kinase}

A <receptor phosphorylation assay> asks whether, and how much, a specified <receptor> is modified by <phosphorylation> under defined conditions. It is distinct from a binding assay for <receptor binding occupancy>, a test of <protein kinase> activity on a supplied <substrate>, and a test of downstream <signal transduction>. Those measurements answer related but different questions.

For a <Western blot> implementation, compare matched samples with <ligand> omitted or added, detect a specified phosphorylated <epitope>, and separately detect total <receptor> abundance. Preserve the sampled state by stopping further <enzyme> reactions during sample preparation; otherwise <protein kinase> or <protein phosphatase> activity after sampling can change the result. A general anti-<phosphotyrosine residue> <antibody> recognizes many modified <proteins>; a site-selective <antibody> gives a narrower readout, but still needs <Western blot antibody specificity controls>.

Reason through the controls before interpreting the difference. Loss of the proposed signal after receptor-specific <gene deletion> supports <protein> identity. Treatment with a <protein phosphatase>, alongside preservation of the total-<receptor> signal, tests phosphate-dependent recognition. <Western blot loading controls> and <Western blot transfer controls> address sample recovery; a usable detection range addresses <Western blot response saturation>. Lower signal after perturbing <protein kinase> activity supports dependence on that perturbation, but alternative targets and altered <receptor> abundance can complicate the inference. These are proposed assay comparisons, not outcomes attributed to the historical membrane experiment.

\Image[receptor-phosphorylation-assay-control-logic.png]
{title=Controls connecting a <receptor phosphorylation assay> to a defensible inference}
{description=Original reasoning diagram for a <Western blot> implementation. Separate comparisons address <protein> identity, phosphate-dependent <epitope> recognition, recovery and detection response. Boxes prescribe questions; they contain no measured bands, observed experimental outcomes or synthetic time course.}

An original idealized measurement model explains the normalization. Let $N$ be the retained number of <receptor> <molecules>, $f$ the fraction carrying the selected modified <epitope>, and $c_p,c_t>0$ fixed detection gains. After subtracting their respective backgrounds, suppose the phosphorylated and total-<receptor> signals lie in a linear response range:
$$
A=c_pNf,\qquad B=c_tN,\qquad \frac{A}{B}=\frac{c_p}{c_t}f.
$$
Dividing by total <receptor> cancels $N$ under these assumptions. It differs from total-<protein> <Western blot normalization>, whose denominator includes many other <proteins>. For invented values $c_p=c_t=1$, samples with $(N,f)=(100,0.2)$ and $(200,0.2)$ give $A=20$ and $40$, but both give $A/B=0.2$. Twice the phospho signal here means twice the retained <receptor> amount, with no change in fractional modification. Unequal recovery, changed <epitope> accessibility, nonzero uncorrected background or <Western blot response saturation> invalidates the cancellation. Unknown relative gains prevent reading an absolute modified fraction directly from the <ratio>.

= Ligand-dependent membrane phosphorylation experiment
{parent=Receptor phosphorylation assay}

A motivating experiment used isolated membranes from A-431 <biological cells>, which supplied abundant <EGFR>. Separate membrane aliquots were incubated at $0\,{}^{\circ}\mathrm C$ with <ATP> carrying radioactive <phosphorus> in its terminal <phosphate> group, plus <magnesium> or <manganese> <ions>. Radioactivity in material recovered by <chemical acid>-induced <precipitation (chemistry)> supplied the readout. Adding <EGF> beforehand enhanced incorporation relative to the unstimulated preparation; this placed a ligand-dependent biochemical response in a cell-free membrane system.<Epidermal Growth Factor by Stanley Cohen>[Cohen, Epidermal Growth Factor, printed p. 339: A-431 membrane aliquots, terminally labeled ATP, divalent ions and trichloroacetic acid-insoluble incorporation]{ref}

The follow-up comparisons separated increased addition from suppressed removal: <phosphate> removal was rapid but unaffected by <EGF>, and anti-<EGF> <antibody> reversed activation by removing the <ligand>. These observations support reversible regulation of membrane-associated <protein kinase> activity.<Epidermal Growth Factor by Stanley Cohen>[printed p. 341, numbered observations 1–4: rapid activation, dephosphorylation, lack of soluble kinase release and anti-EGF reversal]{ref} Total incorporation alone cannot identify which <protein> or <amino acid residue> received the label, prove intrinsic receptor <catalysis>, or predict an intact <biological cell>'s response.

The same account records an instructive identification error: receptor <phosphorylation> was initially assigned to the wrong <amino acid residue> because released modified <amino acids> migrated together in the electrophoretic <electric field>. Reanalysis identified <phosphotyrosine residues>. Retained radioactivity is evidence about the assay's separation and detection, not unique chemical identity.<Epidermal Growth Factor by Stanley Cohen>[printed p. 341: co-migration in the electrophoretic system and subsequent identification of tyrosine phosphorylation]{ref}

= Reversible receptor phosphorylation model
{parent=Receptor tyrosine kinase}

<Protein kinase> activity adds modification, while <protein tyrosine phosphatase> activity can remove it. An original one-site model shows why a <receptor phosphorylation assay> cannot determine either process from a single modified fraction. Let $f(t)\in[0,1]$ be the fraction of a fixed <receptor> population carrying one selected <phosphotyrosine residue>. Stipulate constant effective first-order <rate constants> $k_+,k_->0$, with <ATP> availability absorbed into $k_+$, and no <receptor> synthesis, degradation or trafficking:
$$
\frac{df}{dt}=k_+(1-f)-k_-f,\qquad
f_* = \frac{k_+}{k_++k_-},\qquad
\tau=\frac{1}{k_++k_-}.
$$
Here $f_*$ is the <steady state> fraction and $\tau$ the <time constant>. The <ordinary differential equation> has the <exponential function> solution
$$
f(t)=f_*+(f(0)-f_*)e^{-t/\tau}.
$$
The balance equation and a single relaxation step give this result; it is an illustrative model, rather than a fit or a claim about every <RTK>.

With stipulated <rate constants> $(k_+,k_-)=(2,1)\,\mathrm{min}^{-1}$, $f_*=2/3$ and $\tau=1/3\,\mathrm{min}$. Doubling both <rate constants> leaves $f_*=2/3$ but changes $\tau$ to $1/6\,\mathrm{min}$. The balanced <phosphate> turnover per site,
$$
J_*=k_+(1-f_*)=k_-f_*=\frac{k_+k_-}{k_++k_-},
$$
doubles from $2/3$ to $4/3\,\mathrm{min}^{-1}$. Identical <steady state> modification therefore permits different catalytic fluxes. Removing the addition term instead gives $f(t)=f(0)e^{-k_-t}$; this illustrates why a perturbation that stops <protein kinase> activity can expose removal dynamics, subject to the model's unchanged-removal assumption. A maintained <steady state> with ongoing <ATP> consumption is not <chemical equilibrium>.

= Intracellular receptor
{parent=Receptor (biochemistry)}
{wiki}

An <intracellular receptor> recognizes a <ligand> inside a <biological cell>, including in the <cytoplasm> or <cell nucleus>. Some <intracellular receptors> regulate <transcription> after binding a membrane-permeable signal; an <intracellular receptor> does not universally imply direct control of <DNA>. See the receptor-location reference under <receptor>.

= Receptor binding occupancy
{parent=Receptor (biochemistry)}
{title2=$\theta$}

<Receptor binding occupancy> is the fraction of binding sites occupied by a <ligand>. Consider reversible, noncooperative binding at <chemical equilibrium>, with homogeneous independent sites: every site has the same binding properties, and binding at one site does not change binding at another. Let $L$ denote free <ligand>, $R$ an unoccupied <receptor> site and $RL$ the bound complex. In this dilute <concentration> model,
$$
L+R\rightleftharpoons RL,\qquad K_d=\frac{[L][R]}{[RL]},\qquad R_T=[R]+[RL],\qquad \theta=\frac{[RL]}{R_T}=\frac{[L]}{K_d+[L]}.
$$
Square brackets denote <concentrations>; $R_T$ is the total site <concentration>. Here the <dissociation constant> $K_d$ has <concentration> units. Use free <ligand> in excess so binding negligibly depletes it; only under that approximation may the supplied total <ligand> <concentration> substitute for $[L]$. See https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5521016/[Analysis of Receptor–Ligand Interactions: equilibrium binding and saturation].

For an illustrative <dissociation constant> $K_d=10\,\mathrm{nM}$, where $1\,\mathrm{nM}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$, free <ligand> <concentrations> $1$, $10$ and $100\,\mathrm{nM}$ give $\theta=1/11\approx0.091$, $\theta=1/2=0.5$ and $\theta=10/11\approx0.909$, respectively. At $[L]=K_d$, exactly half the sites are occupied in this model. Raising $[L]$ tenfold from $10$ to $100\,\mathrm{nM}$ raises occupancy from $50\%$ to about $91\%$, rather than tenfold: the finite pool of sites causes saturation.

\Image[receptor-binding-occupancy.png]
{title=<Receptor binding occupancy> for a toy <dissociation constant> of $10\,\mathrm{nM}$}
{description=The same independent-site <chemical equilibrium> model on linear and logarithmic <ligand> <concentration> axes. Marked examples are $1/11$, $1/2$ and $10/11$. The logarithmic axis changes the apparent curve shape without introducing cooperative binding. The vertical axis is fraction occupied, not cellular response.}

A short derivation uses the <law of mass action>: at <chemical equilibrium>, association balances dissociation, giving $[RL]=[L][R]/K_d$. Substitute this into $\theta=[RL]/([R]+[RL])$ and cancel $[R]$ to obtain the displayed formula. The limiting values follow directly: zero free <ligand> gives zero occupancy, and increasing $[L]$ far above $K_d$ drives occupancy toward one.

<Receptor binding occupancy> is not automatically the fraction of maximal response. <Signal amplification>, differing effects of bound <ligands>, and downstream saturation can change the relationship. Consequently, $[L]=K_d$ specifies half occupancy here; it does not universally specify either half-maximal or maximal cellular response. These numbers describe a toy binding model, not measured responses of every <receptor>. See https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11267233/[Higham and Colquhoun, 2024: the affinity–efficacy problem].

= G protein
{c}
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

A <G protein> is a <protein> switch whose activity depends on the bound <guanine> <nucleotide>. The three-subunit <G proteins> coupled to <G protein-coupled receptors> relay activation to other <proteins>; they are distinct from the <receptors> themselves. See the Alberts reference under <G protein-coupled receptor>.

= Ligand
{disambiguate=biochemistry}
{parent=Protein}
{wiki}

= Ligand
{synonym}

A biochemical <ligand> is an <ion> or <molecule> that binds a <protein>. For a <receptor>, a <ligand> may activate signaling, prevent activation, or have another effect. This usage concerns <protein> binding; it is distinct from the coordination-chemistry use of the word. See the protein-binding reference under <receptor>.

= Morphogen
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

A <morphogen> is a signal whose spatial <concentration> helps specify different <biological cell> fates or behaviors during <biological development>. A <morphogen gradient> provides a positional cue: <biological cells> at different distances from a source experience different local <concentrations> and can follow different developmental pathways. A spatial <concentration> variation alone does not establish that a substance is a <morphogen>; its graded developmental effects matter. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26825/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition (2002): morphogens as graded developmental signals].

The beautiful idea is that one distributed signal can help organize several regions of a developing body. A continuous <morphogen gradient> can supply the input for distinct domains of <gene expression>, rather than requiring a separate signal for every position. A <morphogen response threshold> is a useful idealization of that interpretation: high, intermediate and low <concentrations> are assigned different outcomes. The <morphogen reaction-diffusion equation> describes one way to generate a <morphogen gradient>; the equation is a model of transport and reaction, while the <morphogen> is the signal being transported.

Detection and interpretation also depend on the receiving <biological cell>. For an extracellular <morphogen>, <receptors> and <signal transduction> connect the local <concentration> to a response; <receptor binding occupancy> is only one step in that connection. The same <morphogen gradient> can be interpreted differently in different tissues because their developmental context differs. Thus a <morphogen response threshold> summarizes a particular response in a particular context, rather than being a universal property of the signal. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9968/[Scott F. Gilbert, Developmental Biology, 6th edition (2000): positional information and context-dependent interpretation].

An important experimental example came in 1988. Wolfgang Driever and Christiane Nüsslein-Volhard used antibodies to examine the bicoid <protein> in early fruit-fly embryos. They reported a <concentration> profile approximately described by an <exponential function>, highest at the front, with the <protein> concentrated in <cell nuclei>. Their abstract proposed <diffusion> from localized <messenger RNA> and dispersed degradation as a likely origin of the <morphogen gradient>, rather than proving a unique transport mechanism. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3383244/[Driever and Nüsslein-Volhard, A gradient of bicoid protein in Drosophila embryos, Cell 54 (1988), 83–93: primary abstract]{ref}

In a companion study, Driever and Nüsslein-Volhard genetically changed the amount and distribution of bicoid <messenger RNA>, measured the resulting <protein> <morphogen gradient>, and compared developmental maps. Increased local bicoid <protein> levels shifted prospective anterior structures toward the rear; decreased levels shifted them toward the front. This linked a measured <concentration> profile to positional effects in <biological development>. The toy calculations below illustrate that logic with arbitrary numbers, not measured bicoid parameters. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3383245/[Driever and Nüsslein-Volhard, The bicoid protein determines position in the Drosophila embryo in a concentration-dependent manner, Cell 54 (1988), 95–104: primary abstract]{ref}

= Morphogen gradient
{parent=Morphogen}
{title2=$c(x)$}

A <morphogen gradient> is a spatially varying <concentration> profile of a <morphogen>. The profile $c(x)$ specifies the local amount; its mathematical <gradient>, $c'(x)$ in one dimension, specifies how rapidly that amount changes with position. In the simplest positional interpretation, a <biological cell> responds to its local <concentration>, without having to measure the <gradient> across itself. Production, transport and removal together determine a <morphogen gradient>.

For a worked example, take a stationary one-dimensional tissue occupying $x\geq0$, constant <diffusion coefficient> $D>0$, and uniform <first-order reaction> removal at rate $k>0$. At <steady state>, this specialization of the <morphogen reaction-diffusion equation> gives the <ordinary differential equation> and <boundary conditions>
$$
D\frac{d^2c}{dx^2}-kc=0,\qquad c(0)=c_0,\qquad \lim_{x\to\infty}c(x)=0.
$$
Here $D$ has units of length squared per time, $k$ has units of inverse time, and $c_0$ is a prescribed source <concentration>. The <Dirichlet boundary condition> fixes the <concentration> at $x=0$. It does not prescribe a source <flux>; any required <flux> follows from the solution. These assumptions define a toy <morphogen gradient>, not a universal account of transport during <biological development>.

Define the <diffusion length> $\ell=\sqrt{D/k}$. Substitution into the <ordinary differential equation> shows that its general solution is $A e^{x/\ell}+B e^{-x/\ell}$. The vanishing <boundary condition> at infinity forces $A=0$, and the source <Dirichlet boundary condition> forces $B=c_0$. Hence
$$
c(x)=c_0e^{-x/\ell},\qquad \ell=\sqrt{D/k}.
$$
Over every additional <diffusion length>, the <concentration> falls by the same factor $e^{-1}$. Faster <diffusion> increases $\ell$; faster removal decreases $\ell$. This makes the <diffusion length> a useful bridge between microscopic transport and the spatial reach of a <morphogen gradient>.

Choose $c_0=100$ arbitrary <concentration> units and $\ell=100\,\mu\mathrm m$, where $1\,\mu\mathrm m=10^{-6}\,\mathrm m$. Then $c(x)=100e^{-x/(100\,\mu\mathrm m)}$: at $x=0$, $100\,\mu\mathrm m$ and $200\,\mu\mathrm m$, the <concentrations> are $100$, approximately $36.79$ and approximately $13.53$. The following diagram combines this <morphogen gradient> with the idealized responses calculated under <morphogen response threshold>.

\Image[morphogen-gradient-and-thresholds.png]
{title=A toy <morphogen gradient> and three idealized developmental bands}
{description=The <steady state> profile uses $c_0=100$ arbitrary <concentration> units and <diffusion length> $100\,\mu\mathrm m$. <Morphogen response thresholds> at $50$ and $20$ place the boundaries at approximately $69.31$ and $160.94\,\mu\mathrm m$. The lower strip shows assigned fates A, B and C, not measured tissues. The plotted domain ends at $350\,\mu\mathrm m$; the mathematical domain continues to infinity.}

= Morphogen response threshold
{parent=Morphogen}
{title2=$\theta$}

A <morphogen response threshold> is a <concentration> level used to distinguish developmental responses to a <morphogen>. In an idealized threshold model, otherwise equivalent <biological cells> exposed to $c\geq\theta$ take one response and those exposed to $c<\theta$ take another. Two <morphogen response thresholds> can assign three bands: fate A for $c\geq50$, fate B for $20\leq c<50$, and fate C for $c<20$, all in the toy arbitrary <concentration> units. The A/B/C labels specify a response rule, not named cell types or a claim that every <morphogen> produces sharp boundaries. See the positional-information model in Gilbert's reference under <morphogen>.

For the decreasing <morphogen gradient> $c(x)=c_0e^{-x/\ell}$ and $0<\theta<c_0$, solve $c(x_\theta)=\theta$ using the <natural logarithm>:
$$
x_\theta=\ell\ln\!\left(\frac{c_0}{\theta}\right).
$$
With $c_0=100$ and <diffusion length> $\ell=100\,\mu\mathrm m$, the <morphogen response thresholds> $50$ and $20$ give
$$
x_{50}=100\ln2\,\mu\mathrm m\approx69.3147\,\mu\mathrm m,\qquad x_{20}=100\ln5\,\mu\mathrm m\approx160.9438\,\mu\mathrm m.
$$
Thus fate A occupies $0\leq x\leq x_{50}$, fate B occupies $x_{50}<x\leq x_{20}$, and fate C occupies $x>x_{20}$ in this idealization. The equality convention belongs to the response rule. If $\theta=c_0$, the crossing is at the source; if $\theta>c_0$, that <morphogen response threshold> is never reached anywhere in the tissue.

Now double the prescribed source <concentration> from $c_0$ to $2c_0$, keeping $D$, $k$, <diffusion length> $\ell$ and a fixed <morphogen response threshold> $0<\theta<c_0$ unchanged. The whole <morphogen gradient> doubles, but the boundary moves by an additive distance:
$$
\Delta x_\theta=\ell\ln\!\left(\frac{2c_0}{\theta}\right)-\ell\ln\!\left(\frac{c_0}{\theta}\right)=\ell\ln2.
$$
For $\theta=20$, the crossing moves from $160.9438\,\mu\mathrm m$ to $100\ln10\,\mu\mathrm m\approx230.2585\,\mu\mathrm m$, a shift of $69.3147\,\mu\mathrm m$. Every fixed <morphogen response threshold> below the original source <concentration> shifts by this same distance in the toy model. The example compares two <Dirichlet boundary conditions>, $c(0)=100$ and $c(0)=200$; doubling an experimentally supplied source <flux> is a different boundary specification.

\Image[morphogen-source-strength-boundary-shift.png]
{title=Doubling source <concentration> shifts a toy <morphogen response threshold> boundary}
{description=Profiles with source <concentrations> $100$ and $200$ share <diffusion length> $100\,\mu\mathrm m$. The same <morphogen response threshold> $20$ is crossed at approximately $160.94$ and $230.26\,\mu\mathrm m$. The arrow marks the predicted $69.31\,\mu\mathrm m$ shift. Source strength here means prescribed boundary <concentration>, with all numbers arbitrary and illustrative.}

These calculations are useful because they separate changes in source <concentration>, <diffusion length> and response rule. Their limits are equally concrete: growing tissues change the geometry, a <transient response> can precede <steady state>, molecular fluctuations can blur a boundary, and <signaling feedback> or altered transport can change the <morphogen gradient> or its interpretation. A local instantaneous <morphogen response threshold> need not capture exposure duration, <signaling adaptation> or the receiving <biological cell>'s state. Experiments on cultured human <biological cells> found different responses to the level and rate of change of developmental signals, illustrating why signal dynamics can matter. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6398983/[Heemskerk et al., Rapid changes in morphogen concentration control self-organized patterning in human embryonic stem cells, eLife 8 (2019), e40526: primary experiments]{ref} Quantitative measurements of bicoid <protein> and its downstream <gene expression> also make precision a question for experiment, rather than something guaranteed by drawing threshold lines. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2253670/[Gregor et al., Probing the limits to positional information, Cell 130 (2007), 153–164: primary measurements]{ref} <Morphogen gradients> provide one mechanism for spatial patterning in <biological development>, alongside other signals and interactions.

= Twin
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

Twins are two offspring from the same gestation. <Monozygotic twins> arise from one fertilized egg, while <dizygotic twins> arise from two. The distinction supplies the biological premise for <twin studies>.

= Biosignature
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

A <biosignature> is observable chemical, morphological or other evidence attributable to biological activity after assessing plausible abiotic explanations. It can concern present or past life on Earth or elsewhere. An <exoplanet biosignature> is the remote planetary specialization, requiring atmospheric and environmental context.

= Mortality
{parent=Biology}

Mortality describes deaths in a defined population during a specified observation window. A cumulative death <probability> is distinct from a death rate per person-time. Comparing treatments requires the same endpoint definition, time origin and ascertainment in both arms.

= Neuroscience
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

The study of nervous systems, from the properties of individual <neurons> to circuits, development, behavior and information processing.

= Voltage clamp
{parent=Neuroscience}
{wiki}

A <voltage clamp> uses electrical feedback to hold a preparation's <membrane potential> near a commanded value while measuring the <electric current> required to maintain it. Holding voltage separates changes in <electrical conductance> from changes in electrical driving force. Imperfect voltage control and finite recording bandwidth limit the inference. The muscle experiment under <ionotropic receptor> used separate intracellular electrodes for voltage control and a sealed patch pipette for recording local <electric current>.<Elementary Steps in Synaptic Transmission Revealed by Currents through Single Ion Channels by Bert Sakmann>[pp. 33–35; Figure 1]{ref}

= Extracellular single-unit recording
{parent=Neuroscience}

<Extracellular single-unit recording> uses an <electrode> outside a <neuron> to detect electrical events attributed to that unit's <action potentials>. It records a <spike train>, not the intracellular <membrane potential> or every input <synapse>. https://xbio-live.s3.amazonaws.com/narratives/57358/Hubel%20and%20Wiesel%201959_Guided%20paper.pdf[Hubel and Wiesel (1959), p. 575: extracellular recording methods]{ref}

= Primary visual cortex
{parent=Neuroscience}
{title2=V1}

The <primary visual cortex>, often abbreviated V1, is an early cortical stage of visual processing. Its <neurons> have <receptive fields>: changing a stimulus in a particular part of the visible scene can change their <spike trains>. <Orientation selectivity> adds a further distinction: at the same location, a properly aligned bar can evoke a strong response while a rotated bar evokes little. <Simple cells> make the dependence on local position and <visual contrast> especially clear. See the original experiments under <orientation selectivity> and <simple cell>.

In their 1962 study, David Hubel and Torsten Wiesel recorded from forty cats under thiopental anesthesia, maintained light anesthesia while monitoring cortical electrical activity, and used succinylcholine to stabilize the eyes. White-light patterns were projected onto a screen $1.5\,\mathrm m$ away. <Extracellular single-unit recording> with tungsten <electrodes> sampled individual units; <receptive fields> were mapped separately for each eye. This connected controlled stimulus geometry to individual <spike trains>. https://www.gatsby.ucl.ac.uk/teaching/courses/tn1-2020/additional/systems/JPhysiol-1962-Hubel-106-54.pdf[Hubel and Wiesel, Receptive fields, binocular interaction and functional architecture in the cat's visual cortex (1962), pp. 107–108: methods]{ref}

These acute, anesthetized-cat recordings do not directly measure human perception or responses during awake behavior. The useful idea is a local description of structure: a population can distinguish where a feature lies, which way it is aligned, and which side is brighter. The <signed spatial filter model of a simple cell> turns that idea into a computable example. One <neuron>'s preferred stimulus does not establish recognition of an entire object. Likewise, <orientation selectivity> in one <neuron> and an orderly map of preferences across <primary visual cortex> are different findings: rodents can have strong <orientation selectivity> without the orderly arrangement typical of some other <species>. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17720489/[Dario L. Ringach, Specificity and randomness in the visual cortex (2007): authored review of species differences]{ref} The existing <correlation-based development of visual cortical maps> and <orientation-map self-organization> sections describe hypotheses about how organization develops.

= Correlation-based development of visual cortical maps
{parent=Primary visual cortex}

Consider afferent activities $z_i$, cortical activity $y_a$ and a constrained <Hebbian learning> rule $\dot w_{ai}=\epsilon\langle y_az_i\rangle-\Lambda_a$. If $y_a=\sum_{b,j}K_{ab}w_{bj}z_j$, then $\langle y_az_i\rangle=\sum_{b,j}K_{ab}w_{bj}C_{ji}$, where $C$ is the input correlation matrix. Thus input correlations and lateral interactions select growing weight modes, while <synaptic normalization> and bounds stop unlimited growth. The model tests how organization can emerge without assigning every final cortical feature in advance.

= Complex cell
{parent=Primary visual cortex}
{wiki}

A <complex cell> typically responds selectively to an oriented edge or bar over a finite spatial region with less dependence on spatial phase and contrast polarity than a <simple cell>. It need not have the simple cell's separated linear ON and OFF subregions. Phase tolerance is local, not invariance to arbitrary displacement outside its <receptive field>.

= Quadrature energy model of a complex cell
{parent=Complex cell}
{title2=$E=(k_e*I)^2+(k_o*I)^2$}

Two oriented linear filters in quadrature have responses $A\cos\varphi$ and $A\sin\varphi$ to a suitably matched narrowband grating. Their squared sum is $A^2$, independent of phase $\varphi$, while orientation and spatial-frequency selectivity remain. A nonlinear output function or normalization can transform this energy into firing rate. Ideal quadrature gives an exact phase identity for the matched stimulus; finite approximate filter pairs need not have perfect invariance for arbitrary images.

= Pooling model of a complex cell
{parent=Complex cell}

Rectify outputs of <simple cells> with similar orientations and varying phases or positions, then sum or pool them. Opposite-polarity subunits allow response to both light and dark features. Pooling gives approximate phase and position tolerance over the subunit coverage; its extent and contrast dependence depend on the pooling rule.

= Ocular dominance column
{parent=Primary visual cortex}
{wiki}

A cortical region whose visual responses preferentially reflect input from one eye. Competitive activity-dependent models can explain segregation of correlated inputs, while anatomical constraints and species differences also matter.

= Activity-dependent ocular dominance segregation
{parent=Ocular dominance column}

Two eye-specific afferent populations compete under <Hebbian learning> and <synaptic normalization>. In a simplified symmetric two-eye model, the eye-difference mode $d_a=w_{aL}-w_{aR}$ evolves with a linear operator containing $K_{ab}(C_{\rm same}-C_{\rm cross})_{ab}$. Dominant spatial modes can produce alternating eye preference, while saturation sets finite contrast. The interaction width, arbor constraints and correlation structure influence the column scale. This is a mechanistic hypothesis whose predictions can be tested, not a unique inference from the existence of columns.

= Orientation selectivity
{parent=Primary visual cortex}
{wiki}

<Orientation selectivity> is a systematic dependence of a visual <neuron>'s response on the axis of a bar, edge or stripe pattern presented in its <receptive field>, under specified stimulus conditions. Here the <angle> $\theta$ measures the stripe axis from horizontal: $0^\circ$ is horizontal and $90^\circ$ vertical. An axis has no arrow, so $\theta$ and $\theta+180^\circ$ describe the same orientation. A preference for one direction of movement across that axis is an additional property; the 1962 recordings included both equal and unequal responses to opposite movements. https://www.gatsby.ucl.ac.uk/teaching/courses/tn1-2020/additional/systems/JPhysiol-1962-Hubel-106-54.pdf[Hubel and Wiesel (1962), p. 112: orientation versus opposite movements]{ref}

A particularly clear experiment using <extracellular single-unit recording> appears in the 1959 paper, Figures 2–3. A bright $1^\circ\times8^\circ$ slit was centered on the same <receptive field> and rotated through successive <angles>; each presentation lasted one second. The vertical slit strongly increased the extracellularly recorded <spike train>, whereas the horizontal slit was ineffective. Small-spot tests had identified <ON and OFF receptive-field subregions>, with a central ON region and opposing flanks: rotation illuminated less of the center and more of the flanks. Geometry and location therefore mattered together. These dimensions belong to that recorded unit, not to every <neuron>. https://xbio-live.s3.amazonaws.com/narratives/57358/Hubel%20and%20Wiesel%201959_Guided%20paper.pdf[Hubel and Wiesel, Receptive fields of single neurones in the cat's striate cortex (1959), pp. 578–579, Figures 2–3: primary recordings]{ref}

An <orientation tuning curve> summarizes a controlled <angle> sweep. Keep location, size and <visual contrast> fixed; for a stripe pattern also specify <spatial frequency> and <phase>. The same <signed spatial filter model of a simple cell> can produce strong <orientation selectivity> and strong dependence on <phase>, as the following original toy illustrates. Its smooth curves are calculated predictions, not digitized or invented biological measurements.

\Image[orientation-selectivity-angle-phase.png]
{title=<Orientation selectivity> and <phase> sensitivity in a toy <Gabor filter>}
{description=A chosen <Gaussian function> times a <cosine> supplies the signed filter. At fixed <phase> its normalized output peaks for vertical stripes; shifting the same stripes changes the signed drive and the rectified output. The <angle> labels refer to the stripe axis, perpendicular to the direction in which brightness varies. Spatial units and all parameters are arbitrary; curves use an infinite-domain <integral>, while the small image panels show only a crop. See <signed spatial filter model of a simple cell> for the calculation.}

= Orientation tuning curve
{parent=Orientation selectivity}
{title2=$r(\theta)$}

An <orientation tuning curve> plots a specified response against the stimulus-axis <angle> $\theta$, with the other stimulus variables controlled. A practical count summary for <extracellular single-unit recording> is
$$
\widehat r(\theta)=\frac{1}{mT}\sum_{k=1}^{m}N_k(\theta),
$$
where $N_k(\theta)$ counts <action potentials> in the same duration $T>0$ on trial $k$, and $m$ is the number of repeats at each <angle>. This estimates spikes per unit time in that window; the window must specify whether it covers onset, sustained stimulation, or both. Record a blank-background response separately, and report whether it has been subtracted. Randomizing <angle> order and repeating each condition helps separate <orientation selectivity> from changes over time, including <neural adaptation>. This is a proposed measurement procedure, not a claim that the original experiments used this estimator.

A <simple cell>'s <orientation tuning curve> can also change when the stimulus shifts. An <angle> sweep at fixed <phase>, a sweep averaging over <phase>, and a sweep of moving stripes ask different questions. Consequently, a single preferred <angle> does not specify the whole <receptive field>. The toy curve under <orientation selectivity> holds <phase> fixed and plots dimensionless output, rather than spikes per second.

= Orientation-map self-organization
{parent=Orientation selectivity}

Correlation-based learning among spatially arranged ON and OFF inputs, with <synaptic competition> and lateral interactions, can select elongated <simple cell> receptive fields. Neighbor interactions coordinate preferred orientations across cortex, while competition allows different orientations to persist. Selectivity within one cell and a spatial map across many cells are separate predictions, and neither follows from unbounded <Hebbian learning> alone.

= Simple cell
{parent=Primary visual cortex}
{wiki}

A <simple cell> is a visual cortical <neuron> whose <receptive field> has spatially separable <ON and OFF receptive-field subregions>. Elongated neighboring subregions give a preferred orientation and a sensitivity to the location and polarity of <visual contrast>. “Simple” describes the response organization; it does not describe a simpler <cell body> or a particular shape of <neuron>.

Hubel and Wiesel's 1962 classification emphasized four observations: distinguishable <ON and OFF receptive-field subregions>, summation within each region, antagonism between regions, and qualitative prediction of responses to differently shaped stimuli from those regions' arrangement. A slit fitting an elongated center was more effective than a smaller spot; a larger patch could invade opposing flanks and lose effectiveness. An edge separating bright and dark regions could be effective when its boundary matched the boundary between subregions. These were observations about <spike trains>, rather than an assertion of exact <linearity>. https://www.gatsby.ucl.ac.uk/teaching/courses/tn1-2020/additional/systems/JPhysiol-1962-Hubel-106-54.pdf[Hubel and Wiesel (1962), pp. 109–112: simple-field criteria and slit/edge experiments]{ref}

The following deliberately coarse <signed spatial filter model of a simple cell> makes the geometry easy to count. A centered bright bar contributes positively at five sites. Rotating it preserves its area but mixes positive and negative weights; shifting it preserves its orientation but changes which weights it samples. The model is useful because it predicts distinct effects of these three manipulations instead of treating any bright stimulus as equivalent.

\Image[orientation-selectivity-signed-bars.png]
{title=A signed toy <simple cell> filter separates orientation, location and <visual contrast> polarity}
{description=The $5\times5$ chosen weight map has a positive central column and negative flanks. Bright, unchanged and dark image values are $+1$, $0$ and $-1$. The <dot product> gives signed drive $D$; a <rectified linear unit> gives toy output $R$. Centered, rotated, uniform, shifted-bright and shifted-dark examples give $D=1,0,0,-1/4,1/4$. Colors and weights are illustrative, not a measured <receptive field> or a map of identified <synapses>.}

A <simple cell> is not an ideal linear sensor. Its <spike train> is nonnegative in event count, and <neural adaptation> can change responsiveness. The <signed spatial filter model of a simple cell> supplies a useful approximation to spatial drive and an explicit output rule. Comparing predictions with unseen stimuli is more informative than assuming the rule from the name “simple”. In contrast, the existing <complex cell> models explore tolerance to <phase> and local displacement.

= Signed spatial filter model of a simple cell
{parent=Simple cell}
{title2=$D=\sum_{i,j}K_{ij}C_{ij},\quad R=\max(0,D)$}

A <signed spatial filter model of a simple cell> assigns a weight $K_{ij}$ to each sampled location and takes its <dot product> with the <visual contrast> image $C_{ij}$. Positive weights favor bright features; negative weights favor dark features. The signed drive
$$
D=\sum_{i,j}K_{ij}C_{ij},\qquad R=\max(0,D)
$$
is a <linear function> of the image, while the <rectified linear unit> output $R$ is nonlinear. $D$ and $R$ below are arbitrary model units. To obtain a predicted firing rate one would need a calibrated gain, background response and possibly a different output function. The model does not generate a <spike train>; a <linear-nonlinear-Poisson cascade model> adds a stochastic event-generation assumption.

For the first diagram, every one of five rows has weights
$$
(K_{i1},K_{i2},K_{i3},K_{i4},K_{i5})=(-1/20,-1/20,1/5,-1/20,-1/20).
$$
A bright central column, with $C_{i3}=1$ and zero elsewhere, gives $D=5/5=1$. A bright middle row gives $D=-1/20-1/20+1/5-1/20-1/20=0$; a uniformly bright patch also gives $D=0$, since every row sums to zero. Move the bright column one site right: $D=5(-1/20)=-1/4$, so $R=0$. Make that shifted column dark instead: $D=1/4$ and $R=1/4$. Thus an OFF-like weight can increase output for darkness. A negative weight is a descriptive sensitivity, not evidence for a particular inhibitory <synapse>.

For a smooth example use the real, even <Gabor filter>
$$
K(x,y)=\exp\!\left(-\frac{x^2}{2\sigma_x^2}-\frac{y^2}{2\sigma_y^2}\right)\cos(qx),\qquad C_{\theta,\varphi}(x,y)=\cos\!\bigl(q(x\sin\theta-y\cos\theta)+\varphi\bigr).
$$
The <Gaussian function> localizes the weights; the <cosine> supplies alternating polarity. Choose $\sigma_x=1/2$, $\sigma_y=1$ and $q=\pi$ in arbitrary spatial units. The stripes' <spatial frequency> is $q/(2\pi)=1/2$ cycles per unit. Their axis makes <angle> $\theta$ with horizontal, while brightness changes across the perpendicular axis. $\varphi$ is their spatial <phase>. Normalize the full-plane <integral> of $KC_{\theta,\varphi}$ by its value at $\theta=\pi/2$, $\varphi=0$. The <Gaussian integral> and the product identity for <cosines> give
$$
D(\theta,\varphi)=A(\theta)\cos\varphi,\qquad R(\theta,\varphi)=\max(0,D(\theta,\varphi)),
$$
where
$$
A(\theta)=\frac{\exp\!\left[-\frac{q^2}{2}\left(\sigma_x^2(\sin\theta-1)^2+\sigma_y^2\cos^2\theta\right)\right]+\exp\!\left[-\frac{q^2}{2}\left(\sigma_x^2(\sin\theta+1)^2+\sigma_y^2\cos^2\theta\right)\right]}{1+\exp(-2\sigma_x^2q^2)}.
$$
At $\varphi=0$, horizontal stripes give $R\approx0.004159$, $60^\circ$ stripes give $R\approx0.286741$, and vertical stripes give $R=1$. At the same vertical orientation, $\varphi=\pi/2$ gives $D=R=0$, while reversing the polarity with $\varphi=\pi$ gives $D=-1$ and $R=0$. The preferred axis persists while the response changes dramatically with position. Shifting the vertical stripes by a quarter of their wavelength changes <phase> by $\pi/2$ up to sign, without rotating them.

These are two different toy filters, not discretizations of the same fitted <receptive field>. The discrete filter exactly rejects a uniform contrast patch; this <Gabor filter> need not reject uniform contrast because its weight <integral> need not vanish. Neither toy includes stimulus history, recurrent interactions, saturation or a measured response baseline. Their value is a transparent prediction: <orientation selectivity> can emerge from spatial summation, and it does not by itself imply tolerance to <phase>.

= Neuron
{parent=Neuroscience}
{wiki}

= Nerve cell
{synonym}

A <neuron> is a specialized <biological cell> that receives, integrates and transmits signals. Its shape separates places where inputs arrive from places where outputs reach other <biological cells>: this functional asymmetry is <neuronal polarity>. Understanding a <neuron> therefore means connecting its anatomy to changes in <membrane potential>, rather than treating it as a wire that merely forwards every input.

In a common <multipolar neuron>, branching <dendrites> receive inputs at <synapses>; the <soma> contains the <cell nucleus> and most major <organelles>; the <axon> carries signals to distant or nearby targets; and <axon terminals> form output contacts. All these regions belong to one <biological cell> enclosed by a continuous <cell membrane>. An <axon> can branch, allowing one <neuron> to influence several receiving <biological cells>.<Anatomy and Physiology 2e by J. Gordon Betts and others/12.2 Nervous Tissue>[§12.2, Parts of a Neuron: nucleus, organelles, processes, branching axon, initial segment and terminals]{ref}

At an input <chemical synapse>, a <neurotransmitter> acts on a <receptor>, changing an <electric current> directly or through <signal transduction>.<Neuronal Dynamics: From Single Neurons to Networks and Models of Cognition by Wulfram Gerstner and others/3.1 Synapses>[§3.1, opening paragraphs: ionotropic and metabotropic receptors and postsynaptic currents]{ref} A resulting <synaptic potential> contributes to <synaptic integration> in the <dendrites> and <soma>. In many spiking <neurons>, sufficiently strong <depolarization> recruits <voltage-gated ion channels> in the <axon initial segment>, initiating an <action potential> that propagates along the <axon>. At an output <chemical synapse>, <calcium> entry through <voltage-gated ion channels> promotes <synaptic vesicle> <exocytosis>, releasing <neurotransmitter>. An <action potential> is thus regenerated electrically along a <cell membrane>; it is not a packet of <neurotransmitter> travelling from the <dendrites> to the <axon terminals>.<Neuroscience, 2nd edition edited by D. Purves and others>[Chemical Synapses, Figure 5.3: voltage-gated calcium entry and synaptic vesicle exocytosis]{ref}

\Image[neuron-polarized-anatomy-and-diversity.png]
{title=Neuronal anatomy, usual signaling direction and alternative shapes}
{description=Original schematic, not microscopy and not to scale. The upper <multipolar neuron> identifies <dendrites>, the <soma>, the <cell nucleus>, the <axon initial segment>, the <axon> and <axon terminals>. Solid arrows show a common input-to-output route; a dashed arrow indicates possible propagation of an <action potential> back toward <dendrites>. The lower sketches compare a <bipolar neuron> and a <pseudounipolar neuron>, whose <soma> lies off the main transmission route. Shapes and relative dimensions are illustrative.}

The compartments are specializations, not exclusive jobs. Inputs can contact the <soma> or <axon> as well as <dendrites>; <dendrites> can support active <voltage-gated ion channels>. Paired recordings from a <pyramidal neuron>'s <soma> and <dendrite> or <axon> established that an <action potential> can begin in the <axon> and propagate back into the <dendrites>. The usual output direction therefore does not make every electrical signal strictly one-way.<Active propagation of somatic action potentials into neocortical pyramidal cell dendrites by Greg J. Stuart and others>{ref} An <electrical synapse>, which directly couples <cytoplasms> through <gap junctions>, also differs from the sending-and-receiving arrangement of a <chemical synapse>.<Neuronal Dynamics: From Single Neurons to Networks and Models of Cognition by Wulfram Gerstner and others/1.1 Elements of Neuronal Systems>[§1.1.3: electrical and chemical synapses]{ref}

Nor does every <neuron> have the upper diagram's shape. A <bipolar neuron> has two main processes, one <dendrite> and one <axon>; a <multipolar neuron> has several <dendrites>; and a <pseudounipolar neuron> has a single process from the <soma> that divides into peripheral and central branches. In the last arrangement, sensory signals can travel past the <soma> rather than obligatorily through it. These shape categories do not specify every <neuron>'s <receptor>, connectivity or firing behavior.<Anatomy and Physiology 2e by J. Gordon Betts and others/12.2 Nervous Tissue>[§12.2, Types of Neurons and Figure 12.9: multipolar, bipolar and human pseudo-unipolar cells; sensory projection past the cell body]{ref} Some <neurons> in the <retina> communicate through graded changes in <membrane potential> without generating <action potentials>.<Neuronal Dynamics: From Single Neurons to Networks and Models of Cognition by Wulfram Gerstner and others/1.1 Elements of Neuronal Systems>{ref}

\b[Why timing changes the computation.] <Synaptic integration> can combine inputs at different locations, called <spatial summation>, and inputs arriving at different times, called <temporal summation>. A minimal example uses the <leaky integrate-and-fire model> with the illustrative parameters already given there: resting and reset <membrane potential> $-65\,\mathrm{mV}$, firing threshold $-50\,\mathrm{mV}$, <electrical resistance> $R=100\,\mathrm{M\Omega}$, <capacitance> $C=200\,\mathrm{pF}$, <time constant> $\tau=RC=20\,\mathrm{ms}$ and <neuronal refractory period> $2\,\mathrm{ms}$. These are chosen model values, not measured properties of a particular <neuron>. Before firing, a constant inward <electric current> $I$ starting from rest gives
$$
V(t)=-65\,\mathrm{mV}+RI\left(1-e^{-t/\tau}\right).
$$
The <exponential function> expresses the competition between charging the <cell membrane>'s <capacitance> and leakage through its <electrical resistance>.<Neuronal Dynamics: From Single Neurons to Networks and Models of Cognition by Wulfram Gerstner and others/1.3 Integrate-And-Fire Models>[§1.3.1–§1.3.3, equations (1.5), (1.7), (1.12) and (1.13): passive integration, step response, threshold and reset]{ref}

Two $100\,\mathrm{pA}$ inputs, each lasting $40\,\mathrm{ms}$, produce different outputs depending on their timing. Either input alone reaches only $-56.3534\,\mathrm{mV}$. Applied together, they give $I=200\,\mathrm{pA}$ and reach threshold after $20\ln4\,\mathrm{ms}\approx27.7259\,\mathrm{ms}$, producing one <action potential> event during the pulse. If instead the second input begins $20\,\mathrm{ms}$ after the first ends, the earlier <depolarization> has partly decayed: the second pulse peaks at approximately $-55.9229\,\mathrm{mV}$ and neither pulse produces an <action potential>. The two-input protocols deliver the same total <electric charge>, $8\,\mathrm{pC}$, yet only the coincident protocol fires. Counting total input alone misses this timing dependence.

\Image[neuron-input-timing-and-spike-output.png]
{title=The same total input can yield different neuronal outputs}
{description=Synthetic exact <leaky integrate-and-fire model> responses, not empirical recordings. A single $100\,\mathrm{pA}$ pulse is below threshold; two coincident pulses produce one <action potential> event, shown as a tick rather than a biological waveform; two separated pulses deliver the same total <electric charge> as the coincident pair but produce no events. The lower traces show <membrane potential>, its reset and the brief <neuronal refractory period>. All inputs are prescribed <electric currents> in a single compartment, without a modeled <synaptic conductance> or spatial <dendrite>.}

This example makes a <neuron> a detector of sufficiently clustered input, not a perfect logical gate: different parameters change the result. Actual <dendrites> introduce location-dependent filtering and active responses, and an <inhibitory postsynaptic potential> or <shunting inhibition> can change whether another input succeeds. The single-compartment <leaky integrate-and-fire model> omits these mechanisms.<Neuronal Dynamics: From Single Neurons to Networks and Models of Cognition by Wulfram Gerstner and others/1.4 Limitations of the Leaky Integrate-and-Fire Model>[§1.4.2 and §1.4.4: shunting inhibition, spatial structure and active dendritic responses]{ref} Across connected <neurons>, branching <axons> distribute outputs while converging <synapses> combine inputs; <spike trains> can support <temporal coding> and <rate coding>, and <synaptic plasticity> changes the influence of an input. Connectivity and cellular dynamics together determine the computation.<Neuronal Dynamics: From Single Neurons to Networks and Models of Cognition by Wulfram Gerstner and others/1.1 Elements of Neuronal Systems>[§1.1.1–§1.1.2: axonal branching and spike trains]{ref}<Neuronal Dynamics: From Single Neurons to Networks and Models of Cognition by Wulfram Gerstner and others/3.1 Synapses>[§3.1.3: changes in synaptic efficacy with stimulation history]{ref}

\b[Testing the connection between structure and function.] <Whole-cell recording> connects anatomy to a measured <membrane potential>. In a published mouse protocol, a two-photon microscope guides solution-filled glass pipettes to <neurons>; an amplifier and bath reference electrode complete the electrical recording apparatus. A high-resistance seal forms against the <cell membrane>, then brief suction opens the membrane patch beneath the tip. In <current clamp>, the amplifier supplies a chosen <electric current> and records the resulting <membrane potential>, unlike <voltage clamp>, which controls voltage and measures <electric current>. The investigators used $500\,\mathrm{ms}$ current steps of several amplitudes to characterize excitability and firing, and negative current pulses to estimate input <electrical resistance>.<Multiple Two-Photon Targeted Whole-Cell Patch-Clamp Recordings From Monosynaptically Connected Neurons in vivo by Jean-Sébastien Jouhanneau and others>{ref}

The observable is a voltage trace, including graded responses and <action potentials>. Comparing injected <electric current> with that trace tests how input becomes output; it does not identify every underlying <ion channel>. The protocol checks access <electrical resistance>, which can distort voltage and <action potential> measurements, and includes intervals without stimulation to record spontaneous activity. In a teaching replication, repeated zero-input trials and monitoring baseline drift would provide further controls; these are recommended comparisons, not additional results attributed to the paper. A recording at the <soma> also cannot establish the local voltage everywhere in a branching <dendrite>, and this anesthetized preparation does not establish behavior in every awake <neuron>.<Multiple Two-Photon Targeted Whole-Cell Patch-Clamp Recordings From Monosynaptically Connected Neurons in vivo by Jean-Sébastien Jouhanneau and others>{ref}

A complementary experiment used simultaneous <whole-cell recordings> from the <soma> and an apical <dendrite> or <axon> of the same <pyramidal neuron> in a brain slice. The relative onset times of <action potentials>, together with <sodium> currents in excised <dendrite> membrane patches, supported axonal initiation followed by active propagation into <dendrites>. Pairing sites within one <neuron> is a useful comparison because it avoids confusing differences between cells with differences between compartments. Instrument timing and comparable recording quality would be essential controls when reproducing the inference. Only the published abstract was accessible here, so no unverified timing values, drug controls or channel distributions are supplied.<Active propagation of somatic action potentials into neocortical pyramidal cell dendrites by Greg J. Stuart and others>{ref}

= Neuronal polarity
{parent=Neuron}

= Synaptic integration
{parent=Neuron}

<Synaptic integration> is the conversion of inputs at a <neuron>'s <synapses> into a changing <membrane potential> and, in a spiking <neuron>, the timing or absence of <action potentials>. <Spatial summation> concerns inputs at different locations; <temporal summation> concerns inputs at different times. A useful question is whether individually ineffective inputs can act together, and whether an <inhibitory postsynaptic potential> changes that cooperation. The answer depends on location, timing and the receiving <neuron>'s state.

A <synaptic potential> is a voltage response, whereas <synaptic conductance> describes activated <ion channels>. With inward-positive <electric current>, the contribution of input $j$ is
$$
I_j(t)=g_j(t)(E_j-V).
$$
Here $g_j$ is its <synaptic conductance>, $E_j$ its <reversal potential> and $V$ the local <membrane potential>. The driving force $E_j-V$ is an <electric potential difference>. An <EPSP> generally brings the <membrane potential> toward a more positive <reversal potential>, but its driving force shrinks during <depolarization>. An <IPSP> need not produce <hyperpolarization>: a <reversal potential> above rest but below the <neuronal firing threshold> can produce <depolarization> while opposing a larger excursion. At $E_j=V$, the added <synaptic conductance> initially carries zero <electric current> yet can cause <shunting inhibition> of another input.<Neuronal Dynamics: From Single Neurons to Networks and Models of Cognition by Wulfram Gerstner and others/3.1 Synapses>[§3.1, equation (3.1), and §3.1.1: conductance, reversal and voltage-dependent sign; our current convention is inward-positive]{ref}

Two distinct nonlinearities matter. Even a passive <cell membrane> can show sublinear <synaptic integration> when added <synaptic conductances> reduce driving force and effective <electrical resistance>. Active <voltage-gated ion channels> and <NMDA receptors> can instead amplify sufficiently concentrated inputs and produce a <dendritic spike>. Neither summing prescribed <electric currents> nor adding separate measured <EPSPs> is an exact universal rule for a branching <neuron>.<Neuronal Dynamics: From Single Neurons to Networks and Models of Cognition by Wulfram Gerstner and others/1.4 Limitations of the Leaky Integrate-and-Fire Model>[§1.4.2 and §1.4.4: shunting, saturation and active dendritic interactions]{ref}

= Spatial summation
{parent=Synaptic integration}

<Spatial summation> combines inputs arriving at different <synapses>.
