The forward free-energy barriers are therefore $60$ and $40\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$; viewed from the <reaction products>, the reverse barriers are $70$ and $50\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. Both decrease by $20\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, while the endpoint difference stays $-10\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. These are constructed values, not measurements of a particular <enzyme>.

This separates <chemical kinetics> from <chemical equilibrium>. An <enzyme> changes how rapidly the system approaches <chemical equilibrium>; it does not change the <equilibrium constant> for the same overall <chemical reaction> under the same conditions. Faster catalysis therefore supplies no extra driving <energy>. In a <biological cell>, continuing supply of <reactants> and removal of <reaction products> can nevertheless sustain a net flow: the surroundings keep that system away from <chemical equilibrium>. See https://iubmb.qmul.ac.uk/kinetics/ek1t3.html[IUBMB, Symbolism and terminology in enzyme kinetics: basic definitions].

Some <enzymes> require a <cofactor (biochemistry)>, an additional nonprotein component such as a metal <ion> or a small organic molecule. Organic helpers are often called <coenzymes>; they can assist transfers that the <protein> alone cannot readily perform. The <cofactor (biochemistry)> may remain bound or exchange with the surroundings, depending on the system. A <coenzyme> can be chemically changed in one reaction and regenerated by another: regeneration of the <enzyme> does not mean every helper is unchanged in that individual reaction. Some <enzymes> need no additional <cofactor (biochemistry)>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26911/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: small molecules associated with proteins] and https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26838/[Alberts et al., activated carriers and their regeneration].

How much faster does adding <substrate> make a reaction? <Enzyme kinetics> gives a quantitative answer when a model's assumptions fit. For an illustrative reaction obeying <Michaelis-Menten kinetics>, use the <Michaelis-Menten equation> with <substrate> <concentration> $s$, limiting <reaction rate> $V_{\max}$ and <Michaelis constant> $K_M$. At $s/K_M=0.1$, substitution gives $v/V_{\max}=0.1/1.1=1/11\approx0.0909$. At $s/K_M=1$, it gives $1/2$; at $s/K_M=10$, it gives $10/11\approx0.9091$. Thus a hundredfold increase in <substrate> between the first and last examples yields only a tenfold increase in <reaction rate>.

\Image[enzyme-substrate-saturation.png]
{title=Illustrative <Michaelis-Menten kinetics>: $s/K_M=0.1,1,10$ gives $v/V_{\max}=1/11,1/2,10/11$}
{description=Original normalized curve and computed examples, with a logarithmic concentration axis to separate the three values. The dashed upper line is a limiting rate approached at high <substrate> <concentration>, not reached at a finite concentration in this model.}

For a chosen $V_{\max}=110\,\mathrm{\mu M\,min^{-1}}$, those fractions mean <reaction rates> of $10$, $55$ and $100\,\mathrm{\mu M\,min^{-1}}$. Doubling <substrate> from $10K_M$ to $20K_M$ raises the normalized rate only from $10/11$ to $20/21\approx0.9524$, a relative increase of about $4.76\%$. In this simplest model, the catalytic machinery is already nearly fully occupied. Doubling the amount of active <enzyme> instead doubles $V_{\max}$ and the predicted <reaction rates> at fixed free <substrate> <concentration>, provided the other conditions are unchanged. This distinction helps decide whether additional <substrate> or additional catalytic capacity could improve throughput.

The interpretation has limits. The simplest <Michaelis-Menten kinetics> assumes a single <substrate>, fixed active <enzyme> amount, a suitable <quasi-steady-state approximation>, and initial measurements with negligible reverse reaction from accumulated <reaction product>. Substantial depletion, interacting binding sites, or inhibition by excess <substrate> can invalidate this model. Within it, $K_M$ is the <substrate> <concentration> giving half the limiting <reaction rate>; it is not generally the binding <dissociation constant> $K_d$. See https://iubmb.qmul.ac.uk/kinetics/ek1t3.html[IUBMB, enzyme kinetics: steady-state approximation and substrate dissociation] and https://iubmb.qmul.ac.uk/kinetics/ek4t6.html[IUBMB, Michaelis-Menten and non-Michaelis-Menten kinetics]. Measuring activity also requires specified <temperature>, <pH> and necessary <cofactor (biochemistry)> availability: a weak signal can reflect unsuitable conditions rather than little <enzyme>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK92007/[Brooks et al., Assay Guidance Manual: basics of enzymatic assays for HTS].

= Protein kinase
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

A <protein kinase> is an <enzyme> that catalyzes <phosphorylation> of a <protein>. In <receptor tyrosine kinase> signaling, <ATP> supplies the transferred <phosphate> group.

= Autophosphorylation
{parent=Protein kinase}
{wiki}

<Autophosphorylation> is <phosphorylation> of a <protein kinase> by its own catalytic activity or that of another member of the same <protein kinase> assembly. Distinguish modification within one chain from <trans-autophosphorylation> between partners.

= Trans-autophosphorylation
{parent=Autophosphorylation}

In <trans-autophosphorylation>, one <protein kinase> chain phosphorylates another partner chain. “Auto” therefore does not require the modified site and catalytic <active site> to be on the same chain. The <insulin receptor> provides an activation-loop example; <EGFR> shows why <autophosphorylation> and catalytic activation need not be the same event.

= Protein phosphatase
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

A <protein phosphatase> is an <enzyme> that removes a <phosphate> group from a modified <protein> through <hydrolysis>. This restores a modifiable site rather than regenerating <ATP> by simply running <protein kinase> chemistry backward.

= Protein tyrosine phosphatase
{parent=Protein phosphatase}
{wiki}

A <protein tyrosine phosphatase> acts on <phosphotyrosine residues>. Removal can undo a particular receptor modification; its consequences for the whole <signal transduction> pathway depend on the target.

= Phosphoinositide 3-kinase
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

= Protein kinase B
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

= Akt
{c}
{synonym}

= Adenylyl cyclase
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

= Protein kinase A
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

= Phosphorylase kinase
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

= Glycogen phosphorylase
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

<Glycogen phosphorylase> is an <enzyme> involved in <glycogen> breakdown. Its activity is the readout in the extract-transfer experiment under <second messenger>; see that experiment's primary references. A change in <enzyme> activity need not mean production of more <enzyme> <protein>.

= Ribozyme
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

A <ribozyme> is an <RNA> <molecule> whose folded structure provides <catalysis> of a <chemical reaction>. A <protein> <enzyme> builds its <active site> from an <amino acid> chain; a <ribozyme> builds a working <active site> from <RNA>, sometimes with metal <ions> and associated <proteins>. <Base pairing> can help recognize a <substrate>, but recognition alone does not establish <catalysis>: the <RNA> must promote chemical conversion. A passive <messenger RNA> template being read by a <ribosome> is not thereby a <ribozyme>. See https://herschlaglab.stanford.edu/s/39.pdf[Cech and Herschlag, Group I Ribozymes: Substrate Recognition, Catalytic Strategies, and Comparative Mechanistic Analysis, sections 1–3].

The surprise is that an information-bearing <polymer> can also perform chemistry. Examples include the self-splicing <group I intron> of Tetrahymena thermophila and the catalytic <RNA> component of bacterial <RNase P>, discussed below. The <ribosome> supplies an everyday example: its <peptide bond>-forming <active site> is made of <ribosomal RNA>, although the complete machine also contains <proteins>. Nissen and colleagues examined structures of a large <ribosome> subunit with <substrate> analogues and found <RNA> contacting those analogues at the catalytic centre. This supports an <RNA> <active site>, not a claim that isolated <ribosomal RNA> can carry out all of <biological translation>. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10937990/[Nissen, Hansen, Ban, Moore and Steitz, The structural basis of ribosome activity in peptide bond synthesis, Science 289 (2000), 920–930].

For the Tetrahymena <group I intron>, <RNA splicing> removes the <intron> and joins its flanking <exons>. The first chemical step uses the free $3'$ <hydroxyl group> of an external <guanosine> to attack the <phosphodiester bond> at the $5'$ splice junction. This releases the upstream <exon> with a free $3'$ <hydroxyl group> and attaches the <guanosine> to the <intron>. That newly freed <hydroxyl group> then attacks the <phosphodiester bond> at the other splice junction, joining the <exons> and releasing the linear <intron>. Each step exchanges one backbone connection for another; it is not simply separation of <base pairs>. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6101203/[Cech, Zaug and Grabowski, 1981: the phosphoester-transfer mechanism].

\Image[ribozymes-splice-bond-map.png]
{title=<Group I intron> self-splicing: an external <guanosine> starts two exchanges of <phosphodiester bonds>}
{description=Original chemical-connectivity schematic, not an atomic structure or measured reaction trace. Orange junctions are the two bonds replaced; green junctions are the new bonds. The folded <RNA> <active site> and <magnesium> <ions> are omitted. The linear excised <intron> is shown before any subsequent circularization.}

The motivating experiment began with an unexpected control. As Cech recounts, Zaug purified a <ribosomal RNA> precursor from Tetrahymena using a detergent and phenol extraction, then compared test tubes with and without added <cell nucleus> extract. Incubation in salts and <nucleotides> released the same amount of small <intron> <RNA> in both cases, detected by <gel electrophoresis>. Repetition made a missing ingredient or accidental omission less plausible. Activity also survived treatments intended to destroy <proteins>. Nevertheless, absence of added extract did not by itself exclude a tightly attached, resistant <protein>, or prove that the small <RNA> had been newly excised rather than released from an already processed complex. See https://www.nobelprize.org/uploads/2018/06/cech-lecture.pdf[Thomas R. Cech, 1989 Nobel lecture, pp. 655–659: the unexpected control and alternative explanations].

An independent chemical readout addressed the latter problem. Cech mixed precursor <RNA> with <phosphorus>-32-labelled <guanosine> bearing three <phosphate> groups (GTP), and used <gel electrophoresis> followed by exposure to X-ray film to locate the label. Label appeared in the excised <intron> with <magnesium> present; it was absent when <magnesium> was omitted or labelled <ATP> replaced the labelled <guanosine> reagent. This experiment is described in https://www.nobelprize.org/uploads/2018/06/cech-lecture.pdf[Cech, 1989 Nobel lecture, pp. 657–658: labelled-guanosine controls]. The 1981 study established attachment through the <guanosine> $3'$ <hydroxyl group> and showed that <guanosine> without a triphosphate also supported excision: breaking down the added <nucleotide> to supply <energy> was unnecessary. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6101203/[Cech, Zaug and Grabowski, In vitro splicing of the ribosomal RNA precursor of Tetrahymena, Cell 27 (1981), 487–496: primary abstract]. Separately, sequencing and terminal analysis found an extra $5'$ <guanosine> absent from the corresponding <DNA> sequence. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7099968/[Zaug and Cech, The intervening sequence excised from the ribosomal RNA precursor of Tetrahymena contains a 5-terminal guanosine residue not encoded by the DNA, Nucleic Acids Research 10 (1982), 2823–2838]. Together, incorporation and sequence evidence support a new <covalent bond>, beyond a change in how <RNA> associates.

Kruger, Grabowski, Zaug, Sands, Gottschling and Cech's 1982 test removed dependence on a precursor purified from Tetrahymena. They cloned the <intron> and adjacent <exons> into a <DNA> template, made precursor <RNA> with purified bacterial <RNA polymerase> under conditions suppressing <RNA splicing>, and removed <proteins> by detergent–phenol extraction. Denaturing <gel electrophoresis> separated precursor species. Purified precursor was then incubated with the <guanosine> reagent, <magnesium> and salt; matched samples without this incubation supplied a precursor baseline. Their standard assay used $10\,\mathrm{mM}$ magnesium chloride, $0.1\,\mathrm{mM}$ of that <guanosine> reagent, $100\,\mathrm{mM}$ ammonium sulfate and a <buffer solution> at <pH> $7.5$, for $30$ minutes at <temperature> $37\,{}^{\circ}\mathrm C$, with specified variations in individual experiments. <Gel electrophoresis> and radioactive-film detection showed excised <intron> <RNA>; sequencing verified its $5'$ junction. See https://dosequis.colorado.edu/courses/methodslogic/papers/Cech1982.pdf[Kruger et al., Self-Splicing RNA, Cell 31 (1982), 147–157, figures 3–4 and Experimental Procedures].

Excision alone could leave disconnected <exons>. To test joining, the authors paired labelled <RNA> with <DNA> spanning the joined-<exon> sequence, removed exposed <RNA> with <enzymes>, and separated the protected pieces by denaturing <gel electrophoresis>. Spliced samples yielded protected <RNA> long enough to span the junction. See https://dosequis.colorado.edu/courses/methodslogic/papers/Cech1982.pdf[Kruger et al., 1982, figure 6: independent evidence for exon ligation]. The experimental logic matters: an <intron>-sized band identifies a candidate product, while sequence and junction assays test its chemical identity.

These controls support sufficiency of the prepared <RNA> under the assay conditions. They do not show that every <RNA> self-splices, or that <proteins> are dispensable inside a <biological cell>. Many <group I introns> use <proteins> to help attain an active fold. See https://herschlaglab.stanford.edu/s/39.pdf[Cech and Herschlag, Group I Ribozymes, section 6: protein facilitation]. Nor does a failed assay necessarily identify an inactive <nucleotide sequence>: unsuitable <ions>, <pH>, or <temperature> can prevent a working <active site> from forming.

The distinction between self-processing and repeated use became especially clear with bacterial <RNase P>. In 1983 Guerrier-Takada, Gardiner, Marsh, Pace and Altman separated the <RNA> and <protein> components of <RNase P> from two <bacteria>. Purified <RNA> cleaved precursor <transfer RNA> under conditions containing $60\,\mathrm{mM}$ <magnesium> <ions>, or $10\,\mathrm{mM}$ <magnesium> <ions> plus $1\,\mathrm{mM}$ spermidine. The <protein> components alone had no catalytic activity. At $5$–$10\,\mathrm{mM}$ <magnesium> without spermidine, both components were needed. Thus the comparison demonstrated an <RNA> <catalyst> while also explaining why <protein> assistance could matter under other conditions. <RNase P> produces the mature $5'$ end of <transfer RNA> by removing its leader; it acts on a separate <substrate> and can be used again. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6197186/[Guerrier-Takada et al., The RNA moiety of ribonuclease P is the catalytic subunit of the enzyme, Cell 35 (1983), 849–857: primary abstract].

Self-splicing demonstrates <RNA> chemistry, but one precursor completing one rearrangement is not yet evidence for catalytic turnover: repeated conversion of separate <substrates> by a regenerated <catalyst>. The self-splicing precursor changes its own connectivity and incorporates external <guanosine>. In 1986 Zaug and Cech showed that a shortened Tetrahymena <intron> <RNA> could cleave and rejoin separate short <RNA> <substrates> in a sequence-dependent reaction. This demonstrated an <RNA> <enzyme> acting beyond its own self-processing. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3941911/[Zaug and Cech, The intervening sequence RNA of Tetrahymena is an enzyme, Science 231 (1986), 470–475: primary abstract].

\Image[ribozymes-turnover-model.png]
{title=Stipulated <chemical kinetics> models: one self-processing event versus repeated <ribozyme> turnover}
{description=Original analytical illustration, with dimensionless time and amounts normalized by the initial <RNA> amount. Curves are calculated from the equations below, not experimental data or fits to Tetrahymena or <RNase P>. Equal exponential timescales are a chosen teaching convention; the figure does not compare their biological speeds.}

Read the second figure as a counting argument. In a stipulated model, $A$ denotes unprocessed precursor and $P_{\rm self}$ counts completed self-processing events. Set $A(0)=R_0$, $P_{\rm self}(0)=0$ and $dA/d\tau=-A$. The <exponential function> solution gives $P_{\rm self}/R_0=1-e^{-\tau}$, never exceeding one. Here <guanosine> and other necessary reagents are taken to be available in excess; $P_{\rm self}$ counts events, not every separate <reaction product> <molecule>.

For a second stipulated model, let an unchanged <ribozyme> $R$ perform $R+S\longrightarrow R+P$ on a separate <substrate> $S$. Assume the effective <law of mass action> $dS/dt=-kRS$, no reverse reaction, no <ribozyme> loss and no inhibited or long-lived bound states. Set $R=R_0$, $S(0)=5R_0$, $P(0)=0$ and $\tau=kR_0t$. Then $S/R_0=5e^{-\tau}$ and $P/R_0=5(1-e^{-\tau})$, with $S+P=5R_0$. At $\tau=1$, self-processing gives about $0.632$ completed events per initial <RNA>, whereas the regenerated <ribozyme> gives about $3.161$ <reaction product> <molecules> per initial <ribozyme>. More than one <substrate> conversion per initial <ribozyme>, with appropriate background and identity controls, is evidence for turnover. Product accumulation below that threshold alone cannot distinguish a reusable <catalyst> from one-shot chemistry. These models illustrate that distinction; actual <enzyme kinetics> can include folding delays, binding, saturation and product release.

= Ribonuclease P
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

= RNase P
{c}
{synonym}

An <enzyme> that processes the $5'$ end of precursor <transfer RNA>. The bacterial <RNase P> used in the discovery experiments contains catalytic <RNA> and a supporting <protein>; the <ribozyme> body gives the conditions under which its <RNA> component works alone. This description concerns those bacterial complexes, not every form of <RNase P>.

= Active site
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

An <active site> is the region of an <enzyme> where a <substrate> binds and undergoes a <chemical reaction>. In a <protein> <enzyme>, it includes the <amino acid residues> that contact the <substrate> and those that directly assist <catalysis>. These roles can overlap: an <amino acid side chain> can both orient a <substrate> and participate in chemistry. The <active site> occupies only part of the folded <protein>; the remaining <amino acid residues> help establish its <protein structure>, stability and movement. An <active site> need not be a consecutive stretch of <protein primary structure>: <protein folding> can bring distant <amino acid residues> together. A <ribozyme> instead supplies an <RNA> <active site>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26911/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell, Protein Function: binding sites and catalytic sites].

Binding and <catalysis> answer different questions. A <protein binding site> recognizes shape and chemistry through such interactions as <hydrogen bonds>, <electrostatics> and the <hydrophobic effect>. At an <active site>, appropriate positioning also helps a <substrate> reach the <transition state> of a <chemical reaction>. Some <amino acid side chains> transfer a <proton>; others participate transiently in a <covalent bond>. The <enzyme-substrate complex> is the associated <enzyme> and <substrate>, before conversion to a <reaction product>. Merely accumulating this complex does not establish rapid <catalysis>: stabilizing the bound <substrate> without sufficiently stabilizing the <transition state> can leave a large barrier. The important complementarity is chemical as well as geometric. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9921/[Geoffrey M. Cooper, The Cell, The Central Role of Enzymes as Biological Catalysts: substrate binding and transition-state stabilization].

\Image[active-sites-induced-fit-residue-map.png]
{title=Distant <amino acid residues> can cooperate at an <active site>}
{description=Original twelve-residue <polypeptide chain> cartoon, with the same order and eleven connections in both drawings. Positions 3, 7 and 10 approach a generic <substrate> after an imagined fold. Blue means recognition, orange means catalytic chemistry, and purple means both; these assignments are invented for explanation. Dashed contacts represent cooperation, not specified <hydrogen bonds> or actual atomic coordinates. The other nine positions support the imagined <protein structure>; three highlighted positions are not a universal <active site> size.}

The cartoon makes a useful experimental distinction. Changing a contacting <amino acid residue> might weaken <substrate> binding, change the <reaction rate>, or do both. Changing a distant <amino acid residue> can also disrupt the <active site> by changing <protein folding>. Therefore a loss of activity after a <mutation> alone does not locate a catalytic group: compare binding, <protein structure> and <enzyme kinetics> as well. This is an interpretation guide, not a report of a particular <mutation> experiment.

A concrete example is <hexokinase>: it transfers the terminal <phosphate> group of <ATP> to the $6$-position <hydroxyl group> of a <sugar> such as <glucose>. Specificity need not mean only one acceptable <substrate>: the <hexokinase> reaction class includes several possible acceptor <sugars>. Which are used, and at what <reaction rates>, depends on the particular <enzyme> and conditions. See https://enzyme.expasy.org/EC/2.7.1.1[Swiss Institute of Bioinformatics, ENZYME entry EC 2.7.1.1: hexokinase reaction and acceptor range].

Bennett and Steitz compared <yeast> <hexokinase> A crystallized with <glucose> against <hexokinase> B crystallized without <glucose>. <X-rays> scattered by the crystals supplied <diffraction> measurements from which the authors reconstructed <protein structure>; comparing coordinates located movement, rather than measuring a <reaction rate>. The <glucose>-containing cleft was narrower: one lobe was rotated by about $12^\circ$ relative to the other, with some <protein backbone> displacements reaching $8\,\mathrm{\mathring A}$. This makes the <active site> a moving arrangement of interacting groups, rather than a permanent hole. See https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC336218/[William S. Bennett Jr. and Thomas A. Steitz, Glucose-induced conformational change in yeast hexokinase, PNAS 75 (1978), 4848–4852].

The structural comparison has two explicit limitations: different forms of <hexokinase> and different crystal environments were compared, and static coordinates do not record the order of binding and movement. Closure is consistent with <induced fit>, but those structures alone cannot exclude <conformational selection>. The solution experiment under <induced fit> addresses whether compaction also occurs outside a crystal.

= Induced fit
{parent=Active site}

In <induced fit>, a <substrate> binds to an <enzyme> and the resulting <enzyme-substrate complex> subsequently changes its <protein structure>. This adjustment can position interacting groups at the <active site> for <catalysis>. The mechanistic claim concerns order: binding precedes the relevant change. In <conformational selection>, the unbound <enzyme> already visits an alternative <protein structure>, which the <substrate> preferentially binds. Both processes can occur in one system. Let $E$ and $E^*$ denote two structures of the same <enzyme>, and $S$ a <substrate>. The limiting routes are
$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{induced fit:}\quad &E+S\rightleftharpoons ES\rightleftharpoons E^*S,\\
\text{conformational selection:}\quad &E\rightleftharpoons E^*,\qquad E^*+S\rightleftharpoons E^*S.
\end{aligned}
$$
The star labels a structure, not a different <protein primary structure>, and does not by itself mean catalytic activity. Each reversible arrow abbreviates a step, not the whole catalytic cycle. See https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2728963/[Gordon G. Hammes, Yu-Chu Chang and Terrence G. Oas, Conformational selection or induced fit: a flux description of reaction mechanism, PNAS 106 (2009), 13737–13741].

A rigid lock-and-key cartoon usefully suggests selective <substrate> recognition, but it suppresses movement of the <protein> and chemical changes at the <active site>. Replacing it with two static drawings, “open” and “closed”, still does not establish <induced fit>: both routes above can end at $E^*S$. Also, if complete closure prevents a <substrate> entering, the pre-existing structure selected in <conformational selection> might be partly open and undergo further <induced fit> after binding. The usefulness of the distinction is that it turns an attractive shape story into questions about <enzyme kinetics>. Relative traffic through the routes depends on populations, <rate constants> and free <substrate> <concentration>, rather than affinity alone. See https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2728963/[Hammes, Chang and Oas, 2009: coupled pathways, their concentrations and the limitations of endpoint structures].

\Image[active-sites-induced-fit-binding-orders.png]
{title=<Induced fit> and <conformational selection>: two orders, one bound endpoint}
{description=Original reaction-order schematic and an analytical first-event model, not a <hexokinase> structure or measured data. In the upper square, horizontal steps bind or release a <substrate>; vertical steps change <protein structure>. In the lower plot, an initially unbound $E$ can bind with rate $a=k_{\mathrm{on}}s$ or change structure with rate $b$, for chosen $k_{\mathrm{on}}=1\,\mathrm{\mu M^{-1}s^{-1}}$ and $b=1\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. The plotted <probability> $a/(a+b)$ concerns only the first event from $E$, not the fraction completing either full route. Subsequent binding, reversals and <catalysis> are not modelled by that curve.}

For this analytical illustration, hold the free <substrate> <concentration> $s$ constant and assume independent waiting times with <exponential distributions> of constant rates. The <law of mass action> gives the binding rate $a=k_{\mathrm{on}}s$; the competing change has rate $b$. The <probability density function> for binding to happen first at time $t$ is $a e^{-(a+b)t}$, because neither event has happened earlier. Its integral is
$$
P(\text{binding first})=\int_0^\infty a e^{-(a+b)t}\,dt=\frac{a}{a+b}.
$$
With the stated invented <rate constants>, $s=0.1,1,10\,\mathrm{\mu M}$ gives $1/11,1/2,10/11$, respectively. Raising free <substrate> <concentration> makes binding more likely to win this initial competition. This <probability> is deliberately narrower than a full-route fraction: returns to $E$, later changes and the binding properties of $E^*$ would all matter for the latter. The illustration explains why timing and <concentration> must accompany structural evidence, without assigning a binding mechanism to a real <enzyme>.

A motivating experiment tested the crystal interpretation in solution. McDonald, Steitz and Engelman prepared solutions of monomeric <yeast> <hexokinase> B and compared the unbound preparation with sugar-bound preparations. Their apparatus measured small-angle scattering of <X-rays>; the spatial extent inferred from that readout was the <radius of gyration>, rather than a catalytic <reaction rate>. Binding <glucose> reduced the <radius of gyration> by $0.95\pm0.24\,\mathrm{\mathring A}$; binding the phosphorylated <glucose> product reduced it by $1.25\pm0.28\,\mathrm{\mathring A}$, as reported by the authors. The <glucose> change agreed with the reduction calculated from the crystal coordinates. See https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/369601/[Robert C. McDonald, Thomas A. Steitz and Donald M. Engelman, Yeast hexokinase in solution exhibits a large conformational change upon binding glucose or glucose 6-phosphate, Biochemistry 18 (1979), 338–342: primary abstract].

The comparison without added <substrate> supplies a baseline within the same form of <hexokinase>; the second bound preparation tests another binding condition. Together with the crystal comparison, solution compaction supports closure beyond crystal packing. It still gives an average spatial readout, not a movie of individual binding events, and product-associated compaction does not demonstrate ongoing <catalysis>. Neither experiment alone proves that binding precedes movement. To distinguish <induced fit> from <conformational selection>, structural comparisons must be combined with measurements sensitive to timing and with a model of the relevant populations and <enzyme kinetics>.

= Enzyme-substrate complex
{parent=Active site}
{title2=$ES$}

= Conformational selection
{parent=Active site}

= Hexokinase
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

= Cofactor
{disambiguate=biochemistry}
{parent=Enzyme}
{wiki}

= Coenzyme
{parent=Cofactor (biochemistry)}

A small organic helper involved in an <enzyme>'s <catalysis>. It can be transformed in one <chemical reaction> and regenerated in another.

= Substrate
{disambiguate=biochemistry}
{parent=Enzyme}

= Substrate
{synonym}

A <reaction substrate> on which an <enzyme> acts in a <chemical reaction>.

= Flow cytometry
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

<Flow cytometry> records signals from suspended particles passing an illuminated interrogation region. For a <flow cytometric DNA-content assay>, a <laser> excites a <DNA>-binding dye and a detector records its <fluorescence>. An event is a recorded passage, not a guarantee that exactly one intact <biological cell> was measured.

= Flow cytometric DNA-content assay
{parent=Flow cytometry}

Under controlled staining and detector settings, integrated <fluorescence> can be approximately proportional to nuclear <DNA> amount. A <histogram> of accepted single-cell events then separates a $2C$ region, an intermediate region and a $4C$ region. These are ordinarily assigned to <G0 phase>/<G1 phase>, <S phase>, and <G2 phase>/<M phase> respectively in the stated <diploid> model.

\Image[dna-content-assay-illustrative-peaks.png]
{title=Illustrative <DNA>-content assay peaks: equal amount need not mean equal <cell cycle> stage}
{description=Original synthetic curves, not measured data or a fit to an experiment. The horizontal axis is a proportional <fluorescence> scale expressed in $C$ units for a reference <diploid> population. Component shapes and weights are arbitrary. The $2C$ peak combines <G0 phase> and <G1 phase>; the $4C$ peak combines <G2 phase> and <M phase>. The plotted curve cannot itself distinguish these paired states.}

A Cornell protocol fixes a single-cell suspension by adding cold 70% <ethanol> dropwise while mixing, reducing clumping. After washing, its staining solution contains propidium iodide at $50\,\mathrm{\mu g/ml}$; <ribonuclease A> removes interfering <RNA>. It specifies excitation at $488\,\mathrm{nm}$ and emission around $600\,\mathrm{nm}$. The resulting readout is <fluorescence> per event, not a direct count of <chromosomes>. <DNA Cell Cycle Analysis with PI>[DNA Cell Cycle Analysis with PI, p. 1: dye properties, staining steps 1–7 and reagent recipe]{ref}

Useful controls include an unstained sample for background and a reference population with known nuclear <DNA> amount, processed with identical settings. Two attached $2C$ <biological cells> can mimic one $4C$ event. Comparing signal integral with pulse height or width helps exclude such doublets; a gate selecting intact single particles also limits debris. These are proposed assay checks, not measurements underlying the illustration.

A $4C$ peak cannot distinguish <G2 phase> from <mitosis>; a $2C$ peak cannot distinguish <G1 phase> from <G0 phase>. Mixed <ploidy>, uneven staining and aggregates can invalidate phase assignments. Peak areas describe the accepted sample at collection, and do not by themselves establish phase durations, division rates or a particular arrest mechanism. A treatment comparison needs matched handling and another readout appropriate to the claimed mechanism.

= Biological cell
{parent=Biology}
{wiki=Cell_(biology)}

A <biological cell> is a membrane-bounded basic unit of living organization. In a cell-level experiment it can be an <experimental unit>, provided that treatment assignment and relevant interactions are handled at that level.

The shared architecture of cellular life combines a selective <cell membrane>, internal <cytoplasm>, inherited information in <DNA>, and <ribosomes> that assemble <proteins>. <Metabolism> supplies transformations of matter and <energy> needed for maintenance and growth. These components form a coupled system: a boundary retains useful <molecules>, <enzymes> organize <chemical reactions>, and inherited information supports rebuilding the machinery. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-2-prokaryotic-cells[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: prokaryotic cells].

A worked structural comparison: in a typical <prokaryote>, <DNA> occupies a region without a surrounding <cell nucleus>; in a typical nucleated <eukaryote>, much of the <DNA> lies inside a membrane-bounded <cell nucleus>. Both still have a <cell membrane>, <cytoplasm> and <ribosomes>. The extra boundary compartmentalizes processes; it does not create a different basic principle of living organization. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-3-eukaryotic-cells[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: eukaryotic cells].

\Image[cell-compartments.png]
{title=Shared cellular architecture with different DNA compartmentalization}
{description=Original simplified schematics of a <prokaryote> and a nucleated <eukaryote>. Colors identify corresponding components; sizes and component counts are illustrative, and additional structures are omitted.}

Shared architecture describes cellular life and its developmental machinery, rather than requiring every specialized <biological cell> to retain every component. A mature mammalian <red blood cell>, for example, lacks a <cell nucleus>, although it develops from nucleated precursors. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-2-components-of-the-blood[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: components of blood]. This distinction makes cellular unity compatible with striking specialization.

= Cell state
{parent=Biological cell}

A <cell state> describes current properties of a <biological cell>, including its <gene expression>, structure and activity. A snapshot of <cell state> does not alone establish its future <cell fate>.

= Stem cell
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

A <stem cell> can produce both renewed <stem cells> and differentiated descendants. Its developmental range depends on the type and setting; self-renewal does not imply unrestricted <cell fate>.

= Progenitor cell
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

A <progenitor cell> is a precursor with relatively restricted differentiation potential and, generally, more limited self-renewal than a <stem cell>. Usage depends on the lineage and assay; an expression marker alone does not establish renewal capacity.

= Secretory granule
{parent=Biological cell}

A storage compartment that participates in regulated <exocytosis>; the <insulin> example distinguishes storage of a <hormone> from storage of <glycogen> fuel.

= Cell cycle
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

The <cell cycle> is the sequence of events by which a <biological cell> prepares for and completes division, including <DNA replication> and distribution of <chromosomes>. In <Saccharomyces cerevisiae>, changing bud appearance provides an accessible readout; see the Burgess, Powers and Mell account and Hartwell, Culotti and Reid's primary study cited there.

For a typical cycling <diploid> <eukaryotic> <biological cell>, the order is <G1 phase>, <S phase>, <G2 phase>, then <M phase>. <DNA replication> doubles nuclear <DNA> amount during <S phase>; <mitosis> distributes the copies, and <cytokinesis> separates the daughters. The useful distinction is between copying a library and allocating its copies: <DNA replication> changes how much material exists, whereas division changes which <biological cell> contains it.

\Image[cell-cycle-phases-and-dna-content.png]
{title=The <cell cycle>: copying nuclear <DNA> once, then distributing it to two daughters}
{description=Original schematic for a typical cycling <diploid> <eukaryotic> <biological cell>. Each box states nuclear <DNA> content and <chromosome> count; <M phase> is summarized by its completed daughters. Arrow lengths do not represent phase durations. The <G0 phase> branch shows reversible withdrawal where re-entry is possible, rather than claiming that all nondividing <biological cells> can return.}

<Gene expression> continues to supply <RNA> and <proteins> as the <biological cell> grows; it is different from duplicating the <genome>. In the <yeast cross-species complementation experiment>, restored growth requires successful repeated <cell cycles>, but growth alone does not locate a defect within a phase. Bud appearance in <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> provides another readout, while a <flow cytometric DNA-content assay> asks how much nuclear <DNA> is present and <DNA pulse labeling> asks whether new <DNA> was synthesized during an exposure.

= Interphase
{parent=Cell cycle}
{wiki}

<Interphase> comprises <G1 phase>, <S phase> and <G2 phase>, including growth and <DNA replication>.

= G1 phase
{c}
{parent=Interphase}
{wiki}
{title2=$G_1$}

During <G1 phase>, growth and preparation precede <DNA replication>. The ordinary <diploid> nuclear state is $2n,2C$.

= S phase
{c}
{parent=Interphase}
{wiki}
{title2=$S$}

During <S phase>, nuclear <DNA replication> produces paired <sister chromatids>. <DNA> content rises from $2C$ to $4C$, while the <chromosome> count remains $2n$.

= G2 phase
{c}
{parent=Interphase}
{wiki}
{title2=$G_2$}

<G2 phase> follows completed nuclear <DNA replication> and precedes <mitosis>. The ordinary <diploid> state is $2n,4C$. <Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/The Cell Cycle>[Biology 2e, §10.2, Interphase and its G1, S and G2 subsections; Figure 10.5]{ref}

= M phase
{c}
{parent=Cell cycle}
{title2=$M$}

<M phase> includes <mitosis> and ordinarily <cytokinesis>, which can overlap in time.

= Mitosis
{parent=M phase}
{wiki}

<Mitosis> allocates copied <chromosomes> to daughter <cell nuclei>. The <spindle apparatus> aligns <sister chromatids> before separation in <anaphase>.

= Anaphase
{parent=Mitosis}
{wiki}

In mitotic <anaphase>, <sister chromatids> separate into daughter <chromosomes> moving toward opposite poles.

= Cytokinesis
{parent=M phase}
{wiki}

<Cytokinesis> partitions the <cytoplasm>. Without <cytokinesis>, two <cell nuclei> can share one <biological cell>. <Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/The Cell Cycle>[Biology 2e, §10.2, The Mitotic Phase, Figure 10.6 and Cytokinesis]{ref}

= G0 phase
{c}
{parent=Cell cycle}
{wiki}
{title2=$G_0$}

<G0 phase> describes withdrawal from the division cycle. Some <biological cells> re-enter <G1 phase>; others remain nondividing. <Metabolism> and <gene expression> can continue. <Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/The Cell Cycle>[Biology 2e, §10.2, G0 Phase]{ref} In the ordinary <diploid> case, $2C$ alone cannot distinguish <G0 phase> from <G1 phase>.

= Cell-cycle DNA content
{parent=Cell cycle}
{title2=$(2n,2C)\to(2n,4C)\to2\,(2n,2C)$}

Let $n$ count <chromosomes> in one complete set, and let $C$ denote the nuclear <DNA> amount in one unreplicated <haploid> set of this <organism>. <Ploidy> counts sets, whereas $C$ units count material; $C$ is not a universal mass shared by all <species>. Here we count a paired replicated <chromosome> as one until its <sister chromatids> separate. A copied <diploid> <genome> is therefore still <diploid> through <S phase>: $2n$ persists even as $2C$ becomes $4C$.

An original bookkeeping example uses a hypothetical <diploid> <biological cell> with $n=3$. Name its three <homologous chromosome> pairs $A,a$, $B,b$ and $D,d$. Assume ordinary nuclear <DNA replication>, no <chromosome> loss, and one continuous double-stranded <DNA> <molecule> per unreplicated <chromosome> or replicated <chromatid>. Count nuclear material only, excluding <mitochondrion> <DNA>.

\Table
[
\Tr[\Th[Stage and counting compartment]\Th[<Chromosomes>]\Th[Double-stranded <DNA> <molecules>]\Th[Nuclear <DNA> amount]]
\Tr[\Td[One <G1 phase> <biological cell>]\Td[6 unreplicated]\Td[6]\Td[$2C$]]
\Tr[\Td[One <G2 phase> <biological cell>]\Td[6 replicated]\Td[12 in 6 pairs of <sister chromatids>]\Td[$4C$]]
\Tr[\Td[Whole undivided <anaphase> <biological cell>]\Td[12 separated daughter <chromosomes>]\Td[12]\Td[$4C$ total]]
\Tr[\Td[Each completed daughter <cell nucleus>]\Td[6 unreplicated]\Td[6]\Td[$2C$ each]]
]

For example, $A$ becomes the paired copies $A_1,A_2$ during <S phase>. They remain one replicated <chromosome> until separation. Each completed daughter receives one $A$ copy and one $a$ copy, and similarly for the other two pairs. Across both daughters, $2\times6=12$ double-stranded <DNA> <molecules> and $2(2C)=4C$: division allocates the copied material without another round of <DNA replication>.

The whole undivided <anaphase> <biological cell> transiently contains $4n$ separately counted daughter <chromosomes>. This counting change does not establish a four-set <ploidy> in a completed daughter <genome>: each pole receives $2n$, and each completed daughter <cell nucleus> is <diploid>. Always state whether a number refers to one <cell nucleus>, one pole, or the entire undivided <biological cell>. Compare <meiotic DNA content>: after <meiosis I>, a <haploid> <cell nucleus> can contain $2C$, so amount alone cannot determine <ploidy>.

= Cell cycle analysis
{parent=Cell cycle}
{wiki}

<Cell cycle analysis> assigns evidence to stages of the <cell cycle>. A <flow cytometric DNA-content assay> measures accumulated nuclear <DNA>, whereas <DNA pulse labeling> detects synthesis over an exposure window. Agreement between these readouts is useful because an amount and a synthesis event answer different questions.

= DNA pulse labeling
{c}
{parent=Cell cycle analysis}

<DNA pulse labeling> exposes <biological cells> to a detectable precursor for a bounded interval. After removal, later sampling can follow the labeled material and descendants. A short pulse asks about synthesis during that interval; overnight labeling instead accumulates evidence across many hours. Detectable synthesis does not by itself prove completion of a <cell cycle>, and <DNA repair> can also incorporate precursors.

= DNA synthesis labeling with EdU
{c}
{parent=DNA pulse labeling}

= EdU labeling
{c}
{synonym}

<EdU labeling> uses a modified <nucleoside>, 5-ethynyl-2'-deoxyuridine, incorporated into newly synthesized <DNA>, then chemically couples it to a fluorescent reporter.

A 2008 experiment injected adult mice with $100\,\mathrm{\mu g}$ of this label and inspected intestinal sections 96 hours later. Fixed sections on slides received $10\,\mathrm{\mu M}$ Alexa568-azide for 10 minutes. Imaging with a <fluorescence microscope> combined the reporter image with a Hoechst <DNA> counterstain and a transmitted-light view. Labeled <cell nuclei> marked descendants of <biological cells> synthesizing <DNA> during exposure, rather than identifying current <S phase> at collection. <A chemical method for fast and sensitive detection of DNA synthesis in vivo by Adrian Salic and Timothy J. Mitchison>[A chemical method for fast and sensitive detection of DNA synthesis in vivo, Figure 4 legend: mouse pulse, section preparation and imaging]{ref}

The same study's cultured-cell controls compared overnight $10\,\mathrm{\mu M}$ label, no label, and label with $10\,\mathrm{mM}$ <hydroxyurea>. No-label samples had little reporter background; <hydroxyurea> strongly suppressed incorporation. The camera detected residual signal with a 3-second exposure versus 40 milliseconds under otherwise identical settings, demonstrating why acquisition settings matter. These controls support synthesis-dependent labeling, without proving that every labeled <biological cell> completes division. <A chemical method for fast and sensitive detection of DNA synthesis in vivo by Adrian Salic and Timothy J. Mitchison>[Figure 2 legend, A–M: NIH 3T3 controls, fluorescence and differential interference contrast imaging, exposure-time comparison]{ref}

= Spindle apparatus
{parent=Cell cycle}

The <spindle apparatus> distributes <chromosomes> during division of a <cell nucleus>.

= Cell envelope
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

The <cell envelope> comprises a <biological cell>'s surrounding boundary layers, including its <cell membrane> and a <cell wall> or additional outer structures when present. The term is especially useful when discussing <bacteria>, where the layers must be distinguished.

= Bacterial outer membrane
{parent=Cell envelope}
{wiki}

A <bacterial outer membrane> is an additional membrane outside the <peptidoglycan> in typical <Gram-negative bacteria>. It adds a barrier while the inner <cell membrane> remains the boundary of the <cytoplasm>.

= Cell wall
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

A <cell wall> is a supporting structure outside a <cell membrane>. It helps preserve shape and bears the load when <osmosis> raises internal <pressure>. The <cell membrane> supplies the <selective permeability> described in its own article; the <cell wall> supplies mechanical reinforcement. These functions cooperate: a porous supporting layer can restrain expansion without being the boundary that retains dissolved <solutes>. <Turgor pressure> makes this cooperation visible in a firm leaf; <plasmolysis> and a <lysozyme wall-removal experiment> separate the two functions experimentally.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/5.2 Passive Transport>[Biology 2e, §5.2, Tonicity in Living Systems, Figure 5.13: wall support and plasmolysis]{ref}

The material is not universal. Most <bacteria> use <peptidoglycan>, whose linked <sugar> chains and <amino acid> cross-links are explained below. A <plant cell wall> uses <cellulose>; a <fungal cell wall> contains <chitin> and <glucans>. <Animals> lack a <cell wall>, some <bacteria> are naturally wall-free, and <archaea> have different wall chemistry. Thus a <cell wall> is neither a universal cellular component nor one chemical recipe.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/22.2 Structure of Prokaryotes: Bacteria and Archaea>[Biology 2e, §22.2, Figure 22.13 and The Cell Wall of Prokaryotes: wall-free bacteria and archaeal differences]{ref}<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/4.3 Eukaryotic Cells>[Biology 2e, §4.3, Animal Cells versus Plant Cells and The Cell Wall: cellulose and animal comparison]{ref}<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/24.1 Characteristics of Fungi>[Biology 2e, §24.1, Cell Structure and Function: chitin and glucans]{ref}

\Image[cell-envelope-comparison.png]
{title=Representative <cell envelopes>: different supporting layers outside the <cell membrane>}
{description=Original layer-order schematic comparing <Gram-positive bacteria>, <Gram-negative bacteria>, a <plant cell wall>, a <fungal cell wall>, a member of <archaea> with a protein wall and an <animal> cell. Colors distinguish a <cell wall> from a <cell membrane> and a <bacterial outer membrane>. Thicknesses and spacing are illustrative, not measurements. These examples do not exhaust variation within any group.}

The <bacterial outer membrane> in typical <Gram-negative bacteria> is an additional membrane outside the <peptidoglycan>, not another name for that supporting mesh. The <cell envelope> includes the boundary layers together. An extra barrier can change access to the <peptidoglycan>, so response to <lysozyme> cannot be inferred from the mere presence of a <cell wall>.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/22.2 Structure of Prokaryotes: Bacteria and Archaea>[Biology 2e, §22.2, Figure 22.16: inner membrane, peptidoglycan and outer membrane]{ref}

= Plant cell wall
{parent=Cell wall}

A <plant cell wall> provides reinforcement outside the <cell membrane>, with <cellulose> as a major structural component. It is a composite rather than a solid sheet of pure <cellulose>. Wall construction varies with the kind and developmental state of the <biological cell>.

= Fungal cell wall
{parent=Cell wall}

A <fungal cell wall> contains structural <polysaccharides> including <chitin> and <glucans>. Its composition and organization vary among <fungi>; the diagram illustrates a composite rather than a pure <chitin> shell. A <fungus> sharing a <cell wall> with a <plant> does not imply shared wall chemistry.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/24.1 Characteristics of Fungi>[Biology 2e, §24.1, Cell Structure and Function: structural wall polysaccharides]{ref}

= Chitin
{parent=Fungal cell wall}
{tag=Polysaccharide}
{wiki}

<Chitin> is a structural <polysaccharide> built from modified <sugar> units. It contributes reinforcement to a <fungal cell wall>.

= Glucan
{parent=Fungal cell wall}
{tag=Polysaccharide}
{wiki}

A <glucan> is a <polysaccharide> built from <glucose> units. Different linkages produce different structures; some <glucans> contribute to a <fungal cell wall>.

= Turgor pressure
{parent=Cell wall}
{wiki}
{title2=$P_{\mathrm{in}}-P_{\mathrm{out}}$}

<Turgor pressure> is the internal <hydrostatic pressure> above the external <pressure> in a <biological cell>. When <water> enters a walled <biological cell> by <osmosis>, expansion loads the <cell wall>. Rising <pressure> can oppose further net entry while the <cell membrane> retains its <selective permeability>. Mechanical support therefore does not require the <cell wall> to block <water>.

As an original mechanical check, idealize a small flat patch of <cell wall> of <surface area> $A$ under uniform <turgor pressure> $\Delta P=P_{\mathrm{in}}-P_{\mathrm{out}}$. Its outward <force> is $F=\Delta P A$. Doubling $A$ at fixed <pressure> doubles the load; equal internal and external <pressures> give zero net pressure load. This is a force balance, not a measured wall strength or a complete model of a curved, growing <biological cell>. It says nothing about which <solutes> cross the <cell membrane>.

= Plasmolysis
{parent=Cell wall}
{wiki}

<Plasmolysis> is withdrawal of the membrane-bounded contents from parts of a <cell wall> after <water> loss. A sufficiently concentrated external <hypertonic solution>, with <solutes> effectively excluded by the <cell membrane> over the observation time, can cause this. The <cell wall> remains; the enclosed material contracts. <Plasmolysis> is therefore distinct from <osmotic lysis>, which breaks the cellular boundary during excessive swelling. A return to a less concentrated surrounding <chemical solution> can reverse <plasmolysis> if the <biological cell> remains functional.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/5.2 Passive Transport>[Biology 2e, §5.2, Tonicity in Living Systems and Figure 5.13: membrane withdrawal under hypertonic conditions]{ref}

\Image[cell-wall-osmotic-experiment-logic.png]
{title=<Plasmolysis> with a retained <cell wall>, and osmotic challenge after wall removal}
{description=Original qualitative experiment logic. The upper pair compares a turgid <plant> cell with <plasmolysis> in an external <hypertonic solution>. The lower pair compares a supported <protoplast> with <osmotic lysis> after dilution for a susceptible preparation. Arrows indicate interventions or possible net <water> movement, not measured rates. The dilution comparison concerns <lysozyme>-susceptible <bacteria>; the upper comparison concerns <plant> cells. No measured dimensions, times or survival fractions are implied.}

= Protoplast
{parent=Cell wall}
{wiki}

A <protoplast> is the living contents of a <biological cell> bounded by its <cell membrane> after removal of the <cell wall>. Removing the <cell wall> need not immediately destroy the <cell membrane>; stability depends on the preparation and surroundings. <Lysozyme> acts on susceptible bacterial <peptidoglycan>, not on <cellulose> or <chitin>, so it is not a universal wall-removal <enzyme>.

= Lysozyme wall-removal experiment
{parent=Protoplast}

The 1953 paper <The isolation of protoplasts from Bacillus megaterium by controlled treatment with lysozyme by Claes Weibull> (pp. 688–690, Figures 2–3) tested <Bacillus megaterium> strain KM. Apparatus included a Klett-Summerson colorimeter with a red filter and a Spencer no. 8 <phase-contrast microscope>. <Lysozyme> in phosphate buffer produced empty residual bodies; adding $0.2\,\mathrm{M}$ <sucrose> preserved granular spheres. Diluting their surroundings caused rapid <osmotic lysis>.

The same study compared untreated, boiled, <lysozyme>-exposed and <sucrose>-protected samples. Filled, stoppered tubes reduced damage from air exposure. After <centrifugation>, a Beckman DU spectrophotometer measured ultraviolet <wave absorption> of the separated liquid. Protected samples resembled untreated controls in releasing little ultraviolet-absorbing material; boiled and unprotected samples released much more. Later-growth cultures reduced spontaneous breakdown as a confounder.<The isolation of protoplasts from Bacillus megaterium by controlled treatment with lysozyme by Claes Weibull>[pp. 688–689, Materials and Methods; pp. 692–694, release comparison and Figure 6]{ref}

Together, the shape and release readouts support removal of mechanical protection while a permeability barrier persists. They do not establish unchanged <selective permeability> to every <solute>, long-term viability, or universal susceptibility.

An instructive extension of this experimental logic is a matched comparison: <lysozyme> present or absent, crossed with osmotic support present or absent. Keep starting material, buffer, observation interval and handling comparable; include a blank to distinguish optical effects of reagents from changes in the <biological cells>. Then dilute already formed <protoplasts> as a separate intervention. This separates access and wall removal from failure after removal. These are proposed controls for interpreting the mechanism, not additional reported results from the historical paper. A fall in optical signal alone cannot establish rupture: shape changes also alter <scattering of light>.

= Peptidoglycan
{parent=Cell wall}
{wiki}

<Peptidoglycan> is a mesh of linked <sugar> chains cross-linked by short chains of <amino acids> in the <cell walls> of most <bacteria>. Its absence from the <cell walls> of <archaea> helps distinguish envelope chemistry without confusing either <cell wall> with a <cell membrane>. See https://openstax.org/books/microbiology/pages/3-3-unique-characteristics-of-prokaryotic-cells[OpenStax Microbiology, 3.3: cell walls and peptidoglycan].

= Cell membrane
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

= Plasma membrane
{synonym}

The <cell membrane>, also called the <plasma membrane>, is the selective boundary separating a <biological cell>'s <cytoplasm> from its surroundings. Both <prokaryotes> and <eukaryotes> have a <cell membrane>. Where a <cell wall> is present, it is a distinct structure outside the <cell membrane>; the two names are not synonyms. This separation lets a <biological cell> maintain internal conditions while exchanging material through <membrane transport>. See https://www.genome.gov/genetics-glossary/Cell-Membrane-Plasma-Membrane[NHGRI: cell membrane].

The familiar architecture is a <lipid bilayer> containing <membrane proteins>. In a <phospholipid>-based <lipid bilayer>, two <membrane leaflets> face opposite ways: <hydrophilic> heads face the <water> on each side, while <hydrophobic> tails face one another in the interior. The heads are therefore exposed both outside and inside the <biological cell>. This arrangement combines a water-facing surface with a barrier to many water-soluble <solutes>; the schematic represents organization rather than an atomistic structure. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26871/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: the lipid bilayer].

\Image[cell-membrane-selective-barrier.png]
{title=Two <membrane leaflets> and selective routes through a <cell membrane>}
{description=Original <cell membrane> schematic showing <phospholipids>, <hydrophilic> heads, <hydrophobic> tails, an <ion channel>, and a <carrier protein>. Arrows illustrate possible passage, not measured rates. Shapes, sizes, spacing and numbers of <molecules> are illustrative.}

<Selective permeability> means that different substances cross at different rates under comparable conditions. Small <nonpolar> <molecules> can pass through a <lipid bilayer> by <diffusion>, while <ions> encounter a strong barrier without a suitable route. An <ion channel> supplies a selective water-filled pathway when open; a <carrier protein> binds a <solute> and changes shape to expose it to the other side. These <membrane proteins> therefore add specific routes to the barrier. An open <ion channel> permits <passive transport>, whereas a <carrier protein> can mediate either <passive transport> or <active transport>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26815/[Alberts et al.: principles of membrane transport].

The useful combination is retention plus controlled exchange: a completely impermeable boundary would prevent uptake, whereas unrestricted mixing would erase differences in <concentration> and <membrane potential>. The <fluid mosaic model> connects these functions to an organized but mobile <lipid bilayer> containing <proteins>.

= Selective permeability
{parent=Cell membrane}

<Selective permeability> means that a <cell membrane> permits different substances to pass at different rates under comparable conditions. Both the <lipid bilayer> and the available <membrane proteins> contribute to this selection.

Artificial boundaries can also have <selective permeability>. For <osmosis>, specify which <solutes> cross and over what observation interval: a boundary that retains one <solute> need not retain another. A <semipermeable membrane> is the ideal solvent-only boundary used in the <osmotic pressure> calculation.

= Semipermeable membrane
{parent=Selective permeability}
{wiki}

A <semipermeable membrane> ideally passes the <solvent> while excluding the specified <solutes>. In the aqueous <osmosis> model below, <water> can cross but the counted dissolved particles cannot. Real <selective permeability> is often a matter of different rates, so this idealization needs checking over the measurement interval. Neither an impermeable sheet nor a freely mixing opening realizes this model.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/osmotic pressure>[Gold Book, osmotic pressure: definition of the solvent-only membrane]{ref}

= Membrane protein
{parent=Cell membrane}
{wiki}

= Membrane transport protein
{parent=Membrane protein}
{wiki}

= GLUT4
{c}
{parent=Membrane transport protein}
{wiki}

= Carrier protein
{parent=Membrane transport protein}

A membrane <carrier protein> binds a <solute> and changes its exposed binding site from one side of a <cell membrane> to the other. Depending on its mechanism and coupling, it can mediate <passive transport> or <active transport>.

= Gap junction
{parent=Cell membrane}
{wiki}

= Ion channel
{parent=Cell membrane}
{wiki}

An <ion channel> is a <membrane protein> that provides a selective pathway for <ions> across a <cell membrane>. An open pore permits <passive transport> down the relevant <electrochemical gradient>; it does not supply the <free energy> for uphill transport as a pump involved in <active transport> does. Two things are needed for a net <electric current>: an available pathway and an <electrochemical potential> difference. Opening a pore alone need not produce a net <electric current>. See <Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition by Bruce Alberts and others/Ion Channels and the Electrical Properties of Membranes>.

The <selective permeability> of an <ion channel> depends on interactions between its pore and the passing <ions>, not simply on a hole's diameter. Some <ion channels> strongly favor <potassium> over <sodium>; others admit several kinds of positively charged <ions>. Thus selective does not necessarily mean exclusive to one <ion> species. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK11123/[Purves et al., Neuroscience, 2nd edition (2001): molecular structure of ion channels] and the mixed-channel example in https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK11013/[Purves et al.: principles derived from studies of the neuromuscular junction].

\Image[ion-channel-selective-passage.png]
{title=An open <ion channel> supplies a selective passive pathway}
{description=Original model schematic: selected positive <ions> can traverse a pore in a <membrane protein>, while the differently shaped particles are excluded from this modeled pore. The arrow shows one possible net direction when the corresponding <electrochemical gradient> favors entry; the pore does not enforce that direction. Shapes, particle counts and dimensions are illustrative, not physical microscopic data or a universal selectivity rule.}

Gating is the opening and closing of an <ion channel>'s pathway. A <voltage-gated ion channel> responds to <membrane potential>; an <ionotropic receptor> can respond to a bound <ligand>. Gating changes the available <electrical conductance>, while the <electrochemical gradient> supplies the drive for <passive transport>. For a fixed <reversal potential> in the simple model below, opening more identical <ion channels> increases the magnitude of the <electric current> at a fixed nonzero drive, without choosing its sign independently of <membrane potential>. The <two-state ion-channel gating> model describes how the open fraction can change. See the Alberts reference above.

For a worked approximation, hold an open population's <electrical conductance> at $g=10\,\mathrm{nS}$ and its <reversal potential> at $E_{\mathrm{rev}}=-80\,\mathrm{mV}$. Use $V=V_{\mathrm{inside}}-V_{\mathrm{outside}}$ for <membrane potential> and \b[inward-positive <conventional current>]:
$$
I_{\mathrm{in}}=g(E_{\mathrm{rev}}-V),\qquad g\geq0.
$$
At $V=-100,-80,-60\,\mathrm{mV}$, respectively, the <electric currents> are $+200,0,-200\,\mathrm{pA}$, since $1\,\mathrm{nS}\times1\,\mathrm{mV}=1\,\mathrm{pA}$. Positive <electric current> means net positive <electric charge> entering, or net negative <electric charge> leaving; it does not mean that every <ion> moves inward. For a pore passing only positive <ions>, the three results mean net inward passage, no net passage, and net outward passage. These stipulated values describe an illustrative Ohmic approximation, not measured properties of a particular <ion channel>; see <reversal potential> for the current plot and limitations.

The useful connection is between a molecular gate and a controllable <electric current>. A <ligand> can change <electrical conductance> through an <ionotropic receptor>, or <membrane potential> can change it through a <voltage-gated ion channel>, connecting recognition and electrical signaling. In a historical landmark, Erwin Neher and Bert Sakmann reported currents from individual <ion channels> in frog muscle membranes in 1976. See https://doi.org/10.1038/260799a0[Neher and Sakmann, Single-channel currents recorded from membrane of denervated frog muscle fibres, Nature 260, 799–802 (1976)].

= ATP-sensitive potassium channel
{c}
{parent=Ion channel}
{wiki}

= Membrane transport
{parent=Cell membrane}
{wiki}

<Membrane transport> is the passage of substances across a <cell membrane>. For the small <solutes> considered here, distinguish the available route from its driving force: a <lipid bilayer>, <ion channel>, or <carrier protein> determines how passage can occur, while differences in <chemical potential> and <membrane potential> determine which direction is energetically downhill. A large driving force cannot make an unavailable route conduct. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26815/[Alberts et al.: principles of membrane transport].

For an ideal dilute <chemical solution> at uniform <temperature> and <pressure>, with the same reference molar <chemical potential> on both sides, the molar <electrochemical potential> change for transferring a <solute> from compartment A to B is
$$
\Delta\widetilde\mu_{\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}}=RT\ln\!\left(\frac{c_{\mathrm B}}{c_{\mathrm A}}\right)+zF(V_{\mathrm B}-V_{\mathrm A}).
$$
Here $c_{\mathrm A},c_{\mathrm B}$ are <concentrations>, $T$ is absolute <temperature>, $R$ is the <molar gas constant>, $z$ is signed <ion> charge number, $F$ is the <Faraday constant>, and $V_{\mathrm B}-V_{\mathrm A}$ is the <electric potential> difference. The <natural logarithm> contains a dimensionless ratio. The electrical term vanishes for an uncharged <solute>. More generally, use <thermodynamic activities> in place of the ideal <concentration> ratio. This combines the ideal-solution <chemical potential> difference with electrical <energy> per mole; see https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/E01945[IUPAC Gold Book: electrochemical potential]. For a <biological cell>, taking B inside and A outside makes the voltage term use the existing <membrane potential> convention.

\Image[membrane-transport-gradients.png]
{title=<Passive transport> and <active transport> for a stipulated <concentration> difference}
{description=Ten illustrative neutral <solute> tracers are split 8:2 between equal fixed-<volume> compartments. The lower sketches compare conceptual <free energy> profiles for <passive transport> and the <solute> part of <active transport>. Heights are unscaled and are not experimental barriers; the lower-right curve excludes the driving process, so it is not a full coupled-cycle <free energy> profile.}

= Passive transport
{parent=Membrane transport}
{wiki}

<Passive transport> is net movement down the transported substance's <electrochemical gradient> without coupling that movement to another source of <free energy>. <Facilitated diffusion> is <passive transport> through an <ion channel> or a passive <carrier protein>. Providing such a route can speed passage, but does not turn an uphill transfer into a downhill one. For an uncharged <solute> under the ideal conditions above, net <passive transport> runs from higher to lower <concentration>; for an <ion>, the <membrane potential> also matters. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9847/[Cooper, The Cell: transport of small molecules].

In the figure, stipulate eight neutral <solute> tracers in A and two in B, with equal fixed <volumes>, identical surroundings, and no imposed <electric potential> difference. The <concentration> ratio is $c_{\mathrm A}/c_{\mathrm B}=8/2=4$, so A-to-B transfer has $\Delta\widetilde\mu=-RT\ln4\simeq-1.38629RT$, or $-3.458\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ at the assumed <temperature> $T=300\,\mathrm K$. If this <solute> can cross, <thermodynamic equilibrium> has equal expected <concentrations>: five tracers per side on average. Individual crossings occur in both directions; an observed ten-particle snapshot need not contain exactly five on each side. These stipulated counts illustrate a driving force, not a measured flow rate or a prediction of the time to equilibrate.

For a separate illustrative <ion> calculation, keep $c_{\mathrm A}/c_{\mathrm B}=4$, but assign $z=+1$ and $V_{\mathrm A}-V_{\mathrm B}=-60\,\mathrm{mV}$ at $300\,\mathrm K$. Using $R\simeq8.31446\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$ and $F\simeq96485.33\,\mathrm{C\,mol^{-1}}$, B-to-A transfer gives $RT\ln4-0.060F\simeq3.458-5.789=-2.331\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. Thus an appropriate open <ion channel> can permit <passive transport> toward the higher <concentration>: the electrical contribution outweighs the opposing <chemical potential> contribution. This stipulated <membrane potential> is not inferred from the tracer counts.

= Facilitated diffusion
{parent=Passive transport}
{wiki}

<Facilitated diffusion> is <passive transport> through a <membrane transport protein>, such as an <ion channel> or <carrier protein>, down the relevant <electrochemical gradient>. The <protein> supplies a route; it does not by itself provide <free energy> for uphill transfer.

= Osmosis
{parent=Passive transport}
{wiki}

<Osmosis> is net movement of <water> across a boundary whose permeability differs for <water> and relevant <solutes>. The direction depends on the <chemical potential> of <water>, including both dissolved material and <pressure>. A <cell wall> can let <water> reach the <cell membrane>, yet oppose expansion through <turgor pressure>. Zero net movement does not mean individual <water> <molecules> have stopped crossing.

Imagine pure <water> on side A and a <chemical solution> on side B of a <semipermeable membrane>. At equal <temperature> and <pressure>, dissolved particles lower the <chemical potential> of <water> in B, and net <water> transfer initially favors A to B. Retaining the <solutes> lets <water> redistribute without simply mixing everything. Increasing B's <pressure> opposes entry; enough <pressure> gives <thermodynamic equilibrium> with unequal <solute> <concentrations>. This is why “<water> moves toward more <solute>” requires a pressure condition.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/5.2 Passive Transport>[§5.2, Osmosis and Mechanism: selective passage and pressure opposing entry]{ref}

\Image[osmosis-membrane-pressure-balance.png]
{title=<Selective permeability> and the <pressure> that balances <osmosis>}
{description=Original schematic of a boundary passing <water> and retaining the illustrated <solute>. Left: equal <pressures> allow net transfer toward the <chemical solution>. Right: excess <pressure> on the solution side balances the osmotic drive while <water> exchanges in both directions. Equal opposing arrows denote zero net transfer, not a measured molecular rate. Particle counts and dimensions are illustrative.}

At <thermodynamic equilibrium>, the <chemical potentials> of exchangeable <water> are equal. Individual <water> <molecules> still move and cross in both directions, with equal mean opposing transfer rates and zero net <flux>. A stationary liquid level alone does not prove this: a blocked route can also give no visible transfer. <Selective permeability> provides a route; <water potential> supplies the direction, and neither alone determines the speed. The <cell wall>, <turgor pressure> and <plasmolysis> articles show why a living boundary's mechanical support and its <selective permeability> must be distinguished.

= Water potential
{parent=Osmosis}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Psi$}

<Water potential> expresses the <chemical potential> of <water> relative to a stated pure-<water> reference in units of <pressure>. For a nearly incompressible liquid at fixed <temperature>, use a constant reference <molar volume> $\bar V_w$ and write
$$
\Psi=\frac{\mu_w-\mu_w^{\mathrm{ref}}}{\bar V_w}.
$$
The reference is pure <water> at the same <temperature> and a chosen <pressure> $P_0$. Both sides must use the same reference. Neglecting height differences and interactions with solid surfaces, the approximation is
$$
\mu_w\simeq\mu_w^{\mathrm{ref}}+RT\ln a_w+\bar V_w(P-P_0),\qquad
\Psi\simeq(P-P_0)+\frac{RT}{\bar V_w}\ln a_w=\Psi_p+\Psi_s.
$$
Here $a_w$ is the dimensionless <thermodynamic activity> of <water>, $R$ the <molar gas constant>, and $T$ the absolute <temperature>. The <natural logarithm> is therefore applied to a dimensionless quantity. The <pressure> contribution $\Psi_p=P-P_0$ increases with <pressure>; the <solute potential> $\Psi_s$ is negative when $a_w<1$. Across an available passive water route, net <water> transfer favors higher to lower <water potential>. The familiar <plant> description adds effects of height and interactions with solid surfaces when they matter; they are omitted in the two liquid compartments here.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/30.5 Transport of Water and Solutes in Plants>[Biology 2e, §30.5, Water Potential, Solute Potential, Pressure Potential and Figure 30.32: component balance and direction of transfer]{ref}

Adding <solute> and raising <pressure> can thus cancel in <water potential> while each contribution remains nonzero. Equality of <water potential> does not imply equality of <pressure> or of every <solute> <concentration>. In a supported <plant> cell, the <cell wall> permits a positive <turgor pressure> to balance a negative <solute potential>; <plasmolysis> illustrates the opposite loss of support after <water> leaves.

= Solute potential
{parent=Water potential}
{title2=$\Psi_s$}

= Osmotic potential
{synonym}

<Solute potential> is the contribution to <water potential> from dissolved material, with pure <water> assigned zero. In the nearly incompressible description, $\Psi_s=(RT/\bar V_w)\ln a_w$. In a dilute ideal <chemical solution>, $\Psi_s\simeq-RTc_{\mathrm{osm}}$, where $c_{\mathrm{osm}}$ is the <osmotic concentration> of dissolved particles. Thus <solute potential> is negative while the corresponding <osmotic pressure> is positive: $\Psi_s\simeq-\Pi$. This sign difference distinguishes a contribution to <water potential> from the compensating mechanical <pressure>.<Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others/30.5 Transport of Water and Solutes in Plants>[Biology 2e, §30.5, Solute Potential: negative concentration contribution]{ref}

= Osmotic pressure
{parent=Osmosis}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Pi$}

<Osmotic pressure> is the excess <pressure> needed on a <chemical solution> to maintain osmotic <thermodynamic equilibrium> with pure <solvent> across a <semipermeable membrane>. For a nearly incompressible <solvent>, equality of <chemical potential> gives $\Pi\simeq-(RT/\bar V_w)\ln a_w$. It is an equilibrium property; an observed initial swelling rate is a different measurement.<IUPAC Compendium of Chemical Terminology, fifth edition (Gold Book) by International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry/osmotic pressure>[Gold Book, osmotic pressure: equilibrium definition, solvent activity formula and dilute ideal limit]{ref}

For a dilute ideal <chemical solution> of retained particles,
$$
\Pi=\frac{nRT}{V}=RTc_{\mathrm{osm}}.
$$
Here $n$ is the <amount of substance> of independently dissolved <solute> particles, measured in moles; $V$ is the final <volume> of <chemical solution>, not the starting <volume> of pure <solvent>. Count the resulting <ions> separately if a <solute> dissociates; do not multiply twice if $n$ already counts them. With $c_{\mathrm{osm}}$ in $\mathrm{mol\,m^{-3}}$ and $R$ in $\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$, $RTc_{\mathrm{osm}}$ has units $\mathrm{J\,m^{-3}}=\mathrm{Pa}$. This resembles the <ideal gas law>, although the particles here are dissolved in a liquid.

The assumptions are uniform <temperature>, a dilute ideal <chemical solution>, a nearly incompressible <solvent>, and a <semipermeable membrane> that retains the counted <solutes> while passing <solvent>. Association, incomplete dissociation and interactions change the relation between added material and independent particles; nonideal <thermodynamic activities> invalidate simple particle counting. The formula does not describe a concentrated brine merely because the apparatus contains a membrane.

A short derivation explains why particles are counted. In an ideal mixture, $a_w=x_w=n_w/(n_w+n)$, with $n_w$ the <amount of substance> of <water>. If $n\ll n_w$, then $-\ln x_w=\ln(1+n/n_w)\simeq n/n_w$ and $V\simeq n_w\bar V_w$. Substitution gives $\Pi\simeq RTn/V$. This is a dilute-limit approximation, not a claim that <water> <molecules> stop moving or that dissolved particles are a gas.

= Osmosis concentration-pressure example
{parent=Osmotic pressure}

Take two reservoirs separated by a <semipermeable membrane> that passes <water> and retains one neutral <solute>. Stipulate $T=300\,\mathrm K$, $c_A=10\,\mathrm{mol\,m^{-3}}$ and $c_B=30\,\mathrm{mol\,m^{-3}}$, equivalent to $10$ and $30\,\mathrm{mmol\,L^{-1}}$. Treat both <chemical solutions> as dilute and ideal. These are invented model conditions, not measurements or recommended biological conditions.

For <pressures> measured at the same height beside the membrane, define $\Delta P=P_B-P_A$. The model predicts
$$
\Psi_A-\Psi_B=RT(c_B-c_A)-\Delta P,\qquad
RT(c_B-c_A)\simeq49.89\,\mathrm{kPa}.
$$
At $\Delta P=0$, net <water> transfer favors A to B. At $\Delta P\simeq49.89\,\mathrm{kPa}$, the opposing <pressure> balances the <osmotic pressure> difference, giving zero net transfer with unequal <concentrations>. At $\Delta P=75\,\mathrm{kPa}$, the sign reverses and net <water> transfer favors B to A, toward the more dilute reservoir. Raising <pressure> changes the total <water potential>, rather than changing the membrane into a one-way gate.

\Image[osmosis-concentration-pressure-cases.png]
{title=Stipulated <concentration> and <pressure> conditions determine net <water> transfer}
{description=Original computed comparison for ideal retained <solute> at $300\,\mathrm K$, with A at $10\,\mathrm{mol\,m^{-3}}$ and B at $30\,\mathrm{mol\,m^{-3}}$. The horizontal axis is $P_B-P_A$; the vertical axis is $\Psi_A-\Psi_B$. Three marked cases show forward transfer, balance and reversed transfer. These are instantaneous reservoir conditions, not a time course or measured biological data; slope does not specify permeability or speed.}

Fixed <concentrations> require large maintained reservoirs or an instantaneous comparison. In a closed <membrane osmometer>, entering <water> causes <dilution> of the receiving <chemical solution> and changes its <volume>; the final balance must use final <concentrations>. A supported <cell wall> provides another way to develop the opposing <pressure>, as explained under <turgor pressure>.

= Membrane osmometer
{parent=Osmosis}
{wiki}

A <membrane osmometer> connects <osmosis> to a visible liquid level or a measured balancing <pressure>. In a teaching version, a <solute>-containing pouch is joined to a narrow vertical tube and submerged in external <water>. Entry increases the enclosed <volume> and raises the meniscus. Both the column and any stretched pouch can contribute an opposing <pressure>.

The University of Toronto's <Osmosis (Brine) by University of Toronto Department of Physics> demonstration (activity Me-012b, Description, steps 1–14) specifies soaking a dialysis membrane tube, tying one end closed and fastening the other around a thistle tube's narrow end. A stand holds the tube. Colored brine is cooled to room <temperature> and injected using a syringe to reduce bubbles; the pouch is completely immersed in a $1$ or $2\,\mathrm L$ beaker of distilled <water>. A taped marker records the initial level; subsequent meniscus height is the readout. The protocol proposes another pouch containing distilled <water> as a comparison, and its older instructions explicitly check seals for leaks.<Osmosis (Brine) by University of Toronto Department of Physics>[Osmosis (Brine), Description steps 1–14, water-filled comparison and Old Description A.2: apparatus, readout and leak check]{ref}

A rise is consistent with net <water> entry. To infer <osmotic pressure> quantitatively, use a retained <solute> and establish a stable equilibrium after accounting for <dilution>.
