If both free surfaces are at the same atmospheric <pressure>, the liquids have approximately equal <mass densities> $\rho$, and the pouch adds negligible mechanical load, the <hydrostatic pressure> difference at membrane height is approximately $\Delta P=\rho gh$, where $h$ is the meniscus height above the external surface and $g$ is <gravitational acceleration>. This is the liquid-column inference, not a pressure calibration supplied by the Toronto demonstration. For unequal <mass densities>, compare the two column pressures separately.

Useful additional controls are matched initial levels and geometry, constant <temperature>, replicate runs, and monitoring external <solute> to test retention. These are proposed checks, not reported Toronto results. Leaks, trapped bubbles, <surface tension> in the narrow tube, <evaporation> and membrane stretching can alter the level. In particular, a dialysis membrane may pass dissolved salt: the brine setup illustrates transient movement but cannot be assumed to retain every dissolved <ion>. Color improves visibility without proving salt retention. A stationary level can reflect weakening <concentration> differences or leakage rather than osmotic <thermodynamic equilibrium>; a rising level alone supplies neither $n$ nor a verified value of $nRT/V$.

= Osmotic concentration
{parent=Osmosis}
{wiki}
{title2=$c_{\mathrm{osm}}$}

= Osmolarity
{synonym}

In the dilute ideal particle-count description, <osmolarity> is the total <amount of substance> of independently dissolved <solute> particles per <volume> of <chemical solution>: $c_{\mathrm{osm}}=\sum_j n_j/V$. It includes both membrane-permeating and retained <solutes>; specifying a particular membrane is unnecessary to count particles. A neutral undissociated <solute> contributes one particle per <molecule>, whereas separated <ions> are counted individually. Common units are $\mathrm{osmol\,L^{-1}}$ or $\mathrm{mOsm\,L^{-1}}$, with one osmole denoting one mole of the counted particles. <Osmolality> uses mass of <solvent> instead of <volume> of <chemical solution>; the two numerical values should not be identified without the appropriate mass-to-volume conversion.<Osmosis, Cell Membranes-Structure and Transport, BYU-Idaho>[Cell Membranes—Structure and Transport, Osmosis: paragraphs beginning Earlier this semester and Osmolarity is a useful term; total particle counting]{ref}

This ideal counting definition states its scope: interactions can require a <thermodynamic activity> description of <osmotic pressure>. <Tonicity> asks a different question, namely how that surrounding <chemical solution> changes a specified <biological cell>'s <volume> through its particular <cell membrane>.

= Tonicity
{parent=Osmosis}
{wiki}

<Tonicity> describes how a surrounding <chemical solution> changes cellular <volume> through <osmosis>. It depends on which <solutes> remain effectively excluded on the timescale of interest, not simply on the amount of dissolved material. The following comparisons take a functional <cell membrane>, an initially unpressurized <biological cell>, and effectively excluded <solutes> as the reference; rising <turgor pressure> can oppose entry in a walled <biological cell>.

Operationally, <hypotonic solutions> cause sustained swelling, <hypertonic solutions> sustained shrinking, and <isotonic solutions> no sustained <volume> change in the specified passive comparison. State the reference <biological cell>, membrane properties and observation interval. A <permeating solute> can transiently change <water potential> while crossing; after its <chemical potential> equilibrates, its initial unequal <concentrations> cannot be treated as a permanently retained osmotic load. Thus equal <osmolarities> need not imply <isotonic solutions>. Conversely, a stationary walled <biological cell> may have unequal <osmolarities> balanced by <turgor pressure>.<Osmosis, Cell Membranes-Structure and Transport, BYU-Idaho>[Cell Membranes—Structure and Transport, Osmosis: paragraphs beginning Here is another way and final Note; permeating solutes and transient effects]{ref}

= Permeating solute
{parent=Tonicity}

A <permeating solute> can cross the specified membrane appreciably during the observation interval. This is a property of a <solute>-membrane-time comparison, not an immutable label attached to a <molecule>. Passage can reduce a <concentration> difference through <passive transport> while <water> is also moving; it does not require <water> to wait.

For an original dilute ideal example, stipulate a flexible model <biological cell> with $20\,\mathrm{mmol\,L^{-1}}$ retained particles and an external reservoir with $10\,\mathrm{mmol\,L^{-1}}$ retained particles plus $10\,\mathrm{mmol\,L^{-1}}$ freely permeating neutral <solute>. Initially the total <osmolarities> are equal. Suppose the permeating <solute> eventually has equal internal and external <concentrations>, there is negligible <pressure> difference, no <active transport>, and the internal retained particle amount is conserved. The permeating contributions cancel from the final osmotic balance; the internal retained <concentration> must fall from $20$ to $10\,\mathrm{mmol\,L^{-1}}$. The required <volume> is twice its initial value. This illustrates an initially equal-<osmolarity> but <hypotonic solution>; it assumes the boundary can survive that expansion and predicts no universal time course.

= Hypotonic solution
{parent=Tonicity}

A <hypotonic solution> favors initial net <water> entry under the reference conditions above. A susceptible <biological cell> may undergo <osmotic lysis>; a sufficiently strong <cell wall> can instead support <turgor pressure>.

= Hypertonic solution
{parent=Tonicity}

A <hypertonic solution> favors initial net <water> loss under the reference conditions above. In a walled <plant> cell, enough loss can produce <plasmolysis>.

= Isotonic solution
{parent=Tonicity}

An <isotonic solution> produces no sustained change in cellular <volume> under the stated comparison conditions. <Water> can still exchange in both directions.

= Cytolysis
{parent=Osmosis}
{wiki}

= Osmotic lysis
{synonym}

<Osmotic lysis> is rupture after excessive <water> entry and swelling. It is an outcome for susceptible <biological cells>, not an inevitable response of every <biological cell> to a <hypotonic solution>. <Cell walls> can bear the resulting load, and cellular mechanisms can regulate <water> balance. A <protoplast> challenge tests a particular preparation under particular conditions.

= Active transport
{parent=Membrane transport}
{wiki}

<Active transport> moves a substance uphill in its <electrochemical gradient> by coupling the transfer to a downhill process. Some <carrier proteins> use <ATP> <hydrolysis>; others couple uphill transfer of one <solute> to downhill transfer of another <solute>, often an <ion>. The latter mechanism uses a stored <electrochemical gradient> and need not consume <ATP> directly at that <carrier protein>. Maintaining the driving <electrochemical gradient> still requires a source of <energy>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26896/[Alberts et al.: carrier proteins and active membrane transport].

For the neutral 8:2 example, B-to-A transfer costs $RT\ln4\simeq1.38629RT$ per mole. As an explicitly assumed bookkeeping example, suppose a tightly coupled cycle supplies a downhill <free energy> change of $-2RT$ per mole of transferred <solute>. The total is then $\Delta G_{\mathrm{cycle}}=RT\ln4-2RT\simeq-0.61371RT$. This makes forward cycling energetically possible under those assumptions; it specifies neither a molecular mechanism nor a rate. Merely adding a <carrier protein> cannot supply the missing <free energy>. Conversely, as the transported <concentration> ratio rises, the required uphill cost rises, so a fixed driving process has a finite thermodynamic limit.

= Exocytosis
{parent=Membrane transport}
{wiki}

<Exocytosis> releases the contents of a membrane-bounded compartment to the extracellular space by fusion with the <cell membrane>. <Synaptic vesicle> fusion is an example. Its contents are released, while its membrane becomes connected to the <cell membrane>; see <synaptic vesicle> for recovery and reuse.

= Endocytosis
{parent=Membrane transport}
{wiki}

<Endocytosis> takes material into a <biological cell> by inward budding and separation of part of its <cell membrane>. At a <chemical synapse>, it helps recover membrane and <proteins> after <synaptic vesicle> <exocytosis>. This recovery is distinct from uptake of individual <neurotransmitter> <molecules> through <membrane transport proteins>.

= Fluid mosaic model
{parent=Cell membrane}
{wiki}

The <fluid mosaic model> describes a <cell membrane> as a fluid <lipid bilayer> with embedded <membrane proteins>. It connects a continuous barrier to a mosaic of functional components: <diffusion> within the membrane can coexist with selective passage across it. S. J. Singer and G. L. Nicolson presented the <fluid mosaic model> in Science in 1972; see https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/4333397/[Singer and Nicolson, The fluid mosaic model of the structure of cell membranes]. The useful picture does not require every <protein> to move freely: attachments and interactions can restrict movement, as discussed in https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9898/[Cooper, The Cell: structure of the plasma membrane].

= Cytoplasm
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

The <cytoplasm> is the material inside a <cell membrane> but outside the <cell nucleus>, when one is present. It includes the <cytosol>, the <organelles> suspended in it, and other structures; it is not just a name for the liquid. In a <prokaryote>, which has no <cell nucleus>, the <nucleoid> and <ribosomes> are within the <cytoplasm>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-3-eukaryotic-cells[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 4.3: cytoplasm and cytosol] and https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-2-prokaryotic-cells[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 4.2: prokaryotic organization].

\Image[eukaryotic-cell-compartments.png]
{title=<Cell nucleus>, <cytosol> and <organelles> in a nucleated <eukaryotic> <biological cell>}
{description=Original schematic: the pale blue region represents <cytosol>, while the surrounding <cytoplasm> also includes the depicted <mitochondrion> and <ribosomes>. The <cell nucleus> and <cell nucleolus> are excluded from the <cytoplasm>. A <nuclear pore complex> provides a route across the double <nuclear envelope>. Dimensions and component counts are illustrative; many structures are omitted.}

Try classifying four positions in the diagram. A point in the pale blue region belongs to both <cytosol> and <cytoplasm>. A point inside the <mitochondrion> belongs to an <organelle> included in the <cytoplasm>, but not to the surrounding <cytosol>. A point on a free <ribosome> belongs to the <cytoplasm>, but the <ribosome> itself is not <cytosol>. A point inside the <cell nucleus> belongs to neither <cytoplasm> nor <cytosol>. These distinctions prevent the misleading inference that everything outside the <cell nucleus> shares one undivided liquid environment.

The usefulness of the <cytoplasm> is this combination of shared surroundings and specialized structures: <enzymes> in the <cytosol> carry out many steps of <metabolism>, while <organelles> maintain their own internal conditions. A soluble <protein> made by a free <ribosome> may work in the <cytosol> or be transported elsewhere, including into the <cell nucleus>. Cellular organization therefore combines separation with exchange, rather than assigning each <protein> permanently to its place of synthesis. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26932/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell (2002): traffic between nucleus and cytosol].

= Cytosol
{parent=Cytoplasm}
{wiki}

The <cytosol> is the aqueous part of the <cytoplasm> surrounding its structures, including <organelles>. It contains <water>, <ions> and dissolved <molecules>, including many <proteins>; it is a crowded working environment rather than pure <water>. The liquid inside a <cell nucleus> or a membrane-bounded <organelle> is not the surrounding <cytosol>.

= Nucleoid
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

A <nucleoid> is the region occupied by <chromosome> <DNA> in a <prokaryotic> <biological cell>. It has no surrounding <nuclear envelope>, but its <DNA> is organized rather than freely spread at random. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-2-prokaryotic-cells[OpenStax Biology 2e, 4.2: the nucleoid].

= Organelle
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

An <organelle> is a specialized subcellular structure. Usage varies over whether non-membrane-bounded structures such as <ribosomes> count as <organelles>, so explicitly say membrane-bounded <organelles> when comparing <eukaryotic> compartmentalization with <prokaryotic> organization. <Mitochondria> and <chloroplasts> are membrane-bounded examples. See https://openstax.org/books/microbiology/pages/3-4-unique-characteristics-of-eukaryotic-cells[OpenStax Microbiology, 3.4: cellular compartments].

= Plastid
{parent=Organelle}
{wiki}

A <plastid> is an <organelle> of the lineage that includes <chloroplasts>. A <chloroplast> is a photosynthetic <plastid>; the terms are not interchangeable because some <plastids> are nonphotosynthetic. <Endosymbiotic theory> connects <plastid> origins to <cyanobacteria>, while <endosymbiotic gene transfer> and <organelle protein import> explain genetic and functional integration with the host.

= Mitochondria
{parent=Organelle}
{wiki}

= Mitochondrion
{synonym}

A <mitochondrion> is an <organelle> whose inner membrane houses machinery for much of aerobic <cellular respiration> and <ATP> production in typical <eukaryotic> cells. <Mitochondria> descend from a bacterial <endosymbiosis> and retain a <genome> and <ribosomes>, although many required <proteins> are encoded by nuclear <genes>. This combination connects compartmentalized function to <evolution>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/23-1-eukaryotic-origins[OpenStax Biology 2e, 23.1: mitochondrial ancestry].

= Chloroplast
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

A <chloroplast> is a compartment in photosynthetic <plant> and <algae> <biological cells>. Its internal <thylakoids> carry out <light-dependent reactions>; its surrounding <chloroplast stroma> contains the <enzymes> of the <Calvin cycle>. <Cyanobacteria> perform <oxygenic photosynthesis> without <chloroplasts>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26819/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: chloroplasts and photosynthesis].

= Chloroplast stroma
{parent=Chloroplast}

The <chloroplast stroma> is the fluid compartment inside the <chloroplast> envelope but outside its <thylakoids>. The <Calvin cycle> operates here; the interior of a <thylakoid> is a separate compartment.

= Thylakoid
{parent=Chloroplast}
{wiki}

A <thylakoid> is a flattened membrane-bounded compartment. In a <chloroplast>, its membrane contains the machinery for <light-dependent reactions>, including <chlorophyll>. Stacks of <thylakoids> pack membrane area into a small space within the <chloroplast>; the <light-dependent reactions> schematic simplifies this architecture.

= Cell nucleus
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

The <cell nucleus> is a membrane-bounded compartment characteristic of <eukaryotic> <biological cells>, containing most of the cellular <DNA> in a typical nucleated <biological cell>. Its double <nuclear envelope> separates this <DNA> from the surrounding <cytoplasm>. Most does not mean all: <mitochondria> and <chloroplasts> retain their own <DNA>. Nor does every specialized <eukaryotic> <biological cell> retain a <cell nucleus>: mature mammalian <red blood cells> have lost theirs. <Prokaryotes> organize their <DNA> in a <nucleoid> without a <nuclear envelope>, and still have <ribosomes>. See https://www.genome.gov/genetics-glossary/Nucleus[NHGRI: nucleus], https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/23-1-eukaryotic-origins[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 23.1: organelle DNA]{ref} and https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-2-components-of-the-blood[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 40.2: mature mammalian red blood cells].

Follow one nuclear <protein>-coding <gene>: its <DNA> is used in <transcription> to make <RNA>; after the appropriate processing, <messenger RNA> is exported through a <nuclear pore complex>; a <ribosome> in the <cytoplasm> then uses it in <biological translation> to build an <amino acid> chain. The <DNA> remains in the <cell nucleus>. The <RNA> is a template, not the material converted into a <protein>. The <ribosome> diagram below follows this route; <gene expression> gives a complementary <nucleotide sequence> example. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26932/[Alberts et al.: selective RNA export and protein import].

This architecture makes <gene expression> interesting in a spatial way: nuclear <transcription> and cytoplasmic <biological translation> are separated by a selective boundary. A <messenger RNA> can be processed before export and use, while <proteins> needed for nuclear functions travel inward. Inside the <cell nucleus>, the <cell nucleolus> helps assemble <ribosome> subunits for export. Thus the compartment housing most <DNA> also helps build the machinery that interprets its messages elsewhere.

Robert Brown described and named the <cell nucleus> in his account of <plant> <biological cells>, read to the Linnean Society in 1831 and published in its Transactions in 1833. This historical observation identified a recurring structure, without establishing its later-understood role in <DNA> storage or <gene expression>. See https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1095-8339.1829.tb00158.x[Brown, On the Organs and Mode of Fecundation in Orchideæ and Asclepiadeæ, Transactions 16, 685–738 (1833)]. This is a documented contribution rather than a claim that no one had observed the structure earlier.

= Nuclear envelope
{parent=Cell nucleus}
{wiki}

The <nuclear envelope> is the double-membrane boundary around a <cell nucleus>. Regulated passage connects nuclear processes to the surrounding <cytoplasm>: <transcription> produces <RNA> in the <cell nucleus>, while exported <messenger RNA> can undergo <biological translation> on <ribosomes> outside it. See https://www.genome.gov/genetics-glossary/Nuclear-Membrane[NHGRI: nuclear membrane].

= Nuclear pore complex
{parent=Nuclear envelope}
{wiki}

= Nuclear pore
{synonym}

A <nuclear pore complex> is an assembly of <proteins> spanning the <nuclear envelope> and regulating passage between the <cell nucleus> and <cytosol>. It is a selective gateway, not simply an unregulated hole: <messenger RNA> export and import of nuclear <proteins> require appropriate transport machinery. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26932/[Alberts et al.: nuclear pore complexes].

= Nucleolus
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Cell nucleus}

= Cell nucleolus
{synonym}

The <cell nucleolus> is a region inside a <cell nucleus> without a surrounding membrane, where much <ribosomal RNA> is made and processed and <ribosome> subunits begin to assemble with <proteins>. The subunits are exported to the <cytoplasm>; the <cell nucleolus> is not itself a translating <ribosome>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-3-eukaryotic-cells[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 4.3: nucleolus and ribosomal subunits].

= Ribosome
{parent=Biological cell}
{wiki}

A <ribosome> is the molecular machinery for <biological translation>: it reads <messenger RNA> and joins <amino acids> into a <polypeptide> that can become a <protein>. Both <prokaryotes> and <eukaryotes> have <ribosomes>. A <ribosome> consists of <ribosomal RNA> and <proteins> organized into small and large subunits, with no enclosing membrane. <Ribosomal RNA> contributes to both structure and the <active site> that forms <peptide bonds>; a <ribosome> is therefore not merely a collection of <proteins>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-3-eukaryotic-cells[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 4.3: ribosome composition] and https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10937990/[Nissen et al., The structural basis of ribosome activity in peptide bond synthesis, Science 289, 920–930 (2000)].

\Image[ribosome-translation-schematic.png]
{title=Nuclear <transcription>, <RNA> export and <ribosome>-mediated <biological translation>}
{description=Original schematic of a nuclear <protein>-coding <gene> in a nucleated <eukaryotic> <biological cell>. The upper route tracks <DNA> to <RNA> by <transcription>, <RNA> export, then <biological translation>. The lower example reads three <messenger RNA> <codons> from $5'$ to $3'$ and shows a growing <polypeptide> segment from its <N-terminus> toward its <C-terminus>. Shapes and distances are illustrative; processing, flanking signals, additional machinery and termination are omitted.}

For a computed decoding example, choose the <reading frame> starting at the first displayed <nucleotide> of $5'\text{-}\mathrm{AUG\ GCU\ GAA}\text{-}3'$. Under the standard <genetic code>, AUG specifies <methionine> (Met), GCU specifies <alanine> (Ala), and GAA specifies a <glutamic acid residue> (Glu) in the growing <polypeptide>. Nine <nucleotides> divide into three <codons>, giving the ordered three-<amino acid residue> segment Met–Ala–Glu. The <ribosome> reads the <messenger RNA> $5'\to3'$, and the chain grows from its <N-terminus> toward its <C-terminus>. If synthesis begins at this AUG in an appropriate <eukaryotic> initiation context, these are the first three <amino acid residues> before any subsequent processing. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/15-1-the-genetic-code[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 15.1: standard codon assignments].

For this three-<amino acid residue> segment, joining the three delivered <amino acids> requires two <peptide bonds>, one between Met and Ala and another between Ala and Glu. There is no <stop codon> in the displayed <nucleotide sequence>, so this is a growing segment rather than a complete released <protein>. An isolated nine-<nucleotide> <RNA> is not claimed to supply all signals needed for initiation. This small calculation separates three jobs: the <messenger RNA> specifies order, <transfer RNA> delivers the <amino acids>, and the <ribosome> catalyzes their joining. Each new <amino acid residue> is added at the <C-terminus>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/15-5-ribosomes-and-protein-synthesis[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 15.5: initiation and chain elongation].

The beauty of a <ribosome> is its reusable chemistry: the same machinery can assemble many different <proteins> by following different <messenger RNA> templates. The <genetic code> connects an alphabet of <nucleotides> to an alphabet of <amino acids>, while <transfer RNA> links recognition of the message to delivery of building material. Changing the message can therefore change the product without building a different <ribosome> for every <protein>.

In 1955 George E. Palade described small particles in the <cytoplasm>, both free and associated with membranes, in https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2223592/[A small particulate component of the cytoplasm, Journal of Biophysical and Biochemical Cytology 1, 59–68]. The paper documents the structural observations, rather than the full modern decoding mechanism. Complementary biochemical work by Hoagland, Stephenson, Scott, Hecht and Zamecnik described a soluble <RNA> intermediate in <protein> synthesis in 1958, helping establish the role now assigned to <transfer RNA>; see https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/13538965/[A soluble ribonucleic acid intermediate in protein synthesis, Journal of Biological Chemistry 231, 241–257]. Together, structural observations and biochemical experiments connected visible cellular machinery with its working parts.

= Gliding bacterium
{parent=Biological cell}

A <gliding bacterium> moves along a surface without swimming through the bulk fluid. In a thin-fluid-film model, its relative motion dissipates energy through <Couette flow>, although the actuation mechanism can involve additional dissipation.

= Neutrophil
{parent=Biological cell}

A <neutrophil> is an immune <biological cell>. Its adhesive rolling on a surface involves contact mechanics, fluid stresses, and binding and unbinding of adhesive bonds.

= Red blood cell
{parent=Biological cell}

A red blood cell is a specialized <biological cell> that transports <oxygen> in blood. Its deformability lets it pass through vessels narrower than its unstressed size. A compliant-body <lubrication> model isolates the coupling between its deformation, the surrounding viscous film and a driving <pressure> difference; it is an idealization rather than a complete membrane or cytoplasm model.

= Membrane potential
{parent=Biological cell}
{title2=$V=V_{\mathrm{inside}}-V_{\mathrm{outside}}$}
{wiki}

The voltage difference across a cell membrane. Ion concentration gradients and selective membrane conductances determine it; changing conductance can change the voltage.

= Depolarization
{parent=Membrane potential}
{wiki}

For a negative neuronal <membrane potential>, <depolarization> raises it toward zero or beyond, making the interior less negative relative to the exterior. This describes a voltage change, not necessarily increased <action potential> generation; see the depolarizing inhibition example under <synaptic potential>.

= Hyperpolarization
{parent=Membrane potential}

<Hyperpolarization> makes a neuronal <membrane potential> more negative relative to the exterior. It is a voltage description; inhibition need not produce <hyperpolarization>, as <shunting inhibition> illustrates.

= Reversal potential
{parent=Membrane potential}
{wiki}
{title2=$I=g(E_{\mathrm{rev}}-V)$}

A <reversal potential> $E_{\mathrm{rev}}$ is a <membrane potential> at which a specified open <ion channel>, or specified combination of <ion channels>, carries zero net <electric current>, with the surrounding conditions fixed; the <electric current> changes sign across that voltage. Specifying which current matters: an individual <ion> contribution and a sum of several contributions can reverse at different voltages. Zero net <electric current> does not mean that all <ions> have stopped moving. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK11013/[Purves et al., Neuroscience, 2nd edition (2001): reversal of a mixed-ion channel current].

Throughout these examples, <membrane potential> is $V=V_{\mathrm{inside}}-V_{\mathrm{outside}}$, and \b[<conventional current> is inward-positive]. Positive <electric current> corresponds to net positive <electric charge> entering or net negative <electric charge> leaving. Negative <electric current> corresponds to the reverse charge transfer. A convenient Ohmic approximation is
$$
I_{\mathrm{in}}=g(E_{\mathrm{rev}}-V),\qquad g\geq0,
$$
where $g$ is the available <electrical conductance>. For fixed $g>0$, $V<E_{\mathrm{rev}}$ gives positive <electric current>, $V=E_{\mathrm{rev}}$ gives zero, and $V>E_{\mathrm{rev}}$ gives negative <electric current>; the line has slope $-g$. If $g=0$, the modeled pathway carries zero <electric current> at every voltage: closing it does not locate its <reversal potential>. <Neuronal Dynamics: From Single Neurons to Networks and Models of Cognition by Wulfram Gerstner and others/3.1 Synapses> (§3.1: conductance-based synaptic current) uses outward-positive $g(V-E)$; the formula here reverses that sign to match this wiki's inward-positive convention.

For the stipulated single-pathway example from <ion channel>, $E_{\mathrm{rev}}=-80\,\mathrm{mV}$ and $g=10\,\mathrm{nS}$ give
$$
\begin{array}{c|rrr}
V\ (\mathrm{mV})&-100&-80&-60\\
E_{\mathrm{rev}}-V\ (\mathrm{mV})&20&0&-20\\
I_{\mathrm{in}}\ (\mathrm{pA})&200&0&-200
\end{array}
$$
The open <ion channel> carries zero net <electric current> at $-80\,\mathrm{mV}$ even though $g$ remains positive. Near that point a change of <membrane potential> changes the <electric current>, which is why zero current at one voltage does not make an open pathway electrically irrelevant; see <shunting inhibition>.

\Image[ion-channel-current-and-reversal.png]
{title=Inward-positive <electric current>: one reversal and two-channel cancellation}
{description=Original computed curves for stipulated constant <electrical conductances> and fixed <reversal potentials>. Left: $g=10\,\mathrm{nS}$, $E_{\mathrm{rev}}=-80\,\mathrm{mV}$. Right: a <potassium> pathway with $g_{\mathrm K}=10\,\mathrm{nS}$, $E_{\mathrm K}=-80\,\mathrm{mV}$ and a <sodium> pathway with $g_{\mathrm{Na}}=2\,\mathrm{nS}$, $E_{\mathrm{Na}}=+50\,\mathrm{mV}$. Their net <electric current> vanishes at $-58.3333\,\mathrm{mV}$ while both individual <electric currents> remain nonzero. These are model values, not empirical measurements.}

For an <ion channel> passing only one <ion> species by <passive transport>, its <reversal potential> can equal that species' <Nernst equilibrium potential>, where the chemical and electrical contributions to <electrochemical potential> balance. At that <thermodynamic equilibrium>, passage in both directions can continue with no net transport. For an <ion channel> admitting several <ion> species, opposing contributions can cancel while each species continues to have net transport. Such a <reversal potential> is not generally any one species' <Nernst equilibrium potential>. See the Purves reference above.

To work out that cancellation explicitly, put two Ohmic <ion channel> populations across the same <cell membrane>. Stipulate constant <electrical conductances> and fixed <reversal potentials>:
$$
g_{\mathrm K}=10\,\mathrm{nS},\quad E_{\mathrm K}=-80\,\mathrm{mV},\qquad g_{\mathrm{Na}}=2\,\mathrm{nS},\quad E_{\mathrm{Na}}=+50\,\mathrm{mV}.
$$
The <potassium> and <sodium> contributions add because both pathways experience the same <membrane potential>:
$$
\begin{aligned}
I_{\mathrm{total}}&=g_{\mathrm K}(E_{\mathrm K}-V)+g_{\mathrm{Na}}(E_{\mathrm{Na}}-V)\\
&=(g_{\mathrm K}+g_{\mathrm{Na}})(E_{\mathrm{eff}}-V),\\
E_{\mathrm{eff}}&=\frac{g_{\mathrm K}E_{\mathrm K}+g_{\mathrm{Na}}E_{\mathrm{Na}}}{g_{\mathrm K}+g_{\mathrm{Na}}}
=\frac{-700}{12}\,\mathrm{mV}\approx-58.3333\,\mathrm{mV}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus $I_{\mathrm{total}}=12\,\mathrm{nS}(E_{\mathrm{eff}}-V)$. This <weighted mean> follows simply by expanding and regrouping the two current terms; it assumes additive Ohmic contributions and a positive total <electrical conductance>. At $V=E_{\mathrm{eff}}$, the inward-positive individual <electric currents> are
$$
I_{\mathrm K}=-\frac{650}{3}\,\mathrm{pA}\approx-216.6667\,\mathrm{pA},\qquad
I_{\mathrm{Na}}=+\frac{650}{3}\,\mathrm{pA}\approx+216.6667\,\mathrm{pA}.
$$
The <potassium> contribution is outward and the <sodium> contribution inward. Equal and opposite <electric currents> give $I_{\mathrm{total}}=0$; neither pathway has stopped transporting its <ions>. The calculation treats two distinct pathways. Applying it to one mixed-species <ion channel> would additionally require a justified decomposition into independent Ohmic contributions; a general mixed-channel <reversal potential> cannot be obtained by assuming those hypotheses silently.

This is an instantaneous balance of the specified passive <electric currents>. It would be the stationary voltage of a passive <capacitance> model containing only these constant pathways and no applied <electric current>, but it is not a complete prediction of a living cell's resting <membrane potential>. Other <electric currents>, voltage-dependent gating, and pumps involved in <active transport> can matter. Pumps that transfer net <electric charge> are electrogenic and contribute their own <electric current>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK10857/[Purves et al.: functional properties of the sodium/potassium pump].

The toy model holds <ion> <concentrations> and therefore the stipulated <reversal potentials> fixed. Continuing inward <sodium> and outward <potassium> transport would change finite reservoirs unless <active transport> or external control maintained them. Transport can be a small fraction of the reservoir's <ions> over a short interval, making fixed <concentrations> a useful approximation; it is not a claim that the reservoirs never change. See https://neuronaldynamics.epfl.ch/online/Ch2.S1.html[Gerstner et al., Neuronal Dynamics (2014), §2.1: equilibrium potentials, channel currents and maintained gradients].

A <reversal potential> makes the effect of opening an <ion channel> predictable from the present <membrane potential>. The same open pathway can carry inward or outward <electric current> as $V$ crosses its <reversal potential>. The two-pathway example reveals a particularly useful distinction: zero net charge transfer can coexist with continuing exchange of different substances. That distinction connects <membrane transport> to <electric current> measurements without mistaking electrical balance for <thermodynamic equilibrium>.

= Nernst equilibrium potential
{c}
{parent=Reversal potential}
{title2=$E_{\mathrm{ion}}=\frac{RT}{zF}\ln\!\left(\frac{a_{\mathrm{outside}}}{a_{\mathrm{inside}}}\right)$}

The <Nernst equilibrium potential> is the inside-minus-outside <membrane potential> at which one <ion> species has equal <electrochemical potential> on both sides of a <cell membrane>. At uniform <temperature> $T$, using the same reference <chemical potential> on both sides, $z\neq0$ is the signed <ion> charge number, $R$ the <molar gas constant>, $F$ the <Faraday constant>, and $a$ the dimensionless <thermodynamic activity> of that <ion>. For an ideal dilute <chemical solution>, the activity ratio becomes the <concentration> ratio. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK11102/[Purves et al.: the forces that create membrane potentials and the Nernst equation].

The short derivation is to set the outside-to-inside <electrochemical potential> change from <membrane transport> to zero: $RT\ln(a_{\mathrm{inside}}/a_{\mathrm{outside}})+zFV=0$, and solve for $V$. An open pathway carrying only this species by <passive transport> then has this <reversal potential>. A balance of several nonzero <ion> <electric currents> does not establish this equilibrium condition for each species.

= Voltage-gated ion channel
{parent=Membrane potential}
{wiki}

A membrane channel whose opening depends on <membrane potential>. Regenerative activation and subsequent inactivation or opposing currents help produce an <action potential>.

= Two-state ion-channel gating
{parent=Voltage-gated ion channel}
{title2=$\dot X=\alpha_X(V)(1-X)-\beta_X(V)X$}

<Two-state ion-channel gating> models an individual gate as a random switch between closed and open states. Write $p(t)$ for its open <probability>; this is the $X$ in the header equation. An individual gate's state is always closed or open, rather than partly open. A population of identical gates can have a fraction open near $p(t)$, and averaging repeated identically prepared trials estimates the same <probability>. A finite population's observed fraction fluctuates. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK10841/[Purves and colleagues, Neuroscience, 2nd edition (2001): Ion Channels Underlying Action Potentials] for single-channel steps and their population averages.

At fixed <membrane potential> $V$, let constant rates $\alpha,\beta>0$ govern closed-to-open and open-to-closed transitions respectively, in $\mathrm{s}^{-1}$. This is a two-state <continuous-time Markov chain>. Over a short interval $\Delta t$, an initially closed gate opens with <probability> $\alpha\Delta t+o(\Delta t)$, and an initially open gate closes with <probability> $\beta\Delta t+o(\Delta t)$. Gain minus loss gives the deterministic <ordinary differential equation> for the <probability>:
$$
\frac{dp}{dt}=\alpha(1-p)-\beta p=\frac{p_\infty-p}{\tau},\qquad
p_\infty=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta},\qquad \tau=\frac{1}{\alpha+\beta}.
$$
Here $p_\infty$ is the fixed-voltage limiting open <probability>, and $\tau$ is a <time constant>. The ratio of the rates fixes the limit, while their sum fixes the speed. With initial <probability> $p(0)=p_0\in[0,1]$,
$$
p(t)=p_\infty+(p_0-p_\infty)e^{-t/\tau}.
$$
For a short derivation, subtract $p_\infty$: the deviation obeys $d(p-p_\infty)/dt=-(p-p_\infty)/\tau$, yielding the <exponential function>. After one <time constant>, $1/e$ of the initial deviation remains. The solution stays between $p_0$ and $p_\infty$, so it remains a valid <probability>. The rate and <time constant> formulations are described in https://neuronaldynamics.epfl.ch/online/Ch2.S2.html[Gerstner and colleagues, Neuronal Dynamics, §2.2.2: transition rates and channel kinetics].

For a worked toy condition A, choose $\alpha=\beta=50\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$ and $p_0=0.1$. Then $p_\infty=0.5$, $\tau=0.01\,\mathrm{s}=10\,\mathrm{ms}$, and
$$
p(10\,\mathrm{ms})=0.5-0.4e^{-1}\approx0.352848.
$$
In a separately prepared condition B, choose $\alpha=200\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$, $\beta=50\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$ and the same $p_0=0.1$. Now $p_\infty=0.8$, $\tau=4\,\mathrm{ms}$, and $p(10\,\mathrm{ms})=0.8-0.7e^{-2.5}\approx0.742540$. Condition B approaches a higher limit more quickly. These are two stipulated constant-rate conditions with $V$ held fixed in each experiment, not an empirical function relating rates to voltage or a sequential switch between conditions. The initial $p_0=0.1$ need not be the limiting <probability> at the newly imposed voltage.

\Image[ion-channel-gating-relaxation.png]
{title=<Two-state ion-channel gating>: two constant-rate relaxation conditions}
{description=Original computed <probability> curves, both starting at $p_0=0.1$. Dashed lines show the limiting open <probabilities> $0.5$ and $0.8$; dotted lines mark <time constants> $10$ and $4\,\mathrm{ms}$. The dot marks condition A at $10\,\mathrm{ms}$, where $p\approx0.352848$. Smooth curves represent open <probability> or population averages, not an individual gate's random state trace or an ionic <electric current>.}

In the <Hodgkin-Huxley model>, the rates depend on <membrane potential>: $\alpha_X(V)$ and $\beta_X(V)$. Its $X_\infty(V)=\alpha_X(V)/(\alpha_X(V)+\beta_X(V))$ is the limit for voltage held at that $V$. During an <action potential>, changing $V$ changes the rates and gates feed back onto $V$ through ionic <electric currents>; substituting the present voltage into the constant-rate solution does not solve that coupled system. Gating describes availability of a conducting pathway. Even an open <ion channel> carries zero net ionic <electric current> at its <reversal potential>.

The useful bridge is from abrupt random molecular events to a smooth population prediction. A channel requiring several <independent permissive gates in an ion channel> has an open <probability> equal to their product; this can make channel availability much smaller than each gate's availability. The <Hodgkin-Huxley model> uses $\bar g_{\mathrm{Na}}m^3h$ and $\bar g_{\mathrm K}n^4$ for <sodium> and <potassium> <electrical conductances>, respectively.

= Independent permissive gates in an ion channel
{parent=Two-state ion-channel gating}
{title2=$P_{\mathrm{open}}=p^q$}

A permissive gate allows conduction when it is open. Suppose an <ion channel> conducts only when all $q$ gates are open, where $q$ is a positive <integer>. If the gate-open <events> are mutually <independent events> and each has the same open <probability> $p$ at the time considered, then
$$
P_{\mathrm{open}}=\prod_{j=1}^q p=p^q.
$$
This follows from the multiplication rule for <independent events>. Identical transition rates alone do not guarantee independence: the initial gate states must also be independently prepared, and the model must not couple their transitions. Gates sharing a fluctuating <membrane potential> can be correlated; the simple product requires an appropriate independence assumption, for example independent gates at an externally fixed voltage.

At $p=0.5$, channels requiring $q=1,2,4$ permissive gates have open <probabilities> $0.5$, $0.25$ and $0.0625$. For four independent gates there are $2^4=16$ possible open/closed configurations, equally probable at $p=0.5$; only the all-open configuration conducts. Thus $0.5$ is the gate-open <probability>, while $0.0625$ is the whole-channel open <probability>. Neither is a fractional state of one observed channel. Among $160$ independently prepared identical four-gate channels, the <expected value> of the open count is $160/16=10$, although an actual count fluctuates.

\Image[independent-ion-channel-gates.png]
{title=<Independent permissive gates in an ion channel>: an all-open requirement}
{description=Left: whole-channel open <probability> $p^q$ for one, two and four identical independent permissive gates. Right: all sixteen configurations of four gates at $p=0.5$, each with <probability> $1/16$; filled circles are open gates and empty circles closed gates. Only the highlighted all-open configuration permits conduction. This is an illustrative model, not a molecular structure or an ionic <electric current> measurement.}

For unequal independent gate-open <probabilities> $p_j$, use $P_{\mathrm{open}}=\prod_j p_j$ rather than $p^q$. This is the distinction behind the <Hodgkin-Huxley model>'s $m^3h$: $m$ and $h$ are different activation and inactivation-availability variables, rather than one common $p$. Its $n^4$ uses four identical activation factors. Independence and the all-open requirement are model assumptions; correlated gates or alternative conducting configurations require a different calculation. The simple product is useful because a modest gate-open <probability> can produce sharply suppressed whole-channel availability without any additional gate interaction.

= Metabolism
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

The network of <chemical reactions> that maintains a living system, supplies energy and builds or processes its material. Gas emissions can arise as <primary metabolic byproducts> or <secondary metabolic byproducts>, with different implications for an <atmospheric biosignature gas>.

<Enzymes> make this network operate on biologically useful timescales. Following material through successive <substrates> and <reaction products> explains why a single fast <enzyme> need not make the entire network fast: another step may constrain the flow. Changing <enzyme> amounts or activity therefore changes which routes can carry material, connecting molecular <chemical kinetics> to the maintenance and growth of a <biological cell>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26911/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell: multienzyme complexes and cell metabolism].

= Ethanol
{parent=Metabolism}
{tag=Chemical compound}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{C}_2\mathrm{H}_5\mathrm{OH}$}

<Ethanol> is the <chemical compound> produced by the <alcoholic fermentation> described under <Saccharomyces cerevisiae>. Its two <carbon> <chemical atoms> explain the product bookkeeping there. See https://webbook.nist.gov/cgi/cbook.cgi?ID=C64175&Mask=4[NIST Chemistry WebBook: ethanol formula and identity]; the Biology 2e section cited under <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> identifies <ethanol> as the alcohol product.

= Fermentation
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

In its biochemical sense, <fermentation> is <metabolism> that obtains <ATP> from breakdown of organic <substrates> without a respiratory electron transport chain. The alcoholic route is one example; not every <fermentation> produces <ethanol>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/7-5-metabolism-without-oxygen[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 7.5: fermentation versus anaerobic respiration].

= Ethanol fermentation
{parent=Fermentation}
{wiki}

= Alcoholic fermentation
{synonym}

<Alcoholic fermentation> produces <ethanol> and <carbon dioxide> from <sugars> through an <enzyme>-catalyzed pathway. See <Saccharomyces cerevisiae> for its ideal <glucose> balance and <yeast> for the distinction between living cells and active extracts.

= Glycogen
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Glycogen> is a highly branched <polysaccharide> of <glucose> units, serving as a storage form of <glucose> in animals. Liver and muscle provide familiar examples. The classical <second messenger> experiments measured regulation of an <enzyme> involved in <glycogen> breakdown, <glycogen phosphorylase>. https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/3-2-carbohydrates[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e, 3.2: glycogen]{ref}

= Glycogenesis
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Glycogenesis> builds <glycogen>; <glycogenolysis> breaks it down. Their opposing directions matter in the <insulin> and <glucagon> circuit.

= Glycogenolysis
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

= Gluconeogenesis
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Gluconeogenesis> synthesizes <glucose> from non-<carbohydrate> precursors. It is distinct from <glycogenolysis>, which mobilizes an existing <glycogen> store.

= Lipolysis
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

= Photosynthesis
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Photosynthesis> captures <light> <energy> and transforms it into chemical forms that a <biological cell> can use. In <plants>, <algae> and <cyanobacteria>, this supports <carbon fixation>: incorporation of <carbon> from <carbon dioxide> into <chemical compounds> used to build living material. Capturing <light> and incorporating <carbon> are distinct processes coupled through <metabolism>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-1-overview-of-photosynthesis[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.1: overview of photosynthesis].

For <oxygenic photosynthesis>, a simplified net equation writes a six-<carbon> <sugar>, <glucose>, as the representative product:
$$
6\mathrm{CO}_2+6\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}+\text{light}\longrightarrow\mathrm{C}_6\mathrm{H}_{12}\mathrm{O}_6+6\mathrm{O}_2.
$$
This is net <stoichiometry>, not a one-step mechanism. A worked bookkeeping check counts six <carbon> <chemical atoms>, twelve <hydrogen> <chemical atoms>, and eighteen <oxygen> <chemical atoms> on each side: the product side has six <oxygen> <chemical atoms> in the <sugar> and twelve in <molecular oxygen>. The <light> label supplies <energy>, not additional <chemical atoms>.

Crucially, the released <molecular oxygen> comes from <water>, not <carbon dioxide>. Cancelling <water> made and consumed in different steps hides this distinction in the net equation. The <Calvin cycle> supplies a net three-<carbon> <triose phosphate>, such as <glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate>, for subsequent <sugar> synthesis; it does not directly export <glucose>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-2-the-light-dependent-reactions-of-photosynthesis[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.2: light-dependent reactions] and https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-3-using-light-energy-to-make-organic-molecules[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.3: using light energy to make organic molecules].

\Image[photosynthesis-energy-and-matter.png]
{title=<Photosynthesis>: <water> supplies released <molecular oxygen>; <carbon dioxide> supplies <sugar> <carbon>}
{description=Original schematic of <oxygenic photosynthesis>. Solid blue arrows trace selected material transfers; dashed orange arrows show <energy> transfer and the coupling through <ATP> and <NADPH>. These carriers are also <molecules>, not pure <energy>. Arrows omit intermediate <chemical reactions> and do not specify rates or yields.}

The useful achievement is connecting incoming <light> to both usable <energy> and new biological material. <ATP> is an immediate <energy> currency; <NADPH> supplies <electrons> for synthesis; <carbon dioxide> supplies the <carbon> incorporated into <carbohydrates>. These roles are complementary. <Conservation of energy> still holds: <photosynthesis> transforms <energy>, never creates it, and some absorbed <energy> is dissipated as <heat>. A <plant> also carries out <cellular respiration>, including in <biological cells> that perform <photosynthesis>. Gas exchange reflects both processes. <Oxygenic photosynthesis> remains central to interpreting terrestrial <molecular oxygen> as an <atmospheric biosignature gas>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-3-using-light-energy-to-make-organic-molecules[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.3: photosynthesis and respiration].

= Oxygenic photosynthesis
{parent=Photosynthesis}

<Oxygenic photosynthesis> uses <water> as an <electron> source and releases <molecular oxygen>. <Plants>, <algae> and <cyanobacteria> provide examples. The defining distinction from <anoxygenic photosynthesis> is <molecular oxygen> release, not merely capture of <light>.

= Anoxygenic photosynthesis
{parent=Photosynthesis}
{wiki}

<Anoxygenic photosynthesis> captures <light> without producing <molecular oxygen>. It occurs in some <bacteria> and uses <electron> sources other than <water>. Whether <carbon fixation> accompanies this <light> capture depends on the <organism> and conditions; <photosynthesis> does not imply one universal <carbon>-assimilation pathway. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26819/[Alberts et al.: chloroplasts and photosynthetic bacteria].

= Light-dependent reactions
{parent=Photosynthesis}
{wiki}

In a <chloroplast>, <light-dependent reactions> take place at <thylakoid> membranes. <Chlorophyll> captures <light>; <electron> transfer helps establish an <electrochemical gradient> across the <thylakoid> membrane, enabling formation of <ATP>. The <oxygenic photosynthesis> pathway also forms <NADPH> and releases <molecular oxygen> from <water>. <ATP> and <NADPH> become available in the <chloroplast stroma> for the <Calvin cycle>. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26819/[Alberts et al.: chloroplast light reactions].

\Image[chloroplast-photosynthesis-compartments.png]
{title=<Thylakoid> membranes and <chloroplast stroma> couple two stages of <photosynthesis>}
{description=Original <chloroplast> schematic, not an experimental photograph or a scale drawing. <Light-dependent reactions> supply <ATP> and <NADPH> to the stromal <Calvin cycle>; depleted carriers return for reuse. The <molecular oxygen> arrow leaves the <water>-using stage, while <carbon dioxide> enters <carbon fixation>.}

= Chlorophyll
{parent=Light-dependent reactions}
{wiki}

<Chlorophyll> is a family of <molecules> that absorb <light> for <photosynthesis>. In <chloroplasts>, <chlorophyll> works with <proteins> in <thylakoid> membranes; absorbing <light> initiates <energy> capture rather than directly constructing a <sugar>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-2-the-light-dependent-reactions-of-photosynthesis[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.2: photosynthetic pigments].

= Carbon fixation
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Carbon fixation> incorporates <carbon> from <carbon dioxide> into organic <chemical compounds>. It identifies a material transformation, rather than a particular source of <energy>: <carbon fixation> can be supported by <photosynthesis> or by other <metabolism>. The <Calvin cycle> is one pathway, not the definition of all <carbon fixation>.

= Calvin cycle
{c}
{parent=Carbon fixation}
{wiki}

= Calvin-Benson cycle
{c}
{synonym}

The <Calvin cycle> couples <carbon fixation> to use of <ATP> and <NADPH>. In a <chloroplast> it takes place in the <chloroplast stroma>. Its <chemical reactions> incorporate <carbon dioxide>, use <NADPH>-supplied <electrons>, and regenerate the <molecules> that accept further <carbon dioxide>.

In the standard <Calvin cycle> bookkeeping, three <carbon dioxide> <molecules> require nine <ATP> and six <NADPH> for one net three-<carbon> <triose phosphate>. A six-<carbon> <sugar> equivalent therefore corresponds to six <carbon dioxide>, eighteen <ATP> and twelve <NADPH>; subsequent <sugar> synthesis is separate. These are pathway requirements, not a universal measured <photosynthesis> yield. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-3-using-light-energy-to-make-organic-molecules[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.3: Calvin cycle bookkeeping].

Calling the <Calvin cycle> light-independent means its individual <chemical reactions> do not directly capture <light>; it does not mean the <Calvin cycle> runs only at night or independently of the supplies from <light-dependent reactions>.

= Cellular respiration
{parent=Metabolism}
{wiki}

<Cellular respiration> transfers <energy> from chemical fuels into usable forms including <ATP>. <Plants> carry out <cellular respiration> as well as <photosynthesis>; in its aerobic form, <cellular respiration> consumes <molecular oxygen> and releases <carbon dioxide>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/8-3-using-light-energy-to-make-organic-molecules[OpenStax Biology 2e, 8.3: energy flow].

= Secondary metabolic byproduct
{parent=Metabolism}

A compound released through specialized functions such as signaling, defense or stress adaptation rather than the core reactions needed for energy and biomass. <Dimethyl sulfide> and <chloromethane> are examples discussed as atmospheric biosignatures. Their specialized origins may improve specificity while lowering the global emission flux.

= Primary metabolic byproduct
{parent=Metabolism}

A product released by reactions needed for energy acquisition, growth or biomass construction. <Methane> from energy-yielding microbial reactions and <molecular oxygen> from oxygenic <photosynthesis> are examples. Abiotic chemistry can make some of the same molecules, so production mechanism and environmental abundance matter.

= Evolution
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

<Evolution> is descent with inherited modification. Within a population, changes in <allele frequencies> provide a precise genetic description; over longer histories, branching descent connects populations through <common descent>. An individual growing larger is <biological development>, whereas an inherited variant becoming more frequent across generations is <evolution>. See https://evolution.berkeley.edu/evolution-101/an-introduction-to-evolution/[UC Museum of Paleontology: the definition of evolution].

<Mutation> supplies new <alleles>, <genetic recombination> reshuffles inherited material, <natural selection> favors variants with greater reproductive contribution in a specified environment, and <genetic drift> changes frequencies through chance. <Gene flow> moves variants between populations. Consequently, an increasing <allele frequency> alone does not identify <natural selection>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/19-2-population-genetics[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: population genetics].

Evidence includes ordered <fossils>, shared inherited structures and similarities in <DNA>; <phylogenetics> uses such observations to infer relationships. A <phylogenetic tree> is an inferred historical account, with uncertainty about particular branches. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/18-1-understanding-evolution[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: evidence of evolution]. Evidence also comes from experiments: Blount, Borland and Lenski reported an <Escherichia coli> lineage able to use <citrate> with <oxygen> present by 31,500 generations, and tested historical dependence by replaying <evolution> from stored ancestors. See https://www.blount-lab.org/uploads/2/3/0/3/23037222/2008_pnas_blount_et_al.pdf[Blount, Borland and Lenski, 2008: original experimental report]. Such observations constrain explanations; no single frequency equation captures every evolutionary process.

= Speciation
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

<Speciation> is the formation of distinct <species> through <evolution>. Detecting a difference between <biological populations> is a starting observation; its interpretation depends on the stated <species concept> and the evidence for lasting separation. <Reproductive isolation> is one important route to limiting <gene flow> in <sexual reproduction>.

= Reproductive isolation
{parent=Speciation}
{wiki}

<Reproductive isolation> consists of barriers that reduce <gene flow> through <sexual reproduction>. Distinguish <premating isolation> from barriers after mating. Barriers before <fertilization> also include incompatible <gametes>; barriers after <fertilization> can reduce offspring survival or fertility. <Biology 2e by Mary Ann Clark and others>[§18.2, “Reproductive Isolation”, opening paragraphs, gametic barriers and hybrid inviability/sterility]{ref}

An assay should state which step it measures. Observing fewer cross-group matings does not measure offspring fertility; observing <fertilization> does not demonstrate that the offspring will reproduce. These distinctions make a partial barrier informative without declaring completed <speciation>.

= Premating isolation
{parent=Reproductive isolation}

<Premating isolation> reduces mating between <biological populations>. The <dietary divergence and mating isolation in Drosophila pseudoobscura> assay measures this stage using observed pairings.

= Postmating reproductive isolation
{parent=Reproductive isolation}

<Postmating reproductive isolation> acts after mating. Specify whether the test concerns <fertilization>, offspring survival, or the offspring's own <reproduction>; those outcomes are not interchangeable.

= Dietary divergence and mating isolation in Drosophila pseudoobscura
{parent=Reproductive isolation}

The 1989 experiment used eight <Drosophila pseudoobscura> <biological populations> from one Bryce Canyon population: four maintained on starch medium, four on maltose, in cages at $25\,{}^\circ\mathrm{C}$. Testing began about a year after establishment. <Reproductive Isolation as a Consequence of Adaptive Divergence in Drosophila pseudoobscura by Diane M. B. Dodd>[p. 1308, population origin, media and cage conditions]{ref}

Before testing, flies spent one generation on cornmeal–molasses–agar. Virgins aged 3–6 days entered multiple-choice chambers: twelve females from each population, followed by males from both populations introduced nearly simultaneously without anesthesia. Observers scored matings for 60–90 minutes under bright indirect lighting. One group's right wing tips were clipped for identification, with clipping reversed between replicates. <Reproductive Isolation as a Consequence of Adaptive Divergence in Drosophila pseudoobscura by Diane M. B. Dodd>[p. 1308, mating-preference procedure and wing-marking control]{ref}

All sixteen cross-diet comparisons had positive <mating assortment contrasts>; eleven departed significantly from <random mating>. Same-diet comparisons did not show significant departures. Common-medium rearing addressed immediate diet conditioning; same-diet controls addressed separation and establishment <population bottlenecks>. Mate choice was not the selection target; the <biological populations> were maintained separately. The result supports partial <premating isolation> accompanying dietary divergence, not completed <speciation>. <Reproductive Isolation as a Consequence of Adaptive Divergence in Drosophila pseudoobscura by Diane M. B. Dodd>[pp. 1309–1310, Tables 1–2 and discussion of conditioning and physical isolation]{ref}

\Image[dietary-divergence-mating-assay.png]
{title=Design of the <dietary divergence and mating isolation in Drosophila pseudoobscura> assay}
{description=Original design schematic. Arrows indicate procedure, not a measured <reproductive isolation> rate. The four mating categories are possible outcomes; no invented counts are shown.}

The <causal inference> remains limited. A negative same-diet <hypothesis test> does not prove zero <reproductive isolation>. One generation under common conditions does not exclude every persistent environmental influence. The chamber readout alone cannot identify a particular <gene>, establish offspring fertility, or predict <gene flow> after sustained contact in nature. Further experiments would need to target those questions separately.

= Mating assortment contrast
{parent=Reproductive isolation}
{title2=$I=(N_{\mathrm{same}}-N_{\mathrm{cross}})/N$}

For observed pairings between two labels $A,B$, let $N_{\mathrm{same}}=N_{AA}+N_{BB}$ and $N_{\mathrm{cross}}=N_{AB}+N_{BA}$, where the first label denotes the female's group and the second the male's. A <mating assortment contrast> is
$$
I=\frac{N_{\mathrm{same}}-N_{\mathrm{cross}}}{N},\qquad N=N_{\mathrm{same}}+N_{\mathrm{cross}}>0.
$$
It lies between $-1$ and $1$. Positive $I$ describes an excess of same-label pairings over cross-label pairings; whether that is evidence of <assortative mating> depends on the null <mathematical model> and the assay. The four counts form a <contingency table>.

Here is an original scope check. Suppose independently paired females and males have <probabilities> $p_f,p_m$ of carrying label $A$. Then
$$
q_{\mathrm{same}}=p_fp_m+(1-p_f)(1-p_m),\qquad \mathbb E[I]=2q_{\mathrm{same}}-1.
$$
With $p_f=p_m=1/2$, the <expected value> is zero. With $p_f=p_m=4/5$, it is $9/25=0.36$, despite pairing being independent of label. This counterexample concerns unequal pool composition; it does not replace the group introductions and <contingency table> tests in the <dietary divergence and mating isolation in Drosophila pseudoobscura> experiment. Even with balanced pools, finite observed samples fluctuate: a positive $I$ alone is not a <hypothesis test>, much less a universal <species> boundary.

= Common descent
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

= Horizontal gene transfer
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

<Horizontal gene transfer> moves genetic material between lineages through routes other than ordinary inheritance from parent to offspring. <Endosymbiotic gene transfer> is a specialized case involving an internal partner or its descendant <organelle>.

= Gene flow
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

= Natural selection
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

<Natural selection> involves systematic differences in expected <reproductive success> associated with variation in <phenotype>. For a continuing evolutionary response, the relevant variation must be inherited: offspring must tend to preserve the differences being favored. Actual offspring counts also fluctuate by chance, producing <genetic drift>. Survival matters insofar as it affects contribution to future generations. <Genotype fitness> is therefore contextual, depending on environment and the life cycle being counted, rather than simply measuring strength or longevity. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/19-1-population-evolution[Clark, Douglas and Choi, Biology 2e: population evolution].

In chapter IV of his 1859 book, Darwin explained <natural selection> through preservation of favorable variations and greater chances of survival and reproduction. See https://darwin-online.org.uk/converted/published/1859_Origin_F373/1859_Origin_F373.html[Darwin, On the Origin of Species, first edition, chapter IV]. The beautiful consequence is cumulative change without foresight: inherited differences are filtered repeatedly by present conditions. <Natural selection> neither generates a needed <mutation> on demand nor guarantees progress toward a predetermined goal.

= Haploid selection recurrence
{parent=Natural selection}
{title2=$p'=\frac{wp}{wp+(1-p)}$}

Consider an explicitly idealized <haploid> population with two inherited types $A,B$, one <allele> per individual at the tracked <genetic locus>, and discrete, nonoverlapping generations. Assume faithful type transmission, no <mutation> or <gene flow>, a constant environment, and reproductive contributions independent of type frequency. Let the <expected values> of offspring surviving to the next census be $m_A,m_B>0$, with relative <genotype fitness> $w=m_A/m_B$.

For $A$ frequency $p$, expected offspring contributions are proportional to $pm_A$ and $(1-p)m_B$. Normalizing gives the deterministic large-population <mathematical model>
$$
p'=\frac{pm_A}{pm_A+(1-p)m_B}=\frac{wp}{wp+(1-p)}.
$$
The denominator is mean relative <genotype fitness>. For $w=1.2$ and $p_0=0.2$, $p_1=0.24/1.04=3/13\approx0.23077$. Equivalently, 200 $A$ and 800 $B$ parents with $m_A=1.2,m_B=1$ contribute expected counts 240 and 800. Fractional per-parent <expected values> are averages, not literal fractional offspring. The ratio of these expectations is a deterministic approximation, not generally the expectation of a random ratio.

The odds multiply particularly simply:
$$
\frac{p_{t+1}}{1-p_{t+1}}=w\frac{p_t}{1-p_t},\qquad p_t=\frac{w^tp_0}{1-p_0+w^tp_0}.
$$
Thus $p_{10}\approx0.60753$: repeated modest advantage can substantially change composition. For $w>1$ and $0<p<1$, $p'-p=(w-1)p(1-p)/(1+(w-1)p)>0$. The deterministic boundary $p=0$ remains unchanged: <natural selection> needs existing variation.

\Image[natural-selection-frequency.png]
{title=Deterministic selection and stochastic sampling in a finite population}
{description=Both panels start at $p_0=0.2$. The left compares the deterministic <haploid selection recurrence> with eight simulated finite-population paths at $w=1.2$; the right shows eight neutral <genetic drift> paths at $w=1$. Each simulation samples $N=50$ offspring independently per generation. These are illustrative <mathematical models>, not experimental measurements.}

A distinct stochastic extension fixes $N$ offspring and samples their types independently using a <binomial distribution>: $K\sim\operatorname{Binomial}(N,q)$, where $q=wp/(wp+1-p)$, then $p'=K/N$. Its conditional <expected value> is $q$ and conditional <variance> is $q(1-q)/N$. With $w=1$, the conditional <expected value> remains $p$, yet realized frequencies wander through <genetic drift>. With $w>1$, <genetic drift> can still lower $p$ or lose $A$ entirely. Over several generations, the deterministic curve need not equal the mean stochastic trajectory, because the recurrence is nonlinear.

= Reproductive success
{parent=Natural selection}
{wiki}

= Phylogenetics
{parent=Evolution}
{wiki}

= Clade
{parent=Phylogenetics}
{wiki}

A <clade> comprises an ancestor and all its descendants. Membership is about <common descent>, not a taxonomic rank or a degree of cellular complexity. In a <two-domain system>, the nuclear lineage of <eukaryotes> nests within <archaea>; a group containing only the <prokaryotic> members excludes descendants and is therefore not that complete <clade>. See https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/20-2-determining-evolutionary-relationships[OpenStax Biology 2e, 20.2: clades and shared ancestry].

= Phylogenetic tree
{parent=Phylogenetics}
{wiki}

A <phylogenetic tree> describes ancestral relationships. In a <probabilistic graphical model> on a rooted <tree>, <latent variables> at ancestral vertices are inferred from observations at leaves using <belief propagation>.

= Felsenstein pruning algorithm
{c}
{parent=Phylogenetic tree}

The <Felsenstein pruning algorithm> computes a site <likelihood function> by <sum-product belief propagation> from leaves to root. For a finite-state <Markov kernel> $K$, the subtree <likelihood function> obeys $L_v(a)=\prod_{w\text{ child of }v}\sum_bK(a,b)L_w(b)$, with observed-state indicators at leaves. Summing against the root <probability distribution> gives the site <likelihood function>. An outward pass gives edge <posterior probabilities> and expected transition counts for the <expectation-maximization algorithm>.

= Calcium in biology
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

Calcium has roles in cell signalling and skeletal structure. Dietary <calcium intake> and <serum calcium> are distinct quantities: physiological regulation means a change in one need not correspond to an equivalent change in the other.

= Serum calcium
{parent=Calcium in biology}

Serum calcium is the concentration of calcium in blood serum. It can be an <exposure> in an <observational study> or <Mendelian randomization>, but genetically changing it need not reproduce the effect of changing dietary <calcium intake>.

= Hypercalcaemia
{parent=Serum calcium}
{wiki}

= Hypercalcemia
{synonym}

Hypercalcaemia is an abnormally elevated <serum calcium> concentration. A diagnosis in medical records depends on testing and diagnostic practice, which can introduce <selection bias> into an <observational study>.

= Calcium intake
{parent=Calcium in biology}

Calcium intake is the amount of calcium consumed, including food and supplements. It is an <exposure> that can be changed by an intervention, and should not be identified automatically with <serum calcium> in a <causal inference> analysis.

= Reproduction
{parent=Biology}

= Asexual reproduction
{parent=Reproduction}

<Asexual reproduction> produces offspring without fusion of <gametes>. An operational <species concept> for such <organisms> needs evidence other than interbreeding.

= Budding
{parent=Reproduction}
{wiki}

<Budding> produces a new individual from an outgrowth of its parent. In <Saccharomyces cerevisiae>, the daughter <biological cell> grows while attached to the mother and later separates; see the Burgess, Powers and Mell account cited there. <Budding> describes <reproduction>, not <fermentation>.

= Sexual reproduction
{parent=Reproduction}

= Gamete
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

A gamete is a reproductive <biological cell> that can fuse with another <gamete> during <fertilization>. In the ordinary <diploid> inheritance model, a <haploid> <gamete> carries one copy at each <genetic locus>.

= Ovum
{parent=Gamete}

An <ovum> is a female <gamete>.

= Fertilization
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

<Fertilization> is fusion of <gametes>. Fusion of two <haploid> <gametes> restores the <diploid> state in the inheritance model used for <Mendelian segregation>.

= Pollination
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

= Pollen
{parent=Pollination}

= Oogenesis
{parent=Sexual reproduction}

<Oogenesis> is development of female <gametes>; the unequal divisions of human <meiosis> produce <polar bodies> alongside the developing <ovum>.

= Polar body
{parent=Oogenesis}

A <polar body> is a small product of an unequal division during <oogenesis>.

= Genetics
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

<Genetics> studies how <genes> are inherited, how their <alleles> vary, and how that variation contributes to <phenotypes>. Its central distinction is between an inherited description, the <genotype>, and what an <organism> actually expresses, the <phenotype>. Following <alleles> through a <pedigree> answers a family-level question; following <allele frequencies> through generations leads to <population genetics> and <evolution>.

The beauty of <Mendelian segregation> is that discrete inherited copies can generate predictable statistical patterns without making every offspring identical. A single <genetic locus> already connects <meiosis>, <probability> and observable variation. The <Hardy-Weinberg principle> then supplies a population baseline: it separates reshuffling inherited copies from processes such as <natural selection> that change their frequencies. These simple models are useful precisely because their assumptions can be stated and checked.

= Strain
{disambiguate=biology}
{parent=Genetics}

= Biological strain
{synonym}

A <biological strain> is a maintained variant or lineage within a <species>. Naming the <species> alone does not specify a laboratory <genotype> or its <genetic background>.

= Genetic background
{parent=Genetics}

The <genetic background> is the rest of the <genotype> against which a focal <allele> or <gene deletion> is studied. Other <genes> can influence the resulting <phenotype>, so comparisons should specify which background is held fixed.

= Functional complementation
{parent=Genetics}

<Functional complementation> is restoration of a specified function or <phenotype> by introducing a <gene> into a deficient recipient. In the <yeast cross-species complementation experiment>, the restored property is growth without supplied <methionine>, under the assay conditions. Rescue need not restore every property of the original <genotype>.

= Molecular genetics
{parent=Genetics}
{wiki}

= Plasmid
{parent=Molecular genetics}
{wiki}

A <plasmid> is a <DNA> element capable of replication separately from the main <chromosomes> of its host. Engineered <plasmids> can carry <genes> for <genetic transformation> and <functional complementation>.

= Genetic transformation
{parent=Molecular genetics}

<Genetic transformation> introduces <DNA> into recipient <biological cells>. It can change their <genotype> and sometimes their <phenotype>; the <yeast cross-species complementation experiment> separates detecting introduction of a <plasmid> from detecting its functional effect.

= Replica plating
{parent=Molecular genetics}
{wiki}

<Replica plating> transfers samples from corresponding <biological colonies> or patches to several growth conditions while preserving their spatial arrangement. A master plate makes it possible to relate growth or failure at one location to the same source population on another plate.

= Genome
{parent=Molecular genetics}
{wiki}

A <genome> is the complete set of genetic information under discussion. For a cellular <organism> it is encoded in <DNA>, including <genes> and other sequences. Specify whether a comparison concerns a nuclear <genome>, a <mitochondrion>'s <genome> or all cellular genetic material: the <cell nucleus> does not contain every <DNA> sequence in a typical <eukaryote>. See https://www.genome.gov/genetics-glossary/Genome[NHGRI: genome].

= DNA sequencing
{c}
{parent=Molecular genetics}
{wiki}

<DNA sequencing> determines the order of <nucleotide> bases in <DNA>. Sequence data can test molecular rearrangements, as in the <functional plastid-to-nucleus gene transfer experiment>, or support <phylogenetics> after a suitable <sequence alignment>.

= Sequence alignment
{parent=Molecular genetics}
{wiki}

A <sequence alignment> places <nucleotide sequences> or <protein> sequences in correspondence for comparison. The choice of corresponding positions affects evolutionary inference; matching a short stretch alone does not establish <common descent> of entire <organisms>.

= Polymerase chain reaction
{parent=Molecular genetics}
{wiki}

The <polymerase chain reaction> amplifies a selected <DNA> region using repeated copying steps. Amplified material can be examined by <DNA sequencing>; appropriate template controls help distinguish a new rearrangement from material already present in the starting preparation.

= Northern blot
{parent=Molecular genetics}
{wiki}

A <Northern blot> separates <RNA> by size and detects selected sequences through hybridization with a probe. Detection of a <messenger RNA> supports <transcription> of a <gene>, but does not alone establish a functional <protein> or its destination.

= DNA replication
{c}
{parent=Molecular genetics}
{wiki}

<DNA replication> copies a double-stranded <DNA> <molecule> into two daughter double-stranded <DNA> <molecules>. In <semiconservative replication>, each daughter retains one parental strand and receives one newly synthesized complementary strand. <DNA polymerases> build the new strands from incoming <nucleotides>; they do not make <RNA> or <proteins> in this copying step. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26850/[Alberts et al., Molecular Biology of the Cell, 4th edition (2002): DNA replication mechanisms].

For a five-<base pair> example, separate $5'\text{-}\mathrm{ATGCC}\text{-}3'$ from its partner $3'\text{-}\mathrm{TACGG}\text{-}5'$. <Base pairing> specifies a new TACGG partner for the old ATGCC strand and a new ATGCC partner for the old TACGG strand. With error-free copying, both daughter <DNA> <molecules> have the original <nucleotide sequence>, but different physical strand histories. One initial duplex contains ten <nucleotides>; one complete round produces two duplexes containing twenty <nucleotides>, ten retained and ten newly incorporated.

\Image[dna-replication-semi-conservative.png]
{title=<Semiconservative replication>: follow two original <DNA> strands through two complete copying rounds}
{description=Blue O1 and purple O2 are the original strands; green strands were made in round 1 and orange strands in round 2. Each arrow connects a parent duplex to its daughters. After round 2, two of four duplexes retain an original strand, and the other two retain a round-1 strand. All eight strands have the appropriate complementary <nucleotide sequences>; colors identify strand age, not different <nucleobases>. The flattened duplexes show ancestry and orientation, not a replication fork or molecular shape.}

This is useful for inheritance: both daughter <biological cells> can receive a copy of the <DNA> record. The elegant feature is that <base pairing> lets each strand specify its missing partner. Direction matters too: a replicating <DNA polymerase> reads its template $3'\to5'$ and extends the new strand at its $3'$ end, so synthesis proceeds $5'\to3'$. These are opposite directions on <antiparallel nucleic acid strands>, not two alternative directions of synthesis. The enzyme needs a pre-existing paired strand end to extend; the diagram omits the machinery that starts and coordinates copying. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26850/[Alberts et al.: template-directed synthesis and the requirement for a primer].

The strand accounting predicts observable differences between copying mechanisms. In 1958, Meselson and Stahl grew <Escherichia coli> with the heavier <nitrogen> <isotope> $^{15}\mathrm{N}$, then supplied $^{14}\mathrm{N}$ and measured extracted <DNA> by density-gradient centrifugation. After one generation their samples contained intermediate-density <DNA>; after two, intermediate-density and light <DNA> occurred in equal amounts. This supported <semiconservative replication> in the tested <Escherichia coli> culture. It was an <isotope> assay of sampled <DNA>, not simultaneous testing of every kind of <DNA> or direct colored-strand photography. See <The replication of DNA in Escherichia coli by Matthew Meselson and Franklin W. Stahl>.

= Semiconservative replication
{parent=DNA replication}
{wiki}

In <semiconservative replication>, each daughter duplex retains one strand of its immediate parent. Under complete copying without strand loss or exchange, one starting duplex gives $2^n$ duplexes after $n$ rounds, but still only two original strands. For $n\geq1$, the fraction of duplexes containing an original strand is $2/2^n=2^{1-n}$: all after one round, half after two, a quarter after three. The fraction of all strands that are original is instead $2^{-n}$. A strand made in round 1 is parental when round 2 begins.

= DNA polymerase
{c}
{parent=DNA replication}
{wiki}

A replicating <DNA polymerase> is an <enzyme> that extends a paired strand end with <nucleotides> selected against a <DNA> template. New <DNA> grows $5'\to3'$ while the template is read $3'\to5'$. <Base pairing> specifies the partner; the <DNA polymerase> catalyzes its incorporation. See https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26850/[Alberts et al.: DNA polymerase].

= DNA replication inhibitor
{c}
{parent=DNA replication}

A <DNA replication inhibitor> interferes with <DNA replication>; the observed effect depends on exposure and assay conditions.

= Hydroxyurea
{parent=DNA replication inhibitor}
{wiki}

<Hydroxyurea> supplies the synthesis-blocking comparison in the experiment under <EdU labeling>.

= DNA repair
{c}
{parent=Molecular genetics}
{wiki}

<DNA repair> addresses damage to <DNA>. Incorporation of a labeled precursor during <DNA repair> need not indicate duplication of the whole <genome> in <S phase>.

= Gene expression
{parent=Molecular genetics}
{wiki}

<Gene expression> uses a <gene>'s information to produce functional <RNA> or a <protein>. <Transcription> makes <RNA> from a <DNA> template; for a <protein>-coding <gene>, <biological translation> then uses <messenger RNA> to specify an <amino acid> chain. Many <genes> instead produce functional <RNA>, including <transfer RNA> and <ribosomal RNA>, without a translated <protein> product.
