If mutation rate per lineage is $\theta/2$ in standard coalescent time units, a tree of total length $L$ has a <Poisson distribution> of mutation count with mean $\theta L/2$.

= Segregating-site likelihood under the neutral coalescent
{parent=Infinite sites mutation model}

For $n\ge2$, the <probability generating function> of the segregating-site count conditional on mutation parameter $\theta$ is
$$
E[z^S\mid\theta]=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{i}{i+\theta(1-z)}.
$$
This follows by integrating the Poisson generating function over the independent exponential epoch lengths. With $W=L/2$, its density is $(n-1)e^{-w}(1-e^{-w})^{n-2}$. Expanding the binomial factor and integrating $w^k e^{-(\theta+r+1)w}$ gives
$$
P(S=k\mid\theta)=(n-1)\theta^k\sum_{r=0}^{n-2}\frac{(-1)^r\binom{n-2}{r}}{(\theta+r+1)^{k+1}}.
$$
For $k=0$ use $\theta^0=1$, including at $\theta=0$. A posterior density divided by a positive prior density is proportional to this likelihood, allowing posterior simulation output to estimate its maximizer.

= Inbreeding
{parent=Population genetics}
{wiki}

= Inbred
{synonym}

Inbreeding is reproduction between genetically related individuals. It increases the chance that an offspring's maternal and paternal copies at a locus are <identical by descent>. Such homozygosity by descent is autozygosity.

= Inbreeding coefficient
{parent=Inbreeding}
{title2=$F$}

The inbreeding coefficient is the <probability> that the two <allele> copies at a locus within an individual are <identical by descent>. It equals the <kinship coefficient> of the individual's parents. It concerns ancestral identity, rather than merely <homozygosity> of observed allelic states.

// Target: biology.bigb

= Inbreeding genotype frequencies
{parent=Inbreeding coefficient}

For a biallelic locus with frequencies $p=1-q$ and $q$, and <inbreeding coefficient> $F$, ancestral-copy identity with probability $F$ gives <genotype> probabilities $P(AA)=p^2+Fpq$, $P(Aa)=2pq(1-F)$ and $P(aa)=q^2+Fpq$. In the IBD component both copies equal a single ancestral draw; in the remaining component the two ancestral alleles are independent population draws. A fully penetrant recessive disease therefore has risk $q^2+Fq(1-q)$.

// Target: biology.bigb

= First-cousin inbreeding coefficient
{parent=Inbreeding coefficient}
{title2=$F=1/16$}

For an otherwise <outbred pedigree>, each of the two shared grandparents contributes $(1/2)^{2+2+1}=1/32$ to the <kinship coefficient> of first cousins. Thus their child has <inbreeding coefficient> $1/16$. Equivalently, cousin kinship is one quarter of the $1/4$ kinship of their sibling parents.

// Target: biology.bigb

= Linkage disequilibrium
{parent=Population genetics}
{wiki}
{title2=$D=P(AB)-P(A)P(B)$}

= LD
{c}
{synonym}

<Linkage disequilibrium> means that knowing an <allele> at one <genetic locus> changes the <probability> of an <allele> at another <genetic locus> in population <haplotypes>. It compares their <joint probability distribution> with the product of their <marginal distributions>. This is useful because a <genetic marker> can carry information about another <genetic locus>: <tag SNPs> exploit this redundancy, without establishing a causal <genetic association>.

Consider two <genetic loci> with <alleles> $A,a$ and $B,b$. Sample one <haplotype>, rather than an unphased <diploid> <genotype>. Write its four <haplotype frequencies> as $f_{AB},f_{Ab},f_{aB},f_{ab}$, summing to one, and set the marginal <allele frequencies> to $p=f_{AB}+f_{Ab}$ and $q=f_{AB}+f_{aB}$. The <linkage disequilibrium> coefficient is
$$
D=f_{AB}-pq=f_{AB}f_{ab}-f_{Ab}f_{aB}.
$$
The second expression follows by substituting $f_{ab}=1-f_{AB}-f_{Ab}-f_{aB}$. The entire two-locus <joint probability distribution> is then
$$
\begin{array}{c|cc|c}
 &B&b&\text{total}\\\hline
A&pq+D&p(1-q)-D&p\\
a&(1-p)q-D&(1-p)(1-q)+D&1-p\\\hline
\text{total}&q&1-q&1
\end{array}
$$
Every row and column sum is unchanged when $D$ varies. <Linkage equilibrium> means $D=0$: all four <haplotype frequencies> equal products of <allele frequencies>, so the two <allele> indicators are <independent random variables>. For $p>0$, the <conditional probability> $P(B\mid A)=q+D/p$ shows directly how positive $D$ enriches $B$ among copies carrying $A$, while negative $D$ depletes it. Positive $D$ also enriches $ab$; negative $D$ enriches $Ab$ and $aB$. Swapping the labels $A$ and $a$ reverses the sign of $D$, so neither sign is intrinsically favorable.

For example, fix the <allele frequencies> at $p=0.60,q=0.40$. At <linkage equilibrium>, the <haplotype frequencies> in the order $AB,Ab,aB,ab$ are $(0.24,0.36,0.16,0.24)$. Positive <linkage disequilibrium> $D=0.12$ instead gives $(0.36,0.24,0.04,0.36)$, with $P(B\mid A)=0.60$; negative <linkage disequilibrium> $D=-0.12$ gives $(0.12,0.48,0.28,0.12)$, with $P(B\mid A)=0.20$. Counting each <allele> separately would miss the difference.

\Image[haplotype-joint-frequencies-fixed-marginals.png]
{title=Three <haplotype> <joint probability distributions> with identical marginal <allele frequencies>, showing negative <linkage disequilibrium>, <linkage equilibrium> and positive <linkage disequilibrium>}

Nonnegative <haplotype frequencies> constrain $D$. Define the nonnegative bounds $L=\min(pq,(1-p)(1-q))$ and $U=\min(p(1-q),(1-p)q)$. The four entries above give
$$
-L\le D\le U,\qquad \max(0,p+q-1)\le f_{AB}\le\min(p,q).
$$
Thus $|D|\le1/4$ globally, but a particular pair of <allele frequencies> usually permits a smaller, asymmetric interval. The <standardized linkage disequilibrium> convention used here retains the sign:
$$
D'=\begin{cases}D/U&D>0,\\0&D=0,\\D/L&D<0,\end{cases}
\qquad -1\le D'\le1.
$$
This convention requires $0<p,q<1$; if either <genetic locus> has only one <allele> in the population, $D=0$ but normalized measures are undefined. Some presentations report only $|D'|$. At $D'=1$ or $-1$, at least one of the four <haplotypes> has zero frequency; this alone does not prove that <genetic recombination> has never occurred.

For the <indicator random variables> $X=\mathbf{1}_{A}$ and $Y=\mathbf{1}_{B}$, their <covariance> is $D$, and their <variances> are $p(1-p)$ and $q(1-q)$. The <allelic correlation> measure is their squared <correlation coefficient>,
$$
r_{\mathrm{LD}}^2=\frac{D^2}{p(1-p)q(1-q)},\qquad
0\le r_{\mathrm{LD}}^2\le\frac{\max(L^2,U^2)}{p(1-p)q(1-q)}\le1.
$$
The subscript distinguishes it from the <recombination fraction> $r$ in <linkage-disequilibrium decay under recombination>. Squaring loses the association's sign. In the illustrated positive and negative examples, $r_{\mathrm{LD}}^2=0.25$ in both, whereas $D'=0.75$ and $-0.50$, respectively. For another original example, take $p=0.10,q=0.50,f_{AB}=0.10$: then $D=0.05,D'=1$, yet $r_{\mathrm{LD}}^2=1/9$. Every $A$ carries $B$, but most copies carrying $B$ lack $A$. The frequency constraints and the distinction between these normalizations are analyzed in <Mathematical properties of the r² measure of linkage disequilibrium by Jenna M. VanLiere and others> (sections 2.1 and 2.5, pp. 131–132 and 135–136).

<Genetic linkage> describes transmission through <meiosis>; <linkage disequilibrium> describes a population's <haplotype frequencies>. Nearby <genetic loci> on a <chromosome> can be in <linkage equilibrium>. Conversely, mixing populations can create <linkage disequilibrium> even between <genetic loci> on different <chromosomes>. For example, mix equal numbers of independently sampled <haplotypes> from two populations, each internally at <linkage equilibrium>, with $p=q=0.80$ in one and $p=q=0.20$ in the other. The pooled <allele frequencies> are $p=q=0.50$, but $f_{AB}=(0.64+0.04)/2=0.34$, giving $D=0.09$. This is a consequence of <population stratification>, not a measurement of the <recombination fraction>.

Measured <haplotype frequencies> also depend on the sample. Finite <sample size>, related individuals, <genetic-study ascertainment>, <genotyping error> and uncertain <haplotype estimation> can change an estimate. A missing rare <haplotype> can give empirical $|D'|=1$ without demonstrating its absence from the population. Report the sample definition and <allele frequencies> alongside the association measure; a nonzero sample estimate alone does not establish nonzero population <linkage disequilibrium>.

A concrete dataset is the 2005 <A haplotype map of the human genome by The International HapMap Consortium>. The International HapMap Consortium used <genotyping> platforms including Illumina BeadArray to obtain <SNP> <genotype> calls from 269 <DNA> samples across four collections: 30 parent–offspring trios from Ibadan, 30 trios from Utah, 45 unrelated individuals from Beijing and 44 from Tokyo. Parent–offspring trios and the PHASE algorithm supplied <haplotype estimation>, rather than direct observation of every <chromosome>. In Figure 7, 36 <SNPs> resolve seven <haplotypes> in a <chromosome> 2 region in the Utah panel; seven <tag SNPs> capture the 36 <SNPs> at $r_{\mathrm{LD}}^2\ge0.8$. The readout demonstrates reusable <allelic correlation>, not causal effects.

Controls required over 80% complete calls, at most one discordance among five duplicate <genotypes>, at most one <Mendelian segregation> error per trio panel, and a <Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium> test with $P>0.001$; blind reassessment checked performance. Minor <allele frequencies> of at least 0.05 were targeted, the collections were not population-representative, and inferred <haplotypes> retain phase uncertainty. These limits restrict extrapolation of <linkage disequilibrium>.<A haplotype map of the human genome by The International HapMap Consortium>[pp. 1300–1302, Table 1; Figure 7, p. 1306; Methods, p. 1316]{ref}

= Linkage equilibrium
{parent=Linkage disequilibrium}
{title2=$D=0$}

For two <genetic loci> with two <alleles> each, <linkage equilibrium> is <independence> of their states on sampled <haplotypes>. It concerns association between <genetic loci>; <Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium> concerns pairing of <alleles> into <diploid> <genotypes>.

= Standardized linkage disequilibrium
{parent=Linkage disequilibrium}
{title2=$D'=D/D_{\max}$}

For positive <linkage disequilibrium> $D$, take $D_{\max}=\min(p_A(1-p_B),(1-p_A)p_B)$; for negative $D$, take $D_{\max}=\min(p_Ap_B,(1-p_A)(1-p_B))$ and retain its negative sign in $D'$. Thus $|D'|\le1$. Its magnitude measures the fraction of the largest disequilibrium possible at the specified allele frequencies. High $|D'|$ need not imply high <allelic correlation>, so it does not alone guarantee good marker prediction.

= Linkage-disequilibrium decay under recombination
{parent=Linkage disequilibrium}
{title2=$D_{t+1}=(1-r)D_t$}

<Genetic recombination> erodes <linkage disequilibrium> by reshuffling <alleles> between <haplotypes>, while preserving marginal <allele frequencies> in the idealized model. Let $f_{AB,t}$ denote the <haplotype frequency> in the <gamete> pool of generation $t$, let $p,q$ denote its marginal <allele frequencies>, and let $r\in[0,1/2]$ be the constant <recombination fraction> between two autosomal <genetic loci>. Assume <random mating> within one population, nonoverlapping generations, fair <Mendelian segregation>, equal reciprocal <gamete> production, the same $r$ in both sexes, and a deterministic population large enough to neglect <genetic drift>. Exclude <natural selection>, <mutation> and <gene flow>. Then
$$
f_{AB,t+1}=(1-r)f_{AB,t}+rpq=f_{AB,t}-rD_t,
\qquad D_{t+1}=(1-r)D_t,
\qquad D_t=(1-r)^tD_0.
$$
Here $p$ and $q$ remain constant: recombining changes which <alleles> occur together, not their total numbers. The other <haplotype frequencies> change by $+rD_t$ for $Ab,aB$ and $-rD_t$ for $ab$. Consequently the <joint probability distribution> stays within its allowed bounds and approaches the product of its <marginal distributions> when $r>0$.

For the original positive example $p=0.60,q=0.40,D_0=0.12$, a <recombination fraction> $r=0.10$ gives $D_1=0.108$ and $D_{10}\approx0.04184$. The negative example $D_0=-0.12$ has exactly the opposite sequence: <genetic recombination> reduces the magnitude without reversing the sign. With $r=0$, the initial <linkage disequilibrium> persists in this model; even <independent assortment> at $r=1/2$ only halves $D$ each generation. A population association does not vanish after one generation merely because the <genetic loci> are unlinked.

\Image[linkage-disequilibrium-ideal-recombination-decay.png]
{title=Idealized <linkage-disequilibrium decay under recombination> at four <recombination fractions>, with faster decay of squared <allelic correlation> when marginal <allele frequencies> remain fixed}

The <geometric sequence> gives a generation scale: for $r>0$, the continuous interpolation reaches $|D_0|/2$ at $t_{1/2}=\log(1/2)/\log(1-r)$. Actual generations are integers; the first at or below half is $\lceil t_{1/2}\rceil$. For small $r$, $t_{1/2}\approx(\log 2)/r$. Because the denominator of <allelic correlation> stays fixed, $r_{\mathrm{LD},t}^2=(1-r)^{2t}r_{\mathrm{LD},0}^2$; its decay is faster than the magnitude of $D_t$.

A short derivation uses <random mating> to assign frequency $2f_{AB}f_{ab}$ to the double <heterozygote> $AB/ab$ and $2f_{Ab}f_{aB}$ to $Ab/aB$. Relative to $r=0$, each loses or gains an $AB$ <gamete> fraction $r/2$, respectively. Their net contribution is $r(f_{Ab}f_{aB}-f_{AB}f_{ab})=-rD$. The other <genotypes> produce no net change in $f_{AB}$, proving the recurrence and, by iteration, its solution.

This temporal model does not identify elapsed generations from physical separation alone. The <recombination fraction> depends on <genetic recombination>, including <recombination hotspots>, while <population stratification>, <natural selection>, <gene flow> or <genetic drift> can create or maintain <linkage disequilibrium>. Repeated sampling across time would also require accounting for <sample size> and <haplotype estimation>. A cross-sectional plot of association against distance between <genetic loci> is therefore a different observation from the idealized trajectory plotted here.

= Allelic correlation
{parent=Linkage disequilibrium}
{title2=$r^2=D^2/[p_A(1-p_A)p_B(1-p_B)]$}

For two segregating biallelic loci, encode the allele on a random population <haplotype> by indicator variables $X,Y$. Their <covariance> is the <linkage disequilibrium> coefficient $D$. The squared <correlation> is $r^2=D^2/[p_A(1-p_A)p_B(1-p_B)]$, between zero and one. It measures how well one marker predicts the other and is undefined if either marker is monomorphic.

// Target: biology.bigb

= Population stratification
{parent=Genetics}
{wiki}

Population stratification is systematic variation in allele frequencies between subpopulations. If those subpopulations also differ in outcome causes, <genetic variant> associations can exhibit <confounding>, violating <instrumental-variable independence> in <Mendelian randomization>.

= Hardy-Weinberg principle
{c}
{parent=Genetics}
{wiki=Hardy–Weinberg_principle}
{title2=$p^2,2pq,q^2;\quad p+q=1$}

= Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium
{c}
{synonym}

= HWE
{c}
{synonym}

= Hardy-Weinberg proportions
{c}
{synonym}

The <Hardy-Weinberg principle> describes the <genotype> proportions produced by independently combining two parental <gametes> from the same <allele> pool. For <alleles> $A,a$ with <allele frequencies> $p,q$, where $p+q=1$, the <Hardy-Weinberg proportions> are
$$
P(AA)=p^2,\qquad P(Aa)=2pq,\qquad P(aa)=q^2.
$$
The two ordered <heterozygote> outcomes contribute $pq+qp$. These proportions sum to $(p+q)^2=1$, and their implied $A$ <allele frequency> is $p^2+(2pq)/2=p$. Reshuffling copies therefore preserves their frequencies.

For stable <Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium> across generations, use a large population with <random mating>, equal <allele frequencies> in the parental pools, ordinary <Mendelian segregation>, and no <natural selection>, <mutation>, <gene flow> or <genetic drift>. Negligible <genetic drift> is an idealization of large population size. The <one-generation random-mating equilibrium> needs independent union of <gametes>; persistence additionally requires processes that change <allele frequencies> to be absent.

At $p=0.70,q=0.30$, the predicted <genotype> proportions are $0.49,0.42,0.09$. For 100 sampled individuals the <expected values> of the counts are $49,42,9$, rather than the $25,50,25$ of a specified $Aa\times Aa$ cross. The latter fixes both parental <genotypes>; <Hardy-Weinberg proportions> average over population mating.

\Image[hardy-weinberg-proportions.png]
{title=Hardy-Weinberg genotype proportions as allele frequency varies, highlighting p = 0.70}

An observed population need not have these proportions. For example, $70\ AA$, no $Aa$, and $30\ aa$ among 100 individuals still gives $p=0.70$, but has no <heterozygotes>. <Random mating> predicts $0.42$ <heterozygosity> in the next generation under the stated model. A finite sample can also depart by chance, so a discrepancy is a starting point for investigating <inbreeding>, <population stratification> or <genotyping error>, rather than proof of one cause.

Hardy's 1908 note made the conceptual payoff explicit: a dominant <phenotype> does not by itself make its <allele> spread. <Complete dominance> describes expression; changes in frequency require a separate mechanism such as <natural selection>. See https://www-leland.stanford.edu/~huatang/gene244/readings/HardyWeinberg.pdf[Hardy, Mendelian Proportions in a Mixed Population, Science 28 (1908), 49–50].

= Multiplicative penetrance preserves Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium
{parent=Hardy-Weinberg principle}
{title2=$\pi_i^*=\pi_i\psi_i/\sum_u\pi_u\psi_u$}

For ordered alleles $I,J$ independently drawn with probabilities $\pi_i$, suppose disease <penetrance> is $c\psi_i\psi_j$. Conditional on disease, the allele draws remain independent with probabilities $\pi_i^*=\pi_i\psi_i/\sum_u\pi_u\psi_u$. This follows by normalizing the product $c\pi_i\pi_j\psi_i\psi_j$. Unordered homozygote probabilities are $(\pi_i^*)^2$ and heterozygote probabilities are $2\pi_i^*\pi_j^*$. Unaffected controls do not generally share this factorization: their genotype weights contain $1-c\psi_i\psi_j$.

// Target: biology.bigb

= One-generation random-mating equilibrium
{parent=Hardy-Weinberg principle}
{title2=$p=u+v/2,\quad(p^2,2p(1-p),(1-p)^2)$}

If parental <genotype> frequencies are $(u,v,w)$ and the two parental gametes are independently sampled from the same <allele> pool, <Mendelian segregation> gives allele frequency $p=u+v/2$. Their independent union has the displayed <Hardy-Weinberg proportions>, irrespective of parental genotype proportions. Equal allele frequencies in the two sexes and no transmission or viability distortion are part of this conclusion.

= Allele-frequency estimator under Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium
{parent=Hardy-Weinberg principle}

From genotype counts in $n$ independent diploid individuals, the unbiased and maximum-likelihood estimator of the frequency of allele $B$ is
$$
\widehat\theta=\frac{2n_{BB}+n_{AB}}{2n}.
$$
It is the sample proportion among the $2n$ sampled alleles, so it is consistent and
$$
\sqrt n(\widehat\theta-\theta)
\xrightarrow{d}N\left(0,\frac{\theta(1-\theta)}2\right).
$$

= Genetic variant
{parent=Genetics}
{wiki}

A genetic variant is a heritable difference in a DNA sequence relative to a chosen reference. Genetic variants can be used as <instrumental variables> in <Mendelian randomization> when the required causal assumptions are credible.

= Single-nucleotide polymorphism
{parent=Genetic variant}
{wiki}

= SNP
{synonym}
{c}

A single-nucleotide polymorphism is variation at one nucleotide position in sampled genomes. Under an <infinite sites mutation model>, the number of such variable positions in a sample equals the number of mutations on the genealogy below its most recent common ancestor.

= Tag SNP
{parent=Single-nucleotide polymorphism}
{wiki}

A tag SNP represents other nearby <single-nucleotide polymorphisms> whose <alleles> are strongly correlated through <linkage disequilibrium>. Tagging reduces the number of markers needed for <association mapping>; it does not establish that the tag itself causes the phenotype.

// Target: biology.bigb

= Quantitative genetics
{parent=Genetics}
{wiki}

Quantitative genetics studies how genetic and environmental variation contribute to continuously varying traits.

= Genetic risk score
{parent=Quantitative genetics}
{title2=$G_{\rm score}=\sum_j w_jG_j$}

A genetic risk score combines allele counts from several <genetic variants>, usually using specified weights. In <Mendelian randomization> it can act as a combined <instrumental variable> for an <exposure>; interpretation still requires <instrument relevance>, <instrumental-variable independence>, and the <exclusion restriction>.

= Heritability
{title2=$h^2$}
{parent=Quantitative genetics}
{wiki}

Heritability is the fraction of phenotypic variance in a specified population and environment attributed to genetic variation. It is a population parameter, not the genetic fraction of one individual's trait.

= Twin study
{parent=Heritability}
{wiki}

A twin study compares trait resemblance between twins with different degrees of genetic relatedness to separate genetic and environmental variance components.

= Monozygotic twin
{title2=MZ twin}
{parent=Twin study}

This is the single-zygote subtype of a <twin>.

Monozygotic twins develop from one zygote and are modeled as sharing essentially all additive genetic variation.

= Dizygotic twin
{title2=DZ twin}
{parent=Twin study}

This is the two-zygote subtype of a <twin>.

Dizygotic twins develop from two zygotes and, absent assortative mating and other complications, share one half of additive genetic variation on average.

= ACE model
{c}
{parent=Twin study}
{wiki}

The ACE model decomposes trait variation into additive genetic effects $A$, common environmental effects $C$, and unique environmental effects $E$.

= Falconer's formula
{c}
{parent=ACE model}
{wiki}

Falconer's formula estimates narrow-sense heritability from monozygotic and dizygotic twin correlations:
$$
h^2=2(r_{\rm MZ}-r_{\rm DZ}).
$$
It relies on equal relevant environments and a dizygotic additive-genetic correlation of one half.

= Assortative mating
{parent=Quantitative genetics}
{wiki}

Assortative mating is nonrandom mating in which partners are more or less similar than random pairs for a trait or correlated characteristic. Positive assortment can increase genetic similarity among siblings.

= Pathogen
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= RNA splicing
{c}
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

Processing of a precursor <RNA> that removes an <intron> and joins the flanking <exons>. The <ribozyme> experiments show one self-splicing mechanism; <RNA splicing> need not always be autonomous.

= Intron
{parent=RNA splicing}
{wiki}

A segment represented in a precursor <RNA> that is removed by <RNA splicing>; the corresponding segment of a <gene> is also called an <intron>. An <intron> can interrupt <ribosomal RNA> as well as <messenger RNA>.

= Group I catalytic intron
{parent=Intron}
{wiki}

= Group I intron
{synonym}

A class of <introns> with a conserved <RNA> catalytic core and a two-step <RNA splicing> mechanism initiated by external <guanosine>. Some <group I introns> self-splice under suitable conditions; others require <protein> assistance. The <ribozyme> body explains the Tetrahymena example.

= Exon
{parent=RNA splicing}
{wiki}

A segment retained in the mature <RNA> after <RNA splicing>, or the corresponding segment of a <gene>. An <exon> need not encode a <protein>: the <ribosomal RNA> <exons> in the Tetrahymena <ribozyme> experiment are an example.

= Guanosine
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

A <nucleoside> comprising <guanine> attached to <ribose>. Its free $3'$ <hydroxyl group> initiates <group I intron> <RNA splicing>; <guanosine> is incorporated into the excised <intron>, rather than acting as a regenerated <catalyst>.

= Gel electrophoresis
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

Separation of charged <molecules> through a gel in an <electric field>. Denaturing conditions suppress folded structures, helping compare <RNA> lengths; matching migration alone does not establish an identical <nucleotide sequence> or identical <covalent bonds>. In the <ribozyme> experiments, labelled <RNA> was detected after separation by exposing photographic film.

= SDS-PAGE
{c}
{parent=Gel electrophoresis}
{wiki}

= Sodium dodecyl sulfate polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis
{synonym}

An analytical form of <gel electrophoresis> using an anionic <surfactant> and a <polymer> gel to separate <proteins> predominantly by chain size under denaturing conditions. <Western blot separation> often uses <SDS-PAGE>; the resulting apparent <mass> and subsequent <antibody> binding provide complementary, incomplete evidence about identity.

= ELISA
{c}
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay
{synonym}

= Physiology
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Homeostasis
{parent=Physiology}
{wiki}

<Homeostasis> is the maintenance of usable internal conditions despite disturbances. It concerns selected <regulated physiological variables>, rather than keeping every part of an organism unchanged: <core body temperature> may remain near its usual range while <skin> <temperature>, <sweating> and <blood pressure> change. Regulation makes a relatively dependable environment for <biological cells> out of a changing external world. <Anatomy and Physiology 2e by J. Gordon Betts and others/1.5 Homeostasis>[§1.5, opening paragraphs and Negative Feedback: normal ranges and compensatory responses]{ref}

A useful causal description separates a <physiological sensor>, a <physiological integrator> and a <physiological effector>. The return from the <physiological effector>'s action to the sensed <regulated physiological variable> closes a <feedback control> loop. In <negative feedback>, that return opposes the disturbance. The <insulin> and <glucagon> articles already trace the <blood glucose> example; <synaptic integration> explains how several inputs can jointly influence a <neuron>. These are implementations and ingredients, not a requirement that every regulatory loop use one anatomical control center. <Anatomy and Physiology 2e by J. Gordon Betts and others/1.5 Homeostasis>[§1.5, Negative Feedback, Figure 1.10: sensor, control center and effector]{ref}

\Image[homeostasis-temperature-feedback-circuit.png]
{title=<Homeostasis>: sensing, integration and action, with a synthetic disturbance response}
{description=Original qualitative <thermoregulation> circuit above an explicitly synthetic plot from the <homeostatic temperature model>. The solid return represents <negative feedback> from <core body temperature>; the dashed <skin> input can anticipate a core change. The lower curves use invented <dimensionless variables> and arbitrary time units. They are neither experimental measurements nor fitted human responses.}

<Homeostasis> is useful precisely because internal activity can change: a nearly steady <temperature> can conceal a large increase in <heat transfer>. Conversely, observing a flat trace alone does not identify a regulatory mechanism. The environment might simply be constant, or the measuring instrument too insensitive; a perturbation and an independent response readout provide stronger evidence. The <skin-temperature perturbation experiment> below makes that distinction tangible.

The historical motivation was the puzzle of stability in changing living material. In his 1932 https://www.panarchy.org/cannon/homeostasis.1932.html[The Wisdom of the Body], <Walter Bradford Cannon> emphasized that an organism exchanges material with its surroundings and continually replaces its own structure, yet maintains relatively steady internal conditions. This authored excerpt presents <homeostasis> as active compensation for external and internal disturbances, rather than a static body. <The Wisdom of the Body by Walter Bradford Cannon/Homeostasis (excerpt from The Wisdom of the Body)>[Homeostasis excerpt, paragraphs beginning The wonder increases, Resistance to changes and In short; hosting page identifies book and 1932 date]{ref}

= Regulated physiological variable
{parent=Homeostasis}

A <regulated physiological variable> is a specified internal quantity whose departures elicit actions tending to restore an appropriate range. Examples include <core body temperature>, <blood glucose> <concentration>, <blood pressure>, and the <pH> and <osmolality> of <extracellular fluid>. Specify the compartment and readout: <skin> <temperature> and <core body temperature> are different quantities, and an <insulin> <concentration> is a regulatory message rather than the <blood glucose> it helps control.

= Blood pressure
{parent=Regulated physiological variable}
{wiki}

= Osmolality
{parent=Regulated physiological variable}

<Osmolality> counts dissolved particles per mass of <solvent>; it characterizes a <chemical solution>, rather than the total amount of <water> in an organism.

= Physiological sensor
{parent=Homeostasis}

A <physiological sensor> detects a relevant condition and changes a signal. In <thermoregulation>, temperature-sensitive <neurons> supply information about internal and <skin> <temperature>. Sensing a disturbance and sensing its internal consequence need not be the same operation.

= Physiological integrator
{parent=Homeostasis}

A <physiological integrator> combines regulatory inputs to select a response; it need not perform the mathematical <integral action> used in <control theory>. In <thermoregulation>, the <hypothalamus> participates in combining internal and <skin> signals. The term describes a role, which can be distributed across interacting <biological cells>, rather than a mandatory single comparator.

= Hypothalamus
{parent=Physiological integrator}
{wiki}

= Physiological effector
{parent=Homeostasis}

A <physiological effector> performs the action that changes the <regulated physiological variable>. In <thermoregulation>, <sweat glands>, changes in <skin> blood flow, and <skeletal muscle> <shivering> contribute different actions. The command, the action and the resulting <temperature> are three distinct readouts. <Anatomy and Physiology 2e by J. Gordon Betts and others/1.5 Homeostasis>[§1.5, Figure 1.10 and thermoregulatory examples following it]{ref}

= Effector saturation
{parent=Physiological effector}

<Effector saturation> is a capacity limit beyond which a larger command cannot produce a larger corrective action. In the <homeostatic temperature model>, take $u=\min(4x,1/2)$ during the sustained unit disturbance. Before reaching the cap, $x=(1-e^{-5t})/5$. The cap is reached at $t_c=\log(8/3)/5$, when $x=1/8$. Thereafter
$$
x(t)=\frac12-\frac38e^{-(t-t_c)},\qquad u(t)=\frac12.
$$
The lasting displacement is now $1/2$, compared with $1/5$ without the cap. The <negative feedback> command still asks for more cooling, but the action cannot supply it. This illustrates a capacity limit without predicting a human <temperature> limit. Increasing <feedback gain> cannot remove a binding cap; <integral action> cannot overcome inadequate physical capacity either.

= Physiological set point
{parent=Homeostasis}

A <physiological set point> is a reference around which regulation is organized, analogous to a <reference signal>. An observed average is not automatically a separately encoded target. A range of acceptable values and different response thresholds can be more informative than a single exact number. <Negative feedback> describes a causal sign; neither perfect constancy nor complete restoration follows from that sign alone.

For <core body temperature>, <circadian rhythms> and <fever> can change the defended level. Thus a response toward the current <physiological set point> need not restore yesterday's <temperature>. A drifting target and imperfect tracking of a fixed target are different explanations for a changing readout. <Fundamental Concepts of Human Thermoregulation and Adaptation to Heat: A Review in the Context of Global Warming by Chin Leong Lim>[§2.3.1, Figure 1 and following paragraphs: circadian and pyrogen-related changes of the temperature set point]{ref}

= Homeostatic temperature model
{parent=Homeostasis}
{title2=$\tau\dot x=d-x-u$}

Here is an explicitly synthetic <mathematical model> of a warming disturbance and its compensatory cooling. It is a teaching analogy for <thermoregulation>, not a measured human heat budget. Write $x=(T-T_r)/\Delta T$, where $T$ is a lumped internal <temperature>, $T_r$ is a fixed reference and $\Delta T>0$ is a chosen <temperature> scale. Let $d$ be an additional heating rate and $u$ an additional cooling rate, both scaled to the same reference rate. All three are <dimensionless variables>, while $\tau>0$ is a <time constant> in the chosen time units. The <ordinary differential equation> is
$$
\tau\dot x=d-x-u,\qquad u=kx,\qquad k\geq0.
$$
The term $-x$ idealizes increased passive <heat transfer> with increased internal <temperature>, around the baseline. The term $-u$ opposes warming through <proportional control> with <feedback gain> $k$. This local signed model does not treat a negative $u$ as negative <sweating>: it would represent decreased cooling relative to baseline. Changes of the environment and multiple body compartments are omitted.

For a constant disturbance $d=D$ and <initial condition> $x(0)=0$, the <exponential function> solution is
$$
x(t)=\frac{D}{1+k}\left(1-e^{-(1+k)t/\tau}\right),\qquad x_*=\frac{D}{1+k},\qquad u_*=\frac{kD}{1+k}.
$$
Substitution checks the <ordinary differential equation> directly. Increasing $k$ reduces the lasting offset and, in this instantaneous model, shortens the response <time constant> to $\tau/(1+k)$. It does not make $x_*$ zero for a nonzero disturbance and finite $k$: a persistent cooling command requires a persistent displacement under <proportional control>. At the <equilibrium point>, the extra heating is balanced by passive loss $x_*$ plus active cooling $u_*$. Zero change of <temperature> means balanced rates, rather than zero rates, consistent with <conservation of energy>.

The first diagram uses $D=1$, $\tau=4$, and a pulse from $t=2$ to $t=10$, comparing $k=0$ with $k=3$. The latter approaches $x_*=1/4$ during the pulse, compared with $1$ without active <feedback control>. After the pulse ends, each displacement decays as $x(10)e^{-(1+k)(t-10)/\tau}$. These are computed curves, not physiological measurements. The $k=0$ curve still has passive loss; it is not a claim that an unregulated body warms without limit.

Real <physiological effectors> take time to respond. To isolate that issue, set $\tau=1$ and replace the instantaneous command by another <ordinary differential equation>:
$$
\dot x=1-x-u,\qquad \dot u=4x-u,\qquad x(0)=u(0)=0.
$$
Here the disturbance remains $1$ from $t=0$ onward and the effector <time constant> is also $1$. The <equilibrium point> is still $(x_*,u_*)=(1/5,4/5)$, but the <characteristic polynomial> is $(\lambda+1)^2+4$. Its <eigenvalues> $-1\pm2i$ give decaying <oscillations> and <asymptotic stability>. The exact response is
$$
x(t)=\frac15+e^{-t}\left(-\frac15\cos(2t)+\frac25\sin(2t)\right),\qquad u(t)=\frac45+e^{-t}\left(-\frac45\cos(2t)-\frac25\sin(2t)\right).
$$
In particular, $\dot x=e^{-t}\cos(2t)$, so the first maximum occurs at $t=\pi/4$, with $x=1/5+(2/5)e^{-\pi/4}\simeq0.3824$. The response exceeds its eventual displacement while remaining above its baseline. The return has the same negative sign as before; the extra state changes the timing. A finite effector <time constant> is distinct from the fixed time lag in the <delayed negative feedback model>.

\Image[homeostasis-feedback-lag-capacity.png]
{title=Synthetic <homeostasis> responses: offset, lag and limited effector capacity}
{description=Original computed plots from the <homeostatic temperature model>, all in arbitrary units and explicitly labelled synthetic. Upper panel: displacement under the same sustained disturbance. Lower panel: corresponding cooling command. Instantaneous <proportional control>, a finite effector <time constant>, and <effector saturation> give different responses despite opposing the same disturbance. No experimental data or clinical limits are shown.}

= Thermoregulation
{parent=Homeostasis}
{wiki}

<Thermoregulation> regulates body <temperature>. <Skin> signals can anticipate a change of <core body temperature>, so a response need not wait for an internal error. <Cortical, thalamic, and hypothalamic responses to cooling and warming the skin in awake humans: A positron-emission tomography study by Gary F. Egan and others>[Introduction, paragraphs beginning The reflex regulation and In humans, the contribution from skin temperature: thermoregulatory effectors and feedforward skin input]{ref}

These responses have costs and environmental limits. <Shivering> consumes metabolic resources; cooling through <sweating> removes <water> that must eventually be replaced. High <humidity> restricts <evaporation>, so secreted sweat and successful cooling are not interchangeable. Maintaining <core body temperature> also depends on maintaining circulation: depleted blood volume can compromise <skin> blood flow and <heat transfer>. Seeking shade or reducing work changes the disturbance itself, whereas a larger regulatory command merely attempts to counter it. <Fundamental Concepts of Human Thermoregulation and Adaptation to Heat: A Review in the Context of Global Warming by Chin Leong Lim>[§2.2.4, Evaporation: sweat secretion versus evaporation and blood-volume depletion; §2.3.1: behavioral heat conservation and dissipation]{ref}

= Core body temperature
{parent=Thermoregulation}
{title2=$T_c$}

<Core body temperature> refers to internal <temperature>, distinguished from <skin> <temperature>. Different measurement sites are proxies with their own limitations, not interchangeable readings of a perfectly uniform interior. <Fundamental Concepts of Human Thermoregulation and Adaptation to Heat: A Review in the Context of Global Warming by Chin Leong Lim>[§2.1.1, Core Temperature: internal sites and indirect measurement]{ref}

= Skin-temperature perturbation experiment
{parent=Thermoregulation}

Twelve resting volunteers wore a water-perfused suit. Tubes covered the torso, upper arms and legs; the right forearm remained uncovered for laser Doppler blood-flow measurement. Six <thermocouples> estimated mean <skin> <temperature>; a sublingual <thermocouple> measured oral <temperature>. A dry-air capsule measured local sweat output from outgoing air moisture. <Cortical, thalamic, and hypothalamic responses to cooling and warming the skin in awake humans: A positron-emission tomography study by Gary F. Egan and others>[Methods, Temperature-Control Procedures: garment, uncovered forearm, blood-flow probe, ventilated capsule and thermocouples]{ref}

After two control scans near $34\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ <skin> <temperature>, the suit cooled and then warmed the <skin>. Mean oral <temperature> was $36.6\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ during both cooling and early warming; mean <skin> readings were $30.5$ and $38.2\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$. Cutaneous vascular conductance fell during cooling and rose during warming. The inference is that compensatory blood-flow responses can precede an appreciable internal <temperature> change. <Cortical, thalamic, and hypothalamic responses to cooling and warming the skin in awake humans: A positron-emission tomography study by Gary F. Egan and others>[Methods, Experimental Protocol; Results, Body Temperature and Hemodynamic Responses; Table 1, SC1 and H1]{ref}

Repeated baselines controlled comparison within participants; closed mouths reduced oral-probe cooling artifacts. A later control with reduced oral <temperature> was excluded. Oral <temperature> is a proxy, local flow is not whole-body <heat transfer>, and the fixed condition order leaves time effects possible. Brain imaging measured regional blood flow, not direct <neuron> firing or causal necessity. <Cortical, thalamic, and hypothalamic responses to cooling and warming the skin in awake humans: A positron-emission tomography study by Gary F. Egan and others>[Methods, Temperature-Control Procedures, Experimental Protocol and Imaging Procedures; Results, exclusion of C3; Table 1]{ref}

= Blood sugar
{parent=Homeostasis}

= Blood glucose
{synonym}

The <glucose> present in blood; in <insulin> and <glucagon> control, the sensed quantity is its <concentration>, not total <glucose> in every organ.

= Synthetic insulin-glucagon response model
{parent=Homeostasis}
{title2=$\tau_I\dot I=I_\infty(g)-I,\quad\tau_H\dot H=H_\infty(g)-H$}

This deliberately synthetic <differential equation> model illustrates response direction and lag. Every plotted number is chosen for teaching; none is a biological measurement or a fitted human parameter. Let externally prescribed, <dimensionless> $g>0$ represent a <glucose> stimulus relative to a reference. Let $I$ and $H$ be normalized <insulin> and <glucagon> response variables, with independently chosen <time constants> $\tau_I=4$ and $\tau_H=6$ in arbitrary time units:
$$
I_\infty(g)=\frac{g^4}{1+g^4},\qquad H_\infty(g)=\frac{1}{1+g^4},\qquad \tau_I\dot I=I_\infty(g)-I,\qquad \tau_H\dot H=H_\infty(g)-H.
$$
Here $I_\infty$ rises and $H_\infty$ falls with $g$. Their targets add to one by construction; the evolving $I+H$ need not equal one because their <time constants> differ. Start with $g=1$ and $I=H=1/2$. Set $g=1.6$ from $t=10$ to $40$, $g=0.65$ from $t=40$ to $70$, and $g=1$ otherwise. Within a constant-input interval beginning at $t_0$, the <exponential decay> solution is
$$
I(t)=I_\infty+[I(t_0)-I_\infty]e^{-(t-t_0)/\tau_I},\qquad H(t)=H_\infty+[H(t_0)-H_\infty]e^{-(t-t_0)/\tau_H}.
$$
Each response is a <continuous function> of time even when the imposed stimulus jumps. The chosen starting values stay between zero and one, since each solution is a <weighted arithmetic mean> of its interval's starting value and target.

\Image[synthetic-insulin-glucagon-step-response.png]
{title=Synthetic <insulin> and <glucagon> responses to imposed <glucose> steps}
{description=Original illustration of the <synthetic insulin-glucagon response model>. All curves are constructed examples in arbitrary units. Top: imposed <dimensionless> stimulus. Middle: responses with unequal <time constants>. Bottom: illustrative <liver> release and tissue uptake tendencies, which do not determine the imposed stimulus. No experimental data or clinical ranges are represented.}

For an illustrative downstream comparison, define release tendency $R=0.3+0.6H/(1+I)$ and uptake tendency $U=(0.25+0.5I)g$, in the same arbitrary flux units. At the reference target, $R=U=0.5$. At the high-stimulus targets, $R\approx0.343$ and $U\approx1.094$; at the low-stimulus targets, $R\approx0.742$ and $U\approx0.212$. These chosen functions encode greater uptake with <insulin> and greater release with <glucagon>, moderated by <insulin>; they are not established <enzyme kinetics>. The $g$ trace is imposed externally, so the figure does not demonstrate self-correction of <blood glucose>. A closed model would also need a <conservation of mass> equation, nutrient input, finite <glycogen> stores, and additional tissue interactions. The qualitative restoring loops are shown separately in the <insulin> circuit.

= Medicine
{parent=Biology}
{wiki}

= Infection
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

An <infection> involves invasion and multiplication of an infectious agent in a <host>. <Bacteria> are one possible cause; an agent capable of causing <disease> is a <pathogen>. The National Cancer Institute defines infection through invasion and growth in the body. https://www.cancer.gov/publications/dictionaries/cancer-terms/def/infection[NCI Dictionary: infection]{ref}.

In a <SIR model>, acquiring <infection> moves a member of the <susceptible compartment> into the <infectious compartment>. That immediate transition is a model assumption: the <SEIR model> instead includes an exposed stage before infectiousness. Specify these states when translating a biological process into <compartmental models (epidemiology)>.

= Immunity
{disambiguate=medical}
{parent=Medicine}

= Immunity
{synonym}

In infection modelling, <immunity> is protection against infection or disease, with its strength and duration represented by the chosen <host> states. A <SIR model> has a permanently protected recovered state, whereas a <SIS model> has no lasting post-recovery protection.

= Isolation
{disambiguate=health care}
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

<Isolation (health care)> separates recognized infected or ill individuals from susceptible contacts to limit transmission. <Quarantine> instead concerns exposed individuals who may or may not be infected.

= Psychoactive drug
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

A psychoactive drug is a substance that changes mental activity, mood or perception. In population <causal inference>, its per-user adverse-event risk and its total population harm are distinct: the latter also depends on how many people use it, for how long, and on substitution between substances.

= Mephedrone
{parent=Psychoactive drug}
{wiki}

Mephedrone is 4-methylmethcathinone, a synthetic stimulant <psychoactive drug>. The chemical identity is described in https://www.euda.europa.eu/publications/drug-profiles/synthetic-cathinones_en[the EUDA synthetic-cathinone profile]. In <causal inference>, an association between increased use and fewer deaths involving another drug does not itself establish substitution: changes in use, population composition and ascertainment must be distinguished.

= MDMA
{c}
{parent=Psychoactive drug}
{wiki}

= Ecstasy
{synonym}

MDMA is 3,4-methylenedioxymethamphetamine, a <psychoactive drug> commonly called ecstasy. A product's street label does not establish its measured chemical contents. Epidemiological comparisons should distinguish single-substance outcomes from outcomes attributed to several drugs.

= Cocaine
{parent=Psychoactive drug}
{wiki}

Cocaine is a stimulant <psychoactive drug>. Comparisons of its population harm require exposure denominators and consistent definitions of drug-related outcomes, rather than death counts alone.

= Informed consent
{parent=Medicine}
{wiki}

Informed consent is voluntary agreement after receiving and understanding relevant information about an intervention or participation in research. In a <clinical trial>, the participant is informed about the study procedures, potential benefits and risks, alternatives and the option to decline or withdraw. Consent precedes <randomization>.

= Migraine
{parent=Neurology}
{wiki}

Migraine is a neurological disorder that can be an outcome in a <clinical trial> or an <observational study>. Its statistical association with an <exposure> does not alone establish a <causal effect>.

= Disease
{parent=Medicine}

= Diabetes

= Type 1 diabetes
{parent=Diabetes}

= Type 2 diabetes
{parent=Diabetes}
{wiki}

= Insulin resistance
{parent=Diabetes}
{wiki}

A reduced biological response to <insulin>; it is a property of response, not simply a low <insulin> <concentration>. The <glucose clamp technique> helps separate these quantities.

= Prion disease
{parent=Disease}

A prion disease is associated with self-propagating abnormal folding of a <protein>. Its epidemiological interpretation requires distinguishing exposure, infection, clinical onset and diagnostic detection.

= Variant Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease
{parent=Prion disease}

= vCJD
{c}
{synonym}

A human <prion disease> associated with exposure to the <BSE> agent. Exposure totals and clinical-onset totals measure different stages of the disease process; <incubation periods> and ascertainment affect their relationship.

= Scrapie
{parent=Prion disease}

A <prion disease> of sheep and goats. A <scrapie> test result and a <BSE> diagnosis are distinct outcomes, even when the clinical appearances overlap.

= Bovine spongiform encephalopathy
{parent=Prion disease}

= BSE
{c}
{synonym}

A <prion disease> of cattle. A positive surveillance test is an observed diagnostic outcome, whose frequency need not equal true infection <prevalence> unless test performance and sampling are accounted for.

= Myelodysplastic syndrome
{parent=Disease}
{wiki}

= MDS
{synonym}
{c}

= Chronic disease
{parent=Disease}

A chronic disease persists over an extended period. A <continuous-time multi-state model> can represent its progression through severity states, but long duration alone does not imply irreversible progression or justify ignoring state misclassification.
